    Basics of Business I — Marketing

    Basics of Business I — Marketing

    (Instructor: Prof. Ramesh Venkateswaran , adjunct faculty, IIM Bangalore — Modules 5 and 6)

    Character of this course: almost no numbers, all frameworks, definitions and attributions. MCQs will therefore be "which of the following is NOT one of the 4 Ps", "AIDA stands for", "who said…", and "match the brand to its positioning". Learn the acronyms cold and the named examples with them.

    1.1Why organisations exist2

    An organisation exists for ONE reason only: BECAUSE SOMEBODY WANTS ITS OUTPUT. If people did not want what it does, the organisation does not exist.

    • This has nothing to do with making money — it applies equally to commercial, not-for-profit, social and government organisations. Why does a passport office exist? Because people want to go abroad.
    • The output = the PRODUCT or SERVICE. The person who wants it = the CUSTOMER. Without those two you do not have an organisation.
    • Convention used throughout the course: the word "product" means "product or service", and "marketing" covers both marketing and sales.

    Product vs service, sales vs marketing

    Term Definition given
    Product Tangible — mobile phone, car, refrigerator
    Service Credit card, teaching, education, medical
    SALES (textbook definition) The TRANSFER OF OWNERSHIP of a good from the SELLER to the BUYER
    Marketing The bigger context — every sales transaction involves many marketing functions

    What is common to ALL sales and marketing transactions? likely MCQ

    EVERY SALES AND MARKETING TRANSACTION IS AN EXCHANGE BETWEEN TWO HUMAN BEINGS — a human seller and a human buyer.

    Therefore marketing is fundamentally about UNDERSTANDING AND MANAGING PEOPLE. The job of a salesperson is "asking the other person to do something which is in the interest of the seller" — i.e. to do something DIFFERENT from what they are doing now.

    Technology is NOT a substitute for the human being. The internet, AI, e-commerce platforms, internet banking, online ticketing — "all of these are INTERMEDIARIES TO FACILITATE THE EXCHANGE." The seller designing them is human; the buyer is human.

    The two great attributions guaranteed MCQ| Who | What they said |

    |---|---| | PETER DRUCKER ("the Vishva Guru") | "The business enterprise has TWO — and only two — basic functions: MARKETING AND INNOVATION. They produce results; ALL OTHERS ARE COSTS." | | The instructor ("the Local Guru") | "The business enterprise actually has only ONE function, and that is MARKETING." Marketing is the only value-added function — because innovation is required in ALL business activities (IT, finance, operations, supply chain, HR), so it is not marketing's peer |

    The logic: supply chain, production, IT, HR, operations, accounts and finance are all important, but they exist to ensure the final objective is met — transferring the product to the customer who has a use for it. "If that transaction does not end moving from one side to the other, all other operations are essentially a waste."

    1.2The competitive context — why marketing matters now5

    The sports analogy the course's signature illustration; likely MCQ on the figures

    The point: in world-class competition the difference between number 1 and number 8 is almost nothing — exactly like business.

    Event Figures given
    110 m hurdles, World Athletics Final, ATHENS 2009 Winner 13.14 s; second 13.15 s; the difference between 1st and 8th = 0.4 seconds
    100 m hurdles, 2024 Olympics Winner 9.784 s; second 9.789 s — ## a difference of 0.005 seconds, in the THIRD DECIMAL PLACE. 1st to 8th = 0.13 seconds
    Tennis, 2001 final Winner 154 points vs runner-up 150 — 4 points out of 304 played
    Tennis, Djokovic vs Federer (year-end final, 11 years later) 96 points vs 95 — one point in 200, i.e. HALF A PERCENT
    Tennis, 2015 2 points out of 292 played
    The prize money asymmetry For a one-point difference: winner $1.24 million, runner-up $0.41 million — ## the prize money is DISPROPORTIONATE to the performance difference

    THE BUSINESS PARALLEL — and the harsher version: "In sports, at least the RUNNER-UP GETS SOME PRIZE. In business, THE RUNNER-UP GETS NO ORDER."

    Everything is a commodity now ← key concept

    DEFINITION OF A COMMODITY: "something where I don't see substantial differences between one and the other."

    The claim: whether it is a soap, a car (BMW vs Audi), a bank, an insurer, or a BILLION-DOLLAR SOFTWARE CONTRACT (Infosys vs Accenture vs TCS vs Wipro vs Cognizant) — ## in the eyes of the customer, the product is essentially a COMMODITY.

    The instructor's formulation to remember: "COMMODITIES ARE TECHNOLOGISED AND TECHNOLOGIES ARE COMMODITISED."

    The two questions every business must answer likely MCQ

    1. HOW DO I STAND OUT IN THIS CROWD? 2. WHAT IS THE REASON WHY THE CUSTOMER SHOULD CHOOSE ME?

    "Marketing is THE ABILITY TO STAND OUT IN A CROWD" — to tailor all your activities to tell the customer why he or she should choose you and not the alternative.

    Why marketing is harder than engineering

    In science and technology, if you do A you get B — connect the circuit correctly and the light comes on; code correctly and it works. ## "But there is NO GUARANTEE that if I do this, my customer will respond, because EVERY HUMAN BEING IS DIFFERENT." There is a science and a method to marketing, but no two-plus-two-equals-four.

    The six forces changing business today likely list MCQ

    1. A FLAT WORLD / GLOBALISATION — no boundaries · 2. THE INTERNET has cut out boundaries (a seller in Bangalore, Rajkot or Batala can reach a customer anywhere) · 3. Products are essentially IDENTICAL / commoditised · 4. COMMUNICATION IS AVAILABLE TO EVERYBODY — nobody has a right over it · 5. CUSTOMER AWARENESS AND EDUCATION LEVELS have risen ("I know more about iPhones than the Samsung salesman, because he only sells his product whereas I look across all products") · 6. ATTITUDES AND EXPECTATIONS have gone up — switching costs nothing

    The Apple statistics guaranteed MCQ| Metric | Figure |

    |---|---| | Share of global smartphone UNITS sold | 15–20% (about 20%) | | Share of smartphone industry REVENUE | About 50% | | Share of smartphone industry PROFITS | About 80% | | Price vs the market | About 2.5× the average of all other sellers | | Other Apple facts cited | First company to hit $1 TRILLION market capitalisation · considered the most innovative company · most valuable brand · held over $400 billion in cash · ## the HIGHEST REVENUE PER SQUARE FOOT of any retail store — more than jewellery companies |

    The explanation offered: "How does this happen? PURELY MARKETING" — convincing the customer to pay four, five, six times for a product available cheaply elsewhere, through communication, advertising and consumer perception management.

    The everyday version of the same point: earphones from ## ₹677 up to ## Apple AirPods at ₹10,592 — both do the same job. ## "Marketing is the subject that actually CREATES THE VALUE IN THE MIND OF THE CONSUMER."

    The product failure statistic likely MCQ

    "On average only 4 to 5% of products introduced in the market SUCCEED. 90 to 95% of products actually FAIL." — "which is very strange, because nobody goes out with an intention of failing."

    1.3What marketing is — definitions and scope4

    DEFINITION 1: "Marketing is the TASK OF CREATING, PROMOTING AND DELIVERING goods and services to consumers."

    DEFINITION 2 (the one to memorise): "Marketing is the PROCESS OF IDENTIFYING AND SATISFYING CUSTOMER NEEDS."

    DEFINITION OF SELLING: "The process of PERSUADING OR CONVINCING a person that your product or service is OF GREATER VALUE THAN THE PRICE."

    THE THREE TYPES OF NEED guaranteed MCQ| Type | Definition | Example given |

    |---|---|---| | STATED | The customer explicitly says it | "I'm building a house, I want tiles" · "I lost my phone, I need a phone" | | IMPLIED | Not specifically stated, but it IS a need | A travelling salesperson on a fixed monthly petrol allowance with a call target → the implied need is ## GOOD MILEAGE. A different salesperson unworried about money → the implied need is ## RELIABILITY / no breakdowns / low maintenance / 100% availability | | LATENT | Not expressed — the customer does not even realise they have the need; brought to the surface by ## DISRUPTIVE INNOVATION | The mobile phone (nobody asked for WhatsApp or video calling) · Uber/Ola ("did I ever know I could order a taxi standing on the roadside?") |

    The THREE Cs likely MCQ

    Whenever we talk of marketing there are always THREE players in the game: THE COMPANY · THE CUSTOMER · THE COMPETITION — "the three Cs."

    The scope of marketing — what can be marketed likely list MCQ

    GOODS · SERVICES · EXPERIENCES (theme parks, fun worlds) · EVENTS (music events) · PEOPLE (politics, PR, personal image on social media) · PLACES (tourism) · PROPERTIES (houses, plots, rentals) · ORGANISATIONS (donations, share participation) · INFORMATION · IDEAS (start-ups convincing venture capital)

    Types of market likely MCQ| Type | Meaning |

    |---|---| | D2C — Direct to Consumer | No intermediary. Platforms are the classic modern example; TESLA famously says "I don't need a dealer" | | Through INTERMEDIARIES | Wholesaler → retailer → consumer | | B2B — Business to Business | Selling a machine tool to an automobile manufacturer, oils, steel — the buyer uses it to make something else | | By geography | National · regional · global markets. Selling globally adds culture and country policy to the equation | | Not-for-profit / social | Different priorities; you cannot use a film-star brand ambassador the way a consumer brand does | | Government markets | Transparency and due diligence requirements are very different from a private company where one person decides |

    1.4What people actually buy — the FOUR LEVELS of a product guaranteed MCQ2

    1. THE CORE PRODUCT or service · 2. THE AUGMENTED product/service · 3. THE ENVIRONMENT · 4. THE EXPERIENCE

    Level What it is Examples given
    CORE The basic product itself The car, the food, the shirt, the mobile phone
    AUGMENTED Everything bundled around it Dealer finance and refinance · buy-back of your old car · extended warranty · pick-up and drop for servicing; in a restaurant, valet parking
    ENVIRONMENT The physical (or digital) setting Showrooms, dealer workshops, retail outlets, petrol pumps, corporate offices — landscaping, horticulture, ambience. ## For a platform, "the environment is NOT a factory — it's the WEBSITE" (navigation, ease of access)
    EXPERIENCE How you were treated; the process of delivery The user experience — ease of use, ease of navigating a website. ## "One of the biggest differentiators today is EXPERIENCE"

    The three teaching stories behind this framework each is a likely MCQ prompt| Story | The lesson | |---|---| | THE ARROW SHIRT SHOWROOM | You are brand-loyal, quality-conscious and reasonable — but the salespeople chat among themselves and ignore you. ## 8 out of 10 people say they would WALK OUT. "Do you walk in to buy a SHIRT, or do you walk in to buy ATTENTION? To feel important? To feel welcome?" → you are buying more than the product | | THE AUDI / BMW / MERCEDES LOGO | A dealer friend offers the car at a discount on condition that you cannot fit the logo. ## The logo costs only ₹2,000–5,000 to make, so economics says take any discount above that. But at ₹20,000 discount, 10 out of 10 students refuse; only at ₹3–5 lakh do hands start going up. ## "Am I buying a car or am I buying the logo? The truth is I'm buying BOTH" — you are buying aspiration, ambition, the envy of colleagues, status | | THE FIVE-STAR HOTEL | You go for the food, but fused bulbs, dirty linen and stains on the tablecloth mean 10 out of 10 say they would not return |

    The entertainment examples — the pizza maker, the ice cream seller and the DUDHWALA (milkman): customers watch and are amused. ## They are not selling pizza, ice cream and milk — they are selling ENTERTAINMENT and an EVENING OF FUN. The key word is FUN.

    The killer observation: at EVERY one of the four levels, competitors are now IDENTICAL. Every car dealer offers buy-back, extended warranty and pick-up (augmented); every dealership, bank branch and phone store looks the same (environment). So experience is what is left to differentiate on.

    1.5When is an organisation successful? And when do people buy?2

    "A company is only successful when you have HAPPY CUSTOMERS." — the one-word answer.

    The three things that happen when a customer is happy likely MCQ

    1. The customer COMES BACK (loyalty) · 2. The customer BUYS MORE from you (other products) · 3. The customer TELLS OTHERS to buy from you

    "We go back to places that make us happy. We avoid places that make us unhappy."

    When do people buy? ← the central concept

    PEOPLE WILL ONLY BUY WHEN THERE IS A GAP BETWEEN WHERE I AM AND WHERE I WANT TO BE. A NEED IS ESSENTIALLY A GAP between what I desire and where I am. THE GAP IS DISSATISFACTION.

    THE RULE: "YOU CANNOT SELL TO A SATISFIED PERSON."

    So the job of marketing is two-step: (1) IDENTIFY the gap/dissatisfaction, or (2) CREATE the dissatisfaction — "I'm happy where I am, but I realise things could be different."

    Today's problem: most gaps have already been filled — so new products (mobile phones, video calling, gaming, apps) create a need that did not exist. ## "We didn't need a Swiggy or a Zomato. Today, without Swiggy and Zomato, I find life difficult. These people have done an excellent job of CREATING A GAP."

    THE AIDA MODEL guaranteed MCQ

    A = AWARENESS → I = INTEREST → D = DESIRE → A = ACTION

    Dates from about 1925 — "about a hundred years since this model came out" — and "it has stood the test of time."

    Stage What it means The dishwasher/vacuum-cleaner illustration
    AWARENESS A product like this exists, or a need like this can be satisfied "I don't need a dishwasher — things get done anyway, we have domestic help"
    INTEREST "Let me find out more" Double-income family; help is hard to get → "maybe a dishwasher would be useful"
    DESIRE "I really must have this" — now start exploring "My friend has got it"; look at models, alternatives, prices, features, delivery
    ACTION The actual purchase Go to the dealer and buy

    Marketing has a distinct set of actions at EACH stage, and those actions differ by product type: mass-market products (toothpaste, shampoo) → mass communication; consumer durables → mass communication PLUS personal selling; B2B → mass advertising does NOT work, FOCUSED TARGET MARKETING is required.

    The simple marketing system

    SELLER → communication (AIDA) → BUYER → order and payment → seller supplies goods and services → the communication loop continues. Essentially: the transfer of goods and services for an equivalent compensation.**

    1.6The football lesson — how marketing fits with every other function1

    Three clips, each ending in a goal: (1) one player takes a solo run; (2) five or six players and a few passes; (3) 44 passes touched by all 10 outfield players.

    The four lessons likely MCQ| # | Lesson | |---|---| | 1 | "MY JOB IS COMPLETE ONLY WHEN I SCORE A GOAL." Passing and fancy manoeuvres are means, not ends. In business: "My job is not to have a great factory or fantastic people — my job is, AM I MAKING A SALE?" | | 2 | "THE GOAL CAN HAPPEN ONLY IF THE BALL IS WITH YOU." So set intermediate objectives: every business must know what key factors it needs to keep under its control — Apple: innovation, creativity, simplicity; Amazon: reliability, wide spread of products, taking back products; Blinkit: 10 minutes | | 3 | THERE IS NO RIGHT OR WRONG WAY. Solo run = high risk, high reward; 44 passes = "the longer I keep the ball, the greater the chances of losing it." "How quick is quick enough? There is no answer." | | 4 | ALL 10 OUTFIELD PLAYERS TOUCHED THE BALL — so WHO was responsible for the goal? EVERYBODY. ## The GOAL SCORER is the SALES/MARKETING person — "the one who converts all the activities into the final result." But "if the 44th pass goes over the goalpost, all your 43 passes are wasted." |

    1.7THE MARKETING MIX — THE 4 Ps guaranteed MCQ7

    PRODUCT · PRICE · PLACE · PROMOTION

    "Marketing is the task of selling the RIGHT PRODUCT at the RIGHT PRICE in the RIGHT PLACE through the RIGHT PROMOTION" — obviously, to the right person.

    Note: modern books add a 5th P (PROCESS) and a 6th P (PEOPLE), "but traditionally and conventionally the four Ps are still very, very relevant."

    P1 — PRODUCT: the five decision areas likely MCQ| # | Decision area | The questions and trade-offs |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | FEATURES | WHY this feature (customer wants it? competition gives it?), WHAT features, and — the marketer's special contribution — ## HOW MUCH of the feature. ## "Design and marketing is the subject of TRADE-OFF: to get something you have to give up something." Longer battery → bigger battery → bigger, heavier phone. Sleek phone → lower battery life → smaller screen (a 5-inch screen consumes more power than a 3-inch). More megapixels → higher cost | | 2 | RANGE / VARIETY | More variety = wider customer choice but higher production, inventory, stock-out and obsolescence costs; and offering variety you can't supply CREATES dissatisfaction. ## APPLE = deliberately FEW models (at one time only 32 GB or 64 GB); ## Samsung/Oppo = WIDE range. "There is nothing to say one is right or wrong." | | 3 | BRAND | See the Audi logo story. ## Two opposite branding strategies: AMUL — one master brand on EVERYTHING (Amul milk, butter, cheese, kulfi, ice-cream); P&G / HUL — the parent is INVISIBLE (Tide, Ariel, Pampers, Gillette, Old Spice are all P&G and "you will not even know"). Building a brand is hugely expensive and hard to exit | | 4 | PACKAGING | Material (plastic, glass, tin, can, soft pack, tetra pack, laminated) and SIZES. A can stacks on a shelf and becomes an ad; a bottle can be reused but doesn't stack. Sizes drive ## SKUs — STOCK KEEPING UNITS; every extra SKU adds cost of production, inventory, stock-out and obsolescence | | 5 | SERVICE | Warranty, what happens on failure, pick-up and drop, accidents, returns. ## AMAZON's service innovations: the PATENTED "ONE CLICK" ordering · reliable delivery-date promises · easy returns/refunds/replacement · recommendations ("people who bought this also bought…"). ## FedEx's famous service feature: a MONEY-BACK GUARANTEE if a packet is delivered after 10 o'clock — "even if we are a second late" |

    P2 — PRICE guaranteed MCQ

    COST IS A FACT. PRICE IS A PERCEPTION. Cost = raw material + direct + indirect. Price is influenced by brand, differentiation, segment, positioning and the whole marketing mix.

    DEFINITION OF VALUE: "Value is when the customer FEELS he or she is GETTING MORE THAN WHAT HE OR SHE GIVES."

    Side of the exchange Components
    What the customer GETS The core product + augmented + environment + experience; the reassurance of service; the image of the brand; emotional value
    What the customer GIVES Not only the purchase price — also TIME, OPPORTUNITY, and the COST OF RISK ("do I know this product? what if it doesn't work?")

    THE FOUR DRIVERS OF PROFIT guaranteed formula MCQ

    PROFIT = REVENUE − COST, where REVENUE = PRICE × VOLUME and COST = FIXED COST + VARIABLE COST. The four levers: PRICE · VOLUME · FIXED COST · VARIABLE COST.

    THE PRICE LEVERAGE ARITHMETIC — memorise this worked example: Selling price ₹100, profit ₹10 (a 10% margin). Raise the price by ₹1 to ₹101 → profit rises from ₹10 to ₹11. A 1% IMPROVEMENT IN PRICE PRODUCES A 10% IMPROVEMENT IN PROFIT. "Price gives you the MAXIMUM LEVERAGING OPPORTUNITY" — and who gets you that 1%? THE MARKETING PEOPLE.

    THE TWO COMPONENTS OF VALUE guaranteed MCQ| Component | Definition | Examples |

    |---|---|---| | FUNCTIONAL value | Measurable performance | Mileage · pickup · maintenance · comfort | | EMOTIONAL / PSYCHOLOGICAL value | Feeling, status, aesthetics | The Mercedes/BMW badge · car colours and accessories · ## the CHILDREN'S TOOTHBRUSH — bright colours and designs add NOTHING to functional performance but carry huge emotional value · laptops in pink, purple, blue, green and red |

    Components of pricing likely list MCQ

    LIST PRICE (MRP) · DISCOUNTED PRICE · SCHEMES (buy one get one free; buy 3 get the 4th free; buy 5 get 20% off the 6th) · CREDIT AND PAYMENT TERMS (a month's credit is worth the interest cost) · BUNDLING (phone plans bundling data, international, roaming and OTT platforms)

    Two legitimate opposite pricing strategies: ## premium brands (Nike, Boss, Apple, BMW, Taj) make money through MARGINS; MARUTI SUZUKI is a "value brand" that makes money through VOLUMES. "You have excellent dhabas and excellent five-star hotels — both deliver value for money at the price they charge, to their set of customers."

    P3 — PLACE

    DEFINITION: "Place is the ability to make the product or service AVAILABLE AT THE RIGHT PLACE AT THE RIGHT TIME" — all the activities between the manufacturer and the customer: transportation, logistics, godowns, warehouses, retail outlets, delivery.

    The three functions of distribution likely MCQ

    1. TRANSACTIONS (enabling the sale itself) · 2. LOGISTICS (moving the product from factory to the customer) · 3. FACILITATION (salespeople, call centres, delivery people)

    The three channel structures:

    DIRECT (seller → buyer, "D2C") · INDIRECT (seller → intermediary → … → buyer) · HYBRID (a combination)

    The three intermediary types: ## RETAILER — the final link to the consumer · WHOLESALER — stocks in bulk because the retailer lacks the finance and resources to hold variety · AGENTS AND BROKERS — get the orders and facilitate between the parties.

    THE BIG SHIFT: "PLACE IS NO LONGER RESTRICTED TO A PHYSICAL LOCATION. The word PLACE has been replaced by the word ACCESS." Amazon, Flipkart, Myntra, Big Basket, Blinkit, dark stores, 10-minute delivery. ## And it is not only access to a PRODUCT — it is ACCESS TO INFORMATION ("people who bought this also bought…"), which means marketing is no longer just satisfying a need but CREATING one.

    P4 — PROMOTION

    Promotion = promoting the availability, the relevance and the characteristics of the product — everything from a large advertising campaign to a personal selling situation**.

    Promotion maps onto AIDA: communication does the work of AWARENESS → INTEREST → DESIRE, ending in ACTION.

    Channels named: advertising · billboards · banners · TV and cinema ads · wall paintings in villages · leaflets and pamphlets in newspapers · personal selling · websites and platform banners · user ratings and reviews.

    ADVERTISING vs PUBLIC RELATIONS — a likely MCQ: "In ADVERTISING YOU PAY FOR EVERYTHING. In PUBLIC RELATIONS you get it as EDITORIAL MATTER." They differ in credibility, reach and number of people who see it.

    1.8THE 4 Ps REFRAMED AS "SAVE" guaranteed MCQ2

    PRODUCT → SOLUTION · PLACE → ACCESS · PRICE → VALUE · PROMOTION → EDUCATION The acronym is SAVE: SOLUTION · ACCESS · VALUE · EDUCATION.

    Old P New term Why
    Product SOLUTION "People are not buying products, they are buying SOLUTIONS" — a solution of convenience, reliability, status, low risk
    Place ACCESS Access to the product, to information, to service, to alternatives — Uber/Ola, Swiggy, doorstep car pick-up
    Price VALUE Price is only the tangible indicator; the customer buys when he believes he got more than he gave
    Promotion EDUCATION Platforms educate: "you bought this, this model has been replaced, you can buy these accessories"

    THE THEODORE LEVITT ATTRIBUTION guaranteed MCQ

    THEODORE LEVITT, a HARVARD professor, wrote the seminal 1960s work "MARKETING MYOPIA" (a Harvard classic), containing the statement: "PEOPLE NEVER BUY QUARTER-INCH DRILLS. THEY BUY QUARTER-INCH HOLES." i.e. PEOPLE BUY PRODUCTS NOT FOR WHAT THEY HAVE, BUT FOR WHAT THEY DO — a SOLUTION.

    1.9STP — SEGMENTATION, TARGETING, POSITIONING guaranteed MCQ6

    S = SEGMENTATION ("who are the groups?") · T = TARGETING ("which group do I go after?") · P = POSITIONING ("what do I say about my product?")

    SEGMENTATION

    DEFINITION: "A SEGMENT IS A GROUP OF PEOPLE WITH SIMILAR CHARACTERISTICS." The purpose: find a HOMOGENEOUS group inside a HETEROGENEOUS population, so that one pricing, communication and media approach works for all of them.

    WHY segment? TO OPTIMISE EFFORT AND RESOURCE UTILISATION. ## The analogy: "I have a gun with limited bullets and a target. One way is to keep shooting and hope. The other is to TAKE AIM. Our resources are limited and our requirements are far more."

    The four B2C bases guaranteed MCQ| Basis | Variables | |---|---| | DEMOGRAPHIC | Age · gender · educational qualification · occupation | | GEOGRAPHIC | Regions, states, districts, countries — "my market is everybody in the Middle East / in Maharashtra / in Uttar Pradesh" | | PSYCHOGRAPHIC | The PERSONALITY of the individual — fun-loving vs serious, extrovert vs introvert, risk-taking vs low-risk-taking. For a low-risk group, the product and communication must minimise the perception of risk | | BEHAVIOURAL | Anything to do with behaviour — the classic split is ## HEAVY USERS vs LIGHT USERS (shampoo once a fortnight vs every day) |

    B2B segmentation bases: ## scale (small / medium / large) · NUMBER OF PEOPLE EMPLOYED (<500, 500–1,000, 1,000–5,000, >5,000) · INDUSTRY CATEGORY (automotive, IT, consumer products) · PARENTAGE (Indian, German, American, Japanese companies — "assuming each has its own nature of buying").

    B2C vs B2B stated plainly: ## B2C goes to the END CONSUMER; B2B goes to an INTERMEDIARY who uses it to produce a product for the end consumer.

    TARGETING — the three criteria guaranteed MCQ| Criterion | The questions |

    |---|---| | 1. ATTRACTIVENESS | How BIG is the market? · What is the GROWTH POTENTIAL? · What is the likely PROFITABILITY / willingness to pay? You may have a large market that isn't growing, or a small one that is | | 2. COMPANY CAPABILITIES AND GOALS | Resources and objectives. ## MARUTI SUZUKI = value for money, MAXIMISING MARKET SHARE and volume. MERCEDES/BMW = premium, MAXIMISING PROFITABILITY — "I don't mind not having market share." Apple is the extreme case of profit maximisation over share | | 3. COMPETITIVE SITUATION | How intense is competition? Two or three strong players holding 70–80% of the market, vs a fragmented market where even the leader holds only 18–20%. "There is no right or wrong way" — mirrors the football lesson |

    POSITIONING guaranteed MCQ

    DEFINITION (the classical one): "POSITIONING IS THE PLACE AN OFFERING OCCUPIES IN THE CONSUMER'S MIND on important attributes RELATIVE TO ALTERNATIVES." The alternative definition: "the consumer's PERCEPTION AND PREFERENCES for a product in relation to its competitors." The key words: THE MIND OF THE CONSUMER.

    THE ATTRIBUTION: the seminal work is by JACK TROUT and AL RIES, in the late 1970s / early 1980s — the book "POSITIONING: THE BATTLE FOR YOUR MIND."

    Why positioning is needed: the market is cluttered with brands and there is an overload of communication, so the consumer is confused. Positioning answers ## "WHO AM I? WHAT DO I STAND FOR?" and is ## a huge INTANGIBLE ASSET built over decades.

    The SIX methods of positioning guaranteed MCQ| # | Method | Examples given |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | By FEATURE / ATTRIBUTE | "INTEL INSIDE" · Pears "with glycerine" · "100% pure cow's milk ghee" on Indian sweets | | 2 | By BENEFIT to the consumer | SURF — "Daag Achhe Hain / daag nikalte hain" (children in dirty clothes, mother unworried) · ## MAGGI — "TWO MINUTES" · toothpastes: bright teeth vs prevents tooth decay vs SENSODYNE for sensitivity | | 3 | By PARENTAGE | TANISHQ — "a TATA product" · "from Raymond", "from the house of…" | | 4 | By METHOD OF MANUFACTURE | Instant coffee — SPRAY-DRIED vs FREEZE-DRIED | | 5 | By CELEBRITY ENDORSEMENT | ROLEX — Federer at Wimbledon, Phil Mickelson / Rory McIlroy in golf · ## NIKE — Michael Jordan and "Air Jordan" · in India, cricketers and film stars | | 6 | (implicitly) By USER / target group | See Liril vs Pears below |

    The classic Indian positioning cases certain match-the-brand MCQ| Brand | Position | Target | Held for |

    |---|---|---|---| | LIRIL | FRESHNESS — the waterfall, the green costume, the lemons | Young people, or "young at heart", who love the open outdoors. ## A Liril ad will NEVER have a child | 40+ years | | PEARS | GENTLE ON THE SKIN; a clear, baby-like COMPLEXION — the daughter asks for "a complexion like my mother", reversing the usual direction | YOUNG MOTHERS — older than the Liril segment. A Pears ad ALWAYS has a mother and child | 40–60 years | | MAGGI | "TWO MINUTES" — positioned NOT as a food but as the answer to a hungry child home from school | Mothers | Never changed — "no other noodle can say 'two minutes', because it belongs to Maggi" | | NIKE | "JUST DO IT" | | | | VICKS (balm) | For CHILDREN, MILD not harsh; for coughs, colds and congestion; applied EXTERNALLY on chest or back, typically at NIGHT. The ad formula worldwide: doorbell → child comes in from the rain with a sneeze → mother rubs Vicks at night → cut to the doorbell again, child returns with a trophy | | 40–60 years | | VOLVO | SAFETY worldwide — BUT in India consumers position it as "a comfortable overnight bus journey." ## This is the classic MISMATCH between the position the COMPANY intends and the position the CONSUMER actually holds | | |

    The instructor's point about execution: "The brilliance of any strategy does not lie only in the STRATEGY — it lies in the EXECUTION."

    1.10Final thoughts

    THE CLOSING LINE TO REMEMBER: "IF YOU DO NOT HAVE PRODUCT QUALITY, FAILURE IS GUARANTEED. BUT IF YOU HAVE PRODUCT QUALITY, SUCCESS IS NOT NECESSARILY GUARANTEED."

    Product quality is now taken for granted — Honda, Toyota, Volkswagen; Reebok, Nike, Skechers, Adidas, Puma are all good products. That is why marketing is the value-added activity: it adds characteristics BEYOND the product to make the business viable.

    Marketing is therefore about: ## going beyond the product · user experience · the value proposition · brand and brand image · building customer relationships · differentiating from competition · building consumer loyalty so people come back and keep buying.

    The instructor's amendment Only ONE function: marketing (because innovation is needed in every function)

    Basics of Business — People Management

    Basics of Business I — People Management & Personal Effectiveness (HRM)

    (Instructor: Prof. Vasanthi Srinivasan , Organizational Behaviour & HRM area, IIM Bangalore; Chairperson, IIMBx Digital Learning Foundation — Modules 0–4)

    Character of this course: frameworks, principles and lists — plus a handful of hard numbers (168 hours, 25 minutes, 80/25, 70/20, 4–6 weeks, 90 days, 624 attributes). MCQs will be "which of the three components of an attitude…", "the Eisenhower matrix quadrant for…", "who wrote Mindset…", and "which support-network role provides clarity".

    2.1Why this module exists

    Effectiveness in the workplace is a combination of TWO dimensions: 1. COMPETENCE (the FinTech knowledge and skills) · 2. DEMONSTRATING THE BEHAVIOURS EXPECTED OF PROFESSIONALS

    The personal-effectiveness module runs alongside the technical content so that you finish "a well-honed professional ready to take on a job."

    2.2Is human behaviour a science?1

    The everyday proof that we already predict behaviour: as a child you came home late; on the way you worked out who would be home, what you would say to each, and what mood they would be in — and adjusted your behaviour accordingly.

    What you actually did — and it is the scientific method in miniature: OBSERVE the phenomena → COLLECT data → LOOK FOR PATTERNS → BUILD HEURISTICS from past experience → PREDICT another person's behaviour. "It's intuitive, it's casual, it is almost unconscious — but the fact that there is a METHOD means there is an element of rules and a basis of SCIENCE."

    The SEVEN disciplines that study human behaviour guaranteed list MCQ| Discipline | What it asks |

    |---|---| | PSYCHOLOGY | What motivates people? Why do people learn as they do? How do children develop? Applied to industry it becomes ## INDUSTRIAL AND ORGANIZATIONAL PSYCHOLOGY | | SOCIOLOGY | How do humans engage as SOCIAL BEINGS? How do STRUCTURES in society influence behaviour? | | SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY | The tensions and the manner in which INDIVIDUAL AGENTS ACT IN THE CONTEXT OF SOCIAL STRUCTURES | | ECONOMICS | Is a human really a UTILITY-MAXIMISING being? Are decisions always rational? To what extent is MONEY a motivator? | | ANTHROPOLOGY | CULTURES — how they are built, norms, practices, ways of living, why cultures differ | | NEUROSCIENCE | THE BRAIN — "the HARDWARE", as distinct from psychology's study of the mind, "the SOFTWARE" | | PHILOSOPHY | "The mother of all sciences" — as old as civilisation |

    2.3THE TWO PRINCIPLES OF HUMAN BEHAVIOUR guaranteed MCQ1

    PRINCIPLE 1: "NO TWO INDIVIDUALS ARE ALIKE." Individual differences are THE HEART OF HUMAN BEHAVIOUR — "the heart of being human is the difference." PRINCIPLE 2: "BEHAVIOUR IS A FUNCTION OF THE INDIVIDUAL AND THE ENVIRONMENT."

    Principle 1 — nature vs nurture

    • Differences arise partly from ## HEREDITY / NATURE / GENETICS and largely from ## NURTURE — upbringing, opportunities, access, context.

    • "There is NO CONCLUSIVE EVIDENCE on how much nature and how much nurture contributes. Research is still AMBIGUOUS."

    • THE MANAGERIAL CONSEQUENCE: because research is ambiguous, managers should START FROM THE ASSUMPTION THAT "NURTURE CONTRIBUTES MORE THAN NATURE" — i.e. "I BELIEVE I CAN NURTURE AND DEVELOP PEOPLE." Your belief about nature vs nurture determines how you engage with your team.

    • Even siblings raised almost identically are very different.

    Principle 2 — the environment

    • The PHYSICAL environment: a normally talkative, sociable, confident person walks into a room of strangers and becomes reserved, withdrawn and quiet until familiar. Same individual, different environment.
    • The PSYCHOLOGICAL environment: an examination room (stress, fear) vs a birthday party vs a wedding vs a FUNERAL — you adapt behaviour to the context.

    • THE MANAGERIAL QUESTION: "Am I creating a SAFE PSYCHOLOGICAL ENVIRONMENT for people to be themselves?" Unless behaviours, attitudes, values and beliefs can come naturally to the fore, people cannot be AUTHENTIC — and the more authentic people are, the more TRUSTING the conversations.

    • The corollary: "IF WE NEED TO CHANGE BEHAVIOURS OF PEOPLE, THEN WE NEED TO CHANGE THE ENVIRONMENT."

    2.4PERSONALITY — the three layers guaranteed MCQ2

    THE METAPHOR: personality is "an ONION VERTICALLY SPLICED" — peel a layer and there is another layer. The alternative metaphor is ## the ICEBERG (the instructor prefers the onion, "coming from a country like India where we see so little ice").

    OUTERMOST → INNERMOST: BEHAVIOURS → ATTITUDES AND BELIEFS → VALUES.

    The research figure to remember: a ## 2014 study titled "What types of terms do people use when describing an individual's personality?" found ## 624 DISTINCT ATTRIBUTES used by participants to describe people they knew.

    Layer Definition Examples
    BEHAVIOURS (easiest to change) "The EXTERNAL, VISIBLE ACTIONS of an individual" — what you see The way a person talks, walks, engages with a group; height, weight, looks, voice, gestures, mannerisms
    ATTITUDES AND BELIEFS (harder) "JUDGMENTS concerning people, events and things" About yourself: "my pay is low", "I don't like folk music". About others: "she is very difficult to work with", "he cannot live up to my standards"
    VALUES (hardest of all) "The CORE for individuals — BASIC CONVICTIONS that people hold; individual judgments about what is RIGHT, GOOD, DESIRABLE and IMPORTANT." ## Simply: things I OUGHT to do, things I OUGHT NOT to do, things OTHERS ought and ought not to do Obedience to elders as a value vs ## EQUALITY — everybody has an equal say to challenge assumptions regardless of age

    THE ORDER OF DIFFICULTY — near-certain MCQ: "Most of the time, what is EASY to do is to change BEHAVIOURS. The MORE DIFFICULT are ATTITUDES, and EVEN MORE DIFFICULT is VALUES."

    More on attitudes and beliefs: - They are formed either through OUR OWN experiences or through HEARD/LISTENED experiences of others — you don't need the experience yourself. - The superstition illustration: the belief in some cultures that a CAT CROSSING THE ROAD is a bad sign — nobody has actually observed the causal link, "yet these beliefs have continued over several generations." - ## "MINDSET" = attitudes and beliefs. When organisations say "people have to change mindsets", that is what they mean — and "it is extremely tough." - ## A large number of CONFLICTS happen because of conflicts between the BELIEFS of two people. - ## Attitudes and beliefs are ENDURING, and humans have a tendency to PICK UP INFORMATION THAT SUPPORTS them and REJECT information that does not. (confirmation bias, in effect)

    On values in practice: a manager may lead in a culture that values the opinions of older people while the organisation's values are equality, openness and challenge. ## Identifying that this conflict between two values is real is a SCIENCE. Engaging with the contradiction while still achieving organisational outcomes is an ART.

    The THREE COMPONENTS OF AN ATTITUDE guaranteed MCQ| Component | What it is | The worked example |

    |---|---|---| | COGNITIVE | The OPINION and BELIEF about something — what I THINK | "My pay is low" | | AFFECTIVE | The EMOTION and FEELING — how I FEEL about it | "Friends of mine get more than me" | | BEHAVIOURAL | The INTENTION TO BEHAVE differently — what I DO about it | Will you QUIT, or look at other aspects of the job beyond pay? |

    THE KEY POINT: these three components MAY NOT BE ALIGNED, and it is because they are not aligned that our actions may not happen. The training example: everybody attends the quality training, but quality does not improve. To create ENDURING behavioural change, you must ensure CONSISTENCY BETWEEN THE COGNITIVE, AFFECTIVE AND BEHAVIOURAL dimensions.

    The four or five things managers say they would do about a poor-quality performer ("people management is an ART — there are as many ways of solving the problem as there are managers, and all of them are right"):

    1. Call the person and GIVE FEEDBACK, find out why · 2. PAIR them with someone who does very high-quality work · 3. Look at the MOTIVATION for not doing high-quality work · 4. LINK it to performance appraisal and incentives

    2.5DIFFICULT CONVERSATIONS4

    DEFINITION: "A difficult conversation is one that YOU'RE PUTTING OFF — you don't want to do it."

    Why difficult? You want to say something but don't know how to say it without hurting the other person, don't know how it will be taken, and don't know what the outcomes and consequences will be. It induces ANXIETY and STRESS — but also MINDFULNESS and REFLECTION.

    "Managing difficult conversations is a SKILL. The more you do it, the better you get at it."

    The SEVEN cases and their categories likely match-the-category MCQ| # | The situation | Category |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | A team member is performing poorly DESPITE having been coached | PERFORMANCE — and SUBJECTIVE (quality? slowness? — and coaching implies behavioural change) | | 2 | A HIGH-PERFORMING team member is rude, disrespectful and does not share information | PERFORMANCE — objective performance is fine; this is an ATTITUDINAL issue | | 3 | Two team members have a personal conflict that is hurting team morale and performance | INTERPERSONAL | | 4 | An employee has PLATEAUED and is not acquiring new skills | JOB AND SKILLS | | 5 | Your most reliable team member expects promotion; you believe he is a great individual contributor but NOT cut out to be a manager | JOB, SKILLS AND POTENTIAL | | 6 | A team member is sharing more information than needed with the client | ETHICS | | 7 | You must let people go for non-performance under a new organisational policy | BEYOND THE MANAGER'S CONTROL — an ORGANISATIONAL POLICY you must implement |

    The instructor's framing: "TOOLS ARE CONTEXT-SPECIFIC, BUT PERSPECTIVES ARE CONTEXT-AGNOSTIC."

    PHASE 1 — PREPARATION guaranteed MCQ

    The preparation checklist: 1. Know the OBJECTIVE of the meeting · 2. Know the DESIRED OUTCOME · 3. Be clear WHAT THE PROBLEM IS · 4. Be clear WHAT THE OTHER PERSON THINKS THE PROBLEM IS. If you are not clear on either, the attitude that always works is: OPEN-MINDEDNESS, LISTENING, AND THE ABILITY TO SEEK SOLUTIONS.

    Practical note: the instructor prepares for ## 30 to 45 MINUTES, IN THE PREVIOUS WEEK.

    Worked example — the layoff conversation: objective = communicate that the policy has changed and the employee is a non-performer under it; outcome = the employee understands the policy change, understands he is negatively impacted, and begins looking for a job elsewhere. "It is only when the employee understands this that the meeting is effective."

    Worked example — the promotion conversation: there is ## an inherent MISMATCH between the employee's self-assessment and the manager's assessment. So the meeting has two objectives: (a) understand WHY the employee believes they are ready, and (b) communicate your assessment of why they are not. You must ask, understand, look at options, and communicate — and your focus is to find as much COMMON GROUND as possible.

    PHASE 2 — HAVING THE CONVERSATION: the principles guaranteed MCQ| # | Principle | Detail |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | ROLE CLARITY — "you have ONLY ONE ROLE: MANAGER" | "You're NOT a friend, you're NOT a coach, you're NOT a mentor. Be 250 PERCENT CLEAR in your head about your role" — because it shows up in how you behave. You act on behalf of the organisation | | 2 | BE UPFRONT AND OPEN — state the OBJECTIVE AND AGENDA at the start | After pleasantries: "The objective of this meeting is to provide you feedback…/ to inform you about a change in organisational policy." No room for ambiguity | | 3 | TALK LESS, LISTEN MORE | "Listen for WHAT IS SAID, but listen MORE FOR WHAT IS NOT SAID" | | 4 | BE CONSIDERATE IF THEY ARE EMOTIONAL — and BE COMFORTABLE WITH SILENCE | "If they are silent, YOU be silent. You don't have to talk." People may be stunned, surprised, shocked; consequences spill over into families, finances, children | | 5 | DON'T PLAY VICTIM | "A lot of managers want to be popular, so they play the victim card — 'I feel so bad for you, I'm so sorry I have to do this.' DON'T. You have a responsibility and an obligation — at the end of the day you EARN YOUR SALARY FROM THE ORGANISATION." Bring DIGNITY and INTEGRITY to the role | | 6 | OFFER SUPPORT | Even in a layoff: "let me look at your resume, let me give you a good reference." For a rude high performer: "what can I do to help you?" | | 7 | SUMMARISE THE MEETING at the end | So both sides have understood the objectives and outcomes; agree what YOU will do and what the EMPLOYEE will do afterwards | | 8 | STICK TO FACTS; when in conflict, return to the LARGER PURPOSE | The larger purpose = enabling performance for the organisation; building strong teams | | 9 | MINDFULLY USE "I", NOT "WE" | "A lot of managers use the word WE. You need to TAKE OWNERSHIP for the feedback you're giving — I feel, I think, I believe, I would suggest." |

    The link back: ## THE THREE Cs of performance management — CONTEXT, CONTENT and CONDUCT — all apply in difficult conversations too.

    PHASE 3 — DOCUMENTATION likely MCQ

    Two kinds of documentation: 1. OFFICIAL documentation FOR THE ORGANISATION — because ## "in each one of these difficult conversations there is a POTENTIAL for it to turn very quickly into a LEGAL ISSUE." Documentation is ## "the process of BEING FAIR AND BEING SEEN TO BE FAIR." 2. A MAIL TO THE EMPLOYEE summarising the discussion, the action points, and how and when you will track them.

    Plus the personal step: ## take 15 MINUTES OF UNINTERRUPTED TIME after the meeting and ask "what went well? what did not go well? what could I have done differently?"

    2.6MANAGING PEERS AND BOSSES6

    DEFINITION OF A STAKEHOLDER: "someone who has a STAKE. Finished." → Who has a stake in YOUR success, and in whose success do YOU have a stake?

    THE COMMON MISTAKE: "believing that their EMPLOYEES / DIRECT REPORTS ARE THEIR ONLY STAKEHOLDERS. NOT TRUE."

    External stakeholders: customers, suppliers, vendors, government members, bankers. Internal stakeholders: your peers, your manager, your MANAGER'S MANAGER (the SKIP-LEVEL manager), your manager's peers in other departments, your peers in other functions, senior management.

    Why PEERS are the hardest to manage likely MCQ

    1. "They DON'T HAVE CRITICAL DEPENDENCIES on you" — and in the absence of interdependency there is little RECIPROCITY / give and take (unlike with a manager or a team member). 2. "Peers are mostly AS COMPETENT AS YOU, and many may be MORE COMPETENT." 3. Peer relationships are NOT about power, authority or hierarchy — they are RELATIONSHIPS OF EQUALS.

    So the question is: "WHAT DO YOU BRING TO THE TABLE THAT YOUR PEER SHOULD VALUE?"

    Why peers matter more now: organisations must continuously create value for customers, which requires the different SILOS of departments to come together — so collaboration, agility, experimentation and innovation happen ACROSS the organisation, not just within your team.

    The THREE reasons a peer will work with you guaranteed MCQ| # | Reason | Detail |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | COMPLEMENTARITY OF CAPABILITIES | They have a technology or domain you don't — you bring SUPPLY CHAIN competence, your peer brings FINANCE competence, and both are needed for an optimisation project | | 2 | RESOURCES | Organisational resources are limited; collaborating harnesses larger resources. ## "Resources are NOT ALWAYS FINANCIAL — it could be competence, or sharing facilities" | | 3 | A NEW IDEA to experiment with | Something different that requires multiple people to execute |

    The FOURTH reason people give — "we are FRIENDS" — is explicitly rejected as insufficient: "friendship can't be the ONLY reason peer relationships work."

    The PARTNERSHIP MINDSET

    "If you see your peer as a COMPETITOR, then it means YOU HAVE NOTHING UNIQUE TO BRING TO THE TABLE — because whatever are your strengths are your peer's strengths. And if you have nothing unique, how will you grow long term?" PARTNERSHIP MINDSET = "being able to see the POSITIVES in each other, and COMPLEMENT, OFFSET AND MITIGATE the weaknesses."

    Its precondition: ## a high degree of SELF-AWARENESS AND INTERPERSONAL AWARENESS — knowing your strengths, weaknesses and work style, AND your peer's.

    The FIVE ways to build peer relationships likely list MCQ

    1. CONNECT BEYOND THE TASK (social connections — "we are also human") · 2. UNDERSTAND THE PAIN POINTS of others and how you can mitigate them (also their dreams and vision, and your role in them) · 3. MAKE SURE YOU DON'T ADD TO YOUR PEER'S PROBLEMS — solve escalations rather than playing the BLAME GAME, which "erodes trust" · 4. PROACTIVELY SOLICIT FEEDBACK from cross-functional peers every 3–6 months · 5. GIVE CREDIT WHEN IT IS DUE — "what do you lose by giving somebody credit? You LOSE A LOT BY HOARDING CREDIT"

    The PEER-TO-MANAGER transition likely MCQ

    Preparation, in four parts:

    1. Have a strong conversation WITH YOUR MANAGER — their agenda for you, their expectations of you and the team, the goals, the opportunities, and what support you will get. 2. UNDERSTAND EACH TEAM MEMBER'S ACTIVITIES — the time spent, the context, the challenges, the resource constraints. "It's one thing to understand as a PEER, another as a MANAGER — because now the expectation is that you will SUPPORT them." 3. IDENTIFY EACH PERSON'S STRENGTHS — "use your previous role to your advantage": as a peer you already know what they're outstandingly good at and what their aspirations are. 4. Decide HOW you will communicate and WITH WHAT FREQUENCY you will hold meetings.

    Then: ## ONE-ON-ONES with all team members — hear what they are doing, their ideas, how they could contribute to the vision; share the vision, the areas of improvement, and their strengths.

    THE NUMBERS TO REMEMBER: "If your promotion is FAIR, at least 75–80% OF YOUR PEERS will willingly go along with your vision." Some who were as good as you but missed out will be upset and angry — ## "GIVE THEM FOUR TO SIX WEEKS to manage their reactions and emotions." In the meantime: do your job, compliment them when they do well, include them in all meetings, communicate as you would to anyone, and NEVER talk badly about them. ## If after six weeks nothing has changed, have the difficult conversation.

    If your BEST FRIEND becomes your direct report likely MCQ

    BE MINDFUL of: (a) giving too much responsibility to your friend and TAKING THEM FOR GRANTED; (b) sharing SENSITIVE AND CONFIDENTIAL information; (c) GOSSIPING or BADMOUTHING anyone to your friend — "till last week you would have all hung out together talking negatively about managers." ## Give people space to adjust — but "DON'T HESITATE TO DRAW THE BOUNDARY very clearly in team meetings, because you have to LEAD FROM THE FRONT."

    MANAGING YOUR BOSS

    This is explicitly NOT about appeasing or currying favour — it is about a meaningful relationship for high impact.

    Why it matters: organisational goals cascade through levels, so ## a large part of YOUR goal sheet is directly related to YOUR BOSS'S goal sheet — a SYMBIOTIC relationship.

    Two warnings before the tips:

    1. "As you point a finger at someone else, remember there are THREE FINGERS POINTING AT YOU." What you say about your boss could be exactly what your direct reports say about you — and you don't know who will carry it. 2. THE ATTRIBUTION ASYMMETRY — a certain MCQ: "WE JUDGE OURSELVES BY OUR INTENTIONS, BUT WE JUDGE THEM BY THEIR ACTIONS." You forgot to send a mail → "my intention was noble, it slipped my mind." Your boss forgot → "why did they exclude me?"

    The coaching story: a benevolent, informal, open-office, always-accessible leader was replaced by one who was quiet, reserved, formal, wanted everything documented and worked through one-on-ones. The coachee kept using his old style (dropping in every morning) and complained about getting no time. ## The fix: WRITE A MAIL WITH AN AGENDA AND THE DOCUMENTS, AND SEEK TIME. The boss responded immediately and spent 45 MINUTES OVER AND ABOVE the time requested — and apologised for not having met sooner. ## The lesson: NO TWO BOSSES ARE THE SAME; you must be ADAPTABLE to differing styles.

    The three groups of tips managers reported guaranteed MCQ| Group | The tips | |---|---| | 1. KNOW YOUR BOSS | Their PRIORITIES and commitments · their STRENGTHS AND WEAKNESSES (tap the strengths, support the weaknesses) · their PREFERRED WAY TO RECEIVE INFORMATION (read vs hear; mail vs formal meetings vs water-cooler; analytical tables and flowcharts vs arguments with evidence) · their DREAMS AND ASPIRATIONS | | 2. MITIGATE PROBLEMS | "DON'T TAKE PROBLEMS TO YOUR BOSS — TAKE SOLUTIONS ALONG WITH THE PROBLEMS." Prepare and sensitise before an escalation; look at issues from your boss's perspective — "if she were looking at this, what would she want to know more?" | | 3. COMMUNICATION | Give feedback and receive it periodically · keep the boss BRIEFED · flag problems that could come and areas already mitigated · ## "NEVER THROW SURPRISES AT YOUR BOSS — MANAGING EXPECTATIONS IS KEY" · be an effective team member, keep a good attitude, stay upbeat, don't complain about the boss |

    2.7MANAGING SELF9

    "SELF-AWARENESS IS FUNDAMENTAL to being an effective manager… self-awareness is a LIFE SKILL." Reflection is the process that produces it.

    The new competency: SHEDDING likely MCQ

    Beyond "learning, unlearning and relearning", the instructor introduces ## SHEDDING: "What is it that you are going to THROW AWAY? What are you NOT going to do in the future? What is it that you believe you are SO COMPETENT AT that you SHOULD NOT BE DOING?"

    CAROL DWECK and the growth mindset guaranteed attribution MCQ

    CAROL DWECK, in her book "MINDSET — How Can You Fulfil Your Potential", describes the GROWTH MINDSET. Its characteristics: ## STRETCHING ONESELF · WILLING TO EXAMINE ONE'S DECISIONS · TAKING FEEDBACK FROM DIFFERENT SOURCES · EXAMINING NEW IDEAS · LOOKING AT COMPLETELY ALTERNATIVE PERSPECTIVES.

    The THREE foundational assumptions of the module guaranteed MCQ

    1. HUMAN POTENTIAL AND THE HUMAN ABILITY TO CHANGE IS INFINITE. 2. "WHAT GOT YOU HERE IS NOT GOING TO GET YOU THERE" — the skills that make you successful today are often the same ones that cause your failure. "YOUR GREATEST STRENGTH IS OFTEN YOUR GREATEST WEAKNESS." 3. In a complex, uncertain world, the ability to BUILD NETWORKS AND RELATIONSHIPS across a wide spectrum of people contributes immensely to career development.

    PERSONAL effectiveness — the three annual questions guaranteed MCQ| # | Question | The logic |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | "DO YOU HAVE A SUCCESSOR?" — someone 100% ready to take over from you today; if not, someone 70% ready? | "If the answer is NO — particularly if you are a manager's manager — then YOUR CAREER HAS PLATEAUED. YOU'VE BECOME TOO INDISPENSABLE. You are so indispensable that you're unlikely to grow." ## Your FIRST agenda on managing self is to CREATE A SUCCESSOR | | 2 | "WHAT DID YOU LEARN LAST YEAR THAT YOU DID NOT KNOW THE PREVIOUS YEAR?" | The professor's line: if you acquired experience in year one and have reproduced it since, you have "ONE YEAR OF EXPERIENCE MULTIPLIED SEVEN TIMES" — not seven years of experience. And "if you don't invest in yourself, how can you ask your team members to invest in themselves?" | | 3 | "WHAT ARE YOU GOING TO STOP DOING?" | Delegate it, automate it (things that are repetitive, routine, boring), or coach others to take it — to free up time for work that is important, critical, meaningful and relevant |

    WHEN to ask them: "every year — ON YOUR BIRTHDAY, on the NEW YEAR, but DEFINITELY NOT DURING YOUR APPRAISAL CYCLE."

    MANAGERIAL effectiveness — the four questions

    1. "WHAT ARE THE KEY TALENT GAPS IN MY TEAM?" — because ## "THE WEAKEST LINK DETERMINES THE STRENGTH OF THE CHAIN, NOT ITS STRONGEST LINK." The least effective member, or the position you couldn't fill, determines your team's strength — not the six high performers. 2. "WHAT KIND OF PEOPLE WILL I NEED NEXT YEAR?" — and ## "LEARNING OPPORTUNITIES DO NOT HAPPEN, THEY HAVE TO BE MADE TO HAPPEN" (secondment, job rotation). 3. Who are the KEY PEOPLE, what opportunities do we give them, how do we keep them engaged? 4. What support should I give the people who are NOT doing well?

    THE TIME-ALLOCATION FINDING guaranteed number MCQ

    "Over 80 PERCENT OF MANAGERIAL TIME went on 25 PERCENT OF THE PEOPLE — who were either HIGH PERFORMERS or NON-PERFORMERS. Both of them take a lot of your time." "And the irony is that the B PERFORMERS — the average performers who continue to deliver every year — receive just 20 PERCENT of the time."

    The argument: ## "the high performers are there IN SPITE OF YOU — under any manager they would still perform. The low performers are there because of personal challenges or because WE MADE A HIRING MISTAKE. But it is the LARGE MIDDLE — 70 PERCENT — that can either be MOVED UP to high performer or SLIP to non-performer, and it gets no managerial attention."

    The TWO requirements for personal development likely MCQ

    1. SELF-AWARENESS (relatively easy — 360-degree feedback, appraisals, conflicts and breakdowns are all feedback opportunities) · 2. DISCIPLINE ("in fact, MORE IMPORTANT").

    The irony to remember: "Isn't it a bit of an irony that people refer to all of these as SOFT SKILLS — BECAUSE THEY ARE THE HARDEST TO ACQUIRE?"

    THE THREE WAYS TO CHANGE BEHAVIOUR guaranteed MCQ

    1. REFLECTION · 2. OBSERVATION · 3. EMULATION (attributed in the course to a CONFUCIUS quote)

    Way What it does The instructor's own example
    REFLECTION Helps you understand the consequences of your actions, and the gap between intentions and actions; helps you link feedback from one context to another The senior leader who asked "do you think I come across as ABRASIVE?" — a comment in the session had triggered recall of past feedback
    OBSERVATION Noticing how others do things; asking about their thought processes when making decisions On joining academia, she sat in on outstanding colleagues' classes for three months — finance, operations, marketing — and saw that "teaching principles are so similar but the styles are so different"
    EMULATION Role modelling — copying the behaviours of others She copied the role-model professor. "Did it work? NOT QUITE the way she pulled it off. But yes, in a very different way" → ## you HYBRIDISE what you observe with your own experience, and over time develop YOUR SIGNATURE managerial style

    JOURNALING likely number MCQ

    A journal = "a tool to help you manage yourself" — a diary, log or notebook to record what went right, what could have been done differently, and to look back dispassionately and less emotionally**, from multiple perspectives.

    THE STATISTICS: "NO MORE THAN 50 PERCENT of people keep the discipline. But the 50% who do, IN ABOUT 90 DAYS begin to see the benefits — because at the end of 90 days YOU CAN SEE YOUR PATTERNS."

    Alternative formats reported: ## AUDIO journaling (recorded while driving home) · MIND MAPS · pictorial · music · poetry.

    THE SUPPORT NETWORK — the six kinds of support guaranteed MCQ

    The motivating research: ## "a lot of COOKERY BOOKS are bought at Christmas and New Year… and GYM MEMBERSHIPS go up in the first week of January. Gyms are full till February, but by November the numbers are not the same." We all have great intentions — translating them into action requires a SUPPORT NETWORK.

    The experience What you need Who provides it
    CONFUSION CLARITY A ROLE MODEL — someone who was in your situation five years ago and navigated it successfully
    ISOLATION (new organisation, new office, new field) SOCIAL CONTACT — to ease the transition more quickly People with COMMON INTERESTS — sport, theatre, movies, hiking, biking, cycling, running
    ALONENESS CARE A FRIEND
    CRISIS (personal or professional) ACTION ORIENTATION — someone directive who says "here are the three things you need to do now"
    DENTED SELF-ESTEEM AFFIRMATION OF YOUR STRENGTHS — "this is not the end of the world, you've done it before"
    PLATEAU ("we may not even know we have plateaued") SOMEONE WHO CHALLENGES YOU and asks whether you are still RELEVANT

    THE WARNING ABOUT FRIENDS — a very likely MCQ: "Friends are very good to have, but in the context of a SUPPORT NETWORK THEY ARE NOT PARTICULARLY USEFUL. WHY? Because friends tend to be SYMPATHETIC, they TAKE YOUR SIDE, they often DON'T GIVE YOU HARD FEEDBACK, and many times they DON'T KNOW YOUR PROFESSIONAL CONTEXT." The common failure pattern in the exercise: people list the SAME friend for every category.

    PERSONAL RESILIENCE — "the most critical quality for the future"

    Resilience is about: ## PERSPECTIVES AND MINDSETS · EMOTIONS AND REGULATING THEM · PURPOSE AND VISION · NETWORKS · and ## "above all, just PHYSICAL ENERGY AND STAMINA."

    The reflection questions given: ## "What is your capacity to REFRAME?" (because EMOTIONS ARE CONTAGION — you can quickly transmit negative emotions to the team) · "Do you have a clear sense of your own VALUES and act consistently with them?" (ethics, integrity, character) · "Do you make enough time for activities that give you JOY or help you RELAX?" ("if you're expending energy on difficult conversations, WHERE ARE YOUR ENERGY INDUCERS?"*).

    The closing thoughts: ## "SELF-DEVELOPMENT IS A JOURNEY, NOT A DESTINATION" · journaling, support network and personal resilience are ways of REFUELLING · "REFLECT, OBSERVE, EMULATE" · and MAHATMA GANDHI: "BE THE CHANGE YOU WISH TO SEE IN THE WORLD."

    2.8PERSONAL EFFECTIVENESS AND TIME MANAGEMENT3

    Why it matters here: this FinTech programme is ## COMPLETELY ASYNCHRONOUS — "the classroom comes to my house" and faculty are available anytime — so the critical capability is ## SELF-MANAGEMENT. DEFINITION: "Self-management is the ability to manage my TIME AND RESOURCES in a way such that I am EFFECTIVE" — also called personal effectiveness. Expected commitment: ## 6 HOURS EVERY WEEK on this programme.

    THE 168 HOURS guaranteed number MCQ

    "ALL OF US HAVE ONLY A TOTAL OF 168 HOURS A WEEK. NO MORE, NO LESS."

    THE TECHNIQUE: "WORK HOUR ANALYSIS" — where does my time actually go?

    The instructor's own worked analysis — "five big things where nearly 80% of my time went":

    Activity Hours per week
    At work ~48
    Sleep ~46
    Chores and personal activities ~40
    Learning / preparing courses and modules 12–15
    SOCIAL MEDIA 12 — "wow. That was a SURPRISE. I thought I did maybe two or three hours a week" (mostly WhatsApp and LinkedIn, found via the smartphone's app-time data)
    With friends and colleagues / hanging out Very little — "Is that good? I don't think so, because all of us need social support"

    The purpose: to become ## MINDFUL OF THE TIME WASTERS — where am I unconsciously spending more time? What have I not been able to do because of my current allocation? "TIME GONE IS TIME LOST" — its value is greater than money, because time does not come back.

    THE CHECKLIST / TO-DO LIST

    DO IT ON A WEEKLY BASIS, not just daily — because you must see the whole week to know when to prepare. (The student example: classes Mon–Tue, a sports event Wed, a quiz Thu afternoon, an external speaker Fri, a presentation Sat — so you must prepare for Thursday's quiz on Monday and Tuesday, since Wednesday is gone and you will be tired.)

    "THERE IS A STORY IN EVERY CHECKLIST" — the to-do list tells you the story of your ability to manage time and priorities. "You may not like it, but the fact is there's a story." Ask why some items got done — because you wanted to, or because somebody forced you?

    THE EISENHOWER MATRIX guaranteed MCQ

    Also called the URGENT–IMPORTANT MATRIX. Two dimensions, four quadrants.

    Quadrant Definition Action Examples
    Q1 — URGENT + IMPORTANT Deadlines with serious consequences if missed; require immediate attention DO IT NOW — but reflect: "they SHOULD HAVE BEEN DONE MUCH EARLIER" Assignment due tomorrow; a critical email from your professor; an entrepreneur fixing a website bug that stops customers checking out
    Q2 — IMPORTANT but NOT URGENT "THE MOST IMPORTANT QUADRANT FROM A PERSONAL GROWTH AND DEVELOPMENT PERSPECTIVE" — long-term growth activities SCHEDULE these — they are the ones you always postpone "I have to read that book but never get to it"; learning a new tool; the course readings you put away saying "I'll look at it later"
    Q3 — URGENT but NOT IMPORTANT Urgent, but do not need YOU personally DELEGATE A document that can go by local courier or a family member; friends asking about weekend plans on a Monday morning
    Q4 — NEITHER urgent NOR important "PURE TIME WASTERS" Eliminate Social media surfing · hanging out longer than you intended · "you watched the ENTIRE SERIES" because you were bored of studying

    The illustration of bad prioritisation: an application form due at 12 noon, still unsubmitted at 9 a.m., then no fuel in the motorbike, arriving 12:15 and pleading with the administration — ## "stress, anxiety, worry, tension, all caused by DELAY on an activity that should have had the HIGHEST PRIORITY."

    The instruction: ## every SUNDAY NIGHT OR MONDAY MORNING, take stock of the week, make a checklist, and prioritise it with the urgent–important matrix — "don't treat this like a WEEKLY PROJECT, but as a way of CHANGING YOUR BEHAVIOURS."

    THE FIVE TIME-MANAGEMENT TECHNIQUES guaranteed MCQ

    The framing: ## "ONE SIZE DOES NOT FIT EVERYONE — it's a little bit like offering a BUFFET." Different people work differently: some plough through for 4–5 hours; some cannot do more than half an hour without a break; some do the easy things first and postpone the difficult.

    # Technique How it works
    1 ALIGN YOUR PRIME TIME / BIO-RHYTHM "Research suggests we all have a BIO-RHYTHM — for TWO TO THREE HOURS a day we are at our productive best." The instructor's is ## 5:30–7:30 a.m.; her best friend's is ## 10 p.m. to midnight. ## The same task takes 1 HOUR at peak time and 2–3 HOURS at any other time. "PROTECT THAT TIME AS SACRED" and use it for the IMPORTANT-BUT-NOT-URGENT work
    2 TIME BOXING "Allocate a FIXED AMOUNT OF TIME to a specific task and aim to complete it within that time" — to stay focused and avoid being sidetracked. Requires a realistic estimate (preparing a class presentation ≈ 45 minutes, so block 45 minutes). Can be combined with bio-rhythm or used independently
    3 THE SWISS CHEESE TECHNIQUE "Swiss cheese has a lot of small holes." Instead of sitting for one hour, make a note whenever an idea comes — little holes at different times. ## "By the time you finish you've eaten your way significantly through these 10-, 5-, 20-minute pieces, and all that's needed is to TIE IT TOGETHER." Works especially while travelling
    4 THE POMODORO TECHNIQUE For people who get DISTRACTED EASILY. Work in short focused bursts of 25 MINUTES, then a STRICT 5-MINUTE BREAK. ## After 4 POMODOROS, take a 30-MINUTE break. Rules: be realistic about what fits in 25 minutes · KEEP YOUR PHONE IN THE NEXT ROOM · tell the family not to knock · ## SET A TIMER NOT ONLY FOR THE POMODORO BUT ALSO FOR THE RETURN FROM THE 5-MINUTE BREAK
    5 THE PREMACK PRINCIPLE Given one EASY and one DIFFICULT task, DO THE DIFFICULT ONE FIRST — "because once you do the more difficult one, THE EASY ONE IS YOUR REWARD." The same applies between a task you like less and one you like more
    (+) REFLECTION ON ACTION The fifth/extra technique: after trying the others, look at what worked, how it worked, and which you could implement effortlessly. ## "Don't be overcritical if you can't do 4 Pomodoros. If you can do ONE to your satisfaction, PAT YOURSELF — because the second one then looks doable"

    The three-point summary of the time module: ## 1. You have 168 HOURS a week — how are you managing it? · 2. Do you have a WEEKLY (not just daily) CHECKLIST? · 3. Categorise it using the EISENHOWER MATRIX.

    Introduction to Strategy

    Introduction to Strategy — Complete Exam Guide

    Prof. Sai Chittaranjan Kalubandi · Beginner Module, Course 3 Prescribed text: Contemporary Strategy Analysis — An Indian Adaptation (Grant & co-authors)

    How to use this: The course runs Modules 1→5. Module 1–2 = the firm, management, and what strategy is. Module 3 = external analysis (PESTEL, Porter, strategic groups). Module 4 = internal analysis (resources → capabilities → core competencies → VRIO → value chain → SWOT). Module 5 = competitive strategy + implementation. MCQs in this course are overwhelmingly definitional (which framework has which components), attributional (which thinker said what, which years), and classificatory (is this internal or external? primary or support? tangible or intangible?). Memorise the lists.

    PART A — THE FIRM AND MANAGEMENT (Modules 1–2)

    1. Why firms exist — the Ram & Jam narrative
    1

    The course opens with a running story: Ram starts making a product, needs inputs from Jam (supplier), hires people, sells to customers. This is used throughout to introduce the firm, stakeholders, and eventually the value stick.

    The core idea: a firm exists because coordinating activities inside an organisation is cheaper and more reliable than transacting for everything in the open market.

    1.1 The four defining features of a firm guaranteed MCQ| # | Feature | What it means |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | Separate legal entity | The firm is a "person" in law — it can own property, sue and be sued in its own name, distinct from its owners | | 2 | Perpetual succession (going concern) | The firm continues to exist even if owners/managers die, retire, or sell out | | 3 | Joint stock corporation | Ownership is divided into transferable shares held jointly by many investors | | 4 | Limited liability | Shareholders' loss is capped at the amount they invested; personal assets are protected |

    MCQ trap: "Perpetual succession" ≠ "the firm never fails". It means the firm's legal existence is independent of the lifespan of any individual owner.

    1.2 Types of firms

    Type Ownership Liability Key point
    Sole proprietorship One person Unlimited Simplest; owner and firm are legally the same
    Partnership Two or more partners Unlimited (jointly & severally) Governed by a partnership deed
    Private limited company Restricted set of shareholders Limited Shares not freely transferable to the public
    Public limited company Public shareholders Limited Can list and raise capital from the public
    Co-operative society Members who are also users/producers Limited One member = one vote, regardless of shareholding. Example: Amul

    MCQ trap: The co-operative's distinguishing feature is one member, one vote — not voting in proportion to capital contributed. That is the opposite of a joint stock company.

    1.3 Stakeholders — primary, secondary, tertiary

    Tier Who Nature of stake
    Primary Shareholders/owners, employees, customers, suppliers, creditors Direct transactional / contractual relationship; the firm cannot survive without them
    Secondary Government, regulators, local community, media, trade associations, competitors Influence and are influenced, but no direct transaction
    Tertiary Future generations, flora and fauna / the natural environment No voice, no vote, but bear consequences — the basis of the sustainability/ESG argument

    MCQ trap: "Future generations" and "flora and fauna" are classified as tertiary stakeholders in this course. This is the item most likely to be tested because it is unusual.

    1. Organisational structures
    1

    Structure How it groups people Advantages Disadvantages
    Functional By function — production, sales, finance, HR Deep specialisation, economies of scale, efficiency, clear career paths Silos; poor cross-functional coordination; slow response; narrow managerial view
    Divisional By product, geography, or customer segment Accountability per division; tailored strategies; faster local response Duplication of functions; higher cost; divisions may compete for resources
    Matrix Dual reporting — functional and project/product Flexible use of scarce expertise; strong cross-functional collaboration Two bosses → conflict, ambiguity, power struggles, slow decisions
    Virtual / Network A small core that contracts out most activities to partners Very low fixed cost; high flexibility; access to best-in-class partners Loss of control; dependence on partners; weak culture; knowledge leakage

    2.1 Supporting structural concepts

    • Scalar chain — the unbroken line of authority from the top of the organisation to the bottom; every communication should ideally follow it.
    • Tall vs flat structures — Tall = many hierarchical levels, narrow span of control, tight supervision, slow communication. Flat = few levels, wide span of control, faster communication, more autonomy, but heavier managerial load.
    • Centralisation vs decentralisation — where decision rights sit. Centralisation gives consistency and control; decentralisation gives speed and local responsiveness. ← guaranteed MCQ on the trade-off
    • Span of control — number of subordinates reporting to one manager. Tall structure ⇒ narrow span. Flat ⇒ wide span. (Inverse relationship.)

    2.2 Effective delegation — three requirements likely MCQ

    1. Assign specific tasks — be concrete about what is to be done.
    2. Grant the authority needed to do it (authority must match responsibility).
    3. Hold the person accountable for the result.

    Barriers to delegation: lack of trust in subordinates; fear of failure / fear of being blamed; micromanagement; the manager's belief that "I can do it faster myself"; subordinates' lack of confidence or skill.

    MCQ trap: Responsibility can be delegated along with authority, but ultimate accountability stays with the delegating manager.

    2.3 Four types of control

    Control type Mechanism Example
    Personal control Direct supervision by an individual Founder watching the shop floor
    Bureaucratic control Rules, procedures, budgets, standard operating procedures Approval matrices in a bank
    Output control Setting targets and measuring results, leaving means to the manager Sales quota, ROI target
    Cultural control Shared values and norms make people self-regulate "The Tata way"

    Sam Walton quote used in the course to make the point that people, not systems, deliver results — culture and empowerment beat pure supervision.

    1. Management fundamentals
    1

    Definition used in the course: management is "the art of getting things done through other people."

    3.1 Levels of management

    Level Who Focus Time horizon
    Top management CEO, MD, board, C-suite Vision, mission, corporate strategy, external environment Long term (3–10 yrs)
    Middle management Divisional/functional heads Translating strategy into tactical plans; coordinating Medium (1–3 yrs)
    Frontline / first-line management Supervisors, shift managers Day-to-day operations, execution, resource use Short (days–months)
    Team leader Working leader of a small team Task coordination within the team Immediate

    3.2 Mintzberg's three managerial role categories guaranteed MCQ| Category | Roles inside it | Essence |

    |---|---|---| | Interpersonal | Figurehead, Leader, Liaison | Dealing with people | | Informational | Monitor, Disseminator, Spokesperson | Handling information | | Decisional | Entrepreneur, Disturbance handler, Resource allocator, Negotiator | Making choices |

    Mnemonic: I-I-D — Interpersonal, Informational, Decisional. Ten roles in three groups.

    3.3 Four managerial skills

    Skill What it is Most needed at
    Conceptual Seeing the whole system, abstract thinking, strategic judgement Top management
    Human / interpersonal Working with, motivating, and leading people All levels equally
    Technical Job-specific expertise and tools Frontline / lower management
    Political Building a power base, networks, influence, coalition-building Increasingly at senior levels

    MCQ trap: Human skills are equally important at all levels. Technical skill matters most at the bottom, conceptual most at the top. The fourth skill — political — is the one people forget in a list question.

    3.4 The POLC framework — the four functions of management

    Planning → Organising → Leading → Controlling.

    • Planning — setting objectives and deciding how to reach them.
    • Organising — arranging resources, structures, and tasks.
    • Leading — motivating, directing, communicating.
    • Controlling — monitoring performance against the plan and correcting deviations.

    MCQ trap: POLC (four functions, modern) vs Fayol's five functions (planning, organising, commanding, coordinating, controlling). Commanding + coordinating were later merged into "leading".

    3.5 The four types of plans

    Plan type Horizon Made by Purpose
    Strategic Long term Top management Overall direction, scope, competitive position
    Tactical Medium term Middle management Translates strategy into functional/divisional actions
    Operational Short term Frontline management Day-to-day activities, schedules, budgets
    Contingency Triggered by events All levels "What if" — pre-planned responses to disruptions

    3.6 MBO — Management by Objectives

    Managers and subordinates jointly set specific, measurable objectives, agree a timeframe, periodically review progress, and evaluate performance against those objectives. Associated with Peter Drucker. Its strength is alignment and clarity; its weakness is rigidity and over-focus on what is measurable.

    1. Evolution of management theory ← very high-yield; expect dates and names

    School Period Key figure(s) Core idea Criticism
    Scientific management Early 20th c. Frederick Winslow Taylor (1856–1915) The one best way to perform a task, found via time-and-motion studies; scientific selection/training of workers; incentives tied to productivity Treats workers like machines; ignores human/social needs
    Administrative theory Early 20th c. Henri Fayol (1841–1925), French industrialist Focus on the whole organisation, not the shop floor. Five functions of management; 14 principles of management; management can be taught and applied universally Overly prescriptive; principles are general
    Bureaucratic school Early 20th c. Max Weber (1864–1920), German sociologist Organisations as rational, impersonal bureaucracies: clear hierarchy, division of labour, formal rules and procedures, impersonality, merit-based advancement — designed to remove favouritism and arbitrary decisions Rigidity, red tape, lack of adaptability
    Behavioural approach 1920s–1950s Elton Mayo (Hawthorne studies), Abraham Maslow, Douglas McGregor, Mary Parker Follett The human side of organisations: motivation, group dynamics, leadership, communication. Hawthorne finding: social factors and employee attitudes significantly affect productivity Can underplay structure and economics
    Quantitative approach From WWII (Operations research teams) Apply mathematics and statistics — operations research, management science, decision models — to optimise resource allocation. Used in logistics, supply chain, finance, project management Neglects human and ethical factors
    Systems approach 1950s–1960s — Organisations are open systems interacting with their environment; internally and externally interrelated subsystems Abstract; hard to operationalise
    Contingency approach 1960s–1970s — "It depends." There is no one best way — the right structure and style depend on the situation Offers no universal prescription

    4.1 Taylor vs Fayol vs Weber — the classic comparison MCQ

    Taylor Fayol Weber
    Level of analysis Shop floor / task Whole organisation / administration Organisational structure / authority
    Nationality American French German
    Dates 1856–1915 1841–1925 1864–1920
    Famous for Time-and-motion, one best way 5 functions, 14 principles Bureaucracy

    4.2 Fayol's 14 principles — the ones named in the course

    Division of work · Delegation of authority (authority and responsibility) · Discipline · Unity of command · Esprit de corps — plus the remaining standard nine (unity of direction, subordination of individual interest to general interest, remuneration, centralisation, scalar chain, order, equity, stability of tenure, initiative).
