    MCQ trap: Unity of command = one employee reports to one boss. Unity of direction = one plan/one head for a group of activities with the same objective. These two are constantly swapped in exam options.

    4.3 Two illustrative cases used early in the course

    • Burberry / Rosemary Bravo (1997) — a fading heritage brand turned around by repositioning and refreshing the brand while retaining its heritage assets. Illustrates leadership + strategic renewal.
    • Starbucks / Tim Jones and Howard Schultz — illustrates how the experience, not just the product, is the offering, and how founder-led vision drives strategy.

    PART B — VALUE AND WHAT STRATEGY IS (Module 2)

    1. The value stick guaranteed MCQ; know every term
    1

    Drawn from the book Better, Simpler Strategy. Visualise a vertical stick:

       ┌──── Willingness to Pay (WTP)  ── customer's maximum
   │        ↕  Customer surplus  = WTP − Price
   ├──── Price
   │        ↕  Firm's value captured (margin) = Price − Cost
   ├──── Cost
   │        ↕  Supplier surplus  = Cost − WTS
   └──── Willingness to Sell (WTS) ── supplier's minimum
    Term Definition
    Willingness to Pay (WTP) The maximum amount a customer would part with to obtain the product. Not the same as price.
    Willingness to Sell (WTS) The minimum price at which a supplier (or employee) would agree to provide inputs (or labour).
    Customer / consumer surplus WTP − Price
    Supplier surplus Price paid to supplier − WTS
    Total value created WTP − WTS (the whole length of the stick)
    Value captured by the firm Price − Cost (the firm's slice in the middle)

    5.1 The worked numbers from the lecture ← memorise these

    • Phone: customer's WTP = ₹40,000; firm charges ₹30,000 ⇒ customer surplus = ₹10,000.
    • Same phone: firm pays supplier ₹20,000; supplier's WTS = ₹18,000 ⇒ supplier surplus = ₹2,000.
    • Employee version: bright coding graduate's minimum acceptable salary = ₹2,00,000; job pays ₹2,20,000 ⇒ employee surplus = ₹20,000.
    • Total value created for the phone = ₹40,000 − ₹18,000 = ₹22,000.

    Key teaching point: suppliers include employees. "Willingness to sell" applies to a graduate selling their labour just as much as to a vendor selling steel.

    5.2 Value creation vs value capture

    • Value creation = making the stick longer — raise WTP (better product, brand, service, innovation, marketing) and/or lower WTS (better working conditions, training and productivity, longer-term supplier relationships, technology).
    • Value capture = the firm's share of that stick (Price − Cost).
    • A firm that creates enormous value but captures little is not sustainable; a firm that captures a large share without creating value invites entry and regulation.

    Examples used: Google, Uber, Apple, Red Bull, Amazon, Toyota — each illustrating either WTP-raising (Apple, Red Bull) or WTS-lowering / cost-side excellence (Toyota, Amazon).

    MCQ trap: Raising price is not value creation. It only shifts surplus from the customer to the firm — the stick's length is unchanged.

    5.3 Market equilibrium, disequilibrium and abnormal profits

    • In equilibrium, competition drives firms to normal profits (just enough to cover cost of capital).
    • Abnormal (supernormal) profits appear in disequilibrium — created by innovation, scarcity, entry barriers, or first-mover positions.
    • Strategy is largely about creating and defending disequilibrium in your favour.

    5.4 Value chain → extended value chain → value ecosystem → value grid

    Concept Scope
    Value chain The sequence of activities inside one firm that create value
    Extended value chain The firm's chain plus its suppliers' and buyers' chains, upstream and downstream
    Value ecosystem The wider web of complementors, platforms, regulators, and partners whose actions affect value
    Value grid Looks vertically, horizontally, and diagonally for value opportunities rather than only along a linear chain — illustrated with Starbucks

    1. What is strategy, and why does it matter?
    4

    Strategy = the set of integrated choices about where to compete and how to win, made to create and capture value and achieve superior, sustainable performance.

    6.1 Six reasons strategy matters likely list MCQ

    1. It provides direction and a shared sense of purpose.
    2. It guides resource allocation — where money, people and attention go.
    3. It creates coordination and alignment across functions and levels.
    4. It builds and defends competitive advantage.
    5. It enables adaptation to a changing environment.
    6. It sets the basis for evaluating performance — you can only judge results against intent.

    6.2 The Jio case guaranteed MCQ; know the numbers| Fact | Value |

    |---|---| | Year of entry | 2016 | | Data price before Jio | ₹250–300 per GB | | Jio's launch offer | Free 4G data and voice calls | | Jio's data price | ~₹50 per GB | | Effective price cut | 80–90% | | Cell towers deployed | ~250,000 | | Subscriber growth | 0 → ~470 million | | Casualties | Aircel and Tata Docomo exited; industry consolidated (Vodafone + Idea) |

    What made entry possible despite high entry barriers? ← classic exam question 1. Deep pockets — backing of the Reliance group, willing to invest at enormous scale. 2. Regulatory change — spectrum sharing was permitted. 3. Aggressive pricing and innovative offers — which lowered customers' switching costs and made them willing to move.

    Lesson stated in the lecture: "Entry barriers are not just about money. They include regulatory environment, technology, and strategy."

    6.3 Marico / Saffola

    Used as the running Indian FMCG example of a firm that repositioned around health and wellness, launched Saffola Active, revamped packaging and marketing, and simultaneously restructured internally (health-and-wellness innovation units, empowered regional brand teams). Reappears in Module 5 as the exemplar of institutionalised feedback loops.

    6.4 Strategy vs tactics guaranteed MCQ| | Strategy | Tactics |

    |---|---|---| | Question answered | Doing the right things | Doing things right | | Horizon | Long term | Short term | | Scope | Broad, organisation-wide | Narrow, functional/task-level | | Reversibility | Hard and costly to reverse | Easily adjusted | | Owner | Top management | Middle and frontline management | | Nature | Effectiveness | Efficiency |

    Memorise the pairing: Strategy = effectiveness = doing the RIGHT things. Tactics = efficiency = doing things RIGHT.

    6.5 The three levels of strategy guaranteed MCQ| Level | Question it answers | Who owns it |

    |---|---|---| | Corporate strategy | What businesses should we be in? (scope, diversification, M&A, portfolio) | Board / CEO / corporate centre | | Business (competitive) strategy | How should we compete in this business? (cost leadership, differentiation, focus) | Business/divisional head | | Functional strategy | How do our functions support the business strategy? (marketing, ops, HR, finance plans) | Functional heads |

    6.6 Strategy is dynamic

    Illustrated with Tata Motors (petrochemical-era conglomerate logic → EV and sustainable mobility push), Apple (computers → devices → services), and N. Chandrasekaran's reshaping of the Tata group portfolio. The point: a strategy that fitted yesterday's environment can destroy value in today's.

    1. The strategic management process — five steps guaranteed MCQ (order matters)
    1

    Step Name Content
    1 Define vision, mission and goals The why and where. Vision = aspirational long-term future ("Where do we want to be?"). Mission = reason for existence ("Why do we exist?"), defines core values and primary stakeholders. Goals = specific, measurable objectives that translate vision/mission into targets
    2 External and internal analysis PESTEL, Porter's Five Forces, strategic groups (external); resources, capabilities, core competencies, VRIO, value chain (internal)
    3 Strategy formulation Choosing corporate, business and functional strategies
    4 Strategy implementation Structure, systems, leadership, culture, resource deployment
    5 Evaluation and control Measuring outcomes, feeding back, correcting

    It is a continuous cycle, not a one-time event.

    Infosys illustration: Vision — "to be a globally respected corporation" (and the number one or two choice for clients); Mission — "to navigate our clients' digital transformation"; Goals — expand digital services, increase global market share, invest in talent and sustainability.

    MCQ trap: Vision = future aspiration (where). Mission = purpose for existing (why). Goals = measurable targets. Options routinely swap vision and mission.

    7.1 Strategy analysis illustrations

    Havells, Ola, Asian Paints are used as strategy-analysis exemplars, alongside a cricket analogy: you study the pitch and the opposition (external), and you know your own squad's strengths (internal), before you set the field.

    PART C — EXTERNAL ANALYSIS (Module 3)

    1. External analysis — the four steps guaranteed MCQ (order matters)
    1

    Step Name What you do
    1 Scanning Broad surveillance for early signals of change
    2 Monitoring Tracking the specific trends you have identified over time
    3 Forecasting Projecting where those trends are heading
    4 Assessing Judging the implications for your firm — so what?

    Mnemonic: S–M–F–A. Assessing is last; it is where analysis becomes strategy.

    8.1 Macro environment vs industry environment

    Macro (general) environment Industry (competitive/task) environment
    Tool PESTEL Porter's Five Forces, strategic groups
    Scope Affects all industries in a country/region Specific to one industry
    Firm's influence Essentially none — you adapt Some — you can shape rivalry, barriers, bargaining

    8.2 Cases of external-analysis success and failure

    • Success — McDonald's India: adapted the menu to Indian social/cultural realities (no beef, no pork, vegetarian lines, the McAloo Tikki). External sensing translated into product design.
    • Failures — Kodak, Nokia, HMT, Kingfisher: each read (or ignored) the external environment badly. Kodak = digital photography; Nokia = smartphone/OS shift; HMT = quartz watches and liberalisation; Kingfisher = cost structure and aviation economics.

    1. PESTEL guaranteed MCQ; know each letter, its analogy, and the Indian examples
    2

    Letter Segment Course analogy Covers
    P Political The referee of the game Government stability, policy, taxation, trade policy, FDI rules
    E Economic The weather Growth, inflation, interest rates, exchange rates, income levels
    S Social (socio-cultural + demographic) The soil businesses grow in / the flavour of a market Demographics; values, beliefs, attitudes, lifestyles
    T Technological The engine of business progress Innovation, R&D, automation, digitalisation, adoption curves
    E Environmental The ecosystem everyone operates in Climate, sustainability, resource scarcity, pollution, environmental regulation
    L Legal The rules of a cricket match Labour law, consumer protection, IPR, competition law, health & safety

    Plus a seventh, cross-cutting segment: the Global environment — analogy: ocean currents that affect all ships. Covers international trade, globalisation, cross-border investment, global supply chains, geopolitics.

    MCQ trap: The course explicitly says global factors are important but can largely be studied under the broader PESTEL framework itself — they are not a formal seventh letter.

    9.1 Political — Indian examples

    GST (indirect tax reform), FDI policy changes, demonetization.

    9.2 Social — split into two sub-segments likely MCQ| Sub-segment | Definition | Examples |

    |---|---|---| | Demographic | The statistical characteristics of a population: size, age structure, gender distribution, geographic distribution, education levels, income distribution, family size, migration patterns | Over 50% of India's population is under 30 — the "youth bulge"; rapid urbanisation; small but growing ageing population | | Socio-cultural | Values, beliefs, attitudes, lifestyles and social trends | Health consciousness (Patanjali, organic foods); joint → nuclear families; women in the workforce; cultural pride ("Make in India", "vocal for local") |

    Demographic consequences named in the lecture: education boom (schools, colleges, coaching, online platforms); organised retail and e-commerce growth; mobility demand (Ola, affordable two-wheelers); double-income nuclear families driving QSRs, Swiggy, Big Basket, Blinkit.

    How firms track socio-cultural change: market research, social media listening, observing popular culture, influencer collaborations, monitoring consumer feedback.

    Risk side: advertising insensitive to cultural or religious sentiment triggers backlash.

    9.3 Technological — Indian examples

    UPI enabling Paytm/PhonePe to scale; the Jio effect (cheap data + affordable smartphones → OTT like Hotstar, online education and test prep); robotics/AI/ML in manufacturing; Flipkart, Amazon, Meesho in e-commerce forcing traditional retail online; telemedicine and health-tech; precision farming / agritech.

    Nuance the lecture stresses: technology does not benefit everyone — it also disrupts brick-and-mortar retailers. Advanced R&D and proprietary technology can be an entry barrier, but rapid change can also make investments obsolete.

    9.4 Environmental — Indian examples

    Renewable/solar push (Tata Power, ReNew Power); air and water pollution → stricter emission norms; water scarcity (Coca-Cola India groundwater protests); single-use plastic bans forcing packaging change. Also: climate impact on supply chains, circular economy models, and ESG (Environmental, Social, Governance) criteria for investors.

    9.5 Legal — Indian examples

    FSSAI (Food Safety and Standards Authority of India) rules on labelling, quality and safety; stronger IP protection encouraging pharma innovation; labour code reforms simplifying compliance.

    9.6 Global — examples

    Indian IT services integrated with US/Europe; pharma generics exports subject to international quality standards; global semiconductor shortage delaying Indian car production; RCEP participation/withdrawal; US visa policy hitting Indian IT; Middle East instability → oil prices → transport costs.

    9.7 The PESTEL activity — the Amul worked example

    Letter Amul factor
    Political Dairy subsidies, co-operative policy
    Economic Fluctuating milk prices, rural income trends
    Social Demand for packaged foods, vegetarian preferences
    Technological Cold-chain innovation, online ordering apps
    Environmental Sustainable packaging, water use in dairy farming
    Legal Food safety regulation, co-operative society law

    1. Defining the industry and its boundaries
    2

    Industry = a group of firms producing products or services that are close substitutes for each other. guaranteed MCQ

    The key word is substitutes — not "identical products", but products satisfying the same customer need.

    10.1 Why boundaries matter

    1. Identifying competitors — you cannot anticipate or benchmark against rivals you have not recognised.
    2. Understanding substitutes — ignore functionally different products serving the same need and you get blindsided (digital wallets/UPI substitute for cash and cards).
    3. Regulatory and market focus — determines which regulations apply and which market data is relevant.

    Analogy: industry boundaries are like the boundaries of a cricket field. If you don't know where the field ends you cannot play the game.

    The trade-off: define it too narrowly → you miss disruptive threats. Define it too broadly → the analysis becomes unfocused.

    10.2 Five inputs for drawing industry boundaries likely list MCQ

    1. Customer needs and preferences — who else serves the same need?
    2. Product substitutability — can the customer swap your product for another's?
    3. Cross-price elasticity — sales of one product rise when the price of another falls; if so, both sit in the same industry.
    4. Regulatory definitions — where a regulator defines the industry (e.g. what constitutes a mutual fund company, or a bank, in India).
    5. Technological convergence — technologies merging over time make previously distinct products close substitutes.

    Boundaries change: telecom and internet services are converging; e-commerce platforms have entered logistics and payments.

    1. Porter's Five Forces ← the single most examinable framework in this course
    4

    Developed by Michael Porter. Purpose: to analyse the competitive forces that shape an industry's profitability and attractiveness.

    Core logic: Strong forces ⇒ more competition ⇒ lower profitability. Weak forces ⇒ pricing power ⇒ higher returns. Industry structure determines the average profitability of firms in it.

    # Force Analogy used in the course
    1 Threat of new entrants A fortress with walls — the walls are entry barriers; Jio found a "secret tunnel"
    2 Bargaining power of suppliers A water vendor — one vendor in a desert can charge anything
    3 Bargaining power of buyers Auction bidders — many sellers and few buyers means buyers drive a hard bargain
    4 Threat of substitutes Alternative routes to a destination
    5 Intensity of rivalry among existing competitors A crowded bazaar with vendors shouting

    11.1 Force 1 — Threat of new entrants: the seven determinants guaranteed MCQ| Determinant | High value of this factor ⇒ |

    |---|---| | Capital requirements | High capital (or scarce specialised talent) needed ⇒ high barriers, low threat | | Economies of scale | Incumbents enjoy large-scale cost advantage ⇒ low threat | | Product differentiation | Strong brands and high customer loyalty ⇒ low threat | | Access to distribution | Channels tightly controlled ⇒ low threat | | Government policy and regulation | Licences, permits, regulatory hurdles hard to obtain ⇒ low threat | | Switching costs | High switching costs for customers ⇒ low threat | | Network effects | Value rises with more users (telecom, social media, two-sided platforms like Facebook/Instagram/LinkedIn) ⇒ low threat |

    Telecom example: licences + network effects + infrastructure + minimum subscriber scale ⇒ very high entry barriers, very low threat of entry — yet Jio still entered (see §6.2).

    Are high entry barriers always good for incumbents? Generally yes — they protect profits. But they breed complacency and inertia, leaving incumbents vulnerable to disruptive entrants.

    11.2 Force 2 — Bargaining power of suppliers: five determinants guaranteed MCQ| Determinant | Supplier power is HIGH when |

    |---|---| | Number / concentration of suppliers | Suppliers are few (they can collude or reach equilibrium quickly) | | Uniqueness of input | The input has no ready substitute (e.g. wafer-thin silicon slicing tech vs. sugar, which has corn-based substitutes) | | Switching costs | It is costly for the buying firm to change suppliers (search cost, re-contracting) | | Supplier threat of forward integration | Suppliers could enter your business and compete with you | | Importance of your business to the supplier | Your industry is a small/unimportant customer for them (e.g. steel supplies many industries, so any one industry depends on steel more than steel depends on it) |

    Example: Indian automobile industry — suppliers of semiconductors and specialised components have high power (global chip shortage caused production delays); suppliers of steel and tyres have low power (many alternatives).

    How firms reduce supplier power: diversify the supplier base; develop alternative sources; vertically integrate (make the input yourself); invest in technology that creates new supply sources (e.g. alternatives to lithium-ion batteries would cut lithium suppliers' power over EV makers).

    11.3 Force 3 — Bargaining power of buyers: five determinants guaranteed MCQ| Determinant | Buyer power is HIGH when |

    |---|---| | Number / concentration of buyers | Buyers are few, large-volume, geographically concentrated, or able to aggregate digitally | | Product standardisation | Products are commoditised / standardised rather than unique | | Switching costs | Switching is cheap and easy for the buyer | | Buyer threat of backward integration | Buyers could make the product themselves | | Price sensitivity | Buyers are motivated primarily by price |

    Examples: Indian FMCG retail — consumers have high power (vast choice, low switching costs) forcing competition on price/quality/promotion. B2B — Big Bazaar and DMart have very high bargaining power over their suppliers.

    How firms reduce buyer power: differentiate the product; build strong brands; loyalty programmes that raise switching costs.

    11.4 Force 4 — Threat of substitutes: four determinants

    Determinant Threat is HIGH when
    Availability of alternatives Other ways exist to satisfy the same need (Bangalore→Hyderabad: bus, train, flight, car)
    Price–performance trade-off Substitutes are cheaper and/or better
    Switching costs Easy for customers to switch
    Trends and external changes New substitutes emerging from technology or regulation

    Example: Indian payments — UPI/digital wallets (Paytm, PhonePe, Google Pay, BHIM) as substitutes for cash, debit and credit cards, accelerating after demonetization. Also: physical albums → digital music.

    Defences against substitutes: innovation, improving value creation, bundling products and services to make switching unattractive.

    MCQ trap: A substitute comes from outside the industry and satisfies the same need differently. A rival is inside the industry. Confusing forces 4 and 5 is the most common error.

    11.5 Force 5 — Intensity of rivalry: six determinants guaranteed MCQ| Determinant | Rivalry is HIGH when |

    |---|---| | Number and size of competitors | Many firms of similar size (note: raw numbers mislead — 100 firms where 4 hold 90% share is a very different structure from 100 equal firms) | | Industry growth rate | The market is stagnant — everyone fights for a share of the same pie (a growing market lowers rivalry) | | Product differentiation | Products are similar / commoditised | | Fixed costs | High fixed costs push firms to chase volume and cut prices to recover the investment | | Exit barriers | High exit barriers — it is cheaper to keep competing than to leave | | Excess capacity | Spare capacity in the market pressures firms to cut prices |

    Examples: Swiggy vs Zomato in food delivery — discounts, area expansion, heavy marketing, price wars, profitability elusive. Telecom post-Jio — intensified rivalry, consolidation, exits.

    Is high rivalry always bad? Bad for company profits, often good for consumers — better prices, quality and innovation.

    11.6 Five Forces at a glance

    Force Sub-factors to memorise (count)
    New entrants 7 — capital, scale, differentiation, distribution, government policy, switching costs, network effects
    Supplier power 5 — concentration, uniqueness, switching costs, forward integration, importance of your business
    Buyer power 5 — concentration, standardisation, switching costs, backward integration, price sensitivity
    Substitutes 4 — availability, price–performance, switching costs, trends/external change
    Rivalry 6 — number/size, growth rate, differentiation, fixed costs, exit barriers, excess capacity

    1. Key Success Factors (KSFs)
    1

    Definition: the critical elements — skills, resources and capabilities — that a company must get right to succeed in a particular industry. They vary by industry and are shaped by the five forces.

    Analogy: the winning strokes in cricket — some win with aggressive batting, others with precision bowling; you must master the strokes that matter in your industry.

    How to identify KSFs (four routes): 1. Analyse the five forces to see what drives competition. 2. Study the successful firms in the industry. 3. Consider customer requirements and expectations. 4. Assess regulatory and technology trends.

    The critical exam sentence: "Key success factors do not by themselves explain superior firm performance, but the lack of key success factors can definitely explain poor performance." KSFs are the green fee / tollgate — the price of being allowed to compete at all, not the source of winning. guaranteed MCQ| Industry | Key Success Factors | |---|---| | Indian FMCG | 1. Distribution reach (HUL, ITC reaching remote villages) · 2. Brand strength · 3. Product innovation adapted to local taste · 4. Cost efficiency in a price-sensitive market | | Indian e-commerce | 1. Logistics and supply-chain efficiency · 2. Robust technology platform · 3. Product assortment · 4. Customer trust (easy returns, strong service) |

    KSFs change over time as technology, preferences and regulation evolve — firms must continuously reassess.

    1. Strategic groups
    2

    Definition: a strategic group is a set of firms within an industry that pursue similar strategies along key dimensions — price, product quality, distribution channels, geographic coverage, technology. guaranteed MCQ Why the concept exists: Porter's Five Forces and PESTEL describe the industry as a whole, but not all firms in an industry are the same. Firms inside a strategic group compete more directly with each other than with firms outside it.

    13.1 The critical distinction guaranteed MCQ| | Strategic group | Market segment |

    |---|---|---| | Refers to | Clusters of FIRMS with similar competitive approaches | Groups of CUSTOMERS with similar needs |

    13.2 The Indian airline example

    Strategic group Members Strategy
    Full-service carriers Air India, (formerly) Vistara Meals, lounges, premium service; targets business and premium travellers
    Low-cost carriers IndiGo, SpiceJet No-frills, low price, high aircraft utilisation; targets price-sensitive travellers
    Regional players (within either category) Focus on specific geographies

    Analogy: analysing the IPL only at league-average level (average runs, average bowling speed, average salary) hides that CSK, MI and RR run very different strategies — star power vs young talent, spin-heavy attacks vs aggressive batting.

    PART D — INTERNAL ANALYSIS (Module 4)

    1. Why internal analysis

    Core question: "What are our firm's unique abilities and resources that can be leveraged to gain and sustain competitive advantage?"

    Analogy: a ship's captain must read the weather and currents (external) and know the strengths and limits of the vessel and crew (internal). Also used: a football coach who studies opponents (external) but must also know which players sprint, defend, and control the ball (internal).

    Key exam line: external and internal analysis are two sides of the same coin. A firm with extraordinary internal capabilities but blind to external change suffers strategic myopia — the example given is Kodak and digital photography.

    14.1 The seven diagnostic questions of internal analysis

    1. What resources do we possess?
    2. What capabilities do these resources yield?
    3. Which capabilities constitute core competencies?
    4. Where are our strengths clustered and where are the weaknesses?
    5. How do our strengths align with external opportunities?
    6. Are we organised and managed to capitalise on our internal advantages?
    7. What resources or capabilities should we develop or acquire?

    14.2 Components of the internal environment

    Resources · Capabilities · Core competencies · Organisational structure and culture · Financial health · Human capital.

    14.3 The five tools of internal analysis (in order)

    Resources → Capabilities → Core competencies → Value chain → VRIO.

    1. Resources — the foundation
    3

    Definition: the assets available to the firm that can be used to conceive and implement strategies.

    15.1 Tangible resources — four categories guaranteed MCQ| Category | Contents | Indian example |

    |---|---|---| | Financial | Cash reserves, access to capital, borrowing capacity, credit lines, government support | Reliance funding the Jio rollout; L&T and Adani winning large infrastructure contracts | | Physical | Buildings, factories, machinery, computers, land, vehicles | Tata Steel plants; Amazon/Flipkart warehouses; Maruti Suzuki, Tata Motors, Mahindra, Hyundai manufacturing footprints | | Organisational | Structure, management systems, communication channels, legal entities, brand portfolios, distribution channels | Infosys's managerial architecture across countries; Flipkart's supply network | | Technological | Proprietary technology, patents, trade secrets, specialised R&D, IPR | Dr. Reddy's, Sun Pharma patent portfolios and formulation technology |

    15.2 Intangible resources — four categories guaranteed MCQ| Category | Contents | Indian example |

    |---|---|---| | Human | Collective skills, knowledge, creativity, motivation, trust embedded in people | TCS, Wipro, Infosys talent pools | | Innovation | R&D capabilities, scientific knowledge, information flows, patents, product development skills | Biocon's biotech leadership; Bharat Biotech's rapid COVID-19 vaccine development | | Reputational | Brand value, customer loyalty, company image | Amul's dairy trust; Tata's trustworthiness; Britannia's quality assurance | | Relational | Networks, alliances, supplier and customer relationships, government ties | ITC e-Choupal rural sourcing network |

    Why intangibles matter more today: "You cannot easily buy a strong brand, knowledge capital, or organisational culture." Tangible resources typically deliver competitive parity; intangible resources deliver differentiation and sustained advantage.

    15.3 The resource audit — five steps likely MCQ (order matters)| Step | Name | What you do |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | Recognition | List all assets — physical, financial, human, technological, reputational | | 2 | Measurement | Assess value: does this asset reduce cost, improve quality, or serve customers better? | | 3 | Classification | Organise into meaningful categories (tangible / intangible) | | 4 | Benchmarking | Compare against competitors to find relative strengths and weaknesses | | 5 | Assess durability and imitability | Which resources can be sustained, and which can be easily copied? |

    Analogy: the captain of an expedition taking a careful inventory of tools and provisions before setting sail.

    Integrative example — Marico: physical manufacturing assets + wide distribution + health/wellness innovation + strong R&D + trusted retailer relationships = "a competitive fortress difficult for competitors to assault."

    1. Capabilities
    1

    Definition: a firm's capacity to deploy resources in a coordinated manner to perform tasks and achieve desired ends.

    Resources Capabilities
    Nature Inputs / productive assets Capacity to perform activities
    Can be Owned, purchased, measured, accumulated, sold Practised — enacted through teams, routines, operations
    Portability Transferable, sellable Not easily portable — embedded in the organisation
    Kitchen analogy The ingredients (flour, spices, vegetables, utensils) The recipes, routines and techniques the chef uses

    Key exam line: "Resources may be owned, but capabilities are practised."

    16.1 How capabilities develop

    Not bought off the shelf — they develop over time through repeated practice, trial and error, and organisational learning. Asian Paints is the flagship Indian example: the resources are pigments, factories, outlets and digital platforms, but the capability is forecasting paint demand across thousands of retail points and delivering on time almost everywhere in India — honed over decades.

    16.2 Resource vs capability — worked examples very likely MCQ format| Firm | Resource | Capability |

    |---|---|---| | Walmart | Trucks, warehouses, IT systems, inventory | Supply chain and inventory management — the cross-docking system | | Google | Talented engineers, servers | Organisational routines in recruitment, retention and innovation-friendly culture | | Amazon | Server infrastructure | Analysing data to predict customer preferences and orchestrating logistics at scale | | ITC e-Choupal | Digital kiosks, connectivity, trained sanchalaks (lead farmers), IT infrastructure | Integrating technology with trust-based farmer relationships into a procurement model that cut out middlemen and gave farmers direct price information | | Maruti Suzuki | Plants, vendor contracts | Vendor development systems and supply-chain synchronisation | | Reliance Jio | Spectrum, capital | Bundling telecom infrastructure with digital services and scaling rapidly through pricing and distribution innovation | | Tata Motors | Manufacturing facilities | Design and engineering processes | | Infosys | Pool of engineering talent | Project delivery model; Mysore training and onboarding | | Amul | Network of dairy farmers | Cooperative governance and distribution system |

    16.3 Capabilities are embedded

    "If Google loses 500 engineers, does it lose its innovative capability? Not necessarily" — because innovation lives in systems, culture, knowledge-sharing routines and leadership, not in individuals. This embeddedness is exactly what makes capabilities harder to copy than resources.

    16.4 The Indian flavour — Jugaad

    Jugaad = creative improvisation. Because institutional infrastructure (legal enforcement, logistics, regulatory clarity) is not always robust, Indian firms build capabilities around improvisation and adaptability. Example: microfinance institutions developing rural outreach and repayment collection through group lending built on trust networks rather than paperwork — i.e. capability built on embedded social capital, not IT systems.

    16.5 Five functional categories of capability

    Category Examples
    Distribution Walmart's supply chain; Reliance Retail logistics
    Human resource management Google's recruitment/culture; Infosys's Mysore training
    Management information systems Amazon's analytics/personalisation; Zomato's matching of demand and restaurant supply
    Marketing P&G's global branding; HUL's rural reach and Surf Excel's "Daag Achhe Hain"
    R&D Pfizer; Dr. Reddy's and Biocon in generics, biosimilars, cost-effective innovation

    16.6 Dynamic capabilities

    Capabilities are not static. In the early 2000s the IT outsourcing capability of Infosys and TCS was rare and valuable; today it is widespread and commoditised, so Indian IT is moving into AI, digital platforms and consulting. Dynamic capabilities = the ability not just to perform well today but to adapt capabilities and resources for tomorrow.

    The one-line takeaway: "Yesterday's strong capability becomes today's industry standard."

    1. Core competencies

    Definition: the collective learning in the organisation, especially how to coordinate diverse production skills and integrate multiple streams of technologies — the select few capabilities that are valuable, rare, hard to copy, and so embedded that they become the essence of the firm.

    Orchestra analogy ← guaranteed MCQ: - Instruments (violin, drums, trumpets) = resources - Musicians' skills and rehearsed coordination = capabilities - The orchestra's unique, harmonious, inimitable sound = core competency

    17.1 The four requirements of a core competency

    1. Deeply embedded in organisational routines and culture.
    2. Arise from a synergistic bundling of multiple resources and capabilities.
    3. Deliver superior customer value relative to competitors.
    4. Enable expansion into new markets or products — i.e. create growth avenues.

    17.2 Managerial implications — four decision areas

    Decision Implication
    Resource allocation Invest in strengthening and protecting core competencies, not diluting effort on non-core activities
    Mergers and acquisitions Should complement or augment core competencies
    Outsourcing Non-core functions can be outsourced to free internal resources
    Innovation focus Continuously nurture and upgrade competencies as the environment changes

    17.3 The American Airlines reservation system case ← recurring example

    Applied to VRIO: - Valuable? Yes — enabled early ticket sales and effective data capture. - Rare? Yes — only a few airlines owned similar systems. - Costly to imitate? Yes — competitors had to pay to join or build their own. - Organised to exploit? Yes — fully embedded in operations and the revenue model. ⇒ A competitive moat: American benefited operationally and by charging rivals for access.

    1. The VRIO framework guaranteed MCQ; know the four tests and the four outcomes
    3

    Developed by Jay Barney; adapted in Contemporary Strategy Analysis.

    Letter Test The question
    V Valuable Does it let the firm exploit an opportunity or neutralise a threat?
    R Rare Is it scarce — possessed by few or no current/potential competitors?
    I Inimitable (costly to imitate) Is it difficult or expensive for competitors to copy or acquire?
    O Organised to capture value Is the firm structured, managed and aligned to exploit it fully?

    MCQ trap: The O in VRIO is "Organised to capture value". In the older VRIN version the fourth letter is N = Non-substitutable. This course teaches VRIO and explicitly notes that being organised also protects against substitutability.

    18.1 The four VRIO outcomes guaranteed MCQ| V | R | I | O | Outcome |

    |:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|---| | ✗ | — | — | — | Competitive disadvantage (and poor performance) | | ✓ | ✗ | — | — | Competitive parity | | ✓ | ✓ | ✗ | — | Temporary competitive advantage | | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | Sustained competitive advantage |

    18.2 The three sources of inimitability guaranteed MCQ| Barrier | Meaning | Example |

    |---|---|---| | Unique historical conditions | Advantages accumulated over time — early-mover benefits, path dependency | Tata's brand equity built across generations | | Causal ambiguity | Competitors cannot identify what exactly makes the firm successful — is it the process, the people, or the culture? Because capabilities are complex, tacit and embedded | Any deeply cultural competitor advantage | | Social complexity | The resource is embedded in interpersonal relationships, teamwork, trust and culture | Amul's cooperative model integrating millions of farmers; family-run businesses |

    18.3 VRIO worked examples

    Firm V R I O Verdict
    Amazon (logistics + recommendation algorithms) Rapid delivery, better experience Few can match scale and integration Immense data, scale and managerial competence Systems fully aligned Sustained competitive advantage
    Infosys (training + global delivery model) Consistent high-quality IT services Rare in the early 2000s Became replicable over time Well organised, strong leadership Advantage erodes unless continuously improved
    American Airlines (reservation system) ✓ ✓ ✓ ✓ Sustained advantage + rival access fees

    18.4 VRIO in practice — five managerial steps

    1. Resource audit — catalogue all resources and capabilities.
    2. VRIO analysis — apply the four questions to each.
    3. Strategic prioritisation — invest selectively to enhance and protect the winners.
    4. Organisational alignment — build structures, culture and processes to exploit and protect them.
    5. Continuous monitoring — what is rare and costly today may not be tomorrow.

    1. Value chain analysis (Michael Porter) guaranteed MCQ; know which activities are primary vs support
    1

    Idea: break the firm's operations into discrete activities that collectively create customer value. Competitive advantage arises when the firm configures these activities to deliver lower cost or distinctive value versus competitors.

    19.1 The five PRIMARY activities (in sequence)

    # Activity Contents Example
    1 Inbound logistics Receiving, storing and distributing raw materials; warehousing; inventory management Amul consolidating milk from millions of farmer-producers while keeping it fresh
    2 Operations Transforming inputs into finished products; quality control, manufacturing flexibility, waste minimisation Tata Motors building affordable vehicles for Indian road conditions
    3 Outbound logistics Warehousing and distributing finished goods; distribution centres; transportation Flipkart's last-mile delivery into rural and urban India
    4 Marketing and sales Informing buyers, promoting the brand, channel management, sales incentives HUL, Dabur — rural distribution, brand campaigns, dealer networks
    5 Service After-sales support, installation, repairs, customer support Infosys, TCS service delivery and client relationship management

    19.2 The four SUPPORT activities

    Activity Contents Example
    Procurement Sourcing raw materials, components, services Reliance Fresh optimising procurement to lower input costs
    Technology development (R&D) Product and process innovation Dr. Reddy's, Biocon R&D facilities and partnerships
    Human resource management Hiring, training, retention Infosys continual upskilling of IT professionals
    Firm infrastructure General management, finance, legal, planning, IT systems Tata group corporate governance enabling coherence across businesses

    MCQ trap: Procurement is a SUPPORT activity; inbound logistics is a PRIMARY activity. They sound similar and are the classic swap in exam options. Likewise technology development is SUPPORT, not primary.

    19.3 Six uses of value chain analysis

    1. Map cost drivers and identify where cost leadership can be achieved.
    2. Detect activities that enhance differentiation and premium pricing.
    3. Diagnose inefficiencies and redundancies.
    4. Decide what to keep in-house vs outsource.
    5. Prioritise investment in activities underpinning core capabilities.
    6. Integrate IT and analytics to optimise logistics, engagement and operations.

    19.4 Two contrasting configurations

    Walmart Apple
    Strategy served Cost leadership Differentiation
    Inbound logistics Shrewd warehouses, inventory control, supplier relationships; low shelf stock, frequent deliveries —
    Operations Streamlined for efficiency, minimal handling cost Control over manufacturing
    Outbound Fast delivery to thousands of stores Exclusive retail experience
    Marketing Everyday low prices funded by operational savings Brand marketing reinforcing a unique identity
    Technology — In-house R&D, tightly integrated hardware and software
    Service Standardised — convenience and reliability Robust post-sale service

    Integration point: advantage comes not from excelling at one activity but from how well the entire chain is coordinated. "Top-tier innovation hampered by poor supply chain management erodes overall competitiveness." Orchestra analogy again: primary activities = the musicians, support activities = the conductor, scores, lighting and sound engineers.

    1. SWOT analysis and the holistic view
    2

    Internal External
    Positive Strengths Opportunities
    Negative Weaknesses Threats

    MCQ trap: Strengths and weaknesses are internal; opportunities and threats are external. This is the single most-asked SWOT question.

    20.1 The stated limits of SWOT likely MCQ

    SWOT alone is too broad and superficial. Without linking to VRIO or the value chain, it produces vague or overstretched recommendations. "A strength like 'strong brand' is meaningful only if that brand is a unique, difficult-to-imitate asset that the firm leverages effectively" — i.e. only if it passes VRIO.

    20.2 How the three tools fit together guaranteed MCQ| Tool | Role |

    |---|---| | SWOT | Provides the snapshot — a broad map of internal vs external, positive vs negative | | VRIO | Filters the internal side — only strengths that pass V, R, I and O count as sources of sustained advantage | | Value chain | Operationalises the diagnosis — breaks activities down to show where value is actually created or lost |

    20.3 Apple SWOT (the worked example)
    Strengths Design excellence; ecosystem integration (both satisfy VRIO)
    Weaknesses Higher prices; limited compatibility in certain markets
    Opportunities Expanding into AI and services — aligned with Apple's R&D capabilities
    Threats Cost-sensitive competitors; technological shifts

    20.4 Kodak — the integrating failure case

    Kodak was a market leader with solid photography expertise, but failed to align core capabilities with shifting market realities, lagged in digital innovation, and did not restructure operations and R&D. Result: loss of key competencies and of survival. Kodak is used three separate times in this course — for external myopia (§14), SWOT/value-chain misalignment (§20), and failure of strategy renewal (§23).

    PART E — COMPETITIVE STRATEGY (Module 5)

    1. Competitive strategy and competitive positioning
    2

    Competitive strategy = the set of coordinated actions and decisions a firm makes to outperform its rivals within its industry. "It is not just about choosing the markets to operate in, but fundamentally about how the firm chooses to compete."

    • Competitive strategy sits at the business level, not the corporate level. guaranteed MCQ
    • Corporate strategy: What businesses should we be in?
    • Business/competitive strategy: How should we compete in these businesses?

    21.1 Four perspectives on competitive strategy

    Perspective Emphasis
    Competitive positioning (Porter) Choosing a distinct position and value proposition — the course's primary lens
    Evolutionary Continuous adaptation and learning in dynamic markets
    Game theory / strategic interaction Anticipating rivals' moves and responses (pricing, entry, capacity)
    Market-based view Industry structure and forces shaping competition

    21.2 Competitive positioning

    Definition: the deliberate and systematic process by which a company defines and establishes a unique and valuable place for itself within the competitive landscape. It answers "for whom and for what will we compete?"

    Analogies used: - Choosing where to sit at a crowded table — not all seats are equal in visibility, influence or fit. - The visible tip of the strategic iceberg — beneath the surface lie resources, capabilities, routines and investments; positioning is the external expression of internal strategic decisions. guaranteed MCQ Consequence of misalignment: customers receive conflicting messages, credibility erodes, and the firm ends up stuck in the middle.

    1. Value proposition
    3

    Definition: the core premise of value the firm intends to deliver — it answers "why should customers buy from us?" It is far more than a slogan or tagline: it is the comprehensive set of tangible and intangible benefits promised and delivered.

    22.1 Four criteria for an effective value proposition

    It must be (1) clear, (2) relevant to the target customers' needs, (3) compelling enough to trigger purchase, and (4) differentiating from competitors.

    22.2 Three core components likely MCQ| Component | Content |

    |---|---| | Customer centricity | Deep, empathetic understanding of customer needs, preferences and jobs to be done | | Benefits offered | Functional (performance, reliability, cost savings) · Emotional (status, trust, security) · Social (community, social responsibility, ethical alignment) | | Uniqueness | Value not easily replicated — from proprietary technology, brand heritage, superior service, or innovative business models |

    22.3 Four types of value proposition guaranteed MCQ| Type | Basis | Positioning it implies |

    |---|---|---| | Superior product features | Advanced technology, exceptional functionality, superior quality, unique design | Premium segment; innovation and quality paramount | | Cost savings | Operating efficiencies enabling lower prices without sacrificing essential quality | Affordability, efficient delivery, no-frills value | | Customer experience | Convenience, personalisation, responsiveness, emotional connection — not just what is sold but how it is sold and experienced | Experiential differentiation | | Brand reputation | Established brand equity — trustworthiness, heritage, prestige, social status | Relational/emotional differentiation |

    Porter's link: choosing among cost leadership, differentiation and focus is choosing the pathway by which your value proposition finds expression in the marketplace.

    22.4 Positioning maps (perceptual maps)

    Visual plots of where a firm and its rivals stand along two dimensions — commonly price vs quality, but also innovation, customer service or convenience. Uses: reveal crowded clusters and market gaps / underserved niches, and guide investment in product, marketing and pricing.

    22.5 Three pitfalls in positioning likely MCQ

    1. Stuck in the middle — trying to compete on broad cost leadership and differentiation without excelling in either; strategy becomes ambiguous and customers see no compelling reason to choose you.
    2. Failure to adapt — clinging to an outdated value proposition in fast-moving markets.
    3. Underestimating new entrants — startups and disruptors with novel value propositions rapidly taking share from complacent incumbents.

    1. Porter's generic strategies guaranteed MCQ; know the 1980 source
    3

    Source: Michael Porter's 1980 book Competitive Strategy.

    Broad target Narrow target
    Lower cost Cost leadership Cost focus
    Differentiation Differentiation Differentiation focus

    Three generic strategies: cost leadership, differentiation, focus — with focus splitting into cost focus and differentiation focus.

    Porter's central warning: pursuing more than one generic strategy without a well-defined hierarchy causes strategic confusion and dilution — "stuck in the middle" — resulting in neither cost efficiency nor meaningful differentiation.

    23.1 Cost leadership

    Goal: become the lowest-cost producer in the industry or segment, offer acceptable products at the lowest price, and profit through volume and efficiency. Most potent where price competition is intense, customers are price sensitive, and product-feature innovation is difficult.

    Five levers to achieve it likely list MCQ| Lever | Mechanism | |---|---| | Economies of scale | Spread fixed costs (machinery, buildings, R&D) over huge volumes | | Efficient operations / process optimisation | Streamlined manufacturing, optimised logistics, minimal inventory — lean manufacturing, Six Sigma, just-in-time | | Technological innovation and automation | Factory automation, supply chain software, predictive analytics for demand forecasting | | Supplier bargaining power | Use purchasing volume to negotiate discounts and preferential credit | | Strict cost controls and financial discipline | Scrutiny of overheads, marketing, travel; a culture of cost consciousness with aligned metrics and incentives |

    Four benefits: (1) market share expansion through price competitiveness; (2) healthy total margins via volume; (3) creates a barrier to entry (rivals cannot match the cost base); (4) a buffer in economic downturns when consumers turn price sensitive.

    Four risks: (1) price wars and margin erosion; (2) perception of inferior quality — the firm looks "cheap" rather than valuable; (3) neglect of innovation → obsolescence; (4) technological disruption — e.g. digital-only banks with minimal physical infrastructure out-competing legacy banks on cost.

    23.2 Differentiation

    Goal: develop products/services customers perceive as unique or distinctively superior, justifying premium pricing, higher margins and stronger loyalty. "It is not about superficial differences or marketing spin" — the differentiation must be meaningful and difficult to replicate.

    Four drivers: (1) innovative features (proprietary R&D, design excellence, novel application of technology); (2) brand equity (trust, prestige, heritage, emotional attachment); (3) superior customer service (personalisation, responsive after-sales, customisation, seamless experience); (4) quality excellence (superior materials, craftsmanship, durability, attention to detail).

    Four benefits: (1) premium prices and higher margins; (2) customer loyalty — customers become less price sensitive; (3) barriers to competition — a protective moat; (4) attracting varied customer segments and premium positioning across a portfolio.

    Four risks: (1) high cost of sustained investment in R&D, marketing, quality; (2) imitation — rapid technological diffusion shortens the life of uniqueness; (3) changing customer preferences making today's features obsolete; (4) over-differentiation — excessive features that complicate the product, confuse customers and add disproportionate cost.

    23.3 Focus

    Goal: concentrate resources on serving a narrow, well-defined segment or niche exceptionally well rather than the broad market. Advantage comes from depth rather than breadth.

    Two variants guaranteed MCQ| Variant | Aim within the niche | |---|---| | Cost focus | Become the lowest-cost producer within a narrowly defined customer group or geography | | Differentiation focus | Offer products uniquely tailored to the tastes, requirements or cultural preferences of the niche, enabling premium pricing |

    Four reasons firms pursue focus: (1) resource limitations — lacking the scale to address entire markets; (2) deep customer understanding within the segment; (3) customisation and specialisation — especially valuable given India's linguistic, cultural and socio-economic diversity; (4) reduced competitive intensity — niches are less crowded.

    Four benefits: (1) strong customer loyalty; (2) competitive shielding from mass-market players; (3) clear strategic direction and organisational alignment; (4) potential for high margins (particularly with differentiation focus).

    Four risks: (1) segment vulnerability — the niche may contract due to demographic shifts, changing preferences or technology; (2) competitive encroachment — a successful niche attracts large, better-resourced entrants; (3) dependence risk — heavy reliance on a concentrated customer base; (4) limitation on growth — niches are by definition limited; once saturated, growth requires stepping outside the focus.

    23.4 Three generic strategies at a glance

    Cost leadership Differentiation Focus
    Competitive advantage sought Low cost Uniqueness Either, within a niche
    Target scope Broad Broad Narrow
    Price Lowest Premium Depends on variant
    Key risk Price wars, quality perception Imitation, cost of innovation Segment shrinks or is invaded
    Control emphasis (§26) Financial controls Strategic controls Mixed

    1. Sustaining competitive advantage — strategy renewal and agility
    1

    Core assertion: competitive advantage is not a static achievement or a one-time event. "Winning in the marketplace today does not guarantee winning tomorrow."

    Marathon runner analogy: crossing one finish line is an accomplishment, but staying a champion across seasons requires continuous training, diet adjustment and responsiveness.

    24.1 Strategy renewal — three intertwined activities likely MCQ| Activity | Content |

    |---|---| | Market sensing | Continuously scanning for evolving customer preferences, emerging technology, shifting regulation, competitor moves | | Strategic moves | Acting — launching innovative products, adopting new business models, repositioning, entering underserved segments | | Capability development | Building and upgrading internal capabilities — talent, processes, technology, a culture that embraces change and learning |

    24.2 Renewal examples

    • Flipkart — online bookstore → category expansion → supply chain partnerships and warehouses → omnichannel with Supermart grocery and exclusive private labels.
    • Tata Motors — aggressive investment in electric vehicles and sustainable mobility, anticipating changes in consumer values and regulation.
    • Netflix — "a masterclass in proactive reinvention."
    • Counter-examples: Kodak (film → digital) and Blockbuster (late pivot against streaming).

    Nature analogy: the chameleon changing colour to match its surroundings — "renew or perish."

    24.3 Strategic agility

    Definition: the organisation's capacity to move fast, decisively and flexibly when opportunities or threats emerge — the practical manifestation of continuous renewal.

    Three enablers: 1. Organisational culture that encourages experimentation, tolerates failure, and rewards learning. 2. Decentralised decision making — empowered teams close to market signals respond faster than rigid hierarchies. 3. Learning orientation — institutionalised feedback loops and knowledge sharing.

    PART F — STRATEGY IMPLEMENTATION (Module 5, second half)

    1. Aligning strategy and structure
    2

    The framing sentence: strategy is the architectural blueprint; the blueprint alone does not make the building. "The best-crafted strategies fail without robust, disciplined implementation."

    Strategy implementation = translating strategy into actionable steps: what structure is required, what control systems must be established, how leadership will drive change, and how culture can support or hinder the goals.

    Strategic drift = the condition where the company's actual practices diverge from its stated goals because strategy and structure are misaligned.

    25.1 Consequences of misalignment likely MCQ

    1. Delayed decisions
    2. Employee confusion about priorities and goals
    3. Initiatives flounder from unclear roles and poorly allocated resources
    4. The organisation becomes less responsive to market shifts

    Indian illustration: public sector banks structured for a monopoly era — rigid hierarchies, complex approval chains — could not execute customer-centric digital banking after liberalisation, leading to missed opportunities, poor service innovation and mounting non-performing assets.

    25.2 Alignment success cases

    Firm Strategy Structural response
    Bharti Airtel (post-deregulation, early 2000s) Rapid national expansion needing both centralised coordination and local responsiveness Outsourced network management to Ericsson and Nokia, retained core strategy and customer-facing operations in-house — freeing focus for branding and customer service
    Infosys / Wipro Focus on global consulting and digital transformation Restructured delivery units into industry-focused verticals and horizontal competency groups instead of geography- or project-based teams
    Amul Empower dairy farmers, build a value chain from milk collection to marketing A three-tier co-operative structure: village-level societies → district-level milk unions → apex body GCMMF — ensuring representation, accountability and efficient resource flow
    Reliance Industries Diversification from petrochemicals into retail, telecom, digital platforms Group structure: distinct business units with specialised leadership and decision autonomy, plus shared services at the corporate centre for capital, governance and brand
    HDFC / ICICI Digital banking and FinTech integration New business units for digital product management, partnerships and risk, separate from legacy structures

    MCQ: Amul's structure is three tiers — village societies, district milk unions, and GCMMF (Gujarat Co-operative Milk Marketing Federation) as the apex body.

    25.3 The A-S-P model guaranteed MCQ

    Analysis → Strategy → Performance.

    Stage Content Analogy
    Analysis Scan the external environment (markets, competitors, technology, regulation) and internal capabilities (resources, culture, systems) A chess player surveying the board before moving
    Strategy Where do we compete? How do we win? Which capabilities matter most? A recipe — useless if the kitchen layout can't execute it
    Performance The outcome — but critically dependent on how the strategy is implemented, which depends on structure A feedback loop, not a terminal point

    The A-S-P process is a flywheel: each cycle of analysis → strategy → performance informs the next. Structural flexibility is what allows lessons from performance to prompt realignment rather than stagnation.

    The Indian kitchen analogy: even the most detailed recipe (strategy) fails in a cluttered, ill-equipped kitchen where tools are scattered (structure).

    1. Chandler's principle and structural evolution
    4

    26.1 "Structure follows strategy" — Alfred Chandler guaranteed MCQ

    Chandler charted how American corporations evolved their structures as their business models diversified: volume expansion → geographic spread → vertical integration → diversification. Core premise: when firms change strategic direction, existing structures become obsolete and must be re-engineered, or inefficiency and internal dissonance result.

    The reciprocal nuance the lecture insists on: "While strategy largely drives structure, structure also certainly shapes what strategy can achieve." An overly bureaucratic structure stifles innovation strategy; an overly decentralised one creates agility but hinders coordination. Structure both supports and limits strategic options. likely "which statement is most accurate" MCQ

    26.2 The three structural stages guaranteed MCQ| Stage | Structure | When it fits | Limitations |

    |---|---|---|---| | 1 | Simple structure | Early-stage organisations — a small manufacturer, a new tech startup. The founder/owner-manager directly oversees nearly all functions: strategy, operations, finance, marketing, crisis resolution. Informal, direct communication, minimal specialisation | Direct control limits scaling; bottlenecks arise as the owner's capacity is stretched; responsiveness stagnates as the firm grows or diversifies | | 2 | Functional structure | Volume and operational complexity outgrow one leader. Division by function — production, sales, finance, HR, marketing — all reporting upward | Efficiency through repetition, technical expertise, standardisation — but departments become silos without cross-functional collaboration | | 3 | Multi-divisional (M-form) structure | Diversification — multiple products, services or industries. Each division operates like a semi-autonomous business with its own leadership, budget and strategy, coordinated by a central corporate office that allocates resources, sets policy and monitors performance | Needs robust coordination mechanisms, shared values, strong governance and cross-divisional synergies — otherwise divisions compete for resources or diverge strategically |

    Analogy for failing to evolve: running a conglomerate with a simple structure is like using a bicycle for a cross-country trek — possible only up to a point.

    26.3 Four drivers of structural adaptation likely list MCQ| Driver | How it forces structural change |

    |---|---| | Market changes | New demand patterns, customer expectations, competitive dynamics; entering new markets or segments | | Technological innovation | New technologies demand new functions and specialised teams | | Competitive pressures | Force diversification, structural decentralisation (pushing decision rights down), and cross-functional collaboration to break silos | | Regulatory and institutional factors | Compliance in banking, telecom, healthcare, energy → new governance committees, dedicated risk units, formalised audit processes; institutional pressure from governments, shareholders and interest groups → transparency and formalised decision rights |

    All of these funnel into strategy shifts that old structures cannot execute.

    26.4 Four frameworks for assessing structural alignment

    1. Fit analysis — consistency between strategic assumptions and current organisational design.
    2. Value chain mapping — align critical value chain activities with the units responsible.
    3. Responsibility charting — clarify roles, decision rights and accountabilities to reduce overlaps and gaps.
    4. Feedback systems — mechanisms for ongoing performance monitoring and structural adjustment.

    26.5 Feedback loops

    Definition: mechanisms connecting performance outcomes back to environmental analysis and strategy review — sales data, customer feedback and competitor moves are regularly analysed, triggering strategy or structural change.

    Marico example (again): consumer shift to health and wellness → launched Saffola Active, revamped packaging and marketing, and created health-and-wellness innovation units with empowered regional brand teams. Leadership regularly reviews business-unit metrics, customer insights and supply chain efficiency.

    Analogy: an advanced sailboat constantly adjusting sails and rudder to wind and current — without feedback-informed adjustment the boat drifts off course.

    1. Performance management
    1

    Definition: the systematic process through which an organisation monitors, measures and manages how effectively it is achieving its strategic objectives and operational goals — translating strategy and structure into measurable outcomes.

    Dashboard analogy ← guaranteed MCQ: performance management is the dashboard of a car. It does not determine the route (strategy) or how the engine is built (structure) — it reports whether you are speeding, low on fuel, or drifting off course.

    Orchestra analogy: composer = strategy (writes the score) · orchestra arrangement = structure · conductor = performance management system (cues entries, balances sound, ensures alignment in timing and expression).

    27.1 Three consequences of no performance management

    1. The organisation keeps investing in the wrong activities.
    2. Misalignment between goals and outcomes goes unnoticed.
    3. Poor execution erodes competitive advantage.

    27.2 Five components of performance management likely MCQ (order matters)

    1. Set performance goals aligned with strategy
    2. Measure KPIs (key performance indicators) reflecting progress
    3. Monitor results against targets
    4. Analyse deviations and understand their causes
    5. Take corrective / improvement action

    1. The Balanced Scorecard guaranteed MCQ
    2

    Developed in the early 1990s by Robert Kaplan and David Norton.

    Purpose: translate vision and strategy into a coherent set of performance measures across four balanced perspectives — because "an exclusive emphasis on financial metrics misses crucial drivers of future performance."

    28.1 The four perspectives

    # Perspective Question it answers Typical metrics Indicator type
    1 Financial How do we look to shareholders? Revenue growth, profitability, cost control, ROI Lagging — backward-looking confirmation
    2 Customer How do customers see us? Customer satisfaction scores, retention rates, market share, brand reputation Leading (foreshadows financial success)
    3 Internal process What must we excel at? Cycle times, quality, innovation rates, supply chain effectiveness Leading
    4 Learning and growth (organisational capacity) Can we continue to improve and create value? Workforce skills, employee engagement, knowledge management, innovation culture Most forward-looking

    28.2 The cause-and-effect chain guaranteed MCQ (direction matters)

    Learning and growth → Internal processes → Customer → Financial

    Investments in learning and growth enable improved internal processes; better processes enhance customer satisfaction and loyalty; strong customer performance drives robust financial results. Articulating these links produces a strategy map.

    MCQ trap: the chain runs upward from learning and growth to financial, not the other way. Financial is the outcome, learning and growth the driver.

    28.3 The seven implementation steps

    Step Action
    1 Define vision and strategy — articulate mission and strategic goals
    2 Develop strategic objectives — identify 3–5 key objectives under each of the four perspectives
    3 Select KPIs — assign measurable indicators and targets
    4 Create a strategy map — diagram the cause-effect links between objectives
    5 Deploy across the organisation — cascade corporate scorecards to department, team and individual scorecards
    6 Integrate with management processes — embed into budgeting, reporting and performance reviews
    7 Review and refine — update as priorities change and lessons accumulate

    Memorise the number: 3–5 objectives per perspective.

    1. Strategic vs financial controls guaranteed MCQ
    2

    Strategic controls Financial controls
    Question "Are we doing the RIGHT things?" "Are we doing things RIGHT?"
    Orientation Future-oriented Backward-looking
    Nature Subjective and qualitative Objective and quantitative
    Focus Whether the firm is pursuing the right strategy given environmental demands and internal capabilities; whether the strategy–structure fit remains appropriate Efficiency, cost management, financial returns
    Typical metrics Progress on innovation projects; customer feedback on new products; speed of responding to market changes; alignment of projects with strategic priorities ROI (return on investment), ROA (return on assets), EVA (economic value added), cost variances, budget adherence
    Communication Frequent, rich communication across management levels Periodic, formal reporting
    Best suited to Differentiation, innovation, and RELATED diversification strategies Cost leadership and UNRELATED diversification strategies

    Two things to memorise together: strategic control ↔ related diversification and differentiation; financial control ↔ unrelated diversification and cost leadership. And note the deliberate echo of §6.4: right things = strategic; things right = financial, exactly as strategy = doing the right things; tactics = doing things right.

    29.1 The dangers of over-reliance on one type

    • Too much financial control ⇒ risk aversion, short-termism, ignoring strategic opportunities.
    • Excessive strategic control ⇒ misalignment, inefficiency, lack of accountability.

    The Balanced Scorecard is presented as the tool that integrates both — financial health alongside customer satisfaction, internal processes and learning.

    29.2 Five challenges in implementing performance management likely list MCQ

    1. Absence of clear, specific, aligned goals across the organisation.
    2. Insufficient feedback and communication — employees uncertain about their contribution.
    3. Resistance to change — fear of unfair evaluation or increased scrutiny.
    4. Managerial capability gaps — managers lacking skills for meaningful, unbiased reviews.
    5. Over-complex or inadequate tools — unintuitive software hinders adoption; oversimplified systems miss critical insight.

    29.3 Integrating performance management into practice

    Embed it into routines and culture (not an occasional bureaucratic event) · link it to budgeting, talent development and resource allocation · maintain communication and transparency · train managers to use it for coaching and empowerment · provide technology platforms for real-time monitoring and dashboards · keep it adaptive as strategies and markets evolve.

    Basics of Financial Management

    Basics of Financial Management — Complete Exam Guide

    Course Instructor: Harshali Damle · Beginner Module

    Course map Module 0 — Introduction Module 1 — Financial accounting foundations: accounting grammar (9 rules) · the accounting equation · P&L account · sales recognition · expenses Module 2 — Balance sheet and beyond: assets (tangible, current, intangible) · inventory · financial instruments · liabilities · equity · cash flow statement Module 3 — Financial statement analysis (vertical/horizontal/ratio) · banking & insurance business models · management accounting · budgeting Module 4 — Corporate finance: time value of money · investment decisions (payback, NPV, IRR) · DCF · risk & return · capital structure · working capital · ALM · startup journey · FinTech regulation

    Exam note: this course is definition-heavy and list-heavy. The 9 accounting rules, the 4 conditions for an asset, the 3 conditions for revenue recognition, the 4 transaction types for sales and for expenses, the ratio formulas, and the regulator-to-activity mapping are the guaranteed MCQ material. Numbers used in worked examples are also fair game.

    MODULE 1 — FINANCIAL ACCOUNTING FOUNDATIONS

    1. What financial accounting is and why it exists
    4

    Financial accounting = "the language of business."

    Formal definition likely MCQ (memorise the four verbs)

    "Financial accounting is the art of recording, classifying, summarising in a significant manner in terms of money, transactions and events which are financial in nature, and interpreting the results thereof."

    The four primary activities: Record → Classify → Summarise → Interpret.

    1.1 Why accounts are created — two reasons

    1. Accountability — "Money with expectation leads to accountability." The pocket-money analogy: money from the grandmother ("do what you want") creates no accountability; money from the mother ("buy a notebook, show the bill, return the change") creates expectations, hence accountability. In business the primary stakeholder is the shareholder/investor, who expects the business to invest, grow and safeguard the money.
    2. Information provision — supplying financial information to stakeholders for decision-making.

    Learning-a-language analogy ← likely MCQ: accounting must be learned as (1) vocabulary (the words and their specific meanings) + (2) grammar/syntax (the rules and principles).

    1.2 The three financial statements guaranteed MCQ| Statement | What it shows | Period or point? | Equation |

    |---|---|---|---| | Balance sheet | Financial position — resources owned and who they belong to | On a particular DATE (a point statement; in India usually 31 March) | Assets = Liabilities + Equity | | Income statement / P&L account | Financial performance — profit or loss | For a PERIOD | Income − Expense = Profit | | Cash flow statement | How changes in the balance sheet and income statement affect cash | For a period | Cash inflow − Cash outflow = Net cash |

    Financial statements are prepared for outsiders, so they give only a broad overview, not day-to-day transaction detail.

    A fourth statement is also required: the Statement of Changes in Equity — explains changes in share capital and reserves, discloses buybacks, new share sales, and reserve movements. Mandatory under Ind AS 1. likely MCQ

    1.3 Users of financial statements

    Internal External
    Shareholders · analysts/advisors · employees · management Government/regulator · lenders (banks, FIs) · suppliers · customers · general public (via the annual report)

    Why the government is interested: to ensure the firm is not committing fraud (which would harm investors, stakeholders and the economy), and for tax calculation.

    1.4 Who sets the rules guaranteed MCQ| Body | Full name | Role |

    |---|---|---| | IASB | International Accounting Standards Board | Created IFRS (International Financial Reporting Standards), mandatory in over 140 countries | | Ind AS | Indian Accounting Standards | A modified form of IFRS, mandatory in India from 2016 | | ICAI | Institute of Chartered Accountants of India | Creates the accounting standards followed in Indian businesses | | MCA | Ministry of Corporate Affairs | Indian regulator for private companies, public companies and LLPs | | SEBI | Securities and Exchange Board of India | Regulates listed companies | | RBI | Reserve Bank of India | Banking sector | | IRDAI | Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India | Insurance sector |

    Accounting standards = rules for recognition of events and transactions, measurement of items, presentation of accounts, and disclosure.

    1. Accounting grammar — the 9 rules guaranteed MCQ; know all nine by name
    1

    # Rule Statement Key illustration
    1 Business entity concept The business enterprise is a separate identity, distinct from its owner and all other stakeholders Flipkart — investors and banks gave money not to Sachin/Binny Bansal but to the artificial legal entity "Flipkart". "There is no person named Flipkart." Accounts are kept for the entity
    2 Money measurement concept Only transactions measurable in money are recorded Excluded: customer loyalty, brand reputation, management quality. In multi-currency operations, one primary currency is chosen and everything is converted to it
    3 Materiality Other concepts may be ignored if the effect is not material. Material = economically significant; it is subjective, differs by business, and is a judgement call Losing ₹50 out of ₹100 vs out of ₹1 lakh. Exception: regulatory requirements — anything the regulator wants must be shown separately, even a ₹10 item
    4 Going concern An assumption that the business will continue operations in the foreseeable future Year ends 31 March; 1 April morning the next year begins. The oldest still-operating company is a Japanese construction company over 1,400 years old (Buddhist temples)
    5 Periodicity concept Accounts are prepared for a specific time period — 1 month, 1 quarter, 6 months, or a year India's financial year: 1 April → 31 March. The US follows a calendar year (1 Jan → 31 Dec)
    6 Conservatism "An accountant should not anticipate future income, but should provide for all possible losses." Income: record only certain profits. Expenses: record certain AND probable expenses Tanishq gold bought at 100. If market value falls to 80 ⇒ record the 20 probable loss. If it rises to 120 ⇒ do NOT record the 20 profit; wait for an actual sale
    7 Consistency Accounting policies are followed consistently period to period; changes only in exceptional circumstances and must be highlighted Key accounting policies are disclosed in the notes to accounts of the annual report
    8 Matching concept Identify and record expenses associated with revenue in the SAME accounting period If income and expenses aren't matched, the profit/loss number is wrong
    9 Accrual concept Record a transaction as and when it occurs, irrespective of the cash element Both cash transactions and promise-based (credit) transactions are recorded

    The most-tested pairing: conservatism (asymmetric treatment of gains vs losses) and matching (expenses go in the same period as the related revenue). Almost every provision in the course traces back to one of these two.

    1. The accounting equation guaranteed MCQ
    1

    3.1 The story

    Shareholders pool capital → create assets (resources from which future economic benefit is expected) → a management team uses those assets to generate sales → sales have a cost of sales → profit = sales − expenses → profit belongs to equity shareholders. If more money is needed, the firm goes to lenders (debt) or other liabilities — but lenders are not owners; the shareholder remains the owner.

    3.2 The two base equations

    $$\textbf{Balance sheet: } \text{Assets} = \text{Liabilities} + \text{Equity}$$ $$\textbf{Income statement: } \text{Income} - \text{Expense} = \text{Profit}$$
