    Interpretation: the asset side tells you what resources the business owns; the liability side tells you who those resources belong to. Equity is the balancing figure — what remains after outsiders have been paid.

    3.3 The unified accounting equation

    Because profit belongs to equity shareholders, combine the two: $$\text{Assets} = \text{Liabilities} + \text{Equity} + \text{Profit}$$ $$\boxed{\text{Assets} = \text{Liabilities} + \text{Opening Equity} + \text{Income} - \text{Expense} - \text{Dividend}}$$

    This single equation is the entire logic of the double-entry bookkeeping (debit/credit) system. Every transaction must keep LHS = RHS — that is the starting point of a balancing balance sheet.

    Transaction = an economic activity where money exchanges hands in some form.

    Running case: Sanjay — school bus driver on weekdays, runs a chaat/snack stall on weekends. 15 transactions in May 2025 are recorded in an Excel template using the unified equation to build a basic balance sheet and income statement.

    1. The Profit & Loss account guaranteed MCQ on the ladder
    1

    Purpose: shows the financial performance (profit or loss) made during a particular period.

    4.1 The P&L ladder — every rung is examinable

    Line Computation
    Sales / Operating revenue / TOP LINE Starting point
    − Cost of goods sold (COGS) / operating expenses
    = Gross profit / Operating profit
    + Other income (non-operating income)
    − Other expenses (admin, selling, advertising)
    = EBITDA Earnings Before Interest, Tax, Depreciation, Amortisation (also PBITDA)
    − Depreciation & Amortisation (D&A)
    = EBIT Earnings Before Interest and Tax (also PBIT)
    − Interest (I)
    = EBT / PBT Earnings/Profit Before Tax
    − Tax
    = EAT / PAT / Net income / BOTTOM LINE This is the final profit that belongs to shareholders

    Mnemonic for the deduction order after EBITDA: D → I → T (Depreciation, Interest, Tax).

    1. Revenue / sales recognition guaranteed MCQ
    4

    Revenue = income arising in the course of ordinary activities; may be called sales, fees, interest, dividend, royalty.

    5.1 Operating vs non-operating revenue — it depends on the nature of the business

    Transaction Company Classification
    Selling a car Tata Motors Operating revenue (core business)
    Selling a spare car Infosys Other income (core business is IT consulting)
    Interest on a fixed deposit A manufacturing company Other income
    Interest received A bank Operating revenue — deposits and lending are its core business

    5.2 The three conditions for recognising revenue ← memorise

    Revenue is recognised only when: 1. The transaction is performed — the entity has done its part (risks and rewards transferred). 2. Revenue is measurable. 3. It is probable that future economic benefits will flow to the entity.

    The third condition is conservatism in action: if economic benefit isn't probable, don't record the sale.

    5.3 The seven worked revenue-recognition cases very likely MCQ source| # | Case | Treatment |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | Big Bazaar sells a shampoo bottle for cash | Clean sale — all three conditions met. Recognise immediately | | 2 | UltraTech ships 500 bags of cement | Depends on the contract — who bears risk in transit. "The one who bears the risk maintains ownership." If delivery terms are ex-warehouse ⇒ recognise when the truck leaves. If delivery at customer's location ⇒ wait until it arrives | | 3 | UltraTech sells 500 bags on 3-month credit (delivered) | Recognise as credit sales even though no cash received — there is a legal claim on the benefits | | 4 | HRX on Myntra, ₹1,000 T-shirt sold 15 March, 1-month return window, year ends 31 March | Recognise the ₹1,000 as revenue AND create a provision for return based on historical return rate. If 10% average ⇒ provision of ₹100. (Matching concept) | | 5 | Mahindra sells tractors with a 3-year warranty | Recognise the sale AND create a provision for warranty/guarantee in the same period | | 6 | L&T builds a factory for Ambuja over 3 years, contract value ₹10 crore | Use the percentage-completion method — take proportionate income each year, based on contract milestones or industry best practice. Recognising all ₹10 cr in year 1 or year 3 would break the matching concept | | 7 | Hotstar — ₹1,200 annual subscription paid 1 Jan 2025, year ends 31 March | Only 3 months' service performed ⇒ recognise ₹300 (₹100/month) this year; ₹900 is DEFERRED REVENUE pushed to next year | | 8 | Infosys — income-tax-department contract: create a website (₹20 lakh) + maintain it (₹80 lakh) | Two separate services with different recognition principles: ₹20 lakh recognised on creation/delivery/QC done; ₹80 lakh recognised pro-rata over the maintenance period |

    5.4 The four sales transaction types guaranteed MCQ| Type | Cash? | Performed? | Effect 1 (P&L) | Effect 2 (Balance sheet) |

    |---|---|---|---|---| | Cash sale | ✓ received | ✓ | Income | Cash ↑ (asset) | | Credit sale | ✗ | ✓ | Income | Trade receivable / debtor ↑ (current ASSET) | | Deferred (unearned) revenue | ✓ received | ✗ | NO income | Liability ↑ ("advance received from customer" — a current LIABILITY) | | Unbilled revenue | ✗ | ✓ but no invoice raised | Income | Unbilled revenue ↑ (a separate CURRENT ASSET account, sitting alongside debtors) |

    The governing rule: "If the company has performed its part in the current year, book income in the current year, irrespective of what happens with cash."

    Why unbilled revenue arises (a service-company phenomenon): delay in raising the invoice; a contractual obligation allowing invoicing only monthly/quarterly/at contract end; or a milestone not yet reached.

    5.5 Gross sales vs net sales likely MCQ

    • Gross sales = sale value + GST/taxes = total billed to the customer.
    • Net sales = gross sales − taxes.
    • The P&L top line is NET SALES, because GST and taxes belong to the government, not the company.

    1. Expenses
    4

    Expenses = costs incurred on various business activities.

    6.1 The Masala Dosa shop example ← memorise the split

    1,000 dosas sold at ₹120 ⇒ Sales = ₹1,20,000

    Expense Rate Total Type
    Batter ₹10/dosa ₹10,000 Product/production
    Potato masala ₹20/dosa ₹20,000 Product/production
    Oil & butter ₹15/dosa ₹15,000 Product/production
    Paper plate ₹5/dosa ₹5,000 Product/production
    Cylinder 3 × ₹1,000 ₹3,000 Product/production
    Helper ₹5,000/month ₹5,000 PERIOD cost
    Shop rent ₹10,000/month ₹10,000 PERIOD cost

    The distinction: "Even if I sell 100 dosas, I pay ₹10,000 rent. Even if I sell 1 lakh dosas, I pay ₹10,000 rent." — that is a period cost. Costs that scale per dosa are product/production costs.

    6.2 Cost of Goods Sold and cost of material guaranteed MCQ on the formula

    COGS = cost of what was actually sold (matching concept). It breaks into cost of material + other direct costs.

    $$\boxed{\text{Cost of material} = \text{Opening stock} + \text{Purchases} - \text{Closing stock}}$$

    Three worked cases (ABC Pvt Ltd, cotton bedsheets):

    Case Opening Purchases Closing Cost of material
    A 0 ₹1,00,000 0 ₹1,00,000
    B ₹50,000 ₹1,00,000 0 ₹1,50,000
    C ₹50,000 ₹1,00,000 ₹20,000 ₹1,30,000

    Why closing stock is subtracted ← the conceptual MCQ: "Till the point the material is in the possession of the company, it is inventory, not an expense. When it goes out of possession at the time of sale, that is when it becomes a cost in the P&L." (Matching concept again.)

    6.3 Direct vs indirect expenses — the factory vs office test likely MCQ| | Direct expenses (production cost) | Indirect expenses (period cost) |

    |---|---|---| | Location | The FACTORY | The OFFICE | | Examples | Manufacturing expenses, factory rent, electricity/power, wages, plant manager's salary | Administrative expenses, strategy/accounts/HR salaries, advertising, financing costs, depreciation on office equipment, taxes |

    6.4 The four expense transaction types guaranteed MCQ

    The governing rule: "If the expense has been incurred — i.e. the benefit has been received — in that period, record it as an expense in that period."

    Type Benefit received? Cash paid? Effect 1 Effect 2
    Cash expense ✓ ✓ Expense in P&L Cash ↓
    Accrued expense / credit purchase ✓ ✗ Expense in P&L Liability ↑ (payables/creditors)
    Prepaid expense ✗ (benefit comes later) ✓ NOT an expense Current ASSET ↑ ("prepaid expenses")
    Provision Not yet — probable future obligation ✗ Expense in P&L Liability ↑ (provision)

    The two mirror-image traps: a prepaid expense is an ASSET (paid, benefit pending); deferred revenue is a LIABILITY (received, service pending).

    6.5 Provisions ← recurring theme

    Definition: allocations for probable but not certain future obligations; estimations made by the business, driven by conservatism.

    The five provisions named in the course likely list MCQ 1. Provision for bad debt — credit sale made, money may not be collected 2. Provision for inventory loss — old inventory that may not sell 3. Provision for return — sales made this year, returns possible next year 4. Provision for guarantee / warranty — warranty period extends into future years 5. Provision for taxes — profits made this year, some tax payable next year

    MODULE 2 — BALANCE SHEET AND BEYOND

    1. Introduction to the balance sheet
    1

    Asha's example ← memorise the numbers:

    Assets ₹ lakh Liabilities ₹ lakh
    Bank balance 10 Bank loan (against house) 20
    House 50 Payable to jeweller 2
    Gold jewellery 10 Equity (net worth) 48
    Total 70 Total 70

    Assets 70 = Liabilities 22 + Equity 48. Asha's net worth is ₹48 lakh.

    Balance sheet definition: indicates financial position as ON A PARTICULAR DATE (not for a period). It tells two things: what resources the company owns and who those resources belong to.

    7.1 The 12-month test guaranteed MCQ| Side | Long term (non-current) | Short term (current) |

    |---|---|---| | Assets | Benefit expected beyond 12 months | Benefit expected within 12 months — realised through sale or consumption within 12 months or one normal operating cycle | | Liabilities | Settled beyond 12 months | Settled within the next 12 months |

    Liability side breaks into three: Equity · Debt · Other liabilities.

    1. Tangible assets
    5

    8.1 The four conditions for an ASSET guaranteed MCQ

    1. It is a resource for the company.
    2. The company has control over it.
    3. The company expects future economic benefits from it.
    4. It can be measured reliably.

    The two-expenditure contrast: studio rent ₹5,000/month — benefit fully exhausted at month end ⇒ expense. Camera ₹50,000 with a 5-year life — future benefit for 4 more years, invoice exists, legally owned ⇒ capital expenditure = ASSET.

    Tangible = has physical substance (land, plant, machinery, electronic goods, vehicles). Intangible = no physical presence.

    8.2 Stage 1 — Acquisition: historical cost guaranteed MCQ

    $$\text{Historical cost} = \text{Purchase cost} + \text{Directly attributable cost}$$

    Component Contents
    Purchase cost Purchase price + non-refundable taxes − discounts
    Directly attributable cost Any expense incurred to bring the asset to its present location and condition for intended use: transportation, insurance in transit, installation, testing/QC, registration fees, architect charges, inspection

    This stage is called capitalisation of an expenditure.

    8.3 Stage 2 — Allocation: depreciation guaranteed MCQ

    Definition: the systematic cost allocation of the depreciable amount over the asset's useful life.

    $$\boxed{\text{Depreciable amount} = \text{Historical cost} - \text{Expected residual (scrap) value}}$$ $$\text{Straight-line depreciation per year} = \frac{\text{Depreciable amount}}{\text{Useful life}}$$

    Useful life = the period over which the asset is expected to be available for use.

    The camera walkthrough (₹50,000, 5-year life, straight line, no scrap):

    Year Opening Depreciation (P&L) Closing (balance sheet)
    1 50,000 10,000 40,000
    2 40,000 10,000 30,000
    3 30,000 10,000 20,000
    4 20,000 10,000 10,000
    5 10,000 10,000 0

    (With a ₹5,000 scrap value, the depreciable amount would be ₹45,000 ⇒ ₹9,000/year.)

    The three depreciation methods likely MCQ| Method | Pattern | Typically used for | |---|---|---| | Straight line (SLM) | Equal usage each year | General assets | | Written down value (WDV) | Higher depreciation in early years, lower later | Technology products — most utility gained early | | Production unit method | Depreciation linked to actual usage/output | Mining — depends on material extracted |

    8.4 Stage 3 — Disposal likely MCQ

    Compare sale price with the net book value (what's left on the balance sheet).

    Camera at end of year 3, book value ₹20,000: - Sold for ₹25,000 ⇒ gain on sale of asset ₹5,000 (to P&L) - Sold for ₹15,000 ⇒ loss on sale of asset ₹5,000 (to P&L)

    8.5 The fixed asset schedule

    Illustrated with Reliance Industries Ltd, annual report 2024. Three sections: - Gross block = historical/purchase cost - Accumulated depreciation = value consumed - Net block = value remaining ⇒ this is what goes on the balance sheet

    1. Current assets
    9

    9.1 Inventory — the four types guaranteed MCQ| Type | Definition | Furniture-manufacturer example |

    |---|---|---| | Raw material | Purchased for production; no processing initiated; typically loses its form in the final product | Logs of wood | | Work in progress (WIP) | Undergoing the production process — half-finished/semi-finished; "on the assembly line" | Partly built chairs | | Finished goods | Fully produced but unsold, lying in the warehouse | Completed chairs and tables | | Stock in trade | Goods purchased specifically for trading/resale | Bought-in items resold as is |

    We value the CLOSING balance as on the balance sheet date. (Last year's closing = this year's opening.)

    9.2 The inventory double effect guaranteed MCQ

    Closing inventory hits both statements: the P&L (via cost of material inside COGS) and the balance sheet (as a current asset).

    Worked example — Sales ₹800, opening stock ₹150, purchases ₹300, correct closing stock ₹100:

    Scenario Closing stock COGS = 150 + 300 − closing Profit = 800 − COGS Current asset
    Correct 100 350 450 100
    Undervalued 80 370 ↑ 430 ↓ 80 ↓
    Overvalued 120 330 ↓ 470 ↑ 120 ↑
    Error COGS Profit Current assets
    Undervaluation of closing inventory Higher Lower Lower
    Overvaluation of closing inventory Lower Higher Higher

    It is a MULTI-YEAR error: this year's wrong closing value becomes next year's wrong opening value.

    9.3 Inventory valuation guaranteed MCQ

    Three things needed: (1) how many different items, (2) the quantity of each, (3) the cost per unit. Total value = Σ (quantity × value per unit).

    Counting the quantity — two systems:

    System Method Used by
    Periodic inventory system Physical inventory count ("taking inventory") at specific intervals, matched with books; adjustments for goods in transit and goods with agents Smaller businesses
    Perpetual inventory system Continuously updated via inventory management systems / databases Larger businesses

    Valuing per unit — the golden rule: $$\boxed{\text{Inventory is valued at the LOWER of COST or NET REALISABLE VALUE (NRV)}}$$ This is conservatism.

    $$\text{NRV} = \text{Normal selling price per unit} - \text{estimated costs of completion and selling}$$

    Cost NRV Value used
    100 90 90
    100 120 100

    What goes INTO cost (matching concept):

    Inventory type Cost includes
    Raw material Product cost only = invoice price + non-refundable taxes − discounts, plus transportation, handling, purchase commission
    WIP and finished goods Product cost + cost of conversion — direct manufacturing costs, labour, direct/production/factory costs

    What is EXCLUDED from inventory cost ← likely MCQ: period costs (admin expenses), general R&D, abnormal items, and financing/interest costs.

    Flow methods named: FIFO (first-in-first-out), LIFO (last-in-first-out), weighted average cost.

    9.4 Financial instruments guaranteed MCQ on the definition

    $$\textbf{A financial instrument is a CONTRACT that creates a financial ASSET for one entity and a financial LIABILITY for another.}$$

    Instrument Asset in the books of Liability in the books of
    Fixed deposit made by ABC Ltd ABC Ltd The bank
    Credit sale ABC → XYZ ABC (receivable) XYZ (payable)
    Loan given by ABC to PQR ABC PQR
    Equity / share capital raised by ABC The shareholders ABC Ltd
    A ₹500 currency note held by ABC ABC (cash) The central bank (RBI)

    The best exam item in this section: the currency note is a contract — "I promise to pay the bearer the sum of ₹500", signed by the Governor of the RBI. Cash is an asset for the holder and a liability for the central bank.

    The four financial assets covered: cash & bank balances · trade receivables · financial investments · derivative instruments.

    9.5 Cash and cash equivalents

    Most liquid asset. Liquidity = the ease with which an asset can be converted into another asset.

    Cash equivalents = short-term highly liquid investments — bank deposits, short-term government bonds, money-market instruments, time deposits quickly convertible to cash.

    The three motives for holding cash ← guaranteed MCQ:

    Motive Purpose
    Transaction motive Day-to-day activities — salaries, routine expenses
    Precautionary motive Unforeseen events / contingencies — e.g. sudden equipment failure needing repair
    Speculative motive Exploit opportunities — M&A, a distressed-buy opportunity

    Why too much cash is a problem: cash generates low returns (savings-account analogy), which reduces overall shareholder return. "Some cash is essential, but too much cash may be a concern for investors."

    9.6 Trade receivables

    Arise from credit sales. Firms extend credit because it is standard business practice, especially B2B, and it helps generate sales. "Extending credit is similar to giving a loan to your customer."

    Credit risk = the risk of financial loss because the other party may fail to discharge an obligation. Losses against trade receivables are called bad debt. Firms mitigate it with a credit appraisal process — background checks, credit history.

    The four stages of a credit loss likely MCQ (order matters)| Stage | Situation | Accounting | |---|---|---| | 1 | Invoice not yet due — credit period running | Ordinary trade receivable; no extra treatment | | 2 | Invoice due, not paid | Start follow-up / collection — calls, reminders | | 3 | Customer still hasn't paid; doubtful | Create a provision for doubtful debts (conservatism) | | 4 | Customer bankrupt or no possibility of collection | Declare a bad debt and write it off — removed from the trade receivable balance |

    The provision is deducted from trade receivables on the balance sheet. It is an adjustment entry passed at the end of every period and reversed at the start of the next — because "no actual transaction has happened."

    Two estimation methods likely MCQ (a) Percentage-of-receivables (percentage-of-sales) method — worked example: - Total sales ₹10 lakh; 70% on credit ⇒ credit sales ₹7 lakh, cash sales ₹3 lakh - No credit loss on cash sales - Management expects 2% of credit sales doubtful - Provision = 2% × ₹7,00,000 = ₹14,000

    (b) Ageing analysis — "the older the outstanding, the higher the probability of bad debt." Firms build an ageing schedule; loss probability increases with the age of the receivable.

    9.7 Factoring / invoice discounting likely MCQ

    A factor is a party that buys receivables from a company, assumes the credit-loss risk, and handles collection.

    Worked example: XYZ Ltd has a ₹7 lakh debtor balance, factor charges 5% and assumes the entire risk. - Cash received by XYZ = ₹7,00,000 − 5% = ₹6,65,000 - ₹35,000 is the factor's fee/gain; the factor bears the credit risk.

    9.8 Investments guaranteed MCQ on the thresholds

    Investments = financial claims on other entities through equity or debt. (Not internal investments in fixed assets or working capital.)

    The differentiator between operating and financial investment is the INVESTOR'S INTENTION — substance over form.

    Type Purpose
    Operating investment Made for business expansion / strengthening operations — long-term, strategic. Motives: diversify into a different product, expand supply chain capability, enter new geographies
    Financial investment Surplus money invested to generate a financial return — usually short-term, smaller stakes

    Control is exercised two ways: power over the investee (via voting rights) and the right to returns from the investment.

    9.9 The four operating investments guaranteed MCQ — thresholds and accounting treatment| Type | Ownership / control threshold | Accounting treatment | Example |

    |---|---|---|---| | Subsidiary | > 50% of voting rights (100% = fully owned; <100% = partly owned) | Consolidated financial statements | WhatsApp and Instagram are subsidiaries of Meta; Flipkart is a subsidiary of Walmart | | Associate | Significant influence — 20%+ shareholding (or control over the board, or major purchase/sale transactions) | Equity method — cost adjusted for the associate's profit/loss. NO consolidation | Tech Mahindra is an associate of Mahindra & Mahindra | | Joint venture | A separate entity jointly controlled by two or more parties; shared management, no party has absolute control | Equity method | Starbucks India = JV of Tata Consumer Products and Starbucks | | Joint operation | Two parties collaborate; NO separate entity is created; control usually equally distributed | Each party recognises its own assets, liabilities, and proportionate revenue and expense in its own books | Collaborative R&D projects |

    Consolidated financial statements: the Companies Act 2013 mandates consolidated statements in addition to standalone statements if the company has one or more subsidiaries. A parent with 2 subsidiaries prepares standalone + consolidated (= parent + sub 1 + sub 2); each subsidiary prepares its own.

    Financial investments are measured at amortised cost or fair value on the balance sheet date.

    1. Intangible assets guaranteed MCQ
    5

    Definition: identifiable non-monetary assets without physical substance. Characteristics: the business has control, and the asset is identifiable/separable — "the business can separate it from itself to generate economic benefits."

    10.1 The five categories

    Category Contents Examples given
    Marketing-related Trademarks, trade names, logos, internet domains, non-compete agreements "Paytm Karo", "Amul — The Taste of India", TATA
    Customer-related Customer lists, customer relationships, databases —
    Artistic-related Books, plays, jingles, videos, photographs, characters Marvel/Avengers characters owned by Disney
    Contract-based Royalty and licensing agreements, construction permits, broadband spectrum rights, operating rights MasterCard's payment network (Razorpay pays royalty/commission); Bewakoof paying licence fees for superhero logos
    Technology-based Patents, software codes Zoho's CRM software

    10.2 The S&P 500 statistic guaranteed MCQ

    Intangibles as a share of total assets of S&P 500 companies:

    1975 → 17% · 2020 → 90%

    10.3 What is NOT an intangible asset guaranteed MCQ| Item | Why it fails |

    |---|---| | Competitive advantage, market share, customer loyalty | No CONTROL — "Can you sell your customer loyalty to someone else? No." | | Employee efficiency, employee skill, management quality (human capital) | The company cannot legally own its employees | | Work ethic, reputation | Not SEPARABLE from the business |

    10.4 Acquired vs internally generated guaranteed MCQ| | Externally acquired | Internally generated |

    |---|---|---| | Treatment | Same as tangible assets. Identifiable intangibles are separated and recorded under their own heads; the unidentifiable balance paid for becomes GOODWILL | Very, very strict. Only if identifiable/separable is it recorded at cost. Unidentifiable/non-measurable intangibles are NOT recorded |

    Depreciation of an intangible asset is called AMORTISATION. Same methods and principles as depreciation. (A patent with a 7-year life is amortised over 7 years.)

    10.5 The goodwill case study — Facebook / WhatsApp, 2014 ← memorise the numbers

    Item Value
    Total paid $22 billion
    Identifiable — user base $2 billion
    Identifiable — trade name "WhatsApp" $448 million
    Identifiable — technology (platform, code, algorithms) $288 million
    Unidentifiable balancing figure = GOODWILL $18.1 billion

    Goodwill may represent future growth prospects, expected synergies — or overpayment. Goodwill is created in M&A/purchase transactions.

    10.6 The internally-generated patent rule (pharma example)

    CAN be capitalised (only once the company is sure the patent will be granted) CANNOT be capitalised
    Registration of the patent · documentation charges · legal fees on the application General R&D cost to develop the idea being patented · any R&D that does not lead to a patent

    10.7 The internally-generated brand rule guaranteed MCQ

    • Apple cannot capitalise its own brand. Tata cannot capitalise its ethical brand image. They are non-separable.
    • BUT — "Pepsi on its own books cannot capitalise its brand. If tomorrow Coca-Cola acquires Pepsi and pays for that brand, Coca-Cola CAN recognise it on its books as an intangible asset."

    Always expensed, never capitalised: R&D expenditure · advertising expense · employee training · CSR expense.

    1. Derivatives and compound instruments

    11.1 Derivatives

    Definition: financial instruments that help companies manage or hedge risk (may also be used to speculate). A derivative is a contract to buy or sell at a future date, but at a price determined today.

    Risks/exposures illustrated: foreign exchange risk (a firm owing 1 million USD a year from now) · raw material price fluctuation (Tanishq and gold) · finished goods price fluctuation (a farmer with onions).

    Instrument Nature
    Forward contract One-to-one, over-the-counter (OTC), non-standardised, created for a specific use. Counterparty could be a bank
    Futures Exchange-regulated, STANDARDISED contracts traded on markets (commodities, Forex)
    Options Include an element of choice — the holder transacts only when the option is favourable. Types: put and call. Traded in the F&O market

    Forward vs future = the classic MCQ: OTC/customised/one-to-one vs exchange-traded/standardised.

    Accounting treatment: derivatives are recorded at fair value to P&L; hedging and speculation use the same treatment. Closed transactions ⇒ profit/loss to P&L. Open contracts ⇒ measured at fair value on the balance sheet, with P/L to the P&L. Higher-value contracts are disclosed in notes to accounts.

    11.2 Compound financial instruments

    Definition: an instrument containing components of both debt and equity.

    Pure debt Pure equity
    Claim Fixed Residual
    Priority Higher Last
    Returns Interest + fixed principal; no share in profits beyond interest Dividends + capital repayment — neither is fixed, both depend on actual profits

    Example: convertible debt — starts as a loan, later convertible into common equity of the borrower. Lenders benefit from a rise in equity prices; interest rate is LOWER than traditional debt; repayment in common equity shares. Other examples: convertible bonds, warrants.

    Repayment priority ladder ← guaranteed MCQ: $$\textbf{1. Pure debt} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{2. Compound instruments} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{3. Pure equity}$$

    1. Liabilities
    7

    12.1 The definition guaranteed MCQ (four elements)

    A liability is: 1. A present obligation 2. Arising from a past transaction or event 3. Unavoidable — it exists independent of the firm's future actions 4. Settled through payment of cash or provision of goods and services

    12.2 The four worked "is it a liability?" cases

    Case Liability? Why
    A Ltd has an open purchase order with B Ltd for ₹1,50,000; goods delivered at month end, payment 30 days after NO Delivery hasn't happened — the transaction has not yet occurred; no present obligation
    S Ltd plans to donate ₹1 lakh to an NGO NO Voluntary activities are not liabilities
    M Ltd must pay corporate tax of ₹55,000 YES Statutory liability — mandatory, unavoidable, based on the past event of having operated and made profit
    D Ltd runs a nuclear plant, licensed on condition that it demolishes and restores the land YES Present obligation from a past event (the licence agreement signed), unavoidable due to legal nature

    12.3 The four classification axes likely MCQ| Axis | Categories |

    |---|---| | Who is owed? | Owner liability (= Equity) vs Outsider/non-owner liability (= Other liabilities) | | When is it due? | Current (≤12 months) vs Non-current (>12 months) | | Can it be measured accurately? | Definite liability (precisely determinable) vs Estimated liability (present but uncertain amount ⇒ provisions, driven by conservatism) | | Is it backed by a specific security? | Secured (backed by a specific asset) vs Unsecured (general claim on the firm's assets) |

    12.4 Operating vs financial liabilities guaranteed MCQ| | Operating liabilities | Financial liabilities |

    |---|---|---| | Source | The firm's operating activities — provision of goods/services, ordinary course of business | Securing finance — primarily debt/borrowings | | Examples | Employee salary payable · supplier accounts payable · government tax payable · accrued expenses · unearned revenue from customers | Debt, equity |

    12.5 Key operating liabilities

    Trade payable — payment for credit purchases from suppliers; the opposite of trade receivable. Settled by payment or set-off against a sale to that party.

    Unearned revenue — advance received from a customer; a liability until goods/services are provided.

    Employee benefits likely MCQ| Type | Definition | Liabilities arising | |---|---|---| | In-service benefits | Paid during the period of service — salary, bonus, healthcare, perks | Salary payable (this month's salary paid next month) · earned leave carried forward (unutilised encashable leave) | | Post-employment benefits | Paid after the employee separates — pension, gratuity, post-retirement healthcare | Estimated using actuary services, which calculate the expected future payment for present and past employees |

    Two post-employment plans: defined contribution plan and defined benefit plan — the promise determines how much liability must be recorded.

    Product warranties — an estimated liability; contractual obligation to bear repair/replacement cost during a promised period. It's a liability because the sale has already been made and the warranty terms are a contractual obligation.

    12.6 Contingent liabilities guaranteed MCQ| | Provision | Contingent liability |

    |---|---|---| | Nature | Present obligation, uncertain amount | POTENTIAL obligation, dependent on an uncertain future event; low likelihood of having to pay | | Recorded on the balance sheet? | YES | NO — disclosed in the NOTES TO ACCOUNTS only |

    Examples: a customer lawsuit the company's lawyers expect to win · a tax dispute the tax experts believe carries no further liability. (Whether an item is a contingent liability or a provision is a management judgement.)

    From Reliance Industries' notes to accounts: disputed claims not acknowledged as debt · guarantees given on behalf of other entities · commitments where work is still pending.

    12.7 Debt vs equity — the three parameters guaranteed MCQ

    (Mr Blue gives debt; Mr Red gives equity.)

    Parameter Debt (Mr Blue) Equity (Mr Red)
    Claim Fixed claim (contractual principal) Residual claim — whatever is left at the end
    Priority in repayment (returns and principal) First Last
    Control / voting rights NONE Yes — via share ownership

    Types of debt:

    Instrument Description
    Bank debt A large amount from a single large lender
    Debenture A large number of small lenders lending small amounts. Some bonds/debentures are traded on stock markets
    Zero-coupon / deep discount bonds No interim interest payments — only a fixed payment at the end of the loan period

    Secured debt is backed by a legal interest in a specific borrower asset via mortgage, pledge or hypothecation.

    12.8 How debt is accounted for guaranteed MCQ

    The rule: the PRINCIPAL only ever touches the balance sheet — new loans taken and repayments are neither income nor expense. Only the INTEREST goes to the P&L.

    Worked example — A Ltd: - Debt 1: opening ₹10 crore @ 10%; ₹1 crore repaid on the LAST DAY of the year ⇒ closing ₹9 crore; interest for the full year on ₹10 cr = ₹1 crore - Debt 2: ₹5 crore taken mid-year @ 10% ⇒ closing ₹5 crore; interest for half a year = ₹25 lakh - Balance sheet borrowings = ₹9 cr + ₹5 cr = ₹14 crore - P&L interest expense = ₹1 cr + ₹25 lakh = ₹1.25 crore - If the interest is unpaid, it appears as a liability on the balance sheet.

    1. Equity
    4

    13.1 Shareholder characteristics likely MCQ

    • Real owners, exercising ownership through voting
    • Separation of ownership and management in larger, professionally-managed businesses — shareholders appoint managers for day-to-day operations and participate only in major decisions (adding management as a stakeholder)
    • Limited liability — a legal protection: the loss is limited to the capital invested; the business cannot claim more. "Limited liability encourages investment in risky assets such as shares."
    • Residual claim — whatever is left after all other claims
    • Transferability of shares — an active secondary market; shareholders change but the company is perpetual

    13.2 The two components of equity

    $$\text{Total Equity} = \textbf{Share Capital} + \textbf{Reserves \& Surplus (Retained Earnings)}$$

    13.3 The three levels of share capital guaranteed MCQ| Level | Definition |

    |---|---| | Authorised share capital | The maximum number of shares the company may issue, specified in the Memorandum of Association, together with the face/par value. Companies pay a fee to the government for this | | Issued share capital | Shares actually offered — a primary market transaction between the company and investors | | Paid-up capital | Shares actually taken up and paid for; the amount actually received by the company. THIS is what appears as share capital on the balance sheet ← the exam point |

    Worked example: authorised 150 crore shares at a face value of ₹10 ⇒ authorised capital = ₹1,500 crore. (Typical Indian face values: ₹10 or ₹5.)

    13.4 Appropriation of profit — two alternatives

    Alternative Name
    Give it back to shareholders in cash Dividend
    Keep/reinvest it in the business Retained earnings / Reserves & Surplus

    Dividend types: - Interim dividend — declared during the year, before the accounts are closed - Final dividend — declared at the end of the year, after the accounts are closed

    Who decides? ← guaranteed MCQ:

    The Board of Directors PROPOSES the dividend; the shareholders APPROVE it. Shareholders may ask for a reduced dividend but CANNOT ask for a higher one in general circumstances.

    Why reserves are created: reinvestment/expansion needs · a potential future liability (e.g. Debenture Redemption Reserve) · statutory requirements (banks and insurance companies must maintain a statutory reserve) · smoothing future dividends when profits are volatile.

    13.5 Preference share capital guaranteed MCQ on the four variations

    Definition: equity with a special right / preference / priority over COMMON shareholders — preference shareholders receive dividends and repayment before common shareholders. Not standardised — depends on the contract.

    Variation Meaning
    Cumulative vs non-cumulative If a loss year means no dividend, cumulative shares get made up in a later year; non-cumulative do not
    Participating vs non-participating Participating shares also share in retained earnings beyond their dividend, and share in the balance at liquidation
    Redeemable vs irredeemable Whether/when the principal must be repaid. In India only REDEEMABLE preference shares are allowed likely MCQ
    Convertible vs non-convertible Whether they convert into common equity at the end of the period

    1. The cash flow statement guaranteed MCQ
    4

    14.1 Why profit ≠ cash

    P Ltd: sales ₹10 lakh, expenses ₹7 lakh ⇒ profit ₹3 lakh in every scenario. All expenses paid in cash.

    Scenario Credit sales Cash in Cash out Net operating cash
    1 ₹3 lakh ₹7 lakh ₹7 lakh 0
    2 ₹5 lakh ₹5 lakh ₹7 lakh −₹2 lakh
    3 ₹10 lakh (all) ₹0 ₹7 lakh −₹7 lakh

    Same profit, three completely different cash positions.

    Why they differ likely list MCQ 1. Non-cash expenses — e.g. depreciation (cash left the business when the asset was purchased, not when it is depreciated) 2. Accrual-based transactions — credit sales and credit expenses 3. Items not in the income statement at all but affecting cash — fixed asset purchases, debt principal taken/repaid

    14.2 Breaking down the three words

    Word Meaning
    CASH Cash and cash equivalents — physical cash, bank balances, time deposits, liquid assets quickly convertible to cash. Anything with no cash element is excluded
    FLOW Movement. Positive number = cash came IN; negative number = cash went OUT
    STATEMENT A prescribed format with three subheadings

    14.3 The three activities guaranteed MCQ| Activity | Contents | Source of numbers |

    |---|---|---| | 1. Operating activities | Cash generated from the core business operations + working capital changes | P&L account + working-capital movements from the balance sheet | | 2. Investing activities | Cash events in investments — primarily long-term: fixed assets and other long-term investments, bought and sold, plus associated income | Balance sheet | | 3. Financing activities | Money in/out from EQUITY and DEBT | Balance sheet |

    The statement also shows the opening and closing cash balances.

    14.4 The two preparation methods

    Method Starting point
    Direct Actual cash receipts and payments
    Indirect (used in the course) Begins with net profit / loss, then adjusts

    14.5 The indirect method — the three adjustments to PBT guaranteed MCQ

    Start with Profit Before Tax (PBT) from the income statement, then: 1. Remove items belonging to investing or financing activities that are sitting inside PBT, and move them to the correct activity. 2. Adjust for non-cash items included in PBT (e.g. depreciation). 3. Adjust for working capital changes — from the balance sheet. Core components: trade payables, trade receivables, inventory.

    (Illustrated with the cash flow statement of Reliance Industries Ltd.)

    MODULE 3 — FINANCIAL STATEMENT ANALYSIS & MANAGERIAL FINANCE

    1. Financial statement analysis
    1

    Definition: "the study of relationships among several financial facts and figures given in the financial statements, and the interpretation thereof to provide relevant information for decision makers." Source data is PUBLIC information — financial statements and stock market data.

    Why analyse — six reasons: assess profitability and operational efficiency · ascertain the relative importance of components · identify reasons for change in profitability or position · judge the ability to repay debt (for lenders) · assess short- and long-term liquidity · make industry comparisons / benchmarking.

    15.1 The three analysis types guaranteed MCQ| Type | Also called | Compares | Base |

    |---|---|---|---| | Horizontal analysis | Time series analysis | Year on year | Previous year | | Vertical analysis | Common size analysis | Within one year | P&L: NET SALES = 100%; Balance sheet: TOTAL ASSETS = 100% | | Ratio analysis | — | One number against another | — |

    15.2 Horizontal analysis — worked example

    $$\text{Growth \%} = \frac{\text{Current year} - \text{Previous year}}{\text{Previous year}} \times 100$$

    2025 2024 Growth
    Sales 14,35,000 11,20,000 28.13%
    Net profit 1,29,000 1,23,000 4.88%
    Total expenses (= sales − profit) 13,06,000 9,97,000 30.99% (~31%)

    The interpretation: sales up 28% but profit up only 5% — because expenses grew 31%, faster than sales.

    15.3 Vertical (common-size) analysis — worked example

    Sales = ₹15,00,000 = 100%

    Line item Amount % of sales
    Sales 15,00,000 100%
    Cost of material 3,00,000 20%
    Production cost 1,50,000 10%
    Operating profit 10,50,000 70%
    Employee salary 25%
    R&D expense 8%
    Sales & advertising 15%
    Interest cost 8%
    Earnings before tax 2,17,500 ~15%

    The insight: the two most significant costs are employee salary (25%) and cost of material (20%) — these are the accounts to focus on.

    1. Ratio analysis ← the highest-yield formula section in this course
    3

    How to read any ratio X/Y: "Assume Y to be 1. How much is X?"

    16.1 Solvency ratios (long-term stability)

    Ratio Formula Interpretation Direction
    Debt to Equity Total Debt ÷ Total Equity For ₹1 of equity, how much debt? Higher = more debt = higher risk
    Debt to Asset Total Debt ÷ Total Assets Of ₹1 of assets, how much is debt? Higher = higher long-term stability risk
    Interest Coverage Ratio EBIT ÷ Interest For ₹1 of interest payable, how much EBIT is generated? Indicates the buffer available if profits fall Higher = better/safer for the LENDER

    16.2 Liquidity ratios (short-term stability)

    Ratio Formula Interpretation
    Current Ratio Current Assets ÷ Current Liabilities For ₹1 of current liability, how much current asset? Current assets can meet current liabilities if the ratio is > 1
    Quick Ratio (Current Assets − Inventory) ÷ Current Liabilities Removes the least liquid current asset

    The liquidity ladder ← likely MCQ: Cash is the most liquid · Receivables are one step away (need collection) · Inventory is furthest away (must first be sold, then collected). Hence inventory is what gets stripped out in the quick ratio.

    16.3 Turnover ratios (asset utilisation efficiency)

    Meaning: how many times was the asset "turned" in the period? Higher = effective utilisation. Lower = idle capacity = inefficiency.

    Ratio Formula
    Total Asset Turnover Sales ÷ Total Assets
    Current Asset Turnover Sales ÷ Current Assets
    Receivable Turnover Sales ÷ Receivables
    Inventory Turnover Sales ÷ Inventory

    Interpretation: a turnover of 5 means ₹1 invested in the asset generates ₹5 of sales.

    16.4 Profitability ratios (all expressed as percentages)

    Ratio Formula Meaning
    Operating / Gross Profit Margin Operating Profit ÷ Sales × 100 Profitability at the operating level — think of it as unit economics
    Net Profit Margin (a.k.a. Return on Sales) EAT (Net income) ÷ Sales × 100 Final profitability after all expenses
    Return on Assets (ROA) Net income ÷ Total Assets × 100 For ₹100 of assets, how much net income?
    Return on Equity (ROE) Net income ÷ Total Equity × 100 (Total equity = share capital + reserves & surplus) For ₹100 invested by shareholders, how much net income?

    16.5 DuPont analysis guaranteed MCQ

    $$\text{ROE} = \frac{\text{Net Income}}{\text{Total Equity}} = \underbrace{\frac{\text{Net Income}}{\text{Sales}}}{\textbf{1. Net Profit Margin}} \times \underbrace{\frac{\text{Sales}}{\text{Total Assets}}}{\textbf{2. Total Asset Turnover}} \times \underbrace{\frac{\text{Total Assets}}{\text{Equity}}}_{\textbf{3. Leverage}}$$

    The three-stage DuPont says shareholder return comes from three sources: (1) profitability, (2) asset utilisation, (3) effective leverage.

    16.6 Working capital ratios guaranteed MCQ on the "days" formulas

    $$\text{Working Capital} = \text{Current Assets} - \text{Current Liabilities}$$

    Working capital is the investment needed to run day-to-day operations. It CAN be negative (when current liabilities exceed current assets) — and for some businesses that is simply how they operate.

    Ratio Formula What it tells you
    Days Sales Outstanding (DSO) Accounts Receivable ÷ (Sales ÷ 365) The average collection period — for how many days does the firm give credit to customers?
    Days Inventory Outstanding (DIO) Inventory ÷ (COGS ÷ 365) How many days' worth of inventory is held?
    Days Payable Outstanding (DPO) Accounts Payable ÷ (COGS ÷ 365) Average credit the firm receives from suppliers
    Cash Conversion Cycle (CCC) DIO + DSO − DPO The cash-to-cash cycle — in how many days does ₹1 invested in working capital come back?

    Worked examples: - DSO: sales ₹730 cr/year ⇒ ₹2 cr/day; receivables ₹50 cr ⇒ 50 ÷ 2 = 25 days - DIO: COGS ₹365 cr/year ⇒ ₹1 cr/day; inventory ₹20 cr ⇒ 20 days - DPO: COGS ₹365 cr ⇒ ₹1 cr/day; payables ₹15 cr ⇒ 15 days - CCC = 25 + 20 − 15 = 30 days

    Why DPO is SUBTRACTED: "the credit given by the supplier reduces my investment need in working capital."

    Indian auto-sector comparison (Maruti Suzuki, M&M, Tata Motors, Bajaj Auto, Hyundai; data from screener.in) likely MCQ - Highest inventory days: M&M (77 days) and Tata Motors (63 days) - Very low receivable days across the board — customers pay in cash or via a bank loan, so the company gets paid quickly - Payables over two months — suppliers extend long credit - ⇒ All five have a NEGATIVE cash conversion cycle, i.e. negative working capital: they fund inventory and receivables entirely out of supplier credit

    Two assignments: (1) Financial Statement Detective Game — fill in blanked-out cells; the two rules are "the balance sheet must balance" and "hints are hidden in the footnotes". (2) ITC Limited — 10-year financial analysis. Ratio spreadsheet exercise uses Asian Paints Ltd (March 2014 – March 2025), data from screener.in.

    1. Banking business model guaranteed MCQ
    2

    A bank is a FINANCIAL INTERMEDIARY — it accepts deposits on one side and lends on the other. It earns through the spread — the difference between borrowing and lending rates.

    17.1 Net Interest Margin (NIM)

    $$\text{NIM} = \frac{\text{Interest earned} - \text{Interest paid}}{\text{Average earning assets (loan book)}}$$

    Simple illustration: lend at 10%, take deposits at 8% ⇒ NIM = 2% — the bank's core profit.

    Three drivers of NIM: lending rates (macro factors + borrower quality) · deposit costs (influenced by CASA) · credit quality (NPAs reduce earning assets).

    The risk-quality question ← guaranteed MCQ: NIM can rise two ways — (a) interest earned ↑ or (b) interest paid ↓. Route (b), via a higher CASA ratio, is the safe route. Route (a) is risky because higher interest earned often means lending to riskier borrowers. "A higher NIM through the first route may not actually create value for the bank in the longer run."

    17.2 CASA

    CASA = Current Account + Savings Account deposits. These are the bank's low-cost source of funding (zero or very low interest paid). A higher CASA ratio ⇒ lower cost of funds ⇒ better NIM. Banks attract "sticky" CASA via digital banking and salary accounts.

    $$\text{CASA Ratio} = \frac{\text{Current Account} + \text{Savings Account balances}}{\text{Total Deposits}}$$

    17.3 Non-Performing Assets (NPAs)

    Definition ← guaranteed MCQ: an asset that has not generated an interest return for over 90 DAYS.

    Double hit: (a) income — the bank must stop accruing interest on it; (b) expenses — the bank must create provisions for losses (conservatism). It also consumes equity buffers, reducing lending capacity.

    Ratio Formula
    Gross NPA % Non-performing loans ÷ total loans — reflects overall asset quality
    Net NPA % (Non-performing loans − provisions already made) ÷ total loans — shows the open exposure / actual loss risk

    (Lower is better for both.)

    17.4 Capital Adequacy Ratio (CAR) guaranteed MCQ

    $$\text{CAR} = \frac{\text{Tier-1 Capital} + \text{Tier-2 Capital}}{\text{Risk Weighted Assets}}$$

    Tier Contents
    Tier-1 (core capital) Equity and retained earnings
    Tier-2 (supplementary capital) Preference shares and certain other eligible investments
    • Required by RBI under Basel-III norms; based on the Basel norms framework
    • Current minimum CAR = 11.5% of risk-weighted assets ← memorise
    • Effects: reduces lending ability (money must sit as reserve rather than be lent), but provides resilience to shocks and protects small depositors

    17.5 A bank's financial statements

    P&L: Interest income − Interest paid = Net interest margin → + other income & fees (locker fees, ATM charges, forex fees) → − other expenses (salaries, admin, advertising) → − provisions for NPAs → Net income

    Balance sheet:

    Liabilities Assets
    Equity + retained earnings (Tier-1) Loans and advances (the most important asset)
    CASA deposits Cash and reserves with RBI (regulatory reserves)
    Other borrowings Other investments

    The mirror-image trap ← guaranteed MCQ: a company's bank balance or fixed deposit is an ASSET in the company's books but a LIABILITY in the bank's balance sheet.

    17.6 Bank comparison (screener.in data) — HDFC, ICICI (private) vs SBI, PNB (public)

    Observation Finding
    NIM Private banks generally higher than public banks — public banks carry priority-sector lending and other mandates that reduce interest-earning capacity. ICICI had the highest NIM
    Gross NPA PNB at 4.09% — highest (poor asset quality). HDFC had the lowest gross NPA = best asset quality
    CASA ICICI best among private banks; SBI comparable among public banks — "SBI trades on trust"
    CAR All above the 11.5% minimum; HDFC highest

    The closing summary: "Banking is a business of balancing — yields (interest charged) against cost (partly from low-cost CASA), against risk (NPAs), against compliance (CAR)."

    1. Insurance business model guaranteed MCQ
    3

    Insurance is a RISK TRANSFER business. The customer pays a premium to transfer future financial risk. The insurer pools risks across thousands of customers and uses statistics to manage them. If actual claims < expected claims, the insurer profits.

    18.1 The income side

    Component Definition
    Underwriting profit Premium collected − claims paid — think of it as the operating profit of an insurance business
    Investment income Returns on bonds, equities and other investments

    Two premium types: Renewal premium (from existing customers — retention) and New business premium (growth/new customers).

    18.2 The FLOAT guaranteed MCQ

    Definition: the time lag between collecting premiums and settling claims. During this interim the insurer can invest the collected premium and earn returns.

    "If an insurer underwrites profitably and manages this float well, it can generate high long-term returns." Example given: Warren Buffett built Berkshire Hathaway by owning and effectively managing the float of multiple insurance businesses.

    18.3 Expenses and reserves

    Expenses: claims paid + operating costs (salaries, fees, commission to agents).

    Reserves / actuarial liabilities — provisions created for: 1. IBNR — claims Incurred But Not Reported as at the balance sheet date (conservatism) 2. Future liabilities, especially in life and health insurance

    Actuaries estimate these using advanced statistical models and discounting techniques. These reserves appear as liabilities and must be funded by high-quality liquid assets.

    Solvency: insurers must maintain a solvency ratio prescribed by the regulator to prove the ability to pay claims.

    18.4 An insurer's financial statements

    P&L: Premium earned − claims paid/settlements − commission and other operating expenses + investment/other income = Net profit

    Balance sheet: Assets = investments made using the float, also called AUM (Assets Under Management). Liabilities = reserves for future claims + equity (the regulator-required capital buffer).

    18.5 The three principles of insurance likely list MCQ

    1. Pricing risk well — how much premium to charge
    2. Managing float effectively — invest well but conservatively
    3. Ability to meet long-term obligations — ensuring trust and sustainability

    "Insurance is a long-term, statistical, capital-intensive business, a central pillar to financial stability."

    1. Management accounting
    8

    Definition: a process that measures, analyses and reports financial AND non-financial information to help managers make decisions to fulfil the organisation's goal.

    Financial accounting = for EXTERNAL parties. Management accounting = for INTERNAL decision making. guaranteed MCQ

    19.1 Cost behaviour guaranteed MCQ| Cost type | In TOTAL | PER UNIT |

    |---|---|---| | Variable cost | Changes in proportion to activity | CONSTANT | | Fixed cost | CONSTANT over the relevant range | Reduces as activity increases (up to 100% capacity) |

    Pen example: plastic costs ₹2 per pen (per-unit constant). Total variable cost: 1 pen ⇒ ₹2; 10 pens ⇒ ₹20; 2 lakh pens ⇒ ₹4 lakh.

    The falling fixed cost per unit as output rises is what we call ECONOMIES OF SCALE.

    19.2 Mixed costs likely MCQ| Type | Behaviour | Example |

    |---|---|---| | Step cost | Fixed over a range, then jumps to a higher level once activity exceeds a threshold | One supervisor handles 10 machines. Machines 1–10 ⇒ 1 supervisor; the 11th machine ⇒ a 2nd supervisor; the 21st ⇒ a 3rd | | Semi-variable cost | Has both a fixed and a variable component | Salesman on a fixed ₹25,000/month salary + 10% commission on sales |

    19.3 Operating leverage guaranteed MCQ

    Leverage indicates FIXED COST.

    Type Source
    Financial leverage Use of debt (financial fixed costs)
    Operating leverage Effect that operating FIXED costs have on changes in profit — interest/financial fixed costs are excluded

    High fixed cost + low variable cost ⇒ HIGH operating leverage ⇒ a small change in sales causes a LARGER change in profit.

    19.4 Cost allocation

    Direct/identifiable costs are assigned straight to the cost object. Indirect/common costs must be allocated.

    Three reasons to allocate ← likely list MCQ: (1) it imparts discipline (needed for department-level profitability) · (2) effective decision making based on total cost · (3) regulatory requirements — cost accounting and taxation rules.

    Method Basis Assessment
    Traditional cost allocation A blanket rate — units produced, labour hours, or machine hours; assumes all overhead results from a SINGLE factor Easy but can lead to inaccuracies
    Activity-Based Costing (ABC) Identifies activities and assigns each activity's cost to products/services according to actual consumption More accurate but MORE DATA-INTENSIVE

    The three ABC definitions required likely MCQ 1. Cost object — what are we finding the cost for? (a product, a department) 2. Activity — an event that incurs cost 3. Activity driver — captures both the intensity AND the frequency of demand for the activity

    19.5 CVP analysis and break-even guaranteed MCQ

    CVP (Cost-Volume-Profit) analysis examines the behaviour of total revenues, total costs and operating income as changes occur in units sold, selling price, variable cost per unit, or fixed costs.

    The CVP flow: $$\text{Sales} - \text{Variable Cost} = \textbf{Contribution} \;\rightarrow\; \text{Contribution} - \text{Fixed Cost} = \textbf{Operating Income}$$

    $$\text{Contribution margin per unit} = \text{Selling price per unit} - \text{Variable cost per unit}$$

    Contribution margin tells you how much each unit sold contributes to fixed costs and operating income.

    Sales, variable cost and contribution can be computed both in total AND per unit. FIXED COSTS ARE ALWAYS AT TOTAL LEVEL. likely MCQ Break-even point: the level of sales where total revenue = total costs, so operating income = zero — the point of no profit, no loss.

    $$\boxed{\text{Break-even point (units)} = \frac{\text{Total Fixed Cost}}{\text{Contribution per unit}}}$$

    CVP uses: profit planning · effective decision making · sensitivity analysis.

    (Case: WorkSmart Limited, a small stationery manufacturer making gel pens and erasers from a single factory, selling to schools, offices and retail stores.)

    19.6 Budgeting

    Definition: a quantitative expression of a proposed plan of action by management for a future period.

    Budgeting is a tool for BOTH planning AND control. likely MCQ Four budget types:

    Type Examples
    Operating budgets Sales budget, production budget
    Financial budgets Cash budget
    Flexible budgets Vary for different planned levels of activity
    Master budget Combines all budgets of the organisation into one

    19.7 Cost-benefit analysis

    A decision process comparing the incremental benefit of an action to its incremental cost. Relevant costs = expected FUTURE costs that DIFFER among alternative courses of action.

    Decision rule: act only if incremental benefit > incremental cost.

    MODULE 4 — CORPORATE FINANCE

    1. Introduction to corporate finance
    3

    Definition: the study of how companies invest in real assets, how they raise money to pay for those investments, and how they return money to investors. The decision maker is the firm; managers act as agents on its behalf.

    20.1 The four key decisions guaranteed MCQ| # | Decision | Question | Also called |

    |---|---|---|---| | 1 | Investment decision | How to allocate capital to real assets (plant, projects)? | Capital budgeting | | 2 | Financing decision | How to raise capital? (debt, equity, internal funds) | Capital structure decision | | 3 | Payout decision | How to return capital to investors? (dividends, share buybacks) | Dividend policy | | 4 | Working capital policy | How to manage operating investment needs? | — |

    20.2 The objective function

    $$\textbf{Maximise SHAREHOLDER VALUE / the value of the firm}$$

    Why shareholders? Because they have the residual claim — maximising their value implies all other obligations have already been met.

    The focus is on LONG-TERM shareholder value, not short-term. likely MCQ

    20.3 The four corporate finance problems guaranteed MCQ| Problem | Definition | Example |

    |---|---|---| | Agency problem | Managers (agents) hired on behalf of shareholders (principals) may pursue their own interest instead of maximising shareholder wealth | The CEO buying a private jet or luxury office space | | Incomplete contracting | A contract that cannot cover every future state, outcome or action due to complexity, uncertainty and limits on information | — | | Information asymmetry | One party has more or better information than the other ⇒ mispricing, mistrust, misallocation | The second-hand car market — sellers know quality, buyers can't verify, so buyers assume all cars are bad and offer very low prices | | Market inefficiency | Prices deviate from intrinsic value. (A market is efficient if prices reflect all available information) | Causes: irrational behaviour (bubbles and crashes) · limited arbitrage · trading/transaction costs · information delay |

    1. Time value of money guaranteed MCQ
    4

    $$\textbf{₹1 today is worth MORE than ₹1 in the future.}$$

    21.1 The four reasons ← memorise all four

    # Reason Explanation
    1 Impatience We want to consume now; making someone wait has a cost
    2 Opportunity cost Money today can be invested and has earning potential; waiting loses that investment window
    3 Uncertainty / risk What if the payer fails? "Money tomorrow always has a component of risk."
    4 Inflation ₹1 lakh today may buy a bike; a year from now prices may have risen

    21.2 The two formulas guaranteed MCQ| Direction | Name | Formula | Operation |

    |---|---|---|---| | Present → Future | COMPOUNDING | $FV = PV \times (1+r)^n$ | Multiply | | Future → Present | DISCOUNTING | $PV = \dfrac{FV}{(1+r)^n}$ | Divide |

    r = the rate of return / required rate of return / interest rate / hurdle rate — "how much extra you want to part with your money." The riskier the transaction, the higher your r.

    21.3 Worked examples ← memorise the numbers

    Example PV r n Answer
    Friend borrows ₹10,000 for 1 year @10% 10,000 10% 1 FV = ₹11,000
    Same loan for 10 years 10,000 10% 10 FV = ₹25,937 (interest on interest)
    Friend can only repay ₹10,000 in 1 year, you still want 10% — 10% 1 Lend only PV = ₹9,090.91
    ₹1 lakh invested for 2 years @10% 1,00,000 10% 2 FV = ₹1,21,000
    ₹1 lakh invested for 5 years @10% 1,00,000 10% 5 FV = ₹1,61,051

    The gold vs FD comparison nice MCQ material Gold at ₹9,000/gram today, expected ₹9,700/gram in a year.

    Alternative FD rate FD value in 1 year Decision
    7% 9,000 × 1.07 = ₹9,630 ₹9,700 > ₹9,630 ⇒ KEEP THE GOLD
    10% 9,000 × 1.10 = ₹9,900 ₹9,900 > ₹9,700 ⇒ SELL THE GOLD and invest

    21.4 Perpetuity guaranteed MCQ

    Perpetuity = forever. The standard formula breaks down because n = ∞, so: $$\boxed{PV = \frac{\text{Payment}}{r}}$$

    Assets giving perpetual streams: perpetual bonds (principal never repaid, adjusted entirely through interest till infinity) and irredeemable preference shares with a fixed dividend.

    Worked example: a preference share paying ₹15,000 fixed forever, required return 12% ⇒ PV = 15,000 ÷ 0.12 = ₹1,25,000.

    1. Investment decisions (capital budgeting)
    1

    Why they matter: resources are scarce; optimal utilisation maximises shareholder wealth. Investment decisions are long-horizon, irreversible, complex, and large in amount.

    The decision rule:

    Invest in assets that earn a return GREATER than the minimum acceptable HURDLE RATE.

    Hurdle rate = the minimum required rate of return. It should reflect the riskiness of the investment and incorporate the cost of capital used to finance it. It may be the same across projects or differ by project risk. Denoted r.

    22.1 The four information requirements likely list MCQ

    1. Initial investment — including fixed asset investment and working capital investment
    2. Life of the project (n, in years)
    3. Subsequent cash flows — both inflows and outflows
    4. Hurdle rate (r)

    Plus a timeline — investment at t = 0, final cash flow at the nth period.

    Critical point ← guaranteed MCQ: capital budgeting uses CASH FLOWS, not profits.

    22.2 The three evaluation techniques

    Technique Time-adjusted?
    Payback period NO — traditional
    Net Present Value (NPV) YES
    Internal Rate of Return (IRR) YES

    1. Payback period
    1

    Definition: the time required to recover the initial investment. Decision rule: select the project with the quickest payback period.

    Advantages Disadvantages guaranteed MCQ
    Easy to calculate, intuitive 1. Ignores the time value of money — ₹1 today and ₹1 in 2 years are treated as equal
    2. Ignores total cash flow — cash flows after the payback point don't affect the analysis

    Computation with unequal cash flows — build cumulative cash flows and find where they cross the initial investment.

    Worked example (ABC Ltd): investment ₹4,50,000; cash flows 1,00,000 / 1,10,000 / 1,40,000 / 1,50,000 / 2,50,000 (total inflows ₹7,50,000).

    Year Cash flow Cumulative
    1 1,00,000 1,00,000
    2 1,10,000 2,10,000
    3 1,40,000 3,50,000
    4 1,50,000 5,00,000
    5 2,50,000 7,50,000

    Investment of ₹4,50,000 is recovered between year 3 and year 4.

    1. Net Present Value (NPV) guaranteed MCQ
    1

    $$\text{NPV} = \text{PV of all cash INFLOWS} - \text{PV of all cash OUTFLOWS}$$

    It considers the total cash flows AND their timing.

    24.1 The two decision rules

    1. NPV > 0 ⇒ ACCEPT the project (it increases firm value). NPV < 0 ⇒ REJECT.
    2. Choosing between projects: pick the HIGHEST NPV — it makes the maximum increase to shareholder wealth.

    24.2 The discount factor

    $$PV = FV \times \underbrace{\left[\frac{1}{(1+r)^n}\right]}_{\textbf{discount factor}}$$

    At n = 0 the discount factor is 1 — "₹1 today is ₹1 today."

    24.3 Worked example ← memorise the answer

    Project A: investment ₹1,00,000 at t=0; cash flows ₹55,000 / ₹15,000 / ₹80,000 in years 1–3; cost of capital r = 10%.

    $$\text{NPV} = -1{,}00{,}000 + \frac{55{,}000}{1.1} + \frac{15{,}000}{1.1^2} + \frac{80{,}000}{1.1^3} = \boxed{\textbf{₹22,501.88}}$$

    NPV > 0 ⇒ ACCEPT.

    1. Internal Rate of Return (IRR) guaranteed MCQ
    1

    Definition: the discount rate that equates the present value of cash inflows with the present value of cash outflows — equivalently, the rate at which the project's NPV becomes ZERO. It is the project's actual rate of return.

    Decision rule: compare the IRR with the hurdle rate. - IRR ≥ hurdle rate ⇒ ACCEPT - IRR < hurdle rate ⇒ REJECT

    (In the ABC Ltd example the three techniques are applied together: payback, NPV at r = 11%, and IRR.)

    1. Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) valuation guaranteed MCQ
    2

    Definition: a valuation method estimating the value of an investment using its expected future cash flows. Value = the sum of all discounted future cash flows.

    What a DCF values: the full business = ENTERPRISE VALUE, which includes equity value + net debt. likely MCQ

    26.1 The four steps

    Step Content
    1. Explicit forecast period A full projection of cash flows for 5–10 years, built from financial history, industry analysis and assumptions
    2. Terminal value Covers year 6 (or 11) to infinity — uses simple assumptions and the PERPETUITY formula, generally with a small fixed growth rate
    3. Required rate of return (r) A measure of risk / expected return from the investor's perspective
    4. Final enterprise value Combines steps 1–3

    Why a terminal value is needed at all: the going concern assumption means businesses continue to infinity; the explicit forecast can't run that long.

    1. Return
    1

    27.1 Return for one year

    $$R = \frac{P_1 - P_0}{P_0} \qquad\qquad \text{with dividend: } R = \frac{(P_1 - P_0) + D}{P_0}$$

    (P₁ − P₀ is capital appreciation; D is the cash dividend received.)

    27.2 Multiple years — average return

    Use the simple average of yearly returns. "One year's return may have deviations; average returns are a more accurate proxy for the return-generation capacity of the firm."

    Average return is also used as a proxy for EXPECTED FUTURE return — assuming history repeats itself.

    Worked example: Stock A, 2019–2024 (6 years) ⇒ average return = 6.6%.

    27.3 CAGR (Compound Annual Growth Rate) guaranteed MCQ

    Also called the Holding Period Return — the annualised return over the whole holding window (not year-by-year buying and selling).

    The Sensex example ← memorise:

    Item Value
    Start (1986) 561.01
    End (2025) 82,516
    Number of periods 39 years
    CAGR 13.7% per annum

    Excel: RRI(nper, pv, fv) — nper = 39, pv = 561, fv = 82,516. (Data source: Google Finance.)

    1. Risk
    2

    Risk = the probability of something bad happening. In finance the most commonly used measure of risk is VOLATILITY, measured by STANDARD DEVIATION.

    Higher volatility = higher risk. "Finance prefers stability — stable returns are easier to predict and forecast."

    Excel: STDEV(...) over the return series.

    Worked example: Stock A — mean return 6.6%, standard deviation 18.9%.

    "The standard deviation is nearly three times the return of the stock — this is a very volatile stock."

    28.1 The risk–return relationship guaranteed MCQ

    $$\textbf{Risk and return are POSITIVELY associated. Higher risk } \Rightarrow \textbf{ higher EXPECTED return.}$$ (Return compensates for risk taken. Risk on the X-axis, return on the Y-axis, upward-sloping.)

    The two objective functions used in portfolio and wealth management: 1. Maximise return for a GIVEN level of risk 2. Minimise risk for a GIVEN level of return

    28.2 The Indian risk ladder likely MCQ (order matters)| Investment | Indicative return | Risk |

    |---|---|---| | Treasury bonds (lending to the Government of India) | 7% | Lowest | | SBI fixed deposits | ~7.5% | One more layer — SBI is a company that could fail | | NBFC fixed deposits | 9–10% | Riskier than SBI | | Index funds (Sensex) | 13.7% | Stock-market volatility | | Large-cap stocks (say 3 stocks) | Higher | More risk than the Sensex (which holds 30 stocks — less diversified) | | Small-cap stocks | Higher | Smaller companies, higher risk | | Startups | Highest | Most risky |

    The caveat stated: "Expectation need not match the real returns. Theory says risk should proxy expected returns; in reality returns can differ, because there is always risk involved."

    1. The financing decision (capital structure) guaranteed MCQ
    5

    Definition: choosing the optimal mix of debt and equity. Objective: MINIMISE the cost of capital, which maximises firm/shareholder value.

    Why equity has a higher expected return than debt: for equity, neither returns nor repayment is fixed; what is not fixed is volatile; what is volatile is more risky ⇒ investors demand a higher return.

    29.1 Modigliani & Miller, 1958 guaranteed MCQ

    The claim: the debt–equity mix is IRRELEVANT to firm value. "The value of the firm comes from the QUALITY OF ASSETS, not the mix of capital."

    But it rests on "perfect capital markets" assumptions likely list MCQ 1. No tax 2. No financial distress cost 3. No transaction costs 4. No information asymmetry 5. All investors have equal access to borrowing/lending 6. Leverage does not affect the quality of returns 7. No arbitrage

    If these fail — which is generally the case — capital structure BECOMES relevant.

    29.2 Trade-off theory guaranteed MCQ| Benefits of debt | Cost of debt |

    |---|---| | Cheaper source of finance ⇒ lower cost of capital | Financial distress cost — if the firm cannot make the fixed payment, there is default, and default is costly | | Tax benefit — interest is tax deductible | |

    The optimal capital structure: as debt rises, the weighted average cost of capital (WACC) initially FALLS. Beyond a point, more debt raises the risk perception of BOTH lenders AND equity holders, so both debt and equity become more costly and WACC rises again.

    The optimal mix is the point where the WEIGHTED AVERAGE COST OF CAPITAL IS MINIMISED. This point differs for each firm.

    29.3 Pecking order theory guaranteed MCQ (order matters)

    Three sources of capital: debt issue · equity issue · retained earnings. (Retained earnings differ from a fresh equity issue because the money is already with the company — no need to go to the market.)

    $$\boxed{\textbf{1. Retained earnings} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{2. Debt} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{3. Equity}}$$

    Why equity is last: it is the costliest source of finance AND it causes dilution of control.

    1. Working capital management
    1

    The cricket-bat business illustration: ₹10 crore carving machine is the fixed asset — but the cycle is cash → import English willow (pay supplier in advance) → production/labour costs → finished goods in inventory → advertising & marketing to sell → credit sale → collection → cash again. "For me to get back even one rupee of cash, I have to incur all these other expenses along with fixed asset needs."

    $$\text{Working Capital} = \text{Current Assets} - \text{Current Liabilities}$$

    Why current liabilities are subtracted: they represent credit received from other parties, which REDUCES the investment need.

    30.1 The four components and their trade-offs guaranteed MCQ| Component | Benefit of holding more | Cost of holding more |

    |---|---|---| | Cash & bank balance | Flexibility — transaction, precautionary and speculative needs | Cash is not a return-generating asset — high balances reduce returns | | Inventory | Encourages/promotes higher sales; fewer stockouts | Money is stuck; storage and carrying costs. (Alternative: just-in-time inventory) | | Trade receivables | Higher sales — more credit ⇒ higher probability and quantum of purchase; happier customers | Credit risk (customer may not pay) and delay in cash collection | | Trade payables | Less of our own investment needed — supplier credit reduces the firm's investment need | Like a loan — financial distress if the firm cannot repay supplier balances |

    30.2 How firms finance working capital

    Credit from suppliers · lines of credit from banks · factoring · short-term secured and unsecured loans.

    "There are several financial products that specifically address working capital needs — this is also a very potent industry for the FinTech sector."

    1. Asset Liability Management (ALM) guaranteed MCQ
    2

    Definition: structuring the firm's assets and liabilities so that cash flows are ALIGNED in TIMING, AMOUNT and RISK.

    What is matched: $$\textbf{Short-term liabilities} \leftrightarrow \textbf{Short-term assets} \qquad \textbf{Long-term liabilities} \leftrightarrow \textbf{Long-term assets}$$

    31.1 The comparison example

    Both companies have identical liabilities: long-term 20, current 30.

    Company A Company B
    Short-term assets (receivables + inventory + cash) 50 20
    Current liabilities due within 12 months 30 30
    Verdict Well matched — expects to receive 50 within 12 months against 30 due Mismatched — only 20 coming in against 30 due ⇒ financial problems

    31.2 The 2018 Indian NBFC crisis guaranteed MCQ| Side | Structure |

    |---|---| | Liabilities (source of funds) | Short-term borrowings — commercial papers with 3–12 month maturity, short-term loans | | Assets (use of funds) | Long-term infrastructure loans of 10–20 years |

    Result: when short-term lenders demanded repayment after a year, the money was locked in long-term loans that could not be quickly liquidated ⇒ liquidity crisis, and the RBI had to step in and regulate the NBFC sector. (Especially infrastructure lenders.)

    1. The startup journey — entrepreneurial finance guaranteed MCQ on the dilution maths
    9

    Running case: SaveBuddy — a digital chit fund for family and friends with automated UPI payments, timely reminders, transparent saving circles, and links to investment options.

    32.1 The funding stages and the dilution table ← memorise this table

    Stage Event Founder Co-founder Angel VC Retail
    1 Founder alone 100% — — — —
    2 Co-founder joins (50-50) 50% 50% — — —
    3 Angel takes 10% (remaining 90 split 50-50) 45% 45% 10% — —
    4 VC takes 30% (remaining 70 split 45:45:10) 31.5% 31.5% 7% 30% —
    5 IPO: fresh issue of 20% (remaining 80 split pro rata) 25.2% ≈ 25% ~25% ~5.6% ~24% 20%

    32.2 Key terms

    Term Definition
    Founder An individual who creates a new business; bears the risks and enjoys the rewards; the primary equity shareholder
    Co-founder Co-owner — sharing of risks and rewards
    Vesting of shares Shares are earned through time and effort, not given at one point — linked to time or work-related milestones
    Bootstrapping Building a company solely on the entrepreneur's savings and initial sales; no external equity investor
    Dilution New shares issued ⇒ existing shareholders' OWNERSHIP PERCENTAGE decreases. ← The NUMBER of shares does not fall; the PERCENTAGE does. Good: raising finance, hopefully at a higher valuation. Bad: loss of control

    32.3 Why a startup can't use public markets or bank loans early likely MCQ

    • Public markets: access to retail investors is regulated — SEBI does not allow small companies to reach retail investors directly.
    • Bank loan: debt requires fixed interest and fixed repayment. A startup just beginning to make sales cannot service that. (The real question is whether a bank would even lend.) ⇒ The remaining option is PRIVATE EQUITY.

    32.4 Angel investors

    Wealthy individuals investing in early-stage startups. They understand the risks and help with money AND non-money aspects — advice and networking. Entrepreneurs prepare a business plan for them. The instrument offered is EQUITY. This is also usually where the startup registers as a private limited company.

    Valuation maths: ₹2 crore raised for 10% ⇒ 100% = ₹20 crore valuation.

    32.5 Pre-money vs post-money valuation guaranteed MCQ

    VC invests ₹60 crore for 30% ⇒ 100% = ₹200 crore.

    Term Definition Value
    Post-money valuation What the company is worth AFTER the money hits the bank ₹200 crore
    Pre-money valuation Post-money − investment. What the company is worth BEFORE the VC writes the cheque ₹200 − ₹60 = ₹140 crore

    "If you are raising funds, always check whether the valuation quoted is pre-money or post-money — your stake calculations change accordingly."

    32.6 The VC/PE fund structure guaranteed MCQ

    Three parties: 1. Limited Partners (LPs) — the investors (institutional/accredited) who pool money; limited liability 2. General Partner (GP) / sponsor — envisions the fund, raises money from LPs, has unlimited liability; includes the sponsor team and any advisory board 3. Portfolio firms / investees — the companies invested in

    The fund itself is a separate entity registered as a Limited Liability Partnership (LLP). It invests in multiple portfolio firms following the principle of diversification, then exits and returns money. Exit proceeds split between the GP (management fee + carry) and the LPs (capital return + gains).

    Supporting ecosystem: incubators (connecting firms to funds), lawyers, venture debt providers, accountants.

    32.7 The VC fund life cycle — 5 stages over ~10 years guaranteed MCQ| Stage | Activity | Timing |

    |---|---|---| | 1. Fundraising | GPs approach LPs and seek commitments | Year 0–1 | | 2. Sourcing and screening | Attend events, review pitch decks, shortlist companies | Years 1–2 | | 3. Investment and contracting | Due diligence, negotiation, term sheets; funds deployed | Up to year 4 | | 4. Value creation | Mentorship role — network and grow the portfolio companies | Middle years | | 5. Exit and return distribution | Exits via IPO, M&A, secondary sale, or write-off | Last two years |

    (Then Fund II begins.) Other pooled vehicles: mutual funds (retail investors, listed shares/debt) and hedge funds (accredited investors, riskier investments).

    32.8 Term sheet clauses guaranteed MCQ

    Co-founders usually hold common equity; subsequent investors (angels and VCs) often get PREFERENCE shares, which are not standardised.

    Clause What it does
    Anti-dilution Protects the investor from dilution — e.g. "I will not dilute below X%", or "I will dilute only in a down round", or "I will never dilute". The founder may bear all the dilution
    Liquidation preference Sets how money is distributed on liquidation, sale of the company, or entry of another investor
    Dividend provision Preference dividends may be cumulative or non-cumulative
    Protective provisions Veto rights over certain major company decisions
    Board composition The right to a seat on the board of directors
    Voting rights Common and preferred shares may carry different weights, for all or for specific major decisions
    Information rights Periodic financial and non-financial updates from the company

    32.9 Bank debt and the IPO
