    Taking a bank loan requires demonstrating REPAYMENT CAPACITY. The corporate finance questions then are: long-term or short-term debt? which currency? fixed or floating rate? secured or unsecured (do you have collateral)? bank vs venture debt vs private loan?

    Debt does NOT change the shareholding structure — no dilution, because debt carries no equity or control rights. likely MCQ IPO (Initial Public Offering) = new shares issued by the company to retail investors, requiring the stock exchange's listing requirements to be met.

    Why SEBI is strict: retail investors have access to public markets, so extra rules protect them.

    What retail investors want (three things) likely list MCQ 1. Safeguard the capital 2. Grow the capital — provide returns 3. Disclose information vital for decision making

    The two reasons to list likely MCQ 1. Access to capital markets — raise significantly more than in private markets 2. Liquidity for shares — investors can buy and sell without the company being directly involved

    1. The Indian FinTech landscape guaranteed MCQ
    4

    33.1 The six segments

    # Segment Key trends Key players named
    1 Banking Large banks investing heavily in tech; rise of neo banks (digital-only, no physical branches) and digital-first banks (prioritise digital over physical channels). Focus on underserved segments — SMEs, millennials, gig workers Jupiter, Fi, Niyo, Open
    2 Lending Use of alternative data (SMS, utility bills, social media, bank statements) for underwriting and credit scoring · Buy Now Pay Later · embedded lending at the point of transaction (EMI options on electronics, holidays, shopping sites) · co-lending between NBFCs and banks · P2P lending LendingKart, KreditBee, ZestMoney
    3 Payments UPI is the backbone · rise of cashless transactions · QR-based merchant acceptance, invoice tools, POS hardware · BNPL and credit on UPI as new monetisation channels PhonePe, Google Pay, Paytm, BharatPe, Razorpay
    4 RegTech (Regulatory Technology) Automates KYC, Anti-Money Laundering, fraud risk, regulatory reporting · enabled by Digital India and India Stack APIs for paperless onboarding via Aadhaar, DigiLocker, video KYC, e-sign · rising demand due to RBI tightening on data security and lending guidelines Signzy, HyperVerge, IDfy, Karza Technologies
    5 Wealth management Shift to an investment mindset · low-cost DIY investing in mutual funds, stocks, debt, ETFs, gold · democratisation via fractional investing, SIPs, zero-commission models · goal-based robo-advisory, algo trading, PMS for HNIs Zerodha, Groww, INDmoney, Kuvera, ScripBox
    6 Insurance Digital platforms simplify discovery, comparison and purchase · reduced turnaround time for policy creation and settlement · growth in micro-insurance, embedded insurance, usage-based plans · focus on claim assistance and health record digitisation for user stickiness Acko, Digit, Turtlemint, PolicyBazaar, Plum

    33.2 Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) — the six layers guaranteed MCQ| Layer | What it is | What it enables |

    |---|---|---| | Aadhaar | Biometric digital identity for over 1.3 billion people | KYC onboarding, authentication | | eKYC / e-Sign | Paperless identity verification and digital signature | Instant account opening, loan processing | | UPI | Real-time payment system | P2P transactions, subscription payments, cashless transactions | | DigiLocker | Cloud vault for official documents | Seamless document access for KYC and verification | | Account Aggregator | Consent-based financial data sharing platform | Sharing/collating banking, tax, insurance and investment data ⇒ loan processing, credit scoring, wealth management | | ONDC (Open Network for Digital Commerce) | Open marketplace decentralising e-commerce protocols | Digital selling, payment-FinTech integration, credit scoring |

    What drives India's FinTech growth: extensive digital public infrastructure + a favourable regulatory push + a massive underserved population ripe for financial inclusion.

    33.3 The regulator map guaranteed MCQ| Activity | Regulator | Examples |

    |---|---|---| | Banking | RBI | Public and private banks, banking innovation | | Insurance / InsurTech | IRDAI | LIC, SBI Insurance, Acko, PolicyBazaar | | Payments and wallets | RBI | PhonePe, Paytm, Razorpay | | Lending | RBI | KreditBee, Slice, P2P platforms | | Wealth management / investments | SEBI | Zerodha, Groww, PMS; alternative investments, private & public equity, debt, gold, commodities | | Pension | PFRDA | NPS platforms, pension schemes | | Crypto | NO sector-specific regulator — regulatory oversight still pending | CoinDCX, WazirX |

    RBI is the most important regulator — it covers roughly 70% or more of the core finance functions in India.

    33.4 The five functions of the RBI guaranteed MCQ

    1. Monetary authority — controls monetary policy, ensuring macroeconomic stability
    2. Issuer of currency — currency supply
    3. Regulator of the banking system
    4. Custodian of foreign exchange — FEMA 1999, all cross-border transactions
    5. Developmental role — promotion and growth of the finance sector through innovation

    "Regulation ensures trust for users of finance and stability for the financial system, and must balance compliance against innovation."

    QUICK-FIRE REVISION TABLE

    Prompt Answer
    Accounting defined in four verbs Recording · classifying · summarising · interpreting
    Accounting = The language of business — learn its vocabulary + grammar
    Two reasons accounts are created Accountability ("money with expectation") and information provision
    The three financial statements Balance sheet · P&L / income statement · cash flow statement
    The fourth mandatory statement Statement of Changes in Equity — mandatory under Ind AS 1
    Balance sheet: period or date? A DATE (a point statement) — usually 31 March in India
    IFRS is mandatory in Over 140 countries
    Ind AS mandatory in India from 2016
    Who sets Indian accounting standards ICAI
    MCA regulates Private companies, public companies, LLPs
    SEBI regulates Listed companies
    Rule 1 — business entity concept The business is a separate identity from its owners (Flipkart)
    Rule 2 — money measurement Only what can be measured in money; excludes brand, loyalty, management quality
    Rule 3 — materiality Material = economically significant; subjective. Exception: regulatory requirements
    Rule 4 — going concern Business continues in the foreseeable future. Oldest firm: a Japanese construction company 1,400+ years old
    Rule 5 — periodicity India 1 Apr–31 Mar; US calendar year
    Rule 6 — conservatism Don't anticipate income; provide for all possible losses (Tanishq gold)
    Rule 7 — consistency Policies followed consistently; disclosed in notes to accounts
    Rule 8 — matching Expenses recorded in the same period as the related revenue
    Rule 9 — accrual Record when the transaction occurs, regardless of cash
    The balance sheet equation Assets = Liabilities + Equity
    The unified accounting equation A = L + Opening Equity + Income − Expense − Dividend
    P&L ladder from EBITDA EBITDA − D&A = EBIT − Interest = EBT − Tax = PAT
    EBITDA expanded Earnings Before Interest, Tax, Depreciation, Amortisation
    Interest on a fixed deposit for a manufacturer vs a bank Other income vs operating revenue
    Three conditions for revenue recognition Performance done · measurable · probable economic benefit
    "Performance done" means Risks and rewards transferred — depends on who bears risk in transit
    L&T's 3-year factory contract Percentage-completion method
    Hotstar ₹1,200 paid 1 Jan, year ends 31 Mar ₹300 revenue, ₹900 DEFERRED
    Four sales transaction types Cash · credit (⇒ receivable, asset) · deferred revenue (⇒ liability) · unbilled revenue (⇒ asset)
    Gross vs net sales Gross includes GST/taxes; the P&L top line is NET sales
    Cost of material formula Opening + Purchases − Closing
    Direct vs indirect expense test Factory vs office
    Four expense transaction types Cash · accrued (liability) · prepaid (ASSET) · provision (liability)
    Prepaid expense is a… / deferred revenue is a… Current asset / current liability
    The five provisions named Bad debt · inventory loss · return · guarantee/warranty · taxes
    Asha's net worth Assets 70 − liabilities 22 = ₹48 lakh
    Four conditions for an asset Resource · control · future economic benefit · reliably measurable
    Historical cost = Purchase cost + directly attributable cost
    Depreciable amount = Historical cost − residual/scrap value
    Three depreciation methods Straight line · Written Down Value · Production unit
    WDV is used for Technology products (higher early usage)
    Production unit method is used in Mining
    Gross block / net block Historical cost / value remaining — this goes to the balance sheet
    Four inventory types Raw material · WIP · finished goods · stock in trade
    Undervalued closing inventory ⇒ COGS ↑, profit ↓, current assets ↓ (and it's a multi-year error)
    Inventory valuation rule LOWER of cost or NET REALISABLE VALUE
    NRV = Normal selling price − estimated completion and selling costs
    Excluded from inventory cost Period/admin costs, general R&D, abnormal items, financing/interest
    Periodic vs perpetual inventory system Physical count at intervals (small firms) vs continuous system (large firms)
    A financial instrument is A contract creating an asset for one entity and a liability for another
    A ₹500 note is An asset for the holder, a LIABILITY for the RBI
    Three motives for holding cash Transaction · precautionary · speculative
    Four stages of credit loss Not due → follow-up → provision for doubtful debts → bad debt written off
    Provision % example Sales ₹10L, 70% credit, 2% doubtful ⇒ ₹14,000
    Ageing analysis principle The older the outstanding, the higher the probability of bad debt
    Factoring: ₹7L, 5% charge Cash received ₹6,65,000; the factor bears the credit risk
    Subsidiary threshold / treatment >50% / consolidated financial statements
    Associate threshold / treatment 20%+ (significant influence) / equity method, NO consolidation
    Joint venture vs joint operation Separate entity, jointly controlled vs NO separate entity created
    Starbucks India JV of Tata Consumer Products + Starbucks
    Tech Mahindra Associate of M&M
    Consolidation is mandated by The Companies Act 2013
    Intangible asset definition Identifiable non-monetary asset without physical substance
    Five intangible categories Marketing · customer · artistic · contract-based · technology-based
    Intangibles as % of S&P 500 assets 17% in 1975 → 90% in 2020
    Why customer loyalty isn't an intangible asset No CONTROL — you can't sell it
    Why reputation isn't an intangible asset Not SEPARABLE from the business
    Depreciation of intangibles is called Amortisation
    Facebook–WhatsApp 2014 $22bn paid; user base $2bn, trade name $448m, technology $288m; goodwill $18.1bn
    Goodwill arises in M&A / purchase transactions — the unidentifiable balancing figure
    Can Apple capitalise its own brand? No (non-separable). But an acquirer paying for it CAN
    Always expensed, never capitalised R&D · advertising · employee training · CSR
    Forward vs future OTC, one-to-one, non-standardised vs exchange-traded, standardised
    Options include The element of choice; put and call; traded in the F&O market
    Compound instrument example Convertible debt — lower interest rate, repaid in equity shares
    Repayment priority ladder Pure debt → compound instruments → pure equity
    Four elements of a liability Present obligation · from a past event · unavoidable · settled by cash or goods/services
    Is an open purchase order a liability? No — the transaction hasn't occurred
    Is a planned donation a liability? No — voluntary
    Definite vs estimated liability Precisely determinable vs uncertain amount ⇒ provisions
    Secured vs unsecured liability Backed by a specific asset vs a general claim
    Operating vs financial liability From operations (salary payable, trade payable, tax payable, unearned revenue) vs from securing finance
    Provision vs contingent liability On the balance sheet vs notes to accounts only
    Debt vs equity — three parameters Fixed vs residual claim · first vs last priority · no control vs control
    Zero-coupon bond No interim interest — one fixed payment at the end
    Debt accounting rule Principal → balance sheet only. Interest → P&L only
    Authorised / issued / paid-up capital Max issuable (in the MoA) / offered / actually received — THIS goes on the balance sheet
    Who proposes and who approves dividends Board proposes; shareholders approve. Shareholders cannot ask for MORE
    Interim vs final dividend Declared during the year vs after accounts are closed
    Preference share variations Cumulative/non-cumulative · participating/non-participating · redeemable/irredeemable · convertible/non-convertible
    In India only which preference shares are allowed? Redeemable
    Why profit ≠ cash Non-cash expenses (depreciation) · accruals (credit sales/expenses) · items outside the P&L (fixed asset purchases, debt principal)
    Three cash flow activities Operating · Investing · Financing
    Indirect method starts from Net profit / PBT
    Three adjustments to PBT Remove investing/financing items · adjust non-cash items · adjust working capital changes
    Working capital components in the CF statement Trade payables, trade receivables, inventory
    Vertical analysis base P&L: net sales. Balance sheet: total assets
    Horizontal analysis = Time series — (current − previous) ÷ previous
    The 2025 example Sales +28.13%, profit +4.88%, expenses +30.99%
    Debt-to-equity / debt-to-asset Total debt ÷ equity / total debt ÷ total assets — higher = riskier
    Interest coverage ratio EBIT ÷ Interest — higher = safer for the lender
    Current ratio / quick ratio CA ÷ CL / (CA − inventory) ÷ CL
    Total asset turnover Sales ÷ total assets
    Operating margin / net margin Op. profit ÷ sales / PAT ÷ sales (= return on sales)
    ROA / ROE Net income ÷ total assets / net income ÷ total equity
    DuPont three stages Net profit margin × total asset turnover × leverage
    DSO / DIO / DPO AR ÷ (Sales/365) / Inventory ÷ (COGS/365) / AP ÷ (COGS/365)
    Cash conversion cycle DIO + DSO − DPO (worked example: 25 + 20 − 15 = 30 days)
    Indian auto sector CCC Negative — supplier credit funds inventory and receivables
    A bank is a Financial intermediary, earning through the spread
    NIM formula (Interest earned − interest paid) ÷ average earning assets
    Safe vs risky route to a higher NIM Lower interest paid (higher CASA) vs higher interest earned (riskier borrowers)
    CASA Current Account + Savings Account — low-cost funding; higher ratio ⇒ better NIM
    NPA definition An asset not generating interest for over 90 days
    Gross vs net NPA Total NPAs ÷ loans vs (NPAs − provisions) ÷ loans
    CAR formula and minimum (Tier-1 + Tier-2) ÷ Risk Weighted Assets; minimum 11.5% under Basel-III (RBI)
    Tier-1 vs Tier-2 capital Equity + retained earnings vs preference shares + other eligible investments
    Bank ratio comparison Highest NIM ICICI · highest gross NPA PNB (4.09%) · lowest NPA and highest CAR HDFC
    Insurance is a Risk transfer business — pool risks, use statistics
    Underwriting profit Premium collected − claims paid
    The float The lag between collecting premium and paying claims — invested to earn returns. Warren Buffett / Berkshire Hathaway
    IBNR Claims Incurred But Not Reported — a provision
    Insurer's AUM Investments made using the float
    Three insurance principles Price risk well · manage float effectively · meet long-term obligations
    Management vs financial accounting Internal decision making vs external reporting
    Variable cost Total varies; per unit CONSTANT
    Fixed cost Total CONSTANT; per unit falls with output ⇒ economies of scale
    Step cost example One supervisor per 10 machines
    Semi-variable cost example ₹25,000 fixed salary + 10% commission
    Operating leverage Effect of operating fixed costs on profit — high fixed cost ⇒ high operating leverage
    Traditional vs ABC costing Single blanket driver vs activities and actual consumption (more accurate, more data-intensive)
    Three ABC definitions needed Cost object · activity · activity driver (captures intensity AND frequency)
    Contribution margin per unit Selling price/unit − variable cost/unit
    Break-even point (units) Total fixed cost ÷ contribution per unit — zero operating income
    Fixed costs in CVP are always At total level (never per unit)
    Budget defined A quantitative expression of a proposed plan of action — a tool for planning AND control
    Four budget types Operating · financial (cash) · flexible · master
    Relevant costs are Expected FUTURE costs that DIFFER among alternatives
    Four corporate finance decisions Investment (capital budgeting) · financing (capital structure) · payout (dividend policy) · working capital
    Objective of corporate finance Maximise LONG-TERM shareholder value
    Four corporate finance problems Agency problem · incomplete contracting · information asymmetry · market inefficiency
    Information asymmetry example The second-hand car market
    Four reasons ₹1 today > ₹1 tomorrow Impatience · opportunity cost · uncertainty/risk · inflation
    Compounding / discounting FV = PV(1+r)ⁿ / PV = FV ÷ (1+r)ⁿ
    ₹10,000 @10% for 10 years ₹25,937
    ₹1 lakh @10% for 5 years ₹1,61,051
    Perpetuity formula PV = Payment ÷ r — e.g. 15,000 ÷ 0.12 = ₹1,25,000
    Perpetuity examples Perpetual bonds; irredeemable preference shares
    Capital budgeting rule Invest if return > hurdle rate
    Capital budgeting uses CASH FLOWS, not profits
    Payback period's two flaws Ignores the time value of money · ignores cash flows after payback
    NPV rule NPV > 0 ⇒ accept; between projects choose the highest NPV
    The NPV worked answer ₹22,501.88 (invest ₹1,00,000; 55/15/80k over 3 yrs at 10%)
    Discount factor at n = 0 1
    IRR The rate at which NPV = 0; accept if IRR ≥ hurdle rate
    A DCF values Enterprise value = equity value + net debt
    DCF's four steps Explicit forecast (5–10 yrs) · terminal value (perpetuity) · r · enterprise value
    Return with dividend (P₁ − P₀ + D) ÷ P₀
    CAGR is also called Holding period return; Excel RRI
    Sensex CAGR 561 (1986) → 82,516 (2025) over 39 years = 13.7% p.a.
    Risk is measured by Volatility = standard deviation
    Stock A example Mean 6.6%, SD 18.9% ⇒ very volatile
    Risk–return relationship Positively associated
    Two portfolio objectives Max return for given risk; min risk for given return
    Risk ladder T-bonds 7% → SBI FD 7.5% → NBFC FD 9–10% → index 13.7% → large cap → small cap → startups
    Modigliani & Miller 1958 Capital structure is IRRELEVANT — value comes from asset quality — under perfect capital markets
    MM's assumptions No tax · no distress cost · no transaction costs · no information asymmetry · equal access to borrowing · leverage doesn't affect return quality · no arbitrage
    Trade-off theory Debt's benefits (cheaper + tax-deductible interest) vs its cost (financial distress)
    Optimal capital structure Where WACC is MINIMISED
    Pecking order Retained earnings → debt → equity
    Why equity is last Costliest and causes dilution of control
    Working capital = Current assets − current liabilities
    Cash trade-off Flexibility vs cash is not a return-generating asset
    Trade payables trade-off Reduces our investment need vs financial distress if unable to repay
    ALM matches Short-term liabilities ↔ short-term assets; long-term ↔ long-term — in timing, amount and risk

    Course map Module 1 — Foundations: role of communication · the professional entrepreneur · the communication process · barriers · effectiveness · types of messages · metacommunication · the communication gestalt · characteristics of business communication (register, 7 Cs, structure/logic/flow, audience orientation) Module 2 — Written business communication: structure (inverted pyramid, OBC) · media release · other structural models (Minto, STAR, SBO) · direct vs indirect approaches · the writing process · Gen AI · email · letters · digital media Module 3 — Interpersonal & group: interpersonal communication · feedback · listening · questions · small group dynamics · managing meetings · formal meetings & minutes · the flat world Module 4 — Presentations: overcoming nervousness · the 4 Ds (design, develop, deliver, defend)

    Exam note: this course is built around named models and acronyms — LESCANT, 7 Cs, AIM, OBC, Johari, STAR, SBO, Minto, VED, KISS, WIIFT, the 4 Ds, the 3 Commandments, the 3 types of feedback, the 4 types of minutes. Those, plus the professor's specific counter-intuitive claims (there is no 7% rule; the sender is responsible for effectiveness; hearing ≠ listening), are the MCQ material.

    MODULE 1 — FOUNDATIONS

    1. Communication in the digital global economy

    The core claim: the startup ecosystem and its stakeholders (investors, customers, suppliers) have changed. Jugaad is necessary as an element of innovation but NOT sufficient beyond a point. What has replaced it is professionalism — and professionalism is what creates competitive advantage.

    Prof. James Hoadley ← likely attribution MCQ: "In the 21st century, the difference between success and failure will be the ability to understand and to communicate."

    Communication is a source of competitive advantage in an increasingly undifferentiated market — "it is a discriminator; it sets you apart."

    "Act local, think global" — companies cited: Amazon, OYO, Airbnb, TripAdvisor, Zomato, Airbus, and Sorocco (an AI company that launched simultaneously in Bangalore, Boston and London from day one).

    Entrepreneur.com quote (July 2023): "In the dynamic environment of a startup, communication extends far beyond the mere exchange of words… Effective communication is crucial and can be the determining factor in the trajectory of a startup."

    1. Communication and the professional entrepreneur
    1

    Why investors care: "Investors invest in people first and foremost, not in the idea or the business model." They evaluate how professional the founders are, because they are looking to minimise risk — will they be able to deliver on the promise?

    A professional is defined as ← likely MCQ: a practitioner who is not only knowledgeable, competent and confident, but also credible and trustworthy. It is both competencies AND behaviours, and must be consistently and continually demonstrated in thought, word and deed.

    Qualities of professionalism named: committed and dedicated · ethical · responsible and accountable · detail-oriented · people- and service-oriented.

    2.1 Professional communication — six requirements

    1. Appropriate language
    2. Professional and courteous tone of voice
    3. Sensitivity to customer needs, cultures and emotion — "emotion is okay; your ability to detect emotion and sentiment in others is very important"
    4. Well formatted and presented — "if you are submitting a proposal to raise $5 million, that proposal must LOOK worth $5 million or more"
    5. Respects privacy and boundaries
    6. Legally compliant

    2.2 Ethical communication — five requirements likely list MCQ

    1. Integrity — telling the truth, avoiding the grey areas
    2. Statutory and legal compliance
    3. Maintaining privacy, confidentiality and intellectual property rights
    4. Avoiding conflicts of interest
    5. Building relationships with competitors — "badmouthing the competition is NOT ethical communication" Plus: fair trade practice — what you say must be reasonable and in line with what is commonly accepted in the market.

    1. The communication process guaranteed MCQ
    2

    3.1 Etymology and definition

    "Communication" derives from the Latin communicare (from communa), meaning "common" or "shared."

    Definition: the activity by which people, organisations and all living creatures exchange meaning in the form of information or messages passed among themselves, usually for a purpose.

    Three key interpretations ← the professor stops on each: 1. Exchange of MEANING, not data or information — also expressed as "the creation of shared understanding." 2. It requires interpretation of meaning, which goes beyond understanding content. "We are not exchanging data or information; ultimately it's meaning and understanding." 3. In business the purpose is to INFLUENCE AND CHANGE BEHAVIOUR.

    3.2 The five-step process ← memorise the order and every term

    Step Name What happens
    1 Ideation The sender has an idea and defines a purpose
    2 Encoding / Synthesis Converting the idea into a message — making choices about content, medium, timing, delivery
    3 Transmission Conveying the encoded message over an appropriate medium/channel
    4 Decoding / Sense making The receiver captures the message and interprets it
    5 Response and Feedback The response is fed back through a closed-loop mechanism, completing the process

    Terminology to memorise: - Sender / initiator — has the idea, defines the purpose, encodes - Message — the unit of information, data, sounds or images transmitted - Medium / channel — the vehicle (newspaper, internet, radio) - Receiver — the recipient(s); decodes - Sense making — "the term for how people interpret and make meaning from messages they get" - Synthesis — "putting the message together from the idea, purpose and audience, combined with assumptions and decisions about the message"

    3.3 The ideation questions

    Whom are you talking to? · What does the receiver need to know? · What does the receiver already know? · What do you want the receiver to DO with this information? ⇒ Who is your audience? What is their current status? What is the desired outcome?

    3.4 Three critical statements ← all three are exam-worthy

    Statement Why
    "Language is NOT communication. Language is an ENCODING TOOL" — one of the parameters of synthesis "People proficient in a language need not be good communicators."
    "The medium is the message" — Marshall McLuhan The same ad in a newspaper, on a hoarding, and in a WhatsApp message has a different impact. Media characteristics dictate how a message is synthesised and interpreted
    The sender LOSES CONTROL the moment the message is transmitted The sender cannot manage or oversee the receiver's decoding — only anticipate it and encode to maximise decoding effectiveness

    3.5 Response vs feedback guaranteed MCQ (they are NOT the same)

    • There is ALWAYS a response — like it, dislike it, indifference, "couldn't care" are all responses. Responses may be actions, thoughts, words or gestures.
    • Feedback requires a CHANNEL. In one-way broadcast (old radio), there is a response but NO feedback because no mechanism exists to send it back.
    • When feedback happens, the receiver becomes the sender — a switch of roles, which may happen many times in one transaction.

    Why study the process? "Understanding of the process helps us to leverage it. If you don't understand a process, you can't be great at it."

    1. Barriers to communication guaranteed MCQ
    2

    Definition: anything that hinders the effective exchange of information. Analogy: roadblocks across a highway. They cause misunderstanding, conflict, and the receiver not behaving as the sender expected.

    The framing question: why does digital data communication (modems, servers, routers, handshakes, protocols, packets) work like a charm but human communication doesn't? "Human beings are not cyborgs. We are not standardised and we are not cybernetic entities."

    4.1 Where the process fails

    Location Causes
    Encoding Too much / too little / irrelevant content · insufficient context · poorly framed or constructed message · language issues · misjudging the audience's knowledge or cognitive level
    Medium Inappropriate medium ("do not break up by text") · wrong assumptions about reach · wrong timing
    Decoding Receiver's language proficiency · knowledge/cognitive capability · lack of shared context
    Feedback No feedback mechanism exists to close the loop

    4.2 The two classes of barrier guaranteed MCQ| | PHYSICAL barriers (tangible/concrete) | PSYCHOLOGICAL barriers (intangible/mental) |

    |---|---|---| | Examples | Noise — external sounds, radio interference, screeching, no line of sight, multitasking · Technological — network load, solar radiation, cable issues, poor signal · Physiological — hard of hearing, illness, age | Interest / motivation · too much OR too little information · bias and prejudice ("filters through which you interpret the message"; stereotyping) · emotional/mental health issues · culture · dialects and semantic differences | | Overcome? | Yes — overcomeable or minimisable (hearing aids, better technology, planning meetings) | The bigger and more impactful barriers |

    The one-line distinction planted here and used again in §14: "Hearing is PHYSICAL. Listening is PSYCHOLOGICAL."

    1. Cultural barriers, context, and time & place
    3

    5.1 Culture

    Definition(s): the things we do — beliefs customary in our society, social forms, traits, and the way we behave. Also: a set of shared attitudes, values, goals and practices that characterise an institution, an organisation, or a group of people.

    Culture is a LENS: "Everything a human being does is passed through the lens of his or her culture. There is no such construct as 'I have no culture.'" Different cultures ⇒ different interpretations — "there's nothing good or bad about it; they are just differences."

    Examples of multicultural exposure: TCS — over 600,000 professionals from 150 countries · IPL/IFL teams · the American Association of Physicians — over 80,000 Indian members. "The level of European football was raised when they brought people from other cultures into their teams."

    5.2 LESCANT — the seven parameters of culture guaranteed MCQ| Letter | Parameter |

    |---|---| | L | Language | | E | Environment | | S | Social organisation | | C | Context (the need for context) | | A | Authority (the concept of authority) | | N | Non-verbal behaviour | | T | Time |

    Illustrations given: - Non-verbal: Japanese bow, they don't shake hands — "the American's got his hand stuck out and the Japanese guy has bowed" ⇒ behavioural asymmetry. Eye contact is essential in some cultures, avoided in others. Hand signals fine in one culture may be offensive in another. - Time: In the US and especially Japan, time is sacrosanct — the story of a Japanese train leaving the platform 12 seconds early, prompting complaints and an apology. In other cultures being late is a sign of respect for the host — "Delhiites invited for dinner at 7:30 don't land up before 9:30."

    5.3 The PepsiCo Taiwan translation disaster memorable MCQ

    English tagline: "Come alive with the Pepsi generation." Translated into Taiwanese (a pictogram-based script, so translation is interpretive, not phonetic) it became:

    "With Pepsi, it will revive your ancestors."

    5.4 Four ways to minimise cultural barriers

    1. Be aware and sensitive to cultural differences; be a conscious communicator
    2. Expose yourself — build knowledge and experience of other cultures by interacting
    3. Avoid generalisation and stereotyping ("if you had a bad experience with a person from Botswana, it doesn't mean all people from Botswana are bad")
    4. In multicultural environments, find the common factors — you can't address each one separately

    5.5 Context guaranteed MCQ

    Definition: the setting, the circumstances that form the setting for an event, statement or idea, which sets the terms by which it can be fully understood.

    $$\boxed{\textbf{MORE context } \Rightarrow \textbf{ LESS ambiguity. The lack of context IS ambiguity.}}$$

    The assumption problem: "If you ask the receiver to fill in the blanks, be sure that those blanks will not be the words you envisioned." Assumptions happen on both sides, and without shared context they will be mismatched.

    The social-media problem ← likely MCQ: context gets lost — you see a message but don't know what preceded it.

    How the sender improves context: analyse the audience (more knowledge ⇒ fewer assumptions) · practise empathy · take a "YOU approach", not an "I approach" · test the message by bouncing it off people familiar with or drawn from the target audience · be conservative with assumptions.

    How much context? "It depends." Use questioning — "Have you been to Bangalore before?" No ⇒ give more background; yes ⇒ let it go.

    The Quintilian quote ← guaranteed attribution MCQ: Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (lived AD 35–100): "We should not write so that it is possible for the reader to understand us, but so that it is impossible for him to misunderstand us." Corollary given: err on the side of MORE, especially with Western audiences, who are low on ambiguity tolerance.

    5.6 Time and place

    Advertising timing examples: Times of India Ascent job ads appear on Wednesdays — "people don't read work-related content on Sundays; they read the matrimonials." Sales/shopping/matrimonial peak at weekends; sporting events (IPL, Olympics, Super Bowl) are prime advertising slots.

    Emotional timing: be sensitive to both parties' emotional state; use observation and non-verbal cues; use a "lead-in" to test the water before plunging in; if in doubt, postpone.

    AMBUSH TIMING ← likely MCQ: "piling onto another person at the wrong time without giving them notice." The remedy: ask in advance — "May I ask you a question? Do you have the time?"

    Consideration: sending messages when the recipient is busy, stressed or distracted can be considered RUDE and could cost orders. "Don't send emails on Friday evening and hope to get a response."

    Place: emotionally loaded messages are best done face-to-face. "Cafe Coffee Day said 'a lot gets done over coffee', but you couldn't have a conversation there — it was the wrong place, and that threw the timing off." Anecdote: many Indian MDs didn't do sales calls until leaders like Mr Premji realised they had to go and meet people, shake hands, build trust.

    1. Effectiveness of communication guaranteed MCQ
    4

    6.1 The definition

    $$\textbf{Effectiveness} = \textbf{the extent to which the DESIRED OBJECTIVE is achieved or fulfilled.}$$ Also expressed as: a measure of how well meaning or understanding was transferred.

    The framing correction: "There are effective communicators, but there are no BAD communicators. Effective is neither good nor bad — good and bad are subjective. The question is: does it deliver the results in line with the purpose?"

    The purposes of business communication: to inform, to instruct, to persuade — all of which tie back to changing behaviour.

    Cost context ← likely numeric MCQ: organisational communication (internal + external) can be anything from 4% to 15% of revenue.

    6.2 Aeschines vs Demosthenes guaranteed MCQ

    Ancient Greece, 300 BC, Athens. Philip of Macedonia (father of Alexander the Great) was threatening the Greek city-states; the Athenians needed to raise an army.

    When Aeschines spoke, they said, "How well he speaks." When Demosthenes spoke, they said, "Let us march against Philip."

    Demosthenes was the more effective speaker — because he got the desired outcome (action).

    $$\boxed{\textbf{AGREEMENT / ACQUIESCENCE is NOT the same as ACTION.}}$$

    6.3 The David Ogilvy corroboration

    David Ogilvy, founder of Ogilvy & Mather:

    "I do not regard advertising as entertainment or as an art form, but as a medium of information. When I write an advertisement, I don't want you to tell me that you found it creative. I want you to find it so interesting that you buy the product."

    (i.e. awards at Cannes ≠ sales.)

    6.4 Realistic outcomes — the elevator-pitch correction likely MCQ

    Many startups believe the desired outcome of an elevator pitch is funding or a sale. Wrong. "At best, an elevator pitch can arouse interest and awareness. A realistic desired outcome is that you get more time to present the business idea — you got your foot in the door, but they're not going to write a cheque at that point."

    The principle: outcomes must be REALISTIC. If the bar is set too high, all communication will be judged ineffective.

    6.5 Measuring effectiveness — examples

    Setting Measure
    Shiv asks team lead Anjali for a month's leave Approval or no approval — everything else is meaningless
    Agency pitches to a client The client SIGNS — "great, we'll get back to you" is not effectiveness
    Social media Likes, reposts, positive or negative responses (if creating an uproar was the goal)
    Dell magazine advertising ← the flagship example Each ad carried a DIFFERENT TELEPHONE NUMBER, so incoming calls could be tracked to the publication. Dell's target: 300 calls a week, or they'd stop advertising in that magazine
    Public relations agencies Column centimetres of coverage — e.g. Intel setting a target of 100 column-cm per month; also number of news items and click-throughs
    Crowdfunding Number of people signing up; quantum of funds raised

    6.6 Who is responsible for effectiveness? guaranteed MCQ

    $$\boxed{\textbf{THE SENDER.}}$$

    The reasoning: "The person responsible is the person who had the purpose" — who wanted something in the first place. The sender is the one who synthesises and puts the message together. "Until the moment the receiver receives the message, he or she is a passive component."

    The sender must therefore: have clear objectives · have pre-defined criteria/measures of effectiveness · enable a channel of response.

    1. Types (forms) of messages guaranteed MCQ
    1

    All communication happens via sensory and cerebral organs — eyes, ears, mouth, lungs and voice box, hands and fingers, nasal apparatus, tongue, skin; the brain is the processor and storage device. Many organs are both input and output devices. We usually deploy more than one sensory capability at a time.

    The five forms

    # Form Encoding basis Organs used Key characteristics
    1 Spoken (verbal) Language — a set of sounds Voice, ears (primary); eyes (secondary) Many world languages are purely spoken and have NO script ← likely MCQ. Also covers the audio channel: singing, music, podcasts, radio (not all of it language-based)
    2 Written Text, language, sometimes symbols/pictograms (Egyptian, Chinese, Japanese) Hands (write), eyes (read), brain (process) — more cerebral More permanent · greater efficiency and reliability · easy to compose, transmit, store, reproduce · digitally storable almost permanently
    3 Visual Symbols, images, text Eyes The oldest form — cave paintings → symbols → images → text came much later. Examples: road signs, emoticons, posters, ads, charts, graphs, infographics, art, sculpture. Very efficient to consume — "a picture is worth a thousand words"
    4 Multimedia Combinations of text, visual and other sensory input Multiple "A product of modern digital technology, trying to recreate real life in digital form." Film, video, video games, AR, VR, 3D and 4D movies (shaking seats, pumped-in odours)
    5 Non-verbal Signals, not messages Body, limbs — plus inanimate objects Sentient beings' looks, movement, use of limbs. Inanimate signals: colour (red lava = hot/danger; traffic lights) and temperature

    The professor's caveat: "Real life IS multimedia. What you see as multimedia today is a small subset of what your sensory organs can achieve."

    1. Metacommunication guaranteed MCQ
    4

    "Meta" is a Greek word meaning greater than, beyond, after, higher than — "more comprehensive or transcending; that which rises above; a higher level." (Compare metamorphosis; metadata = "data about data".)

    $$\textbf{Metacommunication} = \textbf{higher-order communication} = \textbf{"communication ABOUT communication."}$$

    It is descriptive of the underlying components of a message and arrives as CUES — "non-verbal or non-textual signals that provide additional information about a message and its content."

    8.1 The two categories of metacommunication cue

    $$\textbf{1. PARALINGUAL cues} \qquad \textbf{2. NON-VERBAL cues}$$

    8.2 Paralingual cues — VOCALICS

    Para-language = the collective term for secondary non-verbal information embedded in, or wrapping, what is being spoken. It may be added unconsciously or deliberately for effect. The technical term for the sound side is VOCALICS.

    Vocalic Effect
    Accent Usually unconscious; can be faked. (Bengali in Chittagong vs Kolkata)
    Emphasis / word stress Subtly changes meaning
    Speed Fast = excitement; slow = serious, threatening
    Volume Drops with speed for menace; rises for excitement
    Pitch Rises when nervous, anxious or yelling; low and soft when calm
    Intonation and cadence Languages are musical — they have rhythm
    Fillers "the errs, the umms, the ors — and in India, matlab"; indicate hesitation, thinking, or not being finished
    SILENCE Silence is itself a vocalic — a cue. "There are default options — silence means guilty…"

    The four variables applied to spoken messages to embed additional information: speed, pitch, intonation, cadence.

    8.3 The emphasis demonstration guaranteed MCQ

    "I did not tell Tara that you were late."

    Emphasis on Implied meaning
    I Somebody else told her
    not Absolutely did not
    tell I sent an SMS — I did not speak it
    Tara I told Tara's sister, who told her
    you I said Bala was late

    8.4 Punctuation and pausing change meaning memorable MCQ examples| Without punctuation | With punctuation |

    |---|---| | "Raja the tiger is sick" — a tiger named Raja is sick | "Raja, the tiger is sick" — telling Raja that the tiger is sick | | "He is married happily" | "He is married, happily" | | "I find inspiration in cooking my family and my dog" — cannibalism | "I find inspiration in cooking, my family, and my dog" — three separate things |

    (A comma is a shorter pause than a full stop.)

    8.5 Non-verbal cues — the five "-ics" guaranteed MCQ| Term | Domain |

    |---|---| | Kinesics | The body — gestures, use of hands, posture | | Oculesics | The eyes — eye contact. "A very large portion of non-verbal, conveying large quantities of information" | | Proxemics | Space — the distance between you and the other person. "If I have to threaten you, I need to get close to you" | | Haptics | Touch — handshakes, touchscreens, haptic keyboards | | Chronemics | Time — including how long someone takes to respond to a question (used to judge truthfulness) |

    Plus physical attributes: smell, temperature, colour, appearance.

    Charles Darwin (author of The Origin of Species, a naturalist and biologist) ← attribution MCQ: "facial expressions are an external manifestation of a person's internal state."

    Most body-language cues are INVOLUNTARY — they "reveal much more about ourselves than we might wish to." If controlled, they can be used to advantage: "the Japanese as a culture have mastered the art of being expressionless — very difficult to negotiate with."

    MICRO EXPRESSIONS ← likely MCQ: involuntary twitches — mouth movement, flared nostrils, moving eyebrows. "In that millisecond when you see the true expression before it gets masked — that is a micro expression."

    The handshake as haptics — what it reveals: hard/soft/limp · do they push back · do they smile · sweaty or dry (anxiety) · eye contact while shaking · do they let go.

    1. The communication gestalt guaranteed MCQ
    2

    Gestalt is a word of German origin: something made of many parts and yet more than or different from the simple combination or addition of its parts.

    "A movie is much more than a collection of individual scenes — there's a plot, character builds, intangibles." "You cannot separate communication from behaviour. We are gestaltic."

    $$\boxed{\textbf{TOTAL MESSAGE} = \textbf{tangible content (words, sounds, visuals)} + \textbf{PARALINGUAL cues} + \textbf{NON-VERBAL cues}}$$

    Equivalently: the content + the way it is delivered + the context. And total meaning is derived from the total message.

    9.1 Congruence

    Definition: checking whether the words match the way they are said, and whether the body language validates both.

    "When we try to figure out whether somebody is being truthful, we look for congruence." It is a skill that can be brought to the conscious level. Conversely, using your body and voice consistently builds credibility, impact and effectiveness.

    9.2 The 7% rule is a MYTH guaranteed MCQ

    "There is NO formula. There is no magic formula, no back-of-the-book correct answer."

    The Mehrabian model: Professor Albert Mehrabian, University of California Los Angeles, found in one specific study, on a specific set of people, in a specific context that:

    Component Share of impact
    Words / text 7%
    Paralanguage 38%
    Non-verbal 55%

    The professor's point: "This 7% has been generalised into a magic formula, but it's not true. We cannot extend a single research instance to a generalised situation. All we need to know is that there are three components and we must deploy them as best fits the situation." Required reading: the article "The 7% Rule — Fact, Fiction or Misunderstanding."

    1. Characteristics of business communication
    1

    10.1 The business context — three defining features

    1. Purpose-driven / goal-oriented — focused on specific outcomes: sales, profitability, employee satisfaction, growth. "It's not like writing poetry."
    2. Diverse and HETEROGENEOUS audiences — clients, employees, investors, shareholders, public, press, government — each unique segment has unique communication needs and behaviours
    3. Fiduciary implications — financial, legal and ethical; business communication must comply with regulations and ethical guidelines

    10.2 What makes business communication different — four points

    1. Businesses operate on a PROFESSIONAL basis, not a personal one — "even family-run businesses have professionalised, whether it's the Birlas or the Murugappas or the kiranawala at the corner"
    2. Content, format and messaging parameters may be SPECIFIED BY EXTERNAL PARTIES — stock exchanges, banks, legal agreements, minutes of meeting
    3. Structure defines the document: "What makes a letter different from an email, from a report, from a proposal? They have structures, components and content defined — otherwise it's not a letter, it's just a piece of writing"
    4. Risk — done badly it can cause loss of share value and demotivated employees who leave

    10.3 REGISTER guaranteed MCQ

    Definition: the level of formality or informality in language and its use — "how we adjust our language based on the situation, our audience, our purpose." Register = formality + tone.

    Analogy: what will you wear for an evening out? — a party, a movie, a rock show, a Rotary Club meeting, a wedding all call for different dress.

    The gradations within business communication ← memorise the order: $$\textbf{Formal} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{Non-formal} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{Informal} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{Casual}$$

    Register Where used
    Formal Business, law, academia, government — precise language, complex sentence structures, serious tone, specialist vocabulary (using the wrong word in law may lose the case). Used in academic papers, legal contracts, formal speeches, job interviews
    Non-formal / Informal / Casual Non-formal conversations, brainstorming sessions, employee get-togethers, celebratory dinners
    Consultative Advisory, investigative or expert diagnostic situations — doctor–patient, consultant–client
    Intimate Very close relationships — private language, code signals, inside jokes. Not applicable to business

    The thumb rule ← likely MCQ: "If in doubt, always err on the higher side — adopt a MORE FORMAL tone. You can always tone it down, but it's very difficult to go the other way once you've started casual, informal or frivolous."

    1. The 7 Cs of business communication guaranteed MCQ
    1

    Purpose: seven descriptors used to create and to evaluate business messages. (They are called the 7 Cs simply because each begins with a C.)

    $$\textbf{Clear · Concrete · Concise · Complete · Correct · Coherent · Courteous}$$

    C Meaning Notes from the lecture
    Clear Clarity is linked to SIMPLICITY — easy to understand, not ambiguous, not confusing Achieved with simple language, simple sentence construction, good organisation. "Business writing is meant to be easily consumable — it's not a crossword puzzle"
    Concrete Specific, definite, well-defined — not vague nor general; palpable "You will get large returns" = vague. "You can expect as much as 10% return on your investment" = concrete
    Concise Compact, frugal in words — less is better Get to the point quickly · use a direct approach · eliminate fluff · cut redundancy · prioritise key points
    Complete Include everything the audience needs, at the minimum level, to understand and act Reduces assumptions. State your assumptions explicitly. Providing adequate context is part of completeness. "Make your messaging complete and bulletproof"
    Correct Accurate (Listed but not expanded)
    Coherent The LOGIC FLOWS — parts fit together, not jagged, uneven or disconnected; makes sense without effort "This is one of the most important characteristics in the 7 Cs lineup"
    Courteous TONALITY — formal, respectful, cool, calm, collected, impersonal, professional NOT provocative, NOT sensational, NOT aggressive — but it may be ASSERTIVE. Applies protocols: salutation, greeting, summarisation, thanks. Language is professional, neutral and motivating — "even if that person is a competitor"

    Two closing points ← likely MCQ: (1) the 7 Cs are INTERLINKED — "there is some gestaltic interaction amongst them"; (2) there are actually MORE than 7 Cs — these are just the most important ones.

    11.1 Being "managerially appropriate"

    The test: "Is it appropriate in a managerial situation? Does this communication behove a manager?"

    Do: maintain formality as required (far more with EXTERNAL parties than internally) · practise respectful disagreement — "you can discuss, share opinions, be persuasive, but you don't fight, take umbrage, or get aggressive." Don't: slang · offensive language · emoticons · stars and gifs. "It may sound dull and boring, but it's managerially appropriate."

    1. Structure, logic and flow guaranteed MCQ
    4

    12.1 The Michelangelo story

    Pope Julius (16th-century Rome) asked Michelangelo to "do something with the ceiling" of the Sistine Chapel. He did not start splashing paint. He decided a theme (from the Old Testament), created a STRUCTURE, presented the plan for approval, and only then painted — lying on his back, on a ceiling 115 feet high, over many years.

    12.2 What structure does

    Structuring = the organisation of information and message components. It gives form, which: 1. Facilitates UNDERSTANDING 2. Aids RETENTION — creates associations in the mind 3. Aids RECALL 4. Facilitates ACTION

    The shopping-list illustration: a list reading coconuts, milk, rice, dal, chapati, papadam, pickle forces you to crisscross the supermarket. Group the list to match the store's structure (dairy, bakery, vegetables) and it becomes efficient. The consistency illustration: newspapers and phone UIs have fixed structures — "if every day sports moved from page 14 to page 1, you'd go crazy."

    12.3 Ways of structuring likely list MCQ

    Chronological (early→now or now→before) · Prioritisation (most important first — journalistic writing) · Spatial/geographical (north, east, west, south) · Problem–options–solution · Cause and effect · By topic · By logic.

    The Alfred Hitchcock point: "Suspense is about telling people what's going to happen." Indians love Hindi movies because the structure is clear and we know what's coming.

    12.4 Inductive vs deductive reasoning guaranteed MCQ| | INDUCTIVE reasoning | DEDUCTIVE reasoning |

    |---|---|---| | Direction | Specific cases → generalised statement | General statement → specific instance | | Conclusion is called | A HYPOTHESIS | A conclusion | | Guarantee | Never guaranteed — "logically correct but need not necessarily be true" | Valid if the premises are correct | | Structure | — | SYLLOGISM: major premise → minor premise → conclusion | | Gravity example | "I threw a ball up 15 times and every time it fell back. Therefore there is a force pulling it to earth — gravity." | "All planetary bodies are subject to gravitational forces. Therefore every time you throw a ball up, it returns to earth." | | Classic example | "Every quiz so far has been easy, therefore this one will be too." · "The teacher used PowerPoint in the last few classes, therefore she will tomorrow." · "Every year for 100 years there have been hurricanes in the Bay of Bengal, therefore this year too." | Major premise: all men are mortal. Minor premise: Socrates is a man. Conclusion: Socrates is mortal. | | Geometry example | — | Major: the internal angles of a triangle sum to 180°. Minor: this right-angled triangle has one angle of 60°. Conclusion: the third angle = 180 − (90 + 60) = 30° |

    The deductive warning: "If the major premise itself is wrong, or the minor premise is incorrect, the outcome will be incorrect or nonsensical."

    12.5 The four-step structure of an ARGUMENT guaranteed MCQ (order matters)| Step | Content | "The earth is round" example |

    |---|---|---| | 1. Claim | State what you want | "The earth is round." | | 2. Evidence | Provide data supporting the claim | Rotation of the earth, movement of the stars, the sun rising in different places at different times | | 3. Refutation | Discuss and eliminate the opposing viewpoint | People once thought the earth was flat — but Columbus did not fall off the edge, and Magellan circumnavigated it | | 4. Conclusion | Restatement of the claim | "Therefore we can assume as an axiom that the earth is round." |

    1. Audience orientation guaranteed MCQ
    4

    Why: companies must ensure the time, effort and money invested in communication produce the intended outcomes. "The more sharply we focus on the audience, the more likely we achieve favourable returns."

    13.1 The two flagship examples

    • Hero Pleasure scooter — targeted younger females, using Alia Bhatt as brand ambassador and the tagline "Why should boys have all the fun?" — "absolutely popular and almost viral."
    • IIT Guwahati National Science Day speech — the audience was IIT professors and students, school children from 5th to 12th standard, students from other colleges, and government dignitaries. The professor asked "which segment is the LARGEST?", learned it was school children, and pitched at slightly older high-school level. (Pitch too low ⇒ erudite people disengage; too high ⇒ children can't follow.)

    13.2 Multiplicity of audiences — the four types guaranteed MCQ| Type | Who they are |

    |---|---| | Initial / GATEKEEPER audience | Those who approve your document before it goes further — your manager, legal, finance, compliance departments | | PRIMARY audience | The actual recipients who will act on, benefit from, or decide on your report | | SECONDARY audience | People the primary audience passes it on to — departments, experts, think tanks, committees, other agencies | | WATCHDOG audience | NGOs, activist groups, trolls, the press — people with nothing to do with the report who may take strong negative positions and criticise it |

    The rule: "You write for your PRIMARY audience, but keep in mind that your gatekeeper needs to let it through, that secondary audiences don't shoot it down, and that you don't say anything that gives rise to activists or trolls."

    13.3 What to know about your audience

    What do they know? · what media do they consume? · where are they available (venue/location)? · who are their role models (⇒ influencer marketing)? · what are their needs, wants, dreams and aspirations (interest generators)?

    HOT BUTTONS ← likely MCQ: "the TRIGGERS FOR ACTION — what will cause your target audience to actually move to action?" (Cf. Demosthenes.)

    13.4 The AIM model guaranteed MCQ

    $$\textbf{A} = \text{Audience} \qquad \textbf{I} = \text{Intent} \qquad \textbf{M} = \text{Message}$$

    (Also seen in the literature as AOM — Audience, Objective, Message; the course treats them as the same.)

    Key property: the three components interact — "change the audience and the message changes; change the intent and the message changes." It is an ITERATIVE process: modify one component and you must revisit the others until you reach stability.

    13.5 Audience analysis — what to determine

    1. Identify the audience — "Who buys gold at weddings? It's not the bride and groom, it's the parents." The bride and groom are influencers; the parents are the ECONOMIC BUYER — "the person who shells out the money and makes the decision."
    2. Primary and secondary research — demographics, wants, needs, desires, interests, triggers, media consumed, places frequented, communication style
    3. The relationship between the target audience and the sender — "How does the audience perceive the sender? This is why we build brand — brand building is building relationships between the audience and the sender"
    4. The milieu — environment, situation, occasion (Deepavali advertising; pre-budget statements in Dec–Jan)
    5. Timing — when will the audience find it most convenient to consume this?
    6. Barriers — what exists, and build a minimisation strategy

    13.6 Intent has TWO parties guaranteed MCQ

    "People tend to forget: the audience are also human beings and they have their own needs, wants and purposes."

    Which comes first? "It has to be the AUDIENCE'S." The implied logic: if you address their needs and wants, they will do what you want, and therefore your objectives are also fulfilled.

    $$\boxed{\textbf{WIIFT} = \textbf{"What's In It For THEM?"}}$$ Answer that and you can craft a win-win. "If you look at it only from your perspective, it's not likely to be a win-win."

    Recommended reading: Chip Heath and Dan Heath, Made to Stick: Why Some Ideas Survive and Others Die.

    MODULE 2 — WRITTEN BUSINESS COMMUNICATION

    1. Why written communication survives
    2

    Three reasons likely MCQ 1. It is a product of conscious and deliberate effort — you must think, plan and commit 2. It is a written record, easy to store, transmit and reproduce, and almost permanent if stored properly 3. It is LEGALLY VALID — "while oral contracts may be acceptable in certain situations, written communication is far stronger in legal validity and therefore credibility"

    Is it a dinosaur? "Certainly not! Printed written business communication is disappearing, but the actual volumes of written material are INCREASING — we're just not printing them; eBooks, documents and PDFs are replacing printed content."

    14.1 Classification of written business documents by purpose likely MCQ| Purpose | Documents |

    |---|---| | Informative | Executive summaries, memos, letters | | Instructional | Manuals, policy documents | | Persuasive | Proposals, brochures, pitches | | Commercial | Agreements, quotations, bid documents, financial statements | | Decision support | Briefing notes, reports, performance appraisals | | Goodwill / personal | Goodwill messages, personal messages |

    1. Structure in written business communication guaranteed MCQ
    2

    15.1 Pyramid vs INVERTED pyramid

    PYRAMID structure INVERTED PYRAMID structure
    Shape Downward-facing triangle Upward-facing triangle
    Order Details, arguments, plots, subplots first → climax/denouement at the END Core message / conclusion / recommendation FIRST → argument and data below to support it
    Where used Detective novels, fiction, poetry — "Agatha Christie: on page 342 you find out it was the butler" BUSINESS
    Why Suspense "Business documents are not puzzles. Time is money. Managers have very little time and they are not puzzle solvers"

    The port example: not "Kerala has a coastline, there are good roadways, there is this, there is this, therefore we should have the port at XYZ." Instead: "It is the recommendation of the committee that we situate the port at Vizhinjam, for the following reasons: infrastructure, road access, power, labour, it's on the shipping route…"

    15.2 OBC — Opening, Body, Close guaranteed MCQ

    "These are immutable laws, as immutable as the sculpture in Elephanta, where you have the three-headed Trimurti — Opening, Body, Close."

    Part Contents
    OPENING / introduction Defines the background, context, frame or setting · necessary statutory information · greetings and courtesies · ideally states the core message, a summary, the situation, the purpose, the problem and the recommendation. "Theoretically, if I read the introduction, I pretty much know what's going to happen"
    BODY The detailed content, itself internally structured with logical flow
    CLOSE / conclusion Summary/restatement of the major recommendations and arguments · a CALL FOR ACTION or the next steps — what needs to be done and who will do it · in persuasive writing, reinforcement of the benefits of the proposal

    1. The media release likely MCQ
    1

    Why "media release" and not "press release": "because it goes not just to the press, but to other forms, channels and media."

    It is a classic example of AUDIENCE ORIENTATION: it is designed around how journalists use it — the journalist is the gatekeeper/intermediary, but the primary audience is the end reader. It is written for non-specialist, non-technical audiences.

    16.1 The components in order (illustrated with the real IIM Bangalore–Harvard Business Publishing release)

    # Component Purpose
    1 Logos (both parties) Establish credibility and brand
    2 "MEDIA RELEASE" label States clearly what the document is (not a press note, briefing note or minutes)
    3 "For immediate publication" Authorisation/permission to release now. (The alternative is a time embargo)
    4 Headline Succinct, concise summary — written to create INTEREST: "Harvard Business Publishing to distribute IIM Bangalore cases"
    5 Subhead Adds detail to help the editor/sub-editor decide on publication
    6 Dateline Date and place of release (here simultaneously in Boston and Bangalore)
    7 Paragraph 1 — the complete story "If the journalist were to cut everything below the first paragraph and publish only it, it should be a complete enough story"
    8 Quotes in DECREASING order of importance Para 2 = the Director (Prof. Pankaj Chandra); para 3 = the head of Harvard Business Publishing India; later, the professor in charge of research and publication
    9 "End of media release" delimiter Marks where the release matter ends
    10 "About [organisation]" write-ups So the journalist/reader knows who the parties are; often lifted into the story
    11 Contact information Spokespersons for journalists wanting more
    12 The PR agency's handle The media often works through the agency

    Recommended reading: Grammarly's "How to Write a Press Release: A Step-by-Step Guide."

    1. Other structural models
    3

    17.1 The problem-solving / case-report structure — six steps guaranteed MCQ (order matters)| Step | Content |

    |---|---| | 1 | Situational analysis — establish the background | | 2 | Define the problem | | 3 | Criteria and constraints — the measures and limitations. (e.g. "choose the option with the highest rate of return"; a constraint on alcohol is that you cannot advertise, so "advertise the product" is not an admissible option) | | 4 | Generate options | | 5 | Evaluate the options against the criteria and constraints | | 6 | Recommendation |

    (Also exists in a nine-step form; the six-step version is the simplest.)

    17.2 The MINTO PYRAMID PRINCIPLE guaranteed MCQ

    Created by Barbara Minto, who worked for McKinsey and Company (which adopted it); a bestselling book in the United States. It goes "way beyond just structure — into logic and content."

    Three levels: $$\textbf{Level 1: the CONCLUSION / core message / recommendation}$$ $$\textbf{Level 2: the MAJOR SUPPORTING ARGUMENTS}$$ $$\textbf{Level 3: subsidiary arguments, DATA, EVIDENCE, detail}$$

    The worked example: "Germany was the best team [core message] — arguments A, B, C, D, E [e.g. the coach was a critical factor] — supported by specific details and examples [who the coach was and what he did]."

    Its use in presentations (§28): "Every box is one slide. You can build an outline of your entire presentation on the Minto pyramid model."

    17.3 STAR and SBO frameworks guaranteed MCQ| Framework | Expansion | Where used |

    |---|---|---| | STAR | Situation · Task · Action · Result | Academic situations, JOB INTERVIEWS, hiring; provides a clear, concise narrative | | SBO | Situation · Behaviour · Outcome | Summarising a situation and its impact based on the actions taken |

    When the structure is given to you: stock exchanges specify the structure and contents of a red herring prospectus for an IPO; e-tendering sites prescribe proposal structures.

    1. Direct and indirect writing plans guaranteed MCQ
    2

    18.1 The direct writing plan

    Definition: a clear and concise approach that gets straight to the point — the main point in the FIRST SENTENCE. Aligned with the inverted pyramid and conciseness. "No fluff, no preamble."

    Used when conveying: good news · neutral information · a request · an inquiry · routine information.

    The five-part content flow (promotion letter example) likely MCQ 1. State the good news upfront — "I write to inform you that the management has approved your promotion from grade three to grade four…" 2. The CITATION — the rationale/reason for the good news 3. The BENEFITS — the new role, responsibilities, compensation 4. Administrative issues — e.g. return a signed copy to HR within three days 5. Close — invite questions/feedback; optionally a personal message

    Email application: the subject line takes the direct approach — "An investment proposal for you", not "Hi" or "Would you like to make some money?"

    The cultural caveat ← likely MCQ: the direct approach works well in Western cultures (Europe, America), but some cultures — typically ORIENTAL — consider it rude and expect a preliminary lead-in. In such cases the citation may PRECEDE the good news, making it semi-direct or indirect.

    18.2 The indirect writing plan

    Used for: negative or unfavourable news and PERSUASIVE messages.

    Purposes: reduce the shock of bad news · keep negative reaction impersonal · build goodwill and trust · make bad news more palatable · make persuasion more effective.

    The content flow:

    Part Content
    Opening = the BUFFER A positive-to-neutral lead-up that builds the reason for the bad news. "You know the performance of the company these last few months — thanks to Covid, inflation and the Ukraine situation, we've been unsure whether we can stay profitable…" — the recipient is mentally prepared
    (Optional) Forewarning "I'm sorry we're meeting under these circumstances, but I must inform you that I have some not very good news"
    Body — the bad news Stated CLEARLY but MINIMALLY. "Don't volunteer too much information. Don't let your anxiety make you say things you'll regret or can't go back on" · keep it impersonal and reasonable · optionally add improvement areas and administrative issues
    Close End on a POSITIVE note — "we're hopeful the situation will change… I personally assure you I will look at moving your increment forward" · a personal note to rebuild trust

    1. The business writing process guaranteed MCQ
    2

    $$\textbf{PLANNING} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{DRAFTING} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{REVISING} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{EDITING (\& proofreading)}$$

    19.1 Planning — three sub-activities

    1. PRE-WRITING — "before you start to write, THINK." Analyse the communication situation; write down the purpose (yours and the audience's); define the desired response and outcomes; define the target audience (The anecdote: a man kept a photograph of a customer family on his desk — "this is a picture of the people who buy my product… it helps me connect with them; every time I write, I have a visual contact.")
    2. SYNTHESIS — bringing together all components: mechanicals, content, ideas, data. Direct or indirect approach? What argument?
    3. OUTLINING — "I write the headings first, then look at the structure and flow… Word and PowerPoint allow you to outline. Shuffle them around before you start dumping text."

    19.2 The professor's time allocation guaranteed MCQ

    If you had 100 units of time, condense the four stages into three:

    Stage Share of time
    Planning 40%
    Writing / drafting 20%
    Reviewing and editing 40%

    "The more time you spend planning, the less time you spend writing." "If I had eight hours to cut a tree, I'd spend four hours sharpening my knife." "Failing to prepare is preparing to fail."

    19.3 Drafting

    Don't be judgmental — don't write a sentence and agonise. "Write continuously. Get the words down." You may write the introduction and closing later. Write MORE than you need, because you will trim later — "as you chip the marble, the sculpture will emerge from the block of stone."

    19.4 Reviewing and editing

    Review = is it appropriate? per the objective? does the logic flow and structure work? You may produce second and third drafts, vary the sequence and flow, delete, replace or modify. Editing/proofreading = Grammarly, Word Editor, careful reading.

    "There is NO SUBSTITUTE for checking it yourself. Do not leave it to digital editors. If you use generative AI tools, human intervention is a must — editors and AI tools are not perfect."

    1. Using Gen AI to advantage likely MCQ
    2

    Applications named: ChatGPT, Gemini, Copilot (browser-based); Grammarly, Word Editor (embedded).

    20.1 The five risks

    Risk Detail
    HALLUCINATION "Gen AI is capable of coming up with fantastic stuff which is inappropriate and unusable in a business situation" — especially on specialised/niche topics where it has no access to your customer's specifics
    Ethical — misinformation Are you going to verify that everything it said is correct, legal, ethical, not fake, not made up?
    Bias "The AI keeps learning; it has no judgment of its own" — bias is easily built in, as seen in AI-generated political posts
    Data privacy and security "Are you going to share information about your company to an application that will archive that information and use it when somebody else makes a request?"
    Fact-check burden You must verify that cited references and web addresses actually exist and are credible

    20.2 The legal cautionary tale memorable MCQ

    In a real estate case, a lawyer submitted a document generated by Gen AI. The opposing party found that the citations were HALLUCINATIONS — the cases did not exist; the AI had made up its own cases, citations and references. The lawyer did not fact-check. Consequences: fines imposed on the legal firms; risk of disbarment, loss of money and face.

    The verdict: "Can managers rely on content from generative AI? A hundred percent nowhere close to it. No, you cannot rely on it." It is 100% essential to review and edit to ensure accuracy, credibility and coherence.

    1. Email — structure, content and best practices guaranteed MCQ
    3

    21.1 Addressing — To, CC, BCC

    Field Rule
    To Only ESSENTIAL people — your primary audience, those expected to act on or respond to the email. Keep the list short; exclude the redundant and non-essential
    CC For INFORMATION only. "Don't expect people in the copy box to take action." If a person has a role in execution, they belong in To. Beware the "CYA" phenomenon — dumping everybody in to protect yourself. "It doesn't cost anything, so people pile on, but that's bad business practice"
    BCC Use with care — the BCC recipient doesn't know why they're being copied. Use it only when the other party already knows what's going on. It also cannot be formally relied on ("you were not supposed to be on that list")

    The privacy point ← likely MCQ: putting many people in To or CC gives everyone access to everyone's email address, with privacy implications — worsened by Reply All. To hide the list: address the email TO YOURSELF and put everybody in BCC.

    21.2 The subject line

    "INVEST in the subject line" — it is "the difference between getting your mail opened or consigned to cyberspace to rot evermore."

    Three rules: 1. Write it to raise awareness and interest and compel the recipient to open it (direct approach, summarisation, persuasion). 2. Subject lines have a limited SHELF LIFE — on long mail trails the topic drifts ("'Meeting to review quarterly targets' has become 'Where shall we have dinner tomorrow?'"). Change the subject line and prune the mail trail. 3. NEVER use the subject line AS the message — "a blank body is very poor etiquette, and the subject line is not your message."

    21.3 Salutations and sign-offs

    Guidance
    Salutation When in doubt, go FORMAL: Dear Mr…, Dear Professor…, Dear Dr… "You can always become informal later, but the progression does not go the other way." Address all To-recipients (Dear Rama, Vijaya and Shilpa); you may create targeted sections inside the mail ("Dear Rama, could you look into this?"). For large lists: "Hello everyone" doesn't work; "Dear all" is acceptable but impersonal — better to use an email merge or a group identity (Dear members of the engineering department, Dear new recruits)
    Sign-off Keep it formal: Sincerely · With regards · With best wishes. Avoid "warm regards" ("can I send lukewarm regards?"), and avoid colonial hangovers — yours faithfully, yours respectfully
    Thanking Avoid thanking as part of the signature. "I've seen emails with four thanks in one message" — it reads as over-effusive, even submissive, and looks like grovelling. If you must thank, do it upfront, separately and properly
    Signature block Use one — a professional-looking signature adds a lot. Put a photo only if you're an accountant, lawyer, architect or politician — otherwise it "could create bias and prejudice." NO motivational sayings at the bottom of business email

    21.4 The OBC structure applies to email

    "The OBC content structure is not only applicable in email — it is ESSENTIAL in email. Being an email does not let you off from writing well." Use headings, italics, highlights and subheads — "the Outlook editor is as good as Word."

    21.5 Best practices likely list MCQ

    1. Always OPEN the attachment from the email before hitting send — check it's the right version and the right file. Open and check links too.
    2. Apply the four-stage writing process and OBC
    3. Keep tables and charts simple; put charts, graphics and images in ATTACHMENTS — the email viewing plane is limited
    4. In Outlook, disable automatic send from the outbox, so you can review before it actually goes
    5. Avoid sending email when face-to-face or telephonic communication is possible — "build relationships."

    21.6 What NOT to do likely list MCQ

    1. Reply All — "the most dangerous button in any email application"
    2. Continuous CAPS, bold or underline — "writing in caps is the equivalent of SHOUTING"; considered rude
    3. Smileys and emoticons in official email
    4. Routine delivery/read receipts — only when absolutely essential
    5. Marking everything "urgent" / "high priority" — "people will refuse to open it out of spite"
    6. Memes, forwards, libellous, defamatory, offensive, racist or obscene content, or personal email, on your official account

    1. Business letters likely MCQ
    2

    Still relevant: even as PDFs, "the fundamental physical structures and content structures haven't changed very much."

    22.1 The three physical component blocks guaranteed MCQ| Block | Contents |

    |---|---| | PREFATORY components (preceding/introductory) | Letterhead · date line · address block | | MESSAGE components | Following the salutation, subject line and reference line — the body text, tables etc. | | TRAILING components | Signature block · title/designation block · sender information · address line · postscripts · copy lines · attachment information |

    The same three-block structure applies to proposals, white papers and minutes of meeting.

    Layout: the exhibit uses the FULL BLOCK FORMAT — "all of the text is justified to the extreme left; only one single alignment." Companies typically specify the layout and provide templates and style guides (font, font size), so all correspondence looks consistent.

    22.2 Content structure = OBC

    Opening = salutation, subject line, reference line, attention line, protocols and customary courtesies, contextual information. Body = the real content. Close = a call to action, a reiteration of commitment/continuing service, sign-off, signature block, designation, optional postscript, copy line and attachment information.

    Why postscripts are powerful ← likely MCQ: readers' eyes go letterhead → bottom of page (who wrote it) → back up to the address block, so "people actually end up seeing the postscript BEFORE they read the body, and therefore it has a very unique position in persuasive writing."

    1. Writing for digital media likely MCQ
    2

    Why it's different: the medium is the message.

    23.1 Characteristics of the digital medium

    Characteristic Implication
    Interactive and NON-LINEAR Readers jump around, scroll, read the responses before the post
    Changed expectations More pictures, less text, more diagrams
    Immediacy Low attention spans; wants it fast, updated, changing
    Very LOW SHELF LIFE Content is gone among millions of posts. (WhatsApp at least has keyword search)
    People SCAN, they don't read "The average reader looks at a page for the first 15 seconds and decides whether to read it"
    Extremely heterogeneous audiences "In certain cases we don't even know who's reading what we post"
    Zero control over the content It can be distorted, deep-faked, forwarded
    Mobile-first "If my reader is reading this on a mobile phone, how is it going to look?" Buttons and boxes must be clickable on a phone

    23.2 Sniper vs "spray and pray" memorable MCQ

    • SNIPER approach — the precision with which we should target the audience
    • "SPRAY AND PRAY" approach — "spray buckshot into the air and hopefully a bird will fall down" — which is mostly what we're stuck with, because targeting algorithms are "still far from focused or accurate enough."

    23.3 The writing rules

    $$\boxed{\textbf{KISS} = \textbf{Keep It Short and Simple}}$$

    1. You cannot cut and paste existing material into digital media — you must hyperlink, chunk, or cut
    2. Hyperlinks: use them with care; news sites place them towards the MIDDLE of the article so the reader stays on the page
