    3. Language level: "At one time most advertising language was pegged at seventh standard level. I would maybe use standard 10 for general websites" — you can pitch higher on a niche platform
    4. Invest in the HEADLINE and SUBHEAD, then offer three or four bullet points summarising the main points so the reader can decide whether to continue
    5. CHUNKING ← guaranteed MCQ — "breaking up your text into smaller pieces rather than large walls of text" ("envision the Great Wall of China — you don't see anything"). Use shorter paragraphs, white space between them, short sentences and short text lines, and VISUAL HIERARCHIES (headings, colours) so grouped items read as related
    6. Use HIGH-QUALITY visuals — "low-quality visuals lend low credibility, low interest and low click-throughs"
    7. Review and respond to comments (e.g. hotels responding on TripAdvisor)
    8. Use the inverted pyramid and direct approach

    23.4 Microblogging (X, WhatsApp)

    • Context gets lost — threads are non-linear; so build in COMPLETENESS (one of the 7 Cs) by providing backward connection to establish context
    • Establish credibility — "it shouldn't look fake and it shouldn't look cheap"
    • HASHTAGS: be FRUGAL — "sometimes there are more hashtags than there is content… make sure the hashtag takes you to relevant content"
    • Language: semi-formal for business. "Business WhatsApp is terrible — emoticons, incomplete sentences, 'hey dude'. It does nothing for your reputation"
    • Ration your posts — "sometimes I get 15 posts from the same business every day"; send only what is genuinely useful; don't spam

    MODULE 3 — INTERPERSONAL AND GROUP COMMUNICATION

    1. Interpersonal communication — importance and issues
    3

    Why it matters more now: the trend is collaboration in small, often multicultural and geographically dispersed teams ⇒ interaction becomes more complex. Effective interactive communication ⇒ lower conflict and higher individual and company effectiveness.

    24.1 The six characteristics likely list MCQ

    1. Not communicating is NOT an option — you cannot maintain strict silence
    2. It is RISKY — "you can't take it back once you've said something"; commitments made are binding
    3. It is COMPLEX — difficult to teach and practise; involves people who are "mercurial at best", emotions, social dynamics
    4. It happens in REAL TIME — with subtext, hidden agendas, ups and downs
    5. It is CONTEXTUAL — "there is no single formula"; depends on what, when, where and with whom
    6. The RELATIONSHIP matters — especially POWER BALANCE/IMBALANCE. Power may be hierarchical, or derived from connections, knowledge, or money

    Also: socio-cultural backgrounds · emotional state · trust and willingness to share.

    24.2 Fight or flight guaranteed MCQ

    The three brain layers (innermost → outermost): $$\textbf{Reptilian brain (reflex centre)} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{Limbic brain (emotional HQ)} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{Neocortex}$$

    The reptilian brain produces the fight-or-flight survival reflex. Uncontrolled, it produces escalating conversations — "how quickly a minor incident between two vehicles becomes ROAD RAGE, then one person beating another, then a mob."

    The MIRRORING RESPONSE ← likely MCQ: "People subconsciously mirror the other person's emotional state. If you shout, I shout. If you are nice to me, I'm nice to you."

    $$\boxed{\textbf{Good communicators do NOT REACT. They RESPOND.}}$$ i.e. they curtail the fight-or-flight reflex, push the message up to the neocortex, remove the emotion, and come back with a carefully crafted response. "Count to 10."

    24.3 PERSONA (role)

    A persona is illustrated as a MASK sitting on top of your face. "All of us have multiple personae" — as children, parents, spouses, friends, advisors, consultants, and as professional managers.

    The rule: "When you are in interpersonal situations, always be conscious that your PROFESSIONAL persona is what is being used. It's not what's best for ME — it's what's best for the company, the other person, the situation."

    24.4 The Johari Window guaranteed MCQ

    Named after its two creators — JOE and HARRY ("Johari is not an Indian"). A 2×2 based on what is known/not known to yourself and known/not known to others.

    Known to SELF Not known to SELF
    Known to OTHERS 1. OPEN AREA / ARENA — common knowledge, what you share 2. BLIND SPOT — what others perceive about you that you're unaware of
    Not known to OTHERS 3. FAÇADE — what you hide; "the curtain" 4. UNKNOWN — not known to you or anyone else

    The two objectives ← the exam point: 1. MAXIMISE the OPEN AREA (by sharing more about yourself) 2. MINIMISE the BLIND SPOT — "this happens through FEEDBACK from others"; "the best way to figure this out is to do a presentation and tape yourself — you suddenly find out all the things you do which you weren't aware of."

    1. Improving interpersonal communication
    1

    Ten practices likely list MCQ 1. Be conscious you are in an interpersonal situation and don the professional persona — "like a Kathakali dancer putting on costume and makeup" 2. Respond, don't react — count to 10; "I have seen senior managers who never react in the face of grave provocation" 3. You do NOT have to respond immediately — "I'll think about it. Can I get some time?" Deferring is legitimate, in interviews and in conversation 4. Keep cool. Do NOT MIRROR aggression 5. Use your non-verbal skills to read the other person's emotional state and keep the conversation afloat 6. Evaluate PERFORMANCE, not the PERSON — "how well did this person do what he or she was supposed to do?" not "is she a good person?" 7. Be CONSISTENT — "you can't chop and change… 'Nahi nahi woh kal tha, aaj alag hai' doesn't work. It does not build trust or motivation" 8. Preserve FACE ← likely MCQ — "Easterners — Indians, Chinese, Thai, Cambodians — find it difficult to separate the individual persona from the work persona, so criticism is taken personally. Don't scold people in front of others; they lose face." Use tone and content that let them preserve their positive self-image; do not belittle people 9. Genuineness, honesty, openness — "that's what builds great companies" 10. LISTEN to what the other person is saying

    1. Giving and receiving feedback guaranteed MCQ
    4

    $$\textbf{The objective of feedback is ONE thing: to ENCOURAGE MORE EFFECTIVE BEHAVIOUR IN THE FUTURE.}$$

    "That is the ONLY objective. It's not promotion, it's not incentives, it's not 'please give 10 stars'."

    26.1 The three types of feedback ← memorise all three

    Type Purpose Frequency Direction
    FORMATIVE Encourages effective future behaviour; continuous improvement on the job Frequent, informal, continuing Both given and received
    EVALUATIVE Evaluating performance, results, outcomes; often linked to rewards and progression. (A type of formative feedback, since it also seeks improved performance) Scheduled — quarterly, half-yearly, annually, or at the end of a project/assignment Both ways
    PUNITIVE Punishment-oriented — "knocking the person on the knuckles" Rarely or never — "one should never get to that situation" One-way, normally down the hierarchy

    Critical rule for evaluative feedback: it must be done against measures known IN ADVANCE, not arbitrarily. "Not 'today I'm in a bad mood so I'll give you a bad rating'."

    26.2 The seven-step feedback flow likely MCQ (order matters)

    1. Get BUY-IN / agreement from the recipient — "this is what we are going to do and why." Without it there is no discussion
    2. State the evaluation parameter and give the feedback — based on EVIDENCE, not opinion: "looking at the statement, you got only 3 orders from the 35 clients allocated to you"
    3. Base it on OBSERVATION, not hearsay — "'Ramesh told me you waste time at the water cooler' is asking for trouble"; "I have seen from my cabin that you spend 30 minutes every two hours at the water cooler" is acceptable
    4. Check for understanding / ask for confirmation — "Are you in agreement? If there's an issue with the numbers we'll go back to source documents"
    5. Explain the IMPACT on the organisation — state the norm and the consequence. "Avoid the end-game 'we will fire you' situation"
    6. Offer SUPPORT — training, joint calls, teaming, change of territory
    7. Work out an ACTION PLAN

    26.3 Rules for giving feedback

    • It is a CONVERSATION, not a confrontation. "Keep the temperature in the room down. Leave your weapons outside the door"
    • Giving feedback is as stressful as receiving it
    • Ensure CONGRUENCE between your words and body language — "'I'm happy for you' must be said with happy eyes, a smile and a happy tonality"
    • Focus on goals, outcomes and evidence — not the person
    • Keep asking for feedback — "What do you think? Do you agree? Is there something I'm missing?"
    • Be kind and empathetic
    • Don't give feedback when it's not necessary — space it at logical breakpoints; timing is crucial

    26.4 The HAMBURGER METHOD guaranteed MCQ

    $$\textbf{COMPLIMENT} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{CRITICISM} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{COMPLIMENT}$$

    (Bread on top, meat and salad in the middle, bread at the bottom.) Intersperse and cushion the criticism with compliments rather than "beating the person up" — use both the positive and the negative.

    26.5 Receiving feedback — the three steps guaranteed MCQ

    $$\boxed{\textbf{1. RECEIVE} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{2. CLARIFY} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{3. REFLECT}}$$

    1. Receive / catch the ball — suppress the fight-or-flight reaction; listen
    2. Ask for clarity — make sure you understood; "don't jump off the cliff of conclusion or assumption"
    3. Reflect — think about it. NEVER react

    The goal: a win-win, aimed purely at positively impacting future performance — "there's no personal stuff involved."

    1. Listening guaranteed MCQ
    5

    27.1 Hearing vs listening

    HEARING LISTENING
    PHYSICAL phenomenon — perceiving sound/signals A higher-level COGNITIVE facility — perceiving, processing, interpreting, understanding and responding

    Anonymous quote given: "The biggest block to personal communication is our inability to listen intelligently, understandingly and skilfully to another person."

    "We always tend to overestimate our listening abilities. We teach people to read and write… but one of the most neglected things is our ability to listen. What we do mostly is assess hearing — as long as you are not deaf, it's okay."

    Listening effectiveness is measured by: understanding + RETENTION + RECALL. (Unlike writing, speech is ephemeral and memory fades — "if I made the same statement 20 minutes apart, they'd never be the same.")

    27.2 Causes of poor listening

    Physical barriers Psychological barriers
    Poor cell phones, poor reception, inappropriate venues, background noise, multitasking, environmental distractors 1. ANTIPATHY — a negative feeling toward the speaker; lack of credibility in them ⇒ a closed mind. "The most important one of the lot" · 2. LACK of confidence — under stress or emotionally down, listening drops · 3. OVERCONFIDENCE — egoistic, narcissistic ("main toh Sikandar hoon") · 4. Bias, prejudice, preconceived notions, a CLOSED MIND — "Bringi, whatever it is, the answer is no"

    27.3 The speech–thought speed gap guaranteed MCQ

    $$\textbf{A speaker delivers at roughly 80–120 words per minute, but the brain can process far more.}$$ (The Shatabdi Express speakers run at 120 wpm; most Westerners speak at the lower end.)

    This creates IDLE CAPACITY, which produces three listening failures: 1. Selective listening — triggered "internal television screens"/daydreaming; a single word starts an unrelated thread 2. Multitasking — sitting in a meeting while on the phone and looking at the computer 3. Evaluating the speaker's delivery — "picking holes in the way the other person speaks, the words they use, the way they look"

    27.4 ACTIVE LISTENING guaranteed MCQ

    Definition: listening that uses the idle capacity CONSTRUCTIVELY — directing spare cycles toward focus and analysis. The listener is NOT passive.

    What the active listener does: - Pays attention to the STRUCTURE of what's being said, classifying it simultaneously - Listens for the IDEA, not the words — summarising, paraphrasing and synthesising - Weighs the evidence — is this correct? relevant?

    How to do it — six behaviours likely list MCQ 1. Switch listening ON — it doesn't happen by itself; minimise distractions, choose a venue, switch off the cell phone, close the laptop 2. Face the speaker; establish eye contact 3. Use the non-verbal to understand their emotional state; EMPATHISE 4. Open your mind — no closed mind 5. Give feedback by positive signs — eye contact, gestures, asking questions — which motivates the speaker to continue and improve delivery 6. Paraphrase and acknowledge the emotion — restate in your own words, condensed; be supportive

    27.5 TING — the Chinese character for "to listen" guaranteed MCQ

    The Mandarin Chinese pictogram "TING" ("to listen") combines five elements:

    Element Meaning
    EARS Involve your ears
    MIND Engage your mind to process what the ear provides
    EYES Supplementary non-verbal input while you listen
    UNDIVIDED ATTENTION Minimise distractions; open your mind; be empathetic
    HEART Feel for the other person — look at the emotions

    1. Questions guaranteed MCQ
    4

    Vanessa Redgrave ← attribution MCQ: "Ask the right questions if you are to find the right answers." And the course adds: the right questions, at the right time, in the right place, to the right people.

    28.1 Six things questions do

    1. Obtain information or knowledge (and feedback)
    2. Maintain CONTROL — "the person who asks the question directs the flow of the discussion"
    3. Sustain a conversation — "if the discussion is flagging, throwing a question onto the table revives it"
    4. Express INTEREST — a non-verbal message that you're listening (part of active listening)
    5. Clarify
    6. Build relationships and encourage further thought

    28.2 CATEGORIES of questions guaranteed MCQ (seven)| Category | Purpose |

    |---|---| | Interrogative | Purely to gather information; in business, mild and courteous but pointed | | Recall | Designed to jog the memory — "Are you sure he was the person who took out the file?" | | Probing | Go deeper and deeper into a matter | | Clarifying | Asked to provide clarity | | Consultative | Used by advisors and consultants to ensure they've understood the issue | | Rhetorical | Asked for EFFECT, not to elicit a response — the answer is obvious. "Not much encouraged in business." Martin Luther King Jr.: "Can we really call ourselves free people when so many of our brothers still live in chains?" | | Stress | Intrude into privacy and private space, causing stress. "Not much used in business" | | Behavioural | Designed to check how you RESPOND — the intent, not the content. The classic: "Do you have any questions for us?" at the end of an interview. "Sir, will we get lunch?" vs "What are my career prospects?" reveal very different levels of thinking |

    28.3 TYPES of questions (by the response they invite) guaranteed MCQ (three)| Type | Description | Example |

    |---|---|---| | OPEN-ENDED | Routine/standard, general; the response lies completely with the respondent — they may answer any way they choose. Can be prepared for | "Tell me about yourself" — "the world's most asked question"; also a behavioural question. "How do you find the house-hunting process?" | | DIRECT / CLOSED | More probing; fewer options — typically yes/no; used to reinforce or reconfirm answers given to an open question | "Do you find house hunting a challenging activity?" | | INDIRECT | A disguised question — you embed the real question inside a different one. Used to be polite or to avoid asking directly | "What is this?" (direct) → "Do you know what this is?" (indirect). Or, instead of "Are you married?" → "Do you have any children?" — common in Eastern cultures, tied to saving face |

    28.4 The FUNNEL TECHNIQUE guaranteed MCQ

    $$\textbf{Start with OPEN-ENDED questions} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{narrow to CLOSED and PROBING questions} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{arrive at the answer}$$

    Like a laboratory funnel — wide at the top, narrowing down.

    28.5 Questioning STRATEGY

    "You can't do this on the fly." Plan: what questions, to whom, in what sequence, and what next depending on the answer — "if they respond A, what will my next question be? If B? If C?" Always ask questions of people who are IN A POSITION TO ANSWER. (Lawyers in courtrooms write questions down; PR companies anticipate press-conference questions and write the answers, sometimes asking the media to supply questions in advance.)

    28.6 Two rules for asking

    1. Keep questions SHORT — and keep the background even shorter. Long-winded introductions lose the respondent's attention
    2. A statement is NOT a question ← likely MCQ — "especially amongst us Indians, we tend to make statements rather than ask questions… 'That's great — what is your question?'"

    1. Small group meeting dynamics guaranteed MCQ
    4

    Small group communication = a gathering of a small number of people — in the organisational context, 3 to 10 — connected to each other through a common purpose, common influence, or a shared identity.

    Purposes: share ideas, solve problems, make decisions, build relationships.

    29.1 The heterogeneity insight the conceptual MCQ

    "When you look at a group as a collective noun, the typical inference is that it is a self-contained, gestaltic unit. It's not as simple as that. The individuals have not given up their individuality — they are separate actors and they are heterogeneous."

    As the number of people increases, complexity increases EXPONENTIALLY. In decision situations, every individual sees the risk differently — "how would this impact ME?"

    29.2 The SIX inhibitors of small-group effectiveness likely list MCQ| # | Inhibitor | Detail |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | Lack of clarity of objective | No terms of reference or statement of objectives. "I have seen groups three weeks into their working suddenly ask: what are we doing? why are we here?" | | 2 | Time coordination and time management | "90% of the time is wasted… what water cooler to buy can take 90% of a board meeting, and investments worth millions are passed in the last 10%" | | 3 | Dominance by some participants | People with agendas or power positions dominate, stymie the meeting, and stifle participation | | 4 | Weak facilitation | The chair/leader cannot control dominant partners, manage time, or ensure participation ⇒ meetings need to be FACILITATED | | 5 | Lack of preparation | People walk in not knowing what's being discussed, not having read the papers — which demotivates those who prepared | | 6 | Poor meeting culture and distractions | Negative/dismissive attitudes, internal strife, interruptions and side conversations stifle creativity and discourage participation |

    29.3 GROUPTHINK guaranteed MCQ

    Definition: a group-pressure phenomenon in which people don't speak up — the lack of assertiveness caused by dominant personalities or power situations. "They know the group is going wrong, but nobody wants to be the bad guy, the whistle-blower — so they go along with it."

    Consequences: the group's mental efficiency goes down · the testing of ideas and decisions gets warped · the group may lose MORAL JUDGMENT and proceed with something ethically or even legally incorrect · it increases the risk of flawed decisions.

    The antidote: assertiveness and good leadership.

    1. Managing small group meetings guaranteed MCQ
    2

    30.1 The three types of small-group meeting

    Type Definition Size / duration Objective
    DISCUSSION GROUP "A group of interdependent individuals with a common goal" meeting formally or informally to ideate, exchange views, plan, solve problems Focus group: 8–10 people, 30–60 minutes Gather feedback / views
    BRAINSTORMING SESSION Set up to leverage the synergy of a number of people 8–10 people GENERATE IDEAS — not to solve the problem
    OPERATIONAL MEETING Used in manufacturing and by project, product and software teams for daily planning, prioritisation and allocation of work; informal, peer-to-peer 5–15 minutes; STAND-UP, at the start of the shift Decide what to do today and who does what

    Terminology warning ← likely MCQ: a DISCUSSION GROUP (interdependent people with common goals inside an organisation) is NOT a GROUP DISCUSSION (individuals coming together as part of an employment/selection process — not interdependent).

    In IT, operational meetings are called SCRUM.

    30.2 The focus group

    Used in market research to collect consumer feedback, assess needs, gauge reaction to a new or existing product, or ascertain satisfaction. Activities inside: presentations, discussion, surveys, product testing/sampling ("three different juices — which do you like?"). Usually face-to-face. "An excellent way for startups to get structured information about their product prior to launch."

    30.3 Brainstorming rules — the Alex Osborn quote guaranteed MCQ

    Alex Osborn, co-founder of the American advertising agency BBDO (in India, Mudra BBDO):

    "Toss out as many ideas as possible. Don't worry if they're too crazy. Build on the ideas people generate, and don't criticise initially."

    Mechanics: ideas generated in RANDOM sequence (not round-robin) · noted verbatim or near-verbatim on flip charts/whiteboards · grouped using mind maps, flow charts or fishbone diagrams · NO JUDGMENT during the session — evaluation comes separately or at the very end. At the end: weed out the INFEASIBLE, not the unviable. "We are looking at FEASIBILITY, not viability — 'go and get titanium from the surface of the moon' is a great idea but infeasible."

    30.4 The universal three-phase rule

    "INFORMAL does NOT mean UNPLANNED." "It means the ATMOSPHERE is informal, but the meeting itself needs to be structured, planned for, and closed properly."

    Phase Activities
    PRE-meeting Emails and invitations · identify participants and get their buy-in · logistics · for a scrum, one person puts everything on the board at the end of the previous shift so no time is wasted · share information in advance
    IN-meeting Open the meeting: set ground rules, expectations, introductions, courtesies, outline the methodology, establish protocols · assign ROLES: timekeeper, scribe, moderator, chairperson · manage time · intervene if it goes off topic or gets acrimonious — "who is the referee?" · ensure the scribe captures actions and decisions
    POST-meeting Capture images of notes (brainstorming), circulate lists · calls for action — who is doing what · survey forms and closing courtesies (focus group) · adjourn, summarise and thank the participants

    1. Formal meetings and minutes guaranteed MCQ
    5

    Formal meetings have more structure, stricter rules, and are controlled by the CHAIRMAN. "The role of the chairman of a board is to manage the governance of the company, and this is done through board meetings and resolutions."

    Examples: shareholder meetings, board meetings, labour union meetings, negotiation meetings, joint parliamentary committees. In India, hybrid and video-conference meetings are now recognised by law.

    31.1 The three phases

    PRE-MEETING: an agenda note / background note containing the AGENDA, giving NOTICE of the meeting, serving as an INVITATION, and providing venue, technology and process information.

    The AGENDA defines the entire meeting — it is the guideline that must be followed. Made by the company secretary/secretary to the meeting, approved by the CHAIRMAN, then circulated. "The meeting strictly follows the agenda; there is very little scope for deviation" — any addition requires proper notice and later inclusion.

    IN-MEETING: appoint a chairperson, a secretary, and a recorder/documenter. The chairman moderates and runs the meeting — deciding how and when the agenda moves and who speaks. Formal meetings may include a VOTING process conducted by the chairperson.

    POST-MEETING: creation of the MINUTES OF MEETING.

    31.2 Minutes of meeting

    A FORMAL RECORD. "Minutes have to be filed with the Company Law Board, or with the Registrar of Societies in India, or with other regulatory bodies."

    The process: the scribe/secretary notes it down (or transcribes the audio/video) → draft sent to the CHAIRPERSON for approval → circulated to members → RATIFIED at the NEXT meeting.

    Therefore: "One of the FIRST items on the agenda of any meeting is the RATIFICATION of the minutes of the PRECEDING meeting." guaranteed MCQ Minutes are NOT internal documents — "in some ways they're almost public documents", going to regulatory bodies and government agencies.

    31.3 The FOUR types of minutes guaranteed MCQ| Type | Content |

    |---|---| | 1. VERBATIM minutes (notes of meeting) | Every single word, word for word — transcriptions of audio/video (or shorthand, like an American court recorder). Very long, very detailed, very difficult to read | | 2. NARRATIVE minutes | DESCRIPTIVE — it narrates, like a story. "The chairman called the meeting to order at 10:05. He invited speakers 4 and 43 to take the lectern and proceed with agenda number one…" A summary/précis in running text | | 3. RESOLUTION minutes (record of resolution) | Shorter — only the RESOLUTIONS. Who attended, their roles, start and end times, agenda attached, and "be it hereby resolved that… passed by a vote of seven to nine" | | 4. ACTION minutes | Lists the ACTIONS: what is to be done, who is supposed to do it, what administrative decisions were taken, and when it must be reported or completed |

    31.4 Information ALL minutes must contain likely list MCQ

    1. Title — "Minutes of Meeting held on [date] at [place] between [times]"
    2. The CHAIRPERSON in the chair / appointed
    3. List of invitees — those who participated AND those granted LEAVE OF ABSENCE (a formal requirement)
    4. COPY LIST — to whom the minutes will be sent
    5. A privacy/confidentiality section — is it confidential, restricted, eyes only, copyable?
    6. The NAME OF THE SCRIBE(S) at the end — who recorded, transcribed and finalised them, "in case there is conflict, disagreement or later legal issues"

    31.5 The writing conventions for minutes guaranteed MCQ| Attribute | Rule |

    |---|---| | Register | Formal — they are formal documents | | Tone | Neutral, impersonal, managerially appropriate | | Accuracy | Always FACTUAL | | Tense (normally) | PAST tense and PASSIVE voice — recording something that has occurred | | Tense — ACTION minutes | May use PRESENT or FUTURE tense (future events, deadlines) | | Tense — NARRATIVE minutes | Present tense | | Tense — VERBATIM minutes | Exactly as spoken — whatever tense the speaker used | | Person | THIRD person, not first |

    1. Communication in a flat world likely MCQ
    1

    Thomas Friedman, in his book The World Is Flat, argues that technological advancement and globalisation have levelled the business playing field.

    The claim: in a world where "products and services are rapidly becoming indistinguishable" and traditional sources of competitive advantage are eroding, the true differentiator is EFFECTIVE COMMUNICATION. "Communication isn't a small part of business — communication IS business, because without it there is no business."

    Communication as bridge: overcomes geographical distances, bridges cultural gaps, and enables collaboration and teamwork in decentralised environments.

    $$\boxed{\textbf{VUCA} = \textbf{Volatility · Uncertainty · Complexity · Ambiguity}}$$ In a VUCA landscape, being a skilled, versatile, adaptive and intelligent communicator is "a critical requirement for survival and growth — it IS competitive advantage."

    "Master communication and you are the master of this flat world."

    MODULE 4 — BUSINESS PRESENTATIONS

    1. Overcoming nervousness guaranteed MCQ
    2

    Public speaking is rated the NUMBER ONE issue amongst managers.

    $$\boxed{\textbf{GLOSSOPHOBIA} = \textbf{the fear of speaking}}$$

    It is OVERCOMEABLE. "It's not that you don't have the competency. Every one of us is inherently an excellent speaker. The barrier is our own CONFIDENCE and our anxiety about being judged."

    "It is OKAY to be nervous." The trick is to REDEPLOY the nervous energy and use the adrenaline boost to become more mobile and increase your delivery. "If you are not nervous, you will definitely do a bad job."

    33.1 The eight remedies likely list MCQ| # | Remedy | Detail |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | Focus on POSITIVE outcomes | "There is life after presentation. You may have a bad day, but you come back to fight another day" | | 2 | Keep it SIMPLE and SHORT (KISS) | "This doesn't have to be a Walt Disney production in 3D." Business presentations are typically 20 minutes — don't overload in content or technology | | 3 | Focus on a GOOD OPENING | "Like riding a bicycle — there's a wobble at the start, but once you have momentum, balance is perfect." Memorise, script and rehearse the first minute | | 4 | Be well PREPARED in general | | | 5 | MANAGE YOUR TIME — arrive early | Don't rush in while the previous speaker is on, struggling with laptops, USBs, projector incompatibility. Lock everything, assemble, set up, test, be familiar with the venue, the equipment and THE CLICKER | | 6 | It is NOT a test of memory | It is OKAY to use MEMORY AIDS — notes, cue cards, even a script. "The best speakers use cue cards." BUT: "the SLIDES are NOT your memory aids" ← the exam point | | 7 | Connect with the audience — talk to the FRIENDLY FACES | "If you have 15 people, you've got two friendly faces. Talk to them" — they give confidence and non-verbal encouragement | | 8 | BREATH CONTROL | "When you're nervous, your breathing becomes ragged… your mind isn't getting enough oxygen." Take deep breaths; do pranayama before the session |

    1. What a business presentation is guaranteed MCQ
    4

    Definition: a live, DUAL-CHANNEL, multimedia interaction between one or more presenters and an audience, with a clear and well-defined business objective. It is focused, planned and considered, and EXPOSITORY in nature (presenting data, inference, ideas or concepts). It usually addresses a HOMOGENEOUS audience and almost always includes a period of INTERACTION (Q&A).

    34.1 Presentation vs public speaking guaranteed MCQ| Parameter | Business presentation | Public speaking / speeches |

    |---|---|---| | Audience | HOMOGENEOUS — common ground | HETEROGENEOUS | | Objective | Clear BUSINESS objective | Other objectives — vote gathering, a morcha, art/history/science, personal or creative satisfaction | | Messaging | Focused, RATIONAL, LOW EMOTION | Wide coverage, emotional, may apply non-business logic | | Register | Formal or semi-formal | Need not be formal | | Audio-visual support | Almost invariably used (slides, video) | Typically not | | Duration | Short — typically 20 minutes max | Much longer — 30 minutes to an hour | | Interaction | Planned for and included | A broadcast — little or no interaction |

    34.2 The career statistic likely numeric MCQ

    "A survey by a major consultancy firm revealed that executives who excel in presentation skills are 50% more likely to receive a promotion than their less articulate counterparts."

    34.3 Death by PowerPoint and visual storytelling

    "Death by PowerPoint" = presentations that are dull, boring, soporific — "too many slides, too much reliance on the slides, too much material on the slide, and the slide becomes the hero."

    The trend: interesting, engaging, incorporating STORYTELLING — insight relating to the business but in story-like form, plus compelling graphics and images ⇒ VISUAL STORYTELLING.

    NANCY DUARTE ← attribution MCQ — "the person who put together the concept of visual storytelling in the business presentation milieu"; runs a very successful company producing visual storytelling for clients. "There's the VISUAL, there's the STORY, and there's the TELLING."

    Example: Al Gore's climate-change slideshow — "probably the most famous slideshow ever" — built by Al Gore and Nancy Duarte, giving rise to two documentary films.

    Second example: a cover slide of a young girl devastated by war or disaster, captioned "Profits before people" — a deliberate reversal that evokes emotion and builds a storyline.

    34.4 The FOUR Ds — the presentation lifecycle guaranteed MCQ

    $$\boxed{\textbf{1. DESIGN} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{2. DEVELOP} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{3. DELIVER} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{4. DEFEND}}$$

    Phase Content
    DESIGN Strategise and plan
    DEVELOP Build and script — words, text, images, video; both the audio AND the visual
    DELIVER Using voice and body to convey, explain and interpret
    DEFEND Managing interaction and Q&A — "the presentation AFTER the presentation"

    1. Design and planning — via the AIM model
    1

    "Failing to prepare is preparing to fail."

    35.1 AUDIENCE questions

    To whom am I presenting? What are their functions? Who are the decision makers, advisors, influencers, ECONOMIC BUYERS? Demographics and profiles ("check them on LinkedIn")? How long is the presentation? How large is the audience? What is my relationship with them? What is the common ground/common thread that makes them homogeneous?

    The presenter's power ← likely MCQ: "When you are given the floor, there is an abrogation of confidence and responsibility that the audience gives to you. You don't need to feel below the audience. When you present, you are the king — you decide what happens in that presentation."

    35.2 INTENT — the WIIFT question

    "What's in it for THEM?" — know their business problems and how your solution addresses their needs. Use the YOU approach, not the I approach ⇒ a win-win.

    35.3 MESSAGE — the planning decisions

    Decision Guidance
    Direct or indirect approach? Persuasive pitches typically take the INDIRECT approach
    Technology and A/V Projectors, screens, flip charts, whiteboards
    How much content? "MORE IS BETTER DOES NOT WORK." Limit content to the absolute bare minimum required to achieve your objective
    How many main points? THE RULE OF SEVEN ← guaranteed MCQ: "Research shows the maximum number of points an audience can handle in a 20–30 minute presentation is SEVEN."
    Structure Use the MINTO PYRAMID to plan — every box is one slide
    Filter Apply the VED filter — VITAL, ESSENTIAL, DESIRABLE guaranteed MCQ
    Collateral Plan handouts, samples. "You don't put Excel sheets on slide viewing planes — give it as a handout." Decide when to hand it out
    Time and slides "Time is always limited and slides are always too many." Subtract the interaction time first; then decide how many slides the remaining speaking time allows. Textual and visual slides take different amounts of time
    Word budget At ~100 words per minute, a 10-minute presentation ≈ 1,000 words ≈ 3–4 pages, 1.5-spaced, 12-point
    Homework Look up the 10-20-30 rule (the professor deliberately leaves it as an exercise)

    1. Developing the content guaranteed MCQ
    2

    36.1 The THREE COMMANDMENTS ← memorise verbatim

    $$\textbf{1. TELL THEM WHAT YOU'RE GOING TO TELL THEM} \quad (\text{OPENING})$$ $$\textbf{2. TELL THEM} \quad (\text{BODY})$$ $$\textbf{3. TELL THEM WHAT YOU'VE JUST TOLD THEM} \quad (\text{CLOSE})$$

    "A presentation gives you three opportunities to get your core messages across."

    36.2 Retention rates — the case for dual channel guaranteed MCQ| Input | Retention (over ~72 hours / 3 days) |

    |---|---| | AUDIO only | 10–20% (recall declines exponentially) | | VISUAL only | 30–40% | | AUDIO + VISUAL combined | 60–70% |

    Therefore: the NEED FOR SYNC — synchronisation between what is heard and what is seen.

    DISSONANCE ← likely MCQ: a lack of sync — "like a movie where the action and the sound are out of phase. The audience is unable to link what they see and what they hear, and the moment dissonance sets in, they give up."

    36.3 The OPENING

    Element Content
    Courtesies Introduction, thanks for being here, the title
    ATTENTION-GETTER A story, an intriguing remark, a statistic. ← guaranteed MCQ: "Research shows that if you don't get the audience in the first minute and a half, you're pretty much not going to get their wholehearted attention over the rest of your presentation. You have to move from zero to 100 very fast."
    Core message / purpose / PROMISE "Today I'm going to tell you how our company can increase its net profits from the current 4% to a mind-boggling 14%" — attention-getter, core message and purpose rolled into one. "You have to COMMIT yourself to the audience"

    36.4 The BODY

    Limit to 3 or 4 main points/arguments. "There may be 33 things you need to do, but pick the top 3 or 4. If you get the buy-in on that, you'll have all the time in the world to present the other 29."

    CHUNKING (Nancy Duarte's term) ← likely MCQ: for longer presentations, break the big presentation into many MINI-presentations — sections, subsections, logical breakpoints, summarisation at the end of every chunk, and an introduction and link to transition from one chunk to the next.

    36.5 The CLOSE

    "Beginnings are important because you want to get ATTENTION. Endings are important because THAT'S WHAT PEOPLE REMEMBER."

    The warning: "Speakers tend to lose tempo, energy and interest when they reach the summary. Keep your energy up."

    Elements of the close: 1. Recap and summarise the main points, messages, arguments, recommendations 2. MAKE YOUR ASK ← guaranteed MCQ — "You made the PROMISE in step one, you did your TELL in step two; in the close you make your ASK." Give me the order · what are the next steps · do I have your agreement · will you support me · the call for action 3. Craft an EXIT LINE 4. Thank them for their time, promise continued service and goodwill 5. Ask for questions — "Don't let your presentation die some weak death. Stand up and say, 'I'm sure you have questions.' Smile."

    36.6 Scripting

    Script your speech. "The words on the slide should be standalone" — and you SPEAK ABOVE THE SLIDE; the slide is one set of content, what you say is another, and together they create synergy. Tool: PowerPoint's View → Notes Page View — slide image on top, a text box for your speaking notes/script below; printable, and visible on your laptop but not to the audience.

    1. Delivery guaranteed MCQ
    4

    $$\boxed{\textbf{YOU are the presentation — NOT the slides. The hero is YOU.}}$$

    "DO NOT READ FROM THE SLIDE. SPEAK ABOVE THEM." "The audience can read the slide. You are not a reader of the slide but an INTERPRETER of the slide — the guide who takes the audience through it, tells them why it is important, points out the salient features, and guides them to the conclusion you want them to reach."

    (The green-bunnies illustration: don't read out the four points — say "from this slide you will conclude that it is more important to turn out green bunnies rather than blue bunnies.")

    37.1 Using the VOICE

    Reference: Julian Treasure's TED Talk on how to speak so that people will listen.

    (a) BREATHING — "speaking is all about breath." Use stomach / DIAPHRAGM breathing — the diaphragm separates the thoracic cavity from the abdominal cavity and pushes breath up, adding force and volume of air. (Reference: Shankar Mahadevan's "Breathless".) Pranayama helps.

    (b) SAY IT RIGHT — three components guaranteed MCQ| Component | Meaning | |---|---| | PHONEME | The basic unit of SOUND. All languages are a collection of phonemes. Problems arise because phonemes in our native languages differ from English — some sounds don't exist in one or the other. (snack vs snake — "if you pronounce snack as snake, it means something else altogether"). Remedy: a good dictionary (Merriam-Webster, Cambridge, Oxford, Random House) gives phonetic spelling, stress indicators, and an audio clip | | WORD STRESS | Changes pronunciation and therefore meaning: "I had dessert in the desert" · contest / contest · present / present — "I received a present for my presentation" | | ARTICULATION / FULLNESS | "The use of the mouth and the breath to bring out the fullness of every syllable." It slows you down but makes you sound better and more intelligible — you don't swallow sounds and syllables or let words trail off |

    (c) INTONATION and RHYTHM — languages have rhythm; stress plus intonation creates it. (The exercise: how many ways can you say "You are coming home with me today"?)

    (d) SPEED and VOLUME guaranteed MCQ

    INCREASE speed and volume ⇒ gain ATTENTION and show EXCITEMENT. DECREASE speed and volume ⇒ create DRAMA and EMPHASIS.

    MONOTONE (from mono = single, unvarying tone) puts audiences to sleep — "the Shatabdi Express people who go from Hazrat Nizamuddin to Howrah non-stop, 140 words per minute, no change, no stress, no intonation, and nobody can hear them."

    (e) PAUSING — "a technique or a weapon used by good speakers." Four benefits: 1. Regains audience attention 2. Gives the audience time to absorb, think about and reflect on what you said 3. Gives the speaker a break to take a breath 4. Creates emphasis and drama — "the moment you keep quiet even for half a second, your audience gets its antennae up"

    37.2 Using the BODY

    The four elements of body language ← likely list MCQ: posture and movement · gestures · eye contact · personality projection.

    (a) Body and gestures - Most body language reflects anxiety levels and the subconscious - Loosen up — breathing helps; stretching helps. Don't be stiff - Don't fold your hands in front of or behind you — "what you're trying to do is hide the tension leaking out of you"; avoid NCC "Saavdhaan"/"Vishram" positions - The real problem is getting STUCK or REPETITIVE ← the exam point — "the audience realises you're stuck in one position or repeating a gesture, and after some time that becomes DISTRACTING" - "If you're a Bharatanatyam dancer, fabulous — but if you're a business manager, your gestures should be MINIMAL and NATURAL" - How to fix it: practise in front of a mirror, videograph yourself, or get a trusted person to observe and give feedback

    (b) EYE CONTACT - Don't speak ABOVE the audience — "if you track where their gaze is, many speakers aren't looking at people's faces" - "Talk TO your audience, not AT them." - Talk to the friendly faces first — they build your confidence - Don't get stuck on one person — "be like a BUTTERFLY flitting from flower to flower; gently move from person to person, cover the audience with your eyes without getting stuck"

    (c) PERSONALITY PROJECTION / PRESENCE (gravitas) likely MCQ A 2×2 grid: cold ↔ warm/hostile on the X axis; assertive ↔ submissive on the Y axis.

    Target position: SOMEWHERE IN THE MIDDLE — "not hostile, not cold and distant; not too assertive and not too submissive."

    How to build it: DRESS THE PART ("it makes you look good in others' eyes AND increases your own confidence") · carry yourself well · speak in a considered, powerful manner.

    How to learn it — IMITATION before innovation ← the exam point:

    "Imitation is the first step. Don't look for innovation as a first step." Pick role models — Barack Obama, Narendra Modi, TED speakers, Steve Jobs — and pick up one or two things they do well. Reference: Carmine Gallo, who studied Steve Jobs's presentation best practices.

    Closing rule: "You need to fight for every opportunity to present and accumulate frequent speaker miles… But being practised and rehearsed is NOT OPTIONAL. Even the most experienced speakers practise and rehearse."

    1. Defence — managing the interaction guaranteed MCQ
    2

    "The presentation AFTER the presentation" — the MOMENT OF TRUTH. "That's when the rubber meets the road, when decisions are taken, when people decide whether they liked you. The proof of the presentation is in the interaction."

    What the interaction gives you: an opportunity to reinforce and clarify messages, and a way to evaluate how effective your presentation was (their questions reveal their position, understanding and misconceptions).

    38.1 When and how much time?

    • The presenter decides how the slot is split between exposition and interaction, and establishes the GROUND RULES in the OPENING: "I will speak for 10 minutes and then we will take questions at the end."
    • Typically 20–25% of available time; in some cases as much as 50%
    • Why questions come at the END ← two reasons: (1) time management can go awry — presentations get HIJACKED by mid-stream questions; (2) the question may be PREEMPTORY — you were about to cover it anyway
    • If interrupted: allow a clarification question; otherwise "That's a great question — let's hold it till the end"
    • Overrunning on Q&A is usually acceptable and not counted against you; you can ask the chairperson for more time

    38.2 Preparing for the interaction

    1. Anticipate the questions — you know where the weak points and data gaps are, and what the audience is looking for. "Some very bright presenters deliberately leave gaps because they want the question to come from the audience"
    2. Write down questions and answers — "PR companies give clients lists of questions and answers before a press conference"
    3. KNOW MORE THAN WHAT IS ON THE SLIDES — in investor pitches and sales presentations you will face interrogative and closed probing questions; otherwise "you will be shown up as shallow and inadequately prepared"
    4. Prepare BACKUP SLIDES ← guaranteed MCQ — "the last slide of the expository part is a thank-you slide. Put more detailed slides with additional information AFTER the thank-you slide." Benefits: shows preparation · gives more data · may buy you extra presentation time that isn't on your account

    38.3 Handling questions — the techniques

    Technique Detail
    REPEAT the question Because part of the audience won't have heard it
    REPHRASE / GENERALISE it ← the clever bit You can take the sting out of a question and make it more general. (The cricket example: rather than answering a specific question about "Graham in the fourth over of the third test", rephrase as "What do we do when there's a run-out on a no-ball?" so everyone can follow) · It also BUYS YOU THINK SPACE — "rephrasing provides the breathing space to think and respond appropriately"
    RESPOND, don't react Pause and think: Why this question? What should my response be? What is the audience looking for? "You can take a second or two"
    Look at the ENTIRE AUDIENCE, not the questioner "You take the question away from the person asking it and involve the rest of the audience"
    Be BRIEF and CONCISE — do NOT volunteer information "Is it a good idea to brush your teeth before bed? Yes. Zip it"
    Be TRUTHFUL — say "I don't know" "You cannot be expected to know everything about everything"

    38.4 What NOT to do likely list MCQ

    1. Don't interrupt the questioner mid-question (unless they're repeating themselves or going off topic) — "you cannot respond well unless you know the complete question"
    2. Don't lose your cool — "people will try to needle you and get you off balance." Count to 10. Keep facial, body and tonal attributes under control
    3. Don't get into an argument or disagreement — "there's a SNOWBALLING EFFECT; you can't fight a room full of people." Instead: "Good point, but I think we'll take it offline", or agree to disagree politely
    4. Don't get stuck in PRIVATE CONVERSATIONS with one questioner — "meanwhile you have lost the rest of your audience; the conversation becomes too detailed and the others are fragmented and not interested"

    38.5 Shutting down

    Gauge the mood and decide when to close. Announce it: "We've got only five minutes — I'll take three top questions" or "Can I take a last question before we break up?" — "it's a psychological thing."

    If time runs out or the discussion gets too pointed: promise a one-on-one afterwards, or promise to respond later — AND THEN ACTUALLY DO SO.

    "Otherwise you get the reputation of a person who is not sincere… and you will lose credibility as a professional."

    Finally: apologise for lack of time, or simply thank the audience and close. "After some time there is no marginal gain from the next question — you shut it down."

    The closing verdict: "A great handling of the interaction is the difference between a good and an OUTSTANDING presentation. It's not enough to put up the slides and speak well."

    Analytical Foundations for the FinTech Industry — I

    Analytical Foundations for the FinTech Industry — I

    Prof. Ananth Krishnamurthy · Beginner Module (Taught as a dialogue with two students, Tejas and Ashwini — the "gotcha" moments in those dialogues are exactly where the MCQs come from.)

    Course map Module 1 — Descriptive statistics: types of data · visualisation · central tendency · dispersion · z-scores · five-number summary & box plot · Excel ToolPak Module 2 — Basic probability: sample space, events, addition/multiplication laws, conditional probability, Bayes, independence, law of total probability · random variables · linear combinations Module 3 — Discrete distributions: Bernoulli · Binomial · Poisson Module 4 — Continuous distributions: Uniform · Exponential (memoryless) · Normal · Standard Normal · Normal approximation to the Binomial

    Exam note: since you already work in data analytics, the risk here is over-answering. This course has several deliberately counter-intuitive traps (RAM is qualitative; a table's columns are not always variables; probability at a point is zero) — those are the MCQs. Sections marked ← guaranteed MCQ are where the lecture explicitly stopped to correct a student.

    MODULE 1 — DESCRIPTIVE STATISTICS

    1. What "statistics" means
    1

    Two valid interpretations, both stated in the lecture: 1. The numerical facts computed from data — average, median, range, percentages. 2. The process of collecting, analysing, presenting and interpreting data.

    Statistics is described as both a science and an art — a science because there are fundamental principles for collecting and analysing data; an art because subtle judgement varies with the person doing it. likely MCQ Business functions that use statistics (as listed): Accounting & Finance (analyse business performance) · Economics (state of the economy, policy) · Marketing & Production (grow business, increase profit, reduce cost, improve efficiency) · Information Systems (ensure proper functioning of business systems).

    1. Core data terminology guaranteed MCQ
    1

    Term Definition In the household-survey example
    Data Facts and figures collected, analysed and summarised for presentation, interpretation and decision making —
    Data set The entire data collected for a particular study (you may use only part of it for the analysis) The whole 99acres household survey
    Element The entity for which data is collected Each household
    Variable A characteristic of interest for each element Family size, location, ownership, income, monthly payment, satisfaction
    Observation The set of measurements obtained for a specific element The entire row for one household

    MCQ trap #1 — "One number is not an observation." In the household data, "household 1's first income of ₹17 lakh" is not an observation; the whole row is.

    MCQ trap #2 (the big one) — "Just because data is arranged in rows and columns doesn't mean every column is a different variable." In Timappa's Tumkur apartment-rent table with 7 rows × 10 columns, the students said "10 variables, 7 observations". Both were wrong. There are 70 elements (70 households), 1 variable (monthly rent), and 70 observations — every single number in the table is a separate observation.

    2.1 The running examples (the course reuses these constantly)

    Person Location / firm Data
    Manjula Nayak 99acres, Bengaluru Household survey — family size, location, ownership, 1st/2nd income, monthly payment, utilities, debt, satisfaction (rating 1–5 and Likert labels)
    Timappa Tumkur Apartment rents (the 70-element trap)
    Jyothi Hegde IIIT Dharwad, head of placement & alumni relations 103 alumni — gender, age, overall score, core-course score, elective score, work experience, domicile, rating, satisfaction, salary
    Hanumantha Pai Canara Bank, Mangaluru 55 customers — annual income, household size, monthly credit-card spend
    Basavaraja Croma, Ballari 36 responses — brand, price, RAM, hard disk, OS, satisfaction score (0–100)
    Umesh Kamath Nandini Sweets, Udupi 132 weekly store-sales observations + promotions
    Vijay Gowda Magic Bricks, Mysuru 30 flats in Jayalakshmipuram — bathrooms, bedrooms, premium locality, price
    Kedar Apte Pump factory, Sangli Number of defective pumps per day
    Mangesh Nadkarni Ambedkar Intl. Airport, Sonegaon Flights departing late; passenger luggage weights
    Sanchita Shenoy PVR Cinemas KGF-2 and Kantara re-run revenues (the probability example)

    1. Types of data ← the highest-yield section in Module 1
    2

    3.1 The classification tree

    DATA
├── QUALITATIVE (categorical)
│   ├── Non-numeric ──┬── Nominal scale
│   │                 └── Ordinal scale
│   └── Numeric ──────┬── Nominal scale
│                     └── Ordinal scale
└── QUANTITATIVE (always numeric)
    ├── Interval scale
    └── Ratio scale

    3.2 The four scales of measurement guaranteed MCQ| Scale | Applies to | Property | Zero is meaningful? | Ratios meaningful? | Examples from the course |

    |---|---|---|---|---|---| | Nominal | Qualitative | Labels/names only, no ordering | — | — | Location (SW/NW/NE/SE, or coded 1/2/3/4); ownership (0=rented, 1=owned); gender; domicile (1/2); brand; operating system; store; coupons/displays/sampling (0/1) | | Ordinal | Qualitative | All nominal properties + an implicit ranking | — | — | Satisfaction (extremely dissatisfied → extremely satisfied, or coded 1–5); rating 1–5; RAM (16GB, 32GB…); hard disk size | | Interval | Quantitative | Fixed unit of measure; differences are meaningful | No | No | Time of day (2am, 4am, 6am); temperature in °C; date; GPA on a 2–10 scale; standardised test scores (SAT/GMAT starting at 200) | | Ratio | Quantitative | All interval properties + a meaningful zero, so ratios are meaningful | Yes | Yes | Income; profit; age; salary; work experience; price; household size; units sold; a score defined on 0–100 |

    The stated logic: - Interval: "the ratio of 4 a.m. over 2 a.m. does not have much relevance"; "water at 30°C is not three times as hot as water at 10°C" — zero on the Celsius scale has no meaning. - Ratio: "a business making a profit of zero is just breaking even"; "a 40-year-old is twice as old as a 20-year-old"; "a newborn with age 0 has relevance."

    3.3 The four classification traps all four are near-certain MCQs| # | Trap | Correct answer | Why |

    |---|---|---|---| | 1 | Numeric ⇒ quantitative? | No. Qualitative data can be numeric. | Coding Southwest as "1" does not make location quantitative. | | 2 | RAM (16GB, 32GB) — qualitative or quantitative? | Qualitative, numeric, ORDINAL. | It's a product attribute label with an implicit better/worse ordering. Same for hard disk size. | | 3 | Windows 10 vs Windows 11 — ordinal? | No — NOMINAL. | "It is just a later version. Not necessarily better. Would you consider your younger sibling better than you just because they are born later?" | | 4 | Test scores — interval or ratio? | Depends on whether zero is attainable. | Overall score on a 0–100 scale ⇒ ratio. Core-course and elective GPAs on a 2–10 scale ⇒ interval. SAT/GMAT starting at ~200 ⇒ interval. |

    Gender (M/W) Qualitative Non-numeric Nominal
    Age (years) Quantitative Numeric Ratio
    Overall score (0–100) Quantitative Numeric Ratio

    Variable Classification
    Brand (Lenovo, Dell…) Qualitative, non-numeric, nominal
    Price (₹) Quantitative, ratio
    RAM (GB) Qualitative, numeric, ordinal
    Hard disk (GB) Qualitative, numeric, ordinal
    Operating system (Win 10/11, Home/Pro) Qualitative, non-numeric, nominal
