    3.6 Canara Bank / Hanumantha Pai data

    Annual income · household size · monthly credit-card amount — all three are quantitative, numeric, ratio scale. No qualitative variables at all.

    1. Cross-sectional vs time-series data guaranteed MCQ
    1

    Cross-sectional Time series
    Definition Data collected at the same time (or approximately the same point in time), across many entities Data collected over several time periods
    Example Housing permits issued in each district of Karnataka in November Housing permits each month over the last 36 months

    MCQ trap: Jyothi's alumni survey covers people who graduated at different times, but the survey was conducted at a single point in time ⇒ it is cross-sectional, not time series. The student got this wrong first.

    1. Data visualisation
    3

    5.1 Tabular summaries — the three frequency distributions

    Summary Definition Formula
    Frequency distribution Tabular summary showing the number of observations in each of several non-overlapping classes count per class
    Relative frequency distribution Shows the fraction/proportion in each class frequency ÷ n
    Percent frequency distribution Shows the percentage in each class relative frequency × 100

    All three work for both categorical and quantitative data.

    5.2 Rules for forming classes with quantitative data likely MCQ

    1. Keep the class width the same for every class.
    2. The number of classes is usually 5 to 10 — not too few, not too many.
    3. Choose class limits so each data point falls into exactly one class (classes must be non-overlapping).

    (Jyothi's salary data: 7 classes of width 5 — 15–19, 20–24, 25–29, 30–34, 35–39, 40–44, 45–50.)

    5.3 Graphical displays — what to use when guaranteed MCQ| Purpose | Chart | Data type |

    |---|---|---| | Show a distribution | Bar chart / frequency chart | Categorical and quantitative | | Show a distribution (proportions) | Pie chart | Categorical only | | Show a frequency distribution over class intervals | Histogram | Quantitative only | | Compare two variables | Side-by-side bar chart or stacked bar chart | Categorical | | Compare proportions only (ignoring absolute counts) | Stacked percentage bar chart | Categorical | | Show the relationship between two quantitative variables | Scatter plot (optionally with a trend line) | Quantitative |

    5.4 Histogram vs bar/frequency chart guaranteed MCQ| | Frequency (bar) chart | Histogram |

    |---|---|---| | Bars | Separated — natural gap between categories | Adjacent rectangles touch one another — no separation | | Data | Categorical or quantitative | Quantitative | | Why use it | Simple counts | Reveals the shape/form of the distribution, in particular asymmetries (skewness) |

    5.5 Pie chart mechanics and criticism

    • Circle = 360°; each sector's angle = percent frequency × 360°. Worked example: very satisfied = 24% ⇒ 0.24 × 360 = 86.4°.
    • Sector labels may show frequency, relative frequency, or percentage frequency.
    • Stated criticism ← likely MCQ: "many data visualisation experts do not recommend pie charts" because people have difficulty perceiving differences in the areas of sectors. In most cases a bar chart is superior to a pie chart for displaying categorical data.
    • Colours, shading and legends must enhance understanding; overuse can lead to misrepresentation.

    5.6 Data dashboard

    Definition: a set of visual displays that organises and presents information making it easy to monitor the performance of a company or organisation and make fair, quick decisions.

    1. Measures of central tendency
    1

    The five measures of central tendency (location): mean, median, mode, percentiles, quartiles. The five measures of dispersion (variability): range, interquartile range, variance, standard deviation, coefficient of variation.

    MCQ trap: percentiles and quartiles are classified in this course under central tendency / location, whereas interquartile range is classified under dispersion.

    6.1 Mean

    Sample Population
    Symbol x̄ ("x bar") μ (mu)

    Formula: sum of the values ÷ number of data points. Excel: AVERAGE. The sample mean x̄ is a point estimator of the population mean μ.

    6.2 Median

    The middle value when data is arranged in ascending order. - Odd n ⇒ the middle value. - Even n ⇒ the average of the two middle observations. - Excel: MEDIAN.

    The worked demonstration ← memorise: data = 1, 1, 2, 2, 4 ⇒ mean = 2, median = 2. Change the last value to 394 ⇒ mean jumps to 80, median stays at 2. Conclusion: "the mean is very sensitive to extreme values but the median is not." This is why the median is the measure normally reported for annual income and property values — a few very large values inflate the mean.

    6.3 Mode

    The most frequently occurring value. Excel: MODE.SNGL.

    Excel behaviour trap ← likely MCQ: if all values are unique (every value has frequency 1), the MODE function in Excel returns an error (#N/A) to indicate no mode exists.

    6.4 Percentiles guaranteed MCQ on the formula

    Definition: for a data set with n observations, the p-th percentile divides the data so that approximately p% of observations are less than it and approximately (100 − p)% are greater than it.

    Locator formula: $$L_p = \frac{p}{100} \times (n+1)$$

    • If L_p is an integer ⇒ read the value directly off the ascending-ordered data.
    • If L_p is not an integer ⇒ use linear interpolation between the two neighbouring values.

    Worked example: 80th percentile of a data set with n = 200 ⇒ L_p = 0.8 × 201 = 160.8 ⇒ interpolate between the 160th and 161st values.

    Note the (n + 1), not n. Excel: PERCENTILE.EXC — the course explicitly uses the .EXC variant because it matches this formula. Other textbooks use slightly different procedures; all give similar answers for large data sets.

    Interpretation example: an applicant scores 650 on an admission test. That number alone says little — but if 650 is the 80th percentile, then ~80% of applicants scored lower and ~20% scored higher.

    6.5 Quartiles

    Quartiles are just specific percentiles — the same computation applies. They divide the data into four parts of roughly 25% each.

    Quartile Percentile
    Q1 (first quartile) 25th percentile
    Q2 (second quartile) 50th percentile = the MEDIAN
    Q3 (third quartile) 75th percentile

    6.6 Central-tendency results for the running examples ← useful for "which data set has…" questions

    Data set n Mean Median Mode Q1 Q3 Reading
    Jyothi — salary (₹ lakh) 103 25.92 25 25 24 27 Mean ≈ median ⇒ few unusual values. 80th pct = 27, 90th pct = 29
    Hanumantha — credit-card spend (₹/mo) 55 3,852 3,864 3,657 2,890 4,531 Mean ≈ median; mode differs
    Basavaraja — satisfaction score 36 57.31 59 — 53 61.75 Slight difference; some spread
    Umesh — weekly sales 132 135.98 105 91 82.25 136.5 Mean ≫ median ⇒ right-skewed, promotion spikes
    Vijay — property price (₹ lakh) 30 202.57 195 — 149.5 243.75 Mean > median ⇒ some very expensive properties pull the mean up

    1. Measures of dispersion

    7.1 Range

    Range = maximum − minimum. Excel: MAX − MIN.

    Stated weakness ← likely MCQ: "range is based on only two of the observations, therefore it is highly influenced by extreme values" — which is why it is rarely used as the only measure of variability.

    7.2 Interquartile range (IQR)

    $$\text{IQR} = Q_3 - Q_1$$ It is the range of the middle 50% of the data, and it overcomes the dependency on extreme values.

    7.3 Variance

    Key property demonstrated in the lecture: the sum of deviations about the mean, Σ(xᵢ − x̄), is always zero — by the definition of the mean. That is why we square the deviations.

    Sample variance ← the formula this course uses: $$s^2 = \frac{\sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2}{n-1}$$

    Excel: VAR.S.

    Worked example: data 1, 1, 2, 2, 4. Mean = 2. Deviations = −1, −1, 0, 0, +2 (sum = 0). Squared = 1, 1, 0, 0, 4 ⇒ Σ = 6. Variance = 6 ÷ (5 − 1) = 1.5.

    Why n − 1? The lecture defers the technical reason but notes: "dividing by n − 1 or n would not alter the estimate of the variance by much, especially if the number of observations is very large."

    7.4 Standard deviation

    $$s = \sqrt{s^2}$$ — in the worked example, √1.5 = 1.22. Excel: STDEV.S.

    Why it is preferred over variance ← likely MCQ: "the standard deviation is easier to interpret than the variance because it is measured in the same units as the data itself."

    Sample variance and sample standard deviation are point estimates of the true population variance and standard deviation.

    7.5 Coefficient of variation (CV)

    $$\text{CV} = \frac{s}{\bar{x}} \times 100\%$$ A relative measure of variability — it "puts the standard deviation into context" by expressing it relative to the mean.

    7.6 Dispersion results for the running examples

    Data set SD CV IQR Range
    Jyothi — salary 3.66 lakh 14% 27 − 24 = 3 lakh 18 → 50 lakh
    Hanumantha — credit card 1,454 0.38 (38%) 4,531 − 2,890 = 1,641 —
    Basavaraja — satisfaction 6.38 ~11% 61.75 − 53 = 8.75 42 → 66
    Umesh — weekly sales 88.36 0.65 (65%) 136.5 − 82.25 = 54.25 34 → 432
    Vijay — property price 93 lakh 46% 243.75 − 149.5 = 94.25 55 → 411 lakh

    Interpretive point made in the lecture: high variability is not automatically bad — for Umesh's sales, the spread may indicate that his promotions are creating a spike in sales.

    1. z-scores (standardisation) guaranteed MCQ
    1

    $$z_i = \frac{x_i - \bar{x}}{s}$$

    Property Value
    Also called The standardised score
    Sign Positive if xᵢ > x̄, negative if xᵢ < x̄
    Meaning How many standard deviations xᵢ is above or below the mean
    Units Dimensionless — so it can compare variables measured in totally different units (e.g. household size vs property price)
    Mean of all z-scores Always 0
    Standard deviation of all z-scores Always 1
    Excel STANDARDIZE(x, mean, standard_dev) — 3 parameters

    Outlier rule: a common practice is to treat data points with z < −3 or z > +3 as outliers (i.e. more than three standard deviations from the centre).

    Jyothi's salary example: mean 25.92, SD 3.66, min 18, max 50. z ranges from −2.17 to +6.58. Only two points exceed +3 — z = 3.3 (salary ₹38 lakh) and z = 6.58 (salary ₹50 lakh). No point falls below −3.

    1. Five-number summary and box-and-whisker plot guaranteed MCQ
    3

    9.1 The five numbers

    1. Smallest value (minimum)
    2. Q1 (first quartile)
    3. Q2 = median
    4. Q3 (third quartile)
    5. Largest value (maximum)

    9.2 Constructing the box plot

    1. Compute IQR = Q3 − Q1.
    2. Draw the box with ends at Q1 and Q3.
    3. Draw a line inside the box at the median (Q2). (Excel also marks the mean with a small cross.)
    4. Compute limits at Q1 − 1.5 × IQR and Q3 + 1.5 × IQR. Data outside these limits are considered outliers.
    5. Draw whiskers from the ends of the box out to the smallest and largest values that lie inside those limits.
    6. Mark each outlier with a small circle.

    Memorise the constant: 1.5 × IQR.

    9.3 The two outlier methods disagree — and that's the exam point guaranteed MCQ

    "Box plots provide another way to identify outliers, but they do not necessarily identify the same values as those with z-scores less than −3 or greater than +3. Either or both procedures may be used."

    Jyothi's salary data, side by side:

    Method Outliers flagged
    Box plot (1.5 × IQR; box 24–27, whiskers at 21 and 31) ₹18, 32, 35, 38 and 50 lakh — five points
    z-score ( z

    9.4 Excel's Analysis ToolPak

    To activate: File → Options → Add-Ins, then enable the Analysis ToolPak. It computes many descriptive statistics simultaneously, unlike the individual functions (AVERAGE, VAR.S, …) which compute one statistic at a time.

    9.5 Excel function summary likely MCQ| Statistic | Excel function |

    |---|---| | Mean | AVERAGE | | Median | MEDIAN | | Mode | MODE.SNGL | | Percentile | PERCENTILE.EXC | | Sample variance | VAR.S | | Sample standard deviation | STDEV.S | | z-score | STANDARDIZE(x, mean, sd) | | Range | MAX − MIN | | Normal CDF/PDF | NORM.DIST(x, μ, σ, TRUE/FALSE) | | Inverse normal | NORM.INV(probability, μ, σ) |

    MODULE 2 — BASIC PROBABILITY

    1. Fundamentals
    1

    Probability = a numerical measure of the likelihood that an event will occur, defined on a scale of 0 to 1. - 0 ⇒ highly unlikely; 1 ⇒ almost certain; 0.5 ⇒ just as likely as unlikely. - Probability can never be negative or greater than 1.

    Term Definition
    Experiment A process or procedure for which more than one outcome is possible
    Sample space (S) / state space The set of all possible outcomes
    Sample point A single experiment outcome
    Event A subset of the sample space — a collection of outcomes of interest
    Complement (Aᶜ) Everything in S not contained in A. P(Aᶜ) = 1 − P(A)
    Venn diagram Sample space = rectangle; events = circles inside it

    Two basic requirements for assigning probabilities likely MCQ 1. The probability assigned to each outcome must be between 0 and 1 inclusive. 2. The sum of the probabilities of all outcomes in S must equal 1.

    Where probabilities come from: historical data or expert judgement.

    To find P(event): identify the outcomes in that event and add their probabilities.

    10.1 The PVR Cinemas running example ← memorise these four numbers

    Sanchita Shenoy, PVR: Ugadi re-runs of KGF Chapter 2 (Nexus Mall) and Kantara (Orion Mall). - KGF revenue ∈ {3, 4, 5, 6} lakh · Kantara revenue ∈ {4, 5, 6} lakh ⇒ 12 possible outcomes (4 × 3). - A movie is a "hit" if its ticket revenue is ≥ ₹5 lakh. - A = KGF is a hit ⇒ outcomes 7–12 · B = Kantara is a hit ⇒ outcomes 2, 3, 5, 6, 8, 9, 11, 12

    Quantity Value
    P(A) — KGF is a hit 0.34
    P(Aᶜ) 1 − 0.34 = 0.66
    P(B) — Kantara is a hit 0.60
    P(Bᶜ) 0.40
    P(A ∩ B) — both hits 0.18
    P(A ∪ B) — at least one hit 0.76

    1. Laws of probability guaranteed MCQ
    3

    11.1 Union, intersection, addition law

    Concept Definition
    Union (A ∪ B) All sample points in A or B or both (includes the overlap)
    Intersection (A ∩ B) All sample points in both A and B (the overlap only)

    Addition law: $$P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B)$$ Check with the movie numbers: 0.34 + 0.60 − 0.18 = 0.76 ✓

    Two ordering facts stated explicitly: - P(A ∪ B) is at least as big as P(A) or P(B) individually. - P(A ∩ B) is less than or equal to P(A) or P(B) individually.

    11.2 Mutually exclusive events

    Two events are mutually exclusive if they have no sample points in common — if one occurs, the other cannot. In a Venn diagram the circles do not overlap.

    Then P(A ∩ B) = 0, so the addition law simplifies to: $$P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B)$$

    Example: heads and tails on a single coin toss.

    11.3 Conditional probability

    $$P(A \mid B) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)} \qquad\qquad P(B \mid A) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(A)}$$

    11.4 Multiplication law — two equivalent forms

    $$P(A \cap B) = P(A \mid B)\,P(B) \qquad = \qquad P(B \mid A)\,P(A)$$

    11.5 Bayes' theorem — prior vs posterior guaranteed MCQ

    Named after Reverend Thomas Bayes, who first used conditional probability in his research.

    Term Meaning
    Prior probability P(A) The probability of A before knowing anything about any other event
    Posterior probability P(A | B) The updated/revised probability of A given the new information that B has occurred

    Bayes' theorem links the prior to the posterior: $$P(A \mid B) = \frac{P(B \mid A)\,P(A)}{P(B)} = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)}$$

    Worked with the movie numbers ← memorise both results: - P(A | B) = P(A ∩ B)/P(B) = 0.18 / 0.60 = 0.30 (prior was 0.34 ⇒ the posterior fell) - P(B | A) = P(A ∩ B)/P(A) = 0.18 / 0.34 = 0.53 (prior was 0.60 ⇒ the posterior fell)

    Interpretation given: "Both movies being a hit at the same time is tough, as the audience gets split — so one movie's gain is the other movie's loss."

    11.6 Joint probability table

    Rows = A and Aᶜ; columns = B and Bᶜ; interior cells = joint probabilities; margins = marginal probabilities. To get P(B | A): take the joint probability in the A row and divide by the marginal probability of A in that row's margin — 0.18 / 0.34 = 0.53. The table can be extended to more than two rows/columns.

    11.7 Independence guaranteed MCQ

    Events A and B are independent if: $$P(A \mid B) = P(A) \quad\text{or equivalently}\quad P(B \mid A) = P(B)$$ Equivalently, using the multiplication law — the test for independence: $$P(A \cap B) = P(A) \times P(B)$$

    Applied to the movies: P(A) × P(B) = 0.34 × 0.60 = 0.204 ≠ 0.18 = P(A ∩ B) ⇒ the events are NOT independent.

    The critical distinction ← the classic exam question: Two events with non-zero probability cannot be both mutually exclusive AND independent. Reasoning given: if they are mutually exclusive (like heads and tails), then one occurring means the other cannot occur — that is a dependency. So mutually exclusive events with non-zero probability are always dependent.

    Mutually exclusive Independent
    Definition P(A ∩ B) = 0 P(A ∩ B) = P(A)·P(B)
    Knowing A occurred tells you… B definitely did not occur nothing about B

    11.8 Law of total probability

    Two-event version: $$P(A) = P(A \cap B) + P(A \cap B^c)$$

    Generalised version: if B₁, B₂, …, Bₙ are mutually exclusive and collectively exhaustive (their union is the entire sample space), then: $$P(A) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} P(A \cap B_i) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} P(A \mid B_i)\,P(B_i)$$

    Movie illustration: with B₁, B₂, B₃ = Kantara making 4, 5, or 6 lakh (mutually exclusive and collectively exhaustive), P(KGF is a hit) = P(A∩B₁) + P(A∩B₂) + P(A∩B₃) = 0.34 — same answer.

    MCQ trap: the generalised law requires the Bᵢ to be both mutually exclusive and collectively exhaustive. One condition alone is not enough.

    1. Random variables
    2

    12.1 Notation conventions likely MCQ

    • The random variable is a capital letter (X, Y, Z).
    • The particular value it takes is the corresponding small letter (x, y, z).
    • f(x) = probability mass function (PMF) — maps each value to its probability, i.e. P(X = x).
    • F(x) = cumulative distribution function (CDF) — P(X ≤ x).

    Why "mass" function? "Imagine we have an overall probability mass of size 1 and we need to distribute this mass among the values the random variable can take. The PMF decides how much mass is assigned to each value."

    12.2 Discrete vs continuous guaranteed MCQ| | Discrete | Continuous |

    |---|---|---| | Values | Integer values | Fractional values; a range | | Examples given | Orders from 5 sales calls (0–5) · defective radios in a shipment of 50 (0–50) · number of customers arriving at a restaurant/bank (0 → ∞) | Time between two consecutive customer arrivals at a bank · fluid in a "12 oz" can (11.5–12.5 oz) or a "750 ml" bottle (740–760 ml) · reaction temperature (100–150 °C) · ideal pizza baking temperature (275–320 °C) |

    The paired example: at a bank, the number of customers arriving is DISCRETE but the time between arrivals is CONTINUOUS — the same situation contains both types.

    12.3 Discrete random variable — the four quantities

    Quantity Definition
    PMF f(x) P(X = x)
    CDF F(x) P(X ≤ x)
    Expectation E(X) Σ x · f(x), summed over all values
    Variance Var(X) Σ (x − E(X))² · f(x), summed over all values
    Standard deviation √Var(X)

    Unbiased die: f(x) = 1/6 for x = 1…6 ⇒ F(5) = 5/6, E(X) = 3.5, Var(X) = 35/12. Biased die used in the course: P(1) = 1/3; P(2) = P(3) = P(4) = 1/6; P(5) = P(6) = 1/12.

    1. Linear combinations of random variables guaranteed MCQ on the variance rules
    5

    13.1 The four forms

    Form Expression Business meaning given
    1 Y = a₁X₁ + a₂X₂ Total cost = (unit material cost × units of material) + (unit labour cost × units of labour)
    2 Y = a₁X₁ + … + aₙXₙ Total monthly profit of a retail chain = Σ (profit per unit × units sold) across n stores
    3 Y = X₁ + … + Xₙ (all aᵢ = 1) Total monthly expense = Σ expenditures across categories (personnel, marketing, operations, admin)
    4 Y = (X₁ + … + Xₙ)/n (all aᵢ = 1/n) Average sales commission across n sales reps

    13.2 Rule 1 — linear transformation Y = aX + b

    $$E(Y) = a\,E(X) + b \qquad\qquad \boxed{\text{Var}(Y) = a^2\,\text{Var}(X)}$$

    The point the lecture hammers: b does not affect the variance. "The role of b is to shift the random variable. Merely shifting does not affect the spread. However, scaling does." guaranteed MCQ Worked example A — Celsius → Fahrenheit (the potato-fritter example) Bedekar's, Pune. Ideal frying temperature X: μ = 160 °C, σ = 5 °C (so Var = 25). F = (9/5)C + 32 ⇒ a = 9/5, b = 32. - E(Y) = (9/5)(160) + 32 = 320 °F - Var(Y) = (9/5)² × 25 = (81/25) × 25 = 81 ⇒ σ = 9 °F

    Worked example B — ₹ → $ (Nariman Point real estate) X: μ = ₹36,000/sq ft, σ = ₹9,600/sq ft. Exchange rate ₹80 = $1 ⇒ a = 1/80, b = 0. - E(Y) = 36,000/80 = $450/sq ft - Var(Y) = (1/80)² × 9,600² = 14,400 ⇒ σ = $120/sq ft

    13.3 Rule 2 — sum of two random variables

    $$E(X_1 + X_2) = E(X_1) + E(X_2)$$ $$\text{Var}(X_1 + X_2) = \text{Var}(X_1) + \text{Var}(X_2) + \underbrace{2\,\sigma_1\sigma_2\,\rho_{12}}_{\text{covariance term}}$$

    If X₁ and X₂ are independent ⇒ ρ = 0 ⇒ the covariance term vanishes ⇒ Var(X₁ + X₂) = Var(X₁) + Var(X₂).

    13.4 Rule 3 — general linear combination Y = Σ aᵢXᵢ

    $$E(Y) = \sum a_i\,E(X_i) \quad \text{(ALWAYS true — independence not required)}$$ $$\text{Var}(Y) = \sum a_i^2\,\text{Var}(X_i) \quad \text{(ONLY if the } X_i \text{ are independent)}$$

    If they are not independent, add a pairwise term for every distinct pair (i, j): 2 ρᵢⱼ σᵢ σⱼ (times aᵢaⱼ).

    MCQ trap: "Nothing changes with respect to the expectation of Y" when independence fails — only the variance formula changes.

    13.5 The sum-vs-difference trap guaranteed MCQ

    Let X₁, X₂ be independent with the same mean μ and same variance σ². Let Y₁ = X₁ + X₂ and Y₂ = X₁ − X₂.

    E Var
    Y₁ = X₁ + X₂ 2μ (1)²σ² + (1)²σ² = 2σ²
    Y₂ = X₁ − X₂ 0 (1)²σ² + (−1)²σ² = 2σ²

    The student guessed Var(Y₂) = 0 and was wrong. Variances ADD for a difference too, because the coefficient is squared: (−1)² = +1. "Both Y₁ and Y₂ have the same variance even though they have different expectations."

    Worked example — Baburao Phalke's two Nashik stores (independent): Sarafa Bazaar X: μ = 7,000, σ = 1,500 · Tibetan Market Y: μ = 8,000, σ = 2,000

    E σ
    S = X + Y (total) 15,000 √(1500² + 2000²) = 2,500
    D = X − Y (difference) −1,000 √(1500² + 2000²) = 2,500 (same!)

    13.6 Portfolio application — Shweta Kulkarni likely MCQ| Stock | Mean return | SD |

    |---|---|---| | Siemens (X) | 3% | 1.5% | | Trent (Y) | 5% | 3% | | Correlation | −0.6 | |

    Portfolio: P = wX + (1 − w)Y where w = fraction in Siemens. - E(P) = w·E(X) + (1 − w)·E(Y) - Var(P) = w²Var(X) + (1 − w)²Var(Y) + 2w(1−w)σₓσᵧρ

    w (in Siemens) E(P) SD(P) Verdict
    0.5 (50-50) 4% 1.21 Better
    0.9 (90-10) 3.2% 1.19 Dominated

    Key conclusions ← all four are exam-worthy: 1. The 50-50 portfolio's risk (1.21) is LOWER than either individual stock (1.5 and 3.0) — because the two stocks are negatively correlated. 2. The 90-10 portfolio is dominated by the 50-50 portfolio: roughly the same risk but lower return ⇒ never choose it. 3. There is no single optimal portfolio — it depends on risk appetite. After removing all dominated combinations, the remaining set gives increasing return for increasing risk. 4. These non-dominated portfolios are called Pareto optimal portfolios.

    13.7 Sum and average of n IID random variables guaranteed MCQ

    Let X₁, …, Xₙ be independent with common mean μ and variance σ².

    Expectation Variance Standard deviation
    Sum S = ΣXᵢ nμ nσ² σ√n
    Average X̄ = (1/n)ΣXᵢ μ σ²/n σ/√n

    The conceptual point: X̄ is itself a random variable — that's why it has an expectation. (If you toss a die twice and get 1 and 2, x̄ = 1.5; someone else gets 5 and 6, x̄ = 5.5. Repeat many times and you can plot the distribution of x̄, whose expected value is 3.5.)

    Worked example — luggage at Sonegaon airport Luggage weight per passenger: μ = 30 kg, σ = 3 kg. 81 passengers, independent. - E(Y) = 81 × 30 = 2,430 kg - Var(Y) = 81 × 9 = 729 ⇒ σ = 27 kg - CV = 27 / 2430 = 0.011 ≈ 1%

    Worked example — Fergusson College exam scores Score per student: μ = 70, σ = 20. 25 students, independent. X̄ = average score. - E(X̄) = 70 - Var(X̄) = 400 / 25 = 16 ⇒ σ = 4 - CV = 4 / 70 = 0.057 ≈ 5.7%

    MODULE 3 — DISCRETE PROBABILITY DISTRIBUTIONS

    1. Bernoulli random variable
    1

    Definition: a random variable with exactly two outcomes — success and failure.

    Value
    Notation Y = 1 for success (probability p); Y = 0 for failure (probability q = 1 − p)
    PMF f(y) = p if y = 1; = q if y = 0
    E(Y) p
    Var(Y) pq
    SD(Y) √(pq)

    (Note p + q = 1.)

    Derivation shown: E(Y) = 1·p + 0·q = p. Var(Y) = p(1 − p)² + q(0 − p)² = pq(p + q) = pq.

    14.1 Worked Bernoulli examples useful for quick numeric MCQs| Setting | Success defined as | p | q | E(Y) | Var(Y) = pq |

    |---|---|---|---|---|---| | Unbiased die | Roll ≤ 2 | 1/3 | 2/3 | 1/3 | 2/9 | | Biased die | Roll ≥ 5 | 1/6 | 5/6 | 1/6 | 5/36 | | Kedar Apte's pumps | ≤ 1 defective pump/day | 0.65 | 0.35 | 0.65 | 0.23 | | Nadkarni's flights | ≤ 2 late departures | 0.60 | 0.40 | 0.60 | 0.24 | | Priya Phule, insurance agent (Kolhapur) | Customer buys after a call | 0.2 | 0.8 | 0.2 | 0.16 (SD 0.4) | | Baburao Phalke, "star day" | Sarafa Bazaar sales > ₹10,000 | 0.3 | 0.7 | 0.3 | 0.21 (SD 0.46) |

    Insight to remember: any discrete random variable can be turned into a Bernoulli variable by defining a threshold for "success".

    1. Binomial random variable guaranteed MCQ
    2

    15.1 The three properties of a binomial experiment ← memorise, verbatim

    1. The experiment consists of a sequence of identical and independent trials.
    2. Each trial has exactly two outcomes — success and failure (i.e. they are Bernoulli trials).
    3. The probability of success p does NOT change from trial to trial.

    Then the binomial random variable counts the number of successes in n trials.

    In short: a binomial experiment consists of IID (identical and independently distributed) Bernoulli trials. If any one of these assumptions fails — p changes from trial to trial, or one trial's outcome depends on a previous one — it is no longer a binomial experiment.

    15.2 Formulas

    $$f(y) = \binom{n}{y} p^y (1-p)^{n-y} = \binom{n}{y} p^y q^{n-y}, \qquad \binom{n}{y} = \frac{n!}{y!\,(n-y)!}$$

    $$E(Y) = np \qquad\qquad \text{Var}(Y) = npq \qquad\qquad \text{SD}(Y) = \sqrt{npq}$$

    Interpretation of each factor likely MCQ - pʸ — the probability of getting exactly y successes - q^(n−y) — the probability that the remaining n − y trials are failures - ⁿCᵧ — the number of ways those y successes can be arranged among the n trials

    Why E(Y) = np and Var(Y) = npq are "not a coincidence": the binomial is the sum of n independent Bernoulli random variables, each with mean p and variance pq. By the linear-combination rules, the sum has mean n·p and variance n·pq. ← this connection is very likely to be tested.

    Critical point: the binomial only counts the number of successes — it does not care where in the sequence they occur. With n = 8 and y = 5, the five successes could be at the start, the end, or scattered; all such sequences count toward P(Y = 5).

    1. Poisson random variable guaranteed MCQ
    4

    Named after Baron Siméon Denis Poisson (1781–1840), a French mathematician and physicist.

    Use: estimating the number of occurrences of an event over a specified interval of time or space. Y takes values 0, 1, 2, … → ∞.

    Examples given: - Over time: customers arriving at a location in 30 minutes; phone calls arriving at a call centre in 15 minutes. - Over space: number of defects in one kilometre of highway; number of keywords on a page of text.

    16.1 Formulas

    $$f(y) = \frac{e^{-\Lambda}\,\Lambda^{y}}{y!}, \qquad y = 0, 1, 2, \dots$$

    where e ≈ 2.71828.

    $$\boxed{E(Y) = \Lambda \qquad \text{Var}(Y) = \Lambda \qquad \text{SD}(Y) = \sqrt{\Lambda}}$$

    The single most testable Poisson fact: the mean EQUALS the variance, and both equal the parameter Λ.

    Parameter count trap: the binomial needs TWO parameters (n and p); the Poisson needs only ONE (Λ). likely MCQ

    16.2 The three key properties of the Poisson random variable ← memorise

    1. The probability of occurrence of an event is the same for any two intervals of equal length. (P(arrivals in 9–10 am) = P(arrivals in 1–2 pm) — only the length matters, not when it starts.)
    2. The occurrence or non-occurrence of an event in any interval is independent of the occurrence in another interval.
    3. The mean equals the variance.

    16.3 Rate λ vs parameter Λ guaranteed MCQ

    $$\boxed{\Lambda = \lambda \times t}$$

    • small λ = the rate at which events happen (e.g. 10 customers per hour)
    • t = the length of the interval of interest
    • capital Λ = the average/expected number of events in that interval
    Interval Λ = λt Distribution
    λ = 10/hr, t = 2 hours 20 Poisson(20)
    λ = 10/hr, t = 30 min (0.5 hr) 5 Poisson(5)

    (Notation: when time-dependence matters, the random variable is written N(t) rather than Y.)

    16.4 The Poisson process

    • A stochastic process = a series of random events occurring over time.
    • A Poisson process is a stochastic process where the time intervals between successive events are independent random variables, each having the same EXPONENTIAL distribution. guaranteed MCQ
    • N(t) = number of events that have occurred by time t.

    MODULE 4 — CONTINUOUS PROBABILITY DISTRIBUTIONS

    1. Continuous random variables — fundamentals
    1

    17.1 The fundamental difference guaranteed MCQ| | Discrete | Continuous |

    |---|---|---| | f(x) is called | Probability MASS function (PMF) | Probability DENSITY function (PDF) | | Does f(x) give probability directly? | Yes | NO | | Probability is obtained by | Summing f(x) over the values | The AREA under the curve of f(x) over an interval — i.e. integrating | | Total | Σ f(x) = 1 | ∫ f(x) dx = 1 | | f(x) ≥ 0 | Yes | Yes | | P(X = a) for a single point | Can be > 0 | Always ZERO |

    The single most-tested continuous fact: for a continuous random variable, the probability of any particular single value is exactly ZERO, because the area under a curve at a point is zero. Probability is only defined for intervals.

    Consequence used in the lecture: P(Z ≤ −1) and P(Z ≥ −1) are not double-counting, because P(Z = −1) = 0. And you may write ≤ or < interchangeably.

    17.2 The four functions

    Function Discrete Continuous
    PDF/PMF f(x) f(x), with ∫f(x)dx = 1
    CDF F(x) = P(X ≤ x) Σ up to x ∫ from the minimum up to x
    Tail probability F̄(x) = P(X > x) — 1 − F(x)
    E(X) Σ x·f(x) ∫ x·f(x) dx
    Var(X) Σ (x − E(X))²·f(x) ∫ (x − E(X))²·f(x) dx

    P(a ≤ X ≤ b) = ∫ from a to b of f(x) dx = F(b) − F(a) = F̄(a) − F̄(b).

    1. Uniform distribution guaranteed MCQ on the formulas
    2

    Definition: X takes any value between a lower limit L and an upper limit U, and each value is equally likely. Equivalently — "whenever the probability is proportional to the length of the interval, the random variable is uniformly distributed."

    Quantity Formula
    PDF $f(x) = \dfrac{1}{U - L}$ for L ≤ x ≤ U; 0 elsewhere
    CDF $F(x) = \dfrac{x - L}{U - L}$
    Tail $\bar{F}(x) = \dfrac{U - x}{U - L}$
    P(a ≤ X ≤ b) $\dfrac{b - a}{U - L}$
    Expectation $E(X) = \dfrac{L + U}{2}$ — the average of the limits
    Variance $\text{Var}(X) = \dfrac{(U - L)^2}{12}$ ← memorise the 12

    18.1 Worked example — Nilesh Shah, Vadodara → Ahmedabad

    Driving time uniform on [100, 140] minutes. - E(X) = (100 + 140)/2 = 120 minutes - P(X ≤ 130) = (130 − 100)/(140 − 100) = 0.75 - P(X > 105) = (140 − 105)/(140 − 100) = 0.875 - P(X = 120) = 0 ← the trap; the student answered 0.025 and was wrong

    18.2 Worked example — Meena Desai's Samsung Galaxy battery

    Battery life uniform on [8, 12] hours. - P(X ≤ 9) = (9 − 8)/(12 − 8) = 0.25 - P(X ≥ 11) = (12 − 11)/(12 − 8) = 0.25 - 80th percentile: solve (x − 8)/(12 − 8) = 0.8 ⇒ x = 11.2 hours

    Percentile of a random variable = the inverse CDF calculation — find the x such that F(x) = the given probability.

    18.3 The discrete uniform

    The unbiased die (values 1–6, each with probability 1/6) is the discrete version of the uniform distribution — explicitly pointed out in the lecture. likely MCQ

    1. Exponential distribution guaranteed MCQ
    4

    Definition: if an event occurs randomly at a constant average rate λ per hour, then the time BETWEEN two such events is an exponential random variable, with average 1/λ.

    Key framing: the exponential is not counting events — it is measuring the TIME between events. Only defined for x ≥ 0 (positive values).

    Quantity Formula
    PDF $f(x) = \lambda e^{-\lambda x}$, x ≥ 0
    CDF $F(x) = 1 - e^{-\lambda x}$
    Tail $\bar{F}(x) = e^{-\lambda x}$
    P(a ≤ X ≤ b) $e^{-\lambda a} - e^{-\lambda b}$
    Expectation $E(X) = \dfrac{1}{\lambda}$
    Variance $\text{Var}(X) = \dfrac{1}{\lambda^2}$
    Standard deviation $\dfrac{1}{\lambda}$ — equal to the mean

    19.1 The three properties

    1. Takes only positive values (it is a time between events).
    2. Mean = standard deviation (both 1/λ). guaranteed MCQ
    3. The PDF is skewed to the RIGHT — it is not symmetric about the mean.
    4. It has the memoryless property.

    Mean-variance comparison trap ← the classic: - Poisson: mean = VARIANCE = Λ - Exponential: mean = STANDARD DEVIATION = 1/λ These are different statements. Get them the wrong way round and the question is lost.

    19.2 Applications listed

    Time to complete manual tasks (assembling a product; duration of a call answered by an agent) · lifetime of equipment (a bulb, electronic equipment) · time before a component requires repair or maintenance · waiting time of a customer in a queue.

    19.3 The memoryless property guaranteed MCQ

    Statement: let X ~ Exponential(λ). Given that X > x₀ (the event has not yet occurred by time x₀), define Y = X − x₀ = the remaining time. Then Y has the SAME exponential distribution with the same mean 1/λ.

    In words: "the process seems to forget that a time x₀ has already elapsed and acts as though it is starting fresh at time 0."

    Proof shown: $$P(Y > y) = P(X > x_0 + y \mid X > x_0) = \frac{e^{-\lambda(x_0 + y)}}{e^{-\lambda x_0}} = e^{-\lambda y}$$ — identical to the original tail probability. ∎

    19.4 The exponential–Poisson connection guaranteed MCQ

    $$\textbf{Gap between events is EXPONENTIAL} \iff \textbf{Counting process is POISSON}$$

    Gap between events Counting process
    Deterministic (e.g. exactly every 10 min) Deterministic — exactly 6 events in 60 min, no more, no less
    Exponential (mean 10 min) Poisson with Λ = 6 events in 60 min — the number is now random

    Worked example — Shivali Patil, PWD, Maharashtra State Highway 3 (Solapur–Nanded) Defects occur as a Poisson process with Λ = 3 defects per kilometre. - ⇒ The distance between two defects is exponential with λ = 3 per km, mean = 1/3 ≈ 0.333 km. - P(a stretch of at least 0.5 km with no defects) = e^(−3 × 0.5) = 0.223 - P(a stretch of at least 2 km with no defects) = e^(−3 × 2) = 0.002

    1. Normal distribution guaranteed MCQ
    3

    Attribution: credited to Johann Carl Friedrich Gauss (German mathematician & physicist — hence "Gaussian distribution") and Abraham de Moivre (French mathematician, known for de Moivre's formula and his work in The Doctrine of Chances).

    Why it's called "normal" and not by their names: because it models the randomness in many normally occurring phenomena. Also known as the bell-shaped curve**.

    Applications listed: height/weight of a sample of people · test scores · scientific measurements and experiments · rainfall and weather · and above all statistical inference (making inferences about a population from a sample).

    20.1 PDF

    $$f(x) = \frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\; e^{-\frac{(x-\mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}}$$

    20.2 The properties ← every one of these is exam-worthy

    1. The entire family of normal distributions is determined by exactly two parameters: μ (mean) and σ (standard deviation).
    2. The highest point is at the mean, which is also the median AND the mode. guaranteed MCQ
    3. The mean can be any numerical value — negative, zero, or positive; X ranges from −∞ to +∞. (Contrast: the exponential is defined only for x ≥ 0.)
    4. It is symmetric — the curve to the left of the mean is a mirror image of the curve to the right. Skewness = 0.
    5. Both tails extend to infinity and theoretically never touch the horizontal axis.
    6. σ determines flatness/width: larger σ ⇒ wider, flatter curve (more variability); smaller σ ⇒ skinnier, taller curve (less variability). The mean determines location, not shape.
    7. Total area under the curve = 1; area to the left of the mean = 0.5; area to the right = 0.5.

    20.3 The empirical rule guaranteed MCQ — note the exact figures used| Interval | Probability |

    |---|---| | μ ± 1σ | 0.683 (68.3%) | | μ ± 2σ | 0.954 (95.4%) | | μ ± 3σ | 0.997 (99.7%) |

    (This course uses 68.3 / 95.4 / 99.7, not the rounded 68/95/99.7.)

    1. Standard normal distribution
    3

    Definition: a normal random variable with μ = 0 and σ = 1. Denoted by Z (not X).

    $$f(z) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,e^{-z^2/2}$$

    Notation note: many books use φ (small phi) for the PDF and Φ (capital phi) for the CDF of the standard normal, instead of f and F — "the standard normal is a special random variable and it does deserve special treatment."

    21.1 The standardisation formula guaranteed MCQ

    $$\boxed{Z = \frac{X - \mu}{\sigma}}$$

    If X = Then Z =
    μ 0
    μ + σ +1
    μ − σ −1

    Interpretation: Z is the number of standard deviations that X is away from its mean — positive above the mean, negative below.

    Why it matters: "the probabilities for ALL normal distributions, regardless of μ and σ, can be computed using the standard normal distribution." The lecture's analogy: converting Celsius to Fahrenheit, or dollars to rupees — convert, solve, convert back.

    Worked conversion: X ~ Normal(μ = 10, σ = 2). P(10 ≤ X ≤ 14) ⇒ z goes from (10−10)/2 = 0 to (14−10)/2 = 2 ⇒ P(0 ≤ Z ≤ 2) = 0.4772.

    21.2 Critical values Z_{α/2} guaranteed MCQ — memorise the four values

    Definition: for a given α, Z_{α/2} is the value such that the tail probability to its right equals α/2; symmetrically, the cumulative probability to the left of −Z_{α/2} equals α/2. Therefore: $$P(-Z_{\alpha/2} \le Z \le Z_{\alpha/2}) = 1 - \alpha$$

    1 − α α/2 Z_{α/2}
    0.68 0.16 1.00
    0.80 0.10 1.28
    0.90 0.05 1.64
    0.95 0.025 1.96
    0.99 0.005 2.58

    These are the numbers used in every confidence-interval and hypothesis-test question later. 1.96 ↔ 95% and 2.58 ↔ 99% are the two most commonly tested.

    21.3 Excel functions for the normal

    Purpose Function Parameters
    Cumulative probability P(X ≤ x), or the PDF value NORM.DIST(x, μ, σ, toggle) 4 parameters. toggle = TRUE ⇒ cumulative probability; toggle = FALSE ⇒ PDF value likely MCQ
    Inverse — find x given a probability NORM.INV(probability, μ, σ) 3 parameters; probability must be between 0 and 1

    Two question types the course says you will always face: 1. Given z, find the area/probability (forward). 2. Given the area/probability, find z (inverse).

    Stated tip: always sketch the standard-normal curve and shade the required area — it makes the correct calculation obvious.

    21.4 Reference values from the worked examples

    Query Answer
    P(Z ≤ −1) 0.159
    P(Z ≥ −1) 0.841
    P(Z ≥ −1.5) 0.933
    P(Z ≥ −2.5) 0.994
    P(−3 < Z ≤ 0) 0.499
    P(0 ≤ Z ≤ 0.8) 0.288
    P(−1.5 ≤ Z ≤ 0) 0.433
    P(Z ≥ 0.4) 0.345
    P(Z ≥ −0.2) 0.579
    P(Z ≤ 1.2) 0.885
    P(Z ≤ −0.7) 0.242
    Area to the left of z = 0.21 ⇒ z = −0.806
    Area between −z and +z = 0.9 ⇒ z = ±1.64
    Area between −z and +z = 0.2 ⇒ z = ±0.25
    Area to the left of z = 0.955 ⇒ z = 2.58†
    Area to the right of z = 0.692 ⇒ z = −0.502

    †(as read out in the lecture)

    1. Normal approximation to the binomial guaranteed MCQ
    4

    22.1 Why do it

    The binomial PMF contains ⁿCₓ, which requires factorial computations — challenging when n is very large (thousands). The normal probabilities are easy to compute, so we use the normal to approximate the binomial. (The lecture notes that software may itself be using this approximation internally.)

    Motivation: the binomial PMF is by and large bell-shaped, similar in shape to the normal PDF.

    22.2 The conditions ← memorise both

    $$\boxed{np \ge 5 \quad\text{AND}\quad nq \ge 5}$$

    i.e. n is reasonably large and p is not very close to 0 or 1.

    22.3 The procedure

    Step 1 — match the mean and variance: $$\mu = np \qquad \sigma^2 = npq$$

    Step 2 — apply the continuity correction (because you are approximating a discrete distribution with a continuous one).

    22.4 The continuity correction rules guaranteed MCQ| You want | Approximate with |

    |---|---| | P(X ≤ a) — cumulative | P(Y ≤ a + 0.5) — increase the upper boundary by 0.5 | | P(X ≥ a) — tail | P(Y ≥ a − 0.5) — decrease the lower boundary by 0.5 | | P(a ≤ X ≤ b) — interval | P(a − 0.5 ≤ Y ≤ b + 0.5) — do both | | P(X = a) — exact value | P(a − 0.5 ≤ Y ≤ a + 0.5) |

    22.5 Worked examples

    Example A — 16 tosses of a fair coin. n = 16, p = 0.5 ⇒ μ = 8, σ² = 4 (σ = 2).

    Query Exact binomial Normal approximation Correction applied
    P(X ≤ 5) 0.105 P(Y ≤ 5.5) = 0.1056 +0.5 on the upper bound
    P(8 ≤ X ≤ 11) 0.5598 P(7.5 ≤ Y ≤ 11.5) = 0.5586 −0.5 lower, +0.5 upper

    Example B — Kunal Poonawalla, Junagadh: invoice errors. 10% of invoices have errors; sample of n = 100, p = 0.1 ⇒ μ = 10, σ² = 9 (σ = 3).

    Query Exact binomial Normal approximation
    P(X = 12) 0.09 P(11.5 ≤ Y ≤ 12.5) = 0.1052
    P(X ≤ 13) 0.876 P(Y ≤ 13.5) = 0.879

    Irony noted in the lecture: here "success" means an invoice with an error — a reminder that "success" in a Bernoulli/binomial setting is just a label for the outcome being counted, not a value judgement.

    22.6 Normal approximation to the Poisson

    The normal is also used to approximate the Poisson — because the Poisson PDF contains an e^(−Λ) term which is also hard to compute for large Λ.

    The chain of approximations likely MCQ - Poisson mean = variance = Λ. - Binomial mean = np, variance = npq. - If q is small, then npq ≈ np ⇒ the binomial's mean and variance are nearly equal ⇒ the Poisson can be approximated by the binomial. - And since the binomial can be approximated by the normal, the Poisson can in turn be approximated by the normal.

    22.7 Forward reference — the Central Limit Theorem

    The lecture flags the Central Limit Theorem (CLT) for a later module: the normal distribution provides a good approximation for the distribution of the average of a set of identically distributed random variables, irrespective of the distribution of the individual random variables. likely MCQ even at this stage

    z-score formula (xᵢ − x̄) / s
    Mean and SD of z-scores 0 and 1, always
    z-score outlier rule z < −3 or z > +3

    Five-number summary Min · Q1 · Median(Q2) · Q3 · Max

    Excel bundle for many statistics at once Analysis ToolPak (File → Options → Add-Ins)
    Probability range 0 to 1; never negative or > 1
    Sample space / sample point / event All outcomes · one outcome · a subset of S
    Two requirements for assigning probabilities Each between 0 and 1; they sum to 1
    Addition law P(A∪B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A∩B)
    Mutually exclusive ⇒ P(A∩B) = 0, so P(A∪B) = P(A) + P(B)
    Conditional probability P(A|B) = P(A∩B) / P(B)
    Multiplication law P(A∩B) = P(A|B)P(B) = P(B|A)P(A)
    Bayes — named after Reverend Thomas Bayes
    Prior vs posterior Before new information vs revised after knowing B occurred
    Test for independence P(A∩B) = P(A)·P(B)
    Can two non-zero-probability events be both mutually exclusive and independent? NO
    Law of total probability (general) requires B_i to be… Mutually exclusive AND collectively exhaustive
    PVR movie numbers P(A)=0.34 · P(B)=0.60 · P(A∩B)=0.18 · P(A∪B)=0.76 · P(A|B)=0.30 · P(B|A)=0.53
    PMF vs CDF notation f(x) = P(X = x) vs F(x) = P(X ≤ x)
    Bank example: discrete and continuous Number of customers = discrete; time between arrivals = continuous
    E(aX + b) and Var(aX + b) aE(X) + b and a²Var(X) — b does not affect variance
    Var(X₁ − X₂) for independent X Var(X₁) + Var(X₂) — variances ADD ← trap
    Celsius→Fahrenheit fritters μ 160→320 °F; σ 5→9 °F
    ₹→$ Nariman Point μ 36,000→$450; σ 9,600→$120
    Portfolio: 50-50 Siemens/Trent E = 4%, SD = 1.21 — lower than either stock, because ρ = −0.6
    A portfolio with same risk but lower return is… Dominated; the survivors are Pareto optimal
    Sum of n IID: E and Var nμ and nσ²
    Average of n IID: E and Var μ and σ²/n (SD = σ/√n)
    Luggage example 81 passengers, μ30/σ3 ⇒ E 2,430, SD 27, CV ~1%
    Bernoulli E and Var p and pq
    Three properties of a binomial experiment Identical & independent trials · exactly two outcomes · p constant across trials
    Binomial PMF ⁿCᵧ pʸ q^(n−y)
    Binomial E and Var np and npq
    Binomial = sum of… n IID Bernoulli random variables
    Poisson — named after Baron Siméon Denis Poisson (1781–1840), French
    Poisson PMF e^(−Λ) Λ^y / y!
    Poisson E and Var Both = Λ
    Number of parameters: binomial vs Poisson 2 (n, p) vs 1 (Λ)
    Λ = λ × t (rate × interval length)
    Poisson's three properties Equal-length intervals equally likely · independence across intervals · mean = variance
    Poisson process ⇒ inter-event times are… Exponential
    For a continuous RV, P(X = a) = 0, always
    Uniform PDF / E / Var 1/(U−L) · (L+U)/2 · (U−L)²/12
    Nilesh Shah drive [100,140] E = 120; P(≤130) = 0.75; P(>105) = 0.875; P(=120) = 0
    Meena's battery [8,12] P(≤9) = 0.25; P(≥11) = 0.25; 80th pct = 11.2 h
    Discrete uniform example The unbiased die
    Exponential PDF / CDF / tail λe^(−λx) · 1 − e^(−λx) · e^(−λx)
    Exponential E / Var / SD 1/λ · 1/λ² · 1/λ
    Exponential: mean equals… The standard deviation (not the variance)
    Exponential shape Right-skewed, defined only for x ≥ 0
    Memoryless property Remaining time has the same exponential distribution — the process "forgets" elapsed time
    Highway defects, Λ = 3/km Mean gap 0.333 km; P(≥0.5 km clear) = 0.223; P(≥2 km clear) = 0.002
    Normal — credited to Gauss (⇒ Gaussian) and de Moivre (The Doctrine of Chances)
    Normal: mean, median, mode All equal, at the peak
    Normal parameters Exactly two: μ and σ
    Normal skewness 0 (symmetric)
    Larger σ ⇒ Wider and flatter curve
    Empirical rule ±1σ → 68.3%; ±2σ → 95.4%; ±3σ → 99.7%
    Standard normal μ = 0, σ = 1, denoted Z; PDF/CDF often written φ / Φ
    Standardisation formula Z = (X − μ)/σ
    Z_{α/2} for 90% / 95% / 99% 1.64 · 1.96 · 2.58 (and 1.28 for 80%)
    NORM.DIST toggle TRUE ⇒ cumulative; FALSE ⇒ PDF value
    NORM.INV parameters probability, μ, σ (3)
    Normal-approximation-to-binomial conditions np ≥ 5 AND nq ≥ 5
    Match what, when approximating? The mean (np) and the variance (npq)
    Continuity correction — CDF / tail / interval +0.5 upper · −0.5 lower · both
    16 fair coin tosses: P(X ≤ 5) Exact 0.105 vs normal P(Y ≤ 5.5) = 0.1056
    Junagadh invoices, n=100, p=0.1 μ 10, σ 3; P(X=12) exact 0.09, normal P(11.5≤Y≤12.5) = 0.1052
    Why can the Poisson be approximated by the binomial? If q is small, npq ≈ np ⇒ binomial mean ≈ variance, like the Poisson
    Central Limit Theorem (previewed) The average of IID random variables is approximately normal, regardless of the individual distribution
