    PART A — INDIA MACRO AND THE FINTECH OPPORTUNITY

    1. India's macroeconomic trajectory guaranteed MCQ on the numbers
    1

    Metric Today By 2030
    Nominal GDP $4 trillion $7 trillion — the THIRD largest economy in the world
    Nominal GDP growth 9–10% p.a. sustained
    Population ~1.45 billion ~1.5 billion
    Higher-education enrolments ~50 million ~70 million
    Literacy rate — close to 100%
    Per capita GDP ~$2,700 ~$4,700
    Per capita disposable income ~$2,000 ~$4,000 (almost doubling)
    Urbanisation 37% 40%
    Share in world exports — 4.5% (driven by China+1 and manufacturing)

    Vision: Viksit Bharat by 2047, India's 100th Independence Day.

    1.1 The consumption story

    • Private household consumption has always been ~65% of India's GDP — expected to reach ~70%, growing at 9–10% p.a.
    • Households (~330 million) are split into high income · upper middle income · lower middle income · lower income. A high-income household ≈ ₹35 lakh annual income.
    • The top two segments (high + upper-middle) go from ~40% of households to ~55–56%.

    The shape change ← guaranteed MCQ: India's affluence/consumption structure moves from a PYRAMID (heavy at the base) to a DIAMOND (heavy in the middle) — i.e. from a predominantly lower-class economy to a predominantly middle-class economy.

    Four growth drivers: (1) population growth and more households · (2) growth in disposable income · (3) rise in working-age population (India will have the highest share of working-age population of any major country even in 2030) · (4) increasing urbanisation.

    The one-line summary: "Younger, more educated, more affluent, more urban."

    1. Credit penetration guaranteed MCQ
    3

    Metric Value
    India's total consumption $2.4 trillion
    Overall financing (credit) penetration of consumption ~10%
    Household debt as % of GDP ~30–40% — a significant gap vs both emerging and developed economies
    Formal credit (scheduled commercial banks) as % of GDP, FY23 ~50%
    Target for a $7 trillion economy 70–75% of GDP
    Required credit growth 15–20% p.a. to support 10% p.a. GDP growth

    Where credit penetration is high: home purchases and personal vehicle purchases (most are financed), plus consumer durables (thanks to NBFCs like Bajaj Finance). Where it is low (low single digits): travel, education, fashion and lifestyle — i.e. "living expenditure" categories.

    2.1 MSMEs guaranteed MCQ on the percentages| Fact | Value |

    |---|---| | Expected GVA growth | 10–15% p.a., reaching $2–2.5 trillion GVA by 2030 | | Share of workforce employed | at least 20% | | Share of India's EXPORTS | 45% | | Share of MANUFACTURING output | 45% | | Inclusivity | Critical in semi-urban/rural areas and women-led enterprises |

    The problem: MSMEs rely on informal sources (local money lenders) at very high interest rates. Fintechs are needed for working capital loans and capital expenditure loans.

    2.2 The consumer credit demand–supply gap likely MCQ| Metric | Value |

    |---|---| | Total credit DEMAND | $410–420 billion | | Formal credit SUPPLY | ~$200 billion ⇒ a ~50% demand–supply gap | | Supply split | 80% banks : 20% NBFCs | | Penetration by income | High income ~90–95% · upper middle ~70% · lower middle ~40% |

    (Banks target higher credit-worthiness segments; NBFCs the mid-tier and lower.)

    2.3 The digital readiness data points

    Internet base ~850–900 million · twice the data consumption of the US · banking coverage of adults went from 20% in 2009 → 90–95% (thanks to Jan Dhan + India Stack) · ~350 million active UPI users, 500–600 million acquired · ~300 million online shoppers (2023) · 200+ digital insurgent brands funded in the last decade · organised/branded retail grew 2–3× faster than unorganised · digital ad spends ≈ 60% of overall ad spend.

    Why FinTech exists ← the conceptual MCQ: banking growth used to depend on physical distribution (branches, business correspondents, feet-on-street). The deeper you go into tier 2–5 cities, the more you need a low-cost DIGITAL distribution capability — "only then can we have viable business models in financial services."

    1. India Stack — the five layers guaranteed MCQ
    1

    # Layer What it is Examples / scale
    1 PRESENCE-LESS layer (identity) — the foundational layer Unique digital identity issued to everyone Aadhaar — 1.3–1.4 billion holders
    2 PAPERLESS layer Digitisation of customer onboarding and personal/identity documents e-KYC, DigiLocker — 100+ billion Aadhaar-based verifications
    3 CASHLESS layer The payments ecosystem riding on top UPI, RuPay — all run by NPCI
    4 CONSENT layer Consent framework for sharing personal data — India's open banking The Account Aggregator framework under Sahamati
    5 COMMERCE layer (newest) Open digital commerce ONDC — Open Network for Digital Commerce

    Cross-cutting enabler: AI — $250 million+ earmarked for AI-related startups, an indigenous LLM, and ~6,200 recognised AI startups in India.

    The three-part frame ← likely MCQ: DEMAND (consumption) → INFRASTRUCTURE (India Stack + AI) → SUPPLY (what fintechs provide).

    3.1 The four reasons to be bullish on India FinTech

    1. Demand — younger, more educated, more aware, more affluent
    2. Infrastructure — India Stack, the AI mission, the technology to connect demand and supply
    3. Supply — low penetration across financial products ⇒ need for democratisation
    4. Correlation — a strong link between financial product penetration/usage and overall economic growth

    PART B — THE EVOLUTION OF FINTECH IN INDIA

    1. Scale of the Indian FinTech ecosystem guaranteed MCQ
    3

    Metric Value
    FinTechs founded in India ~14,600
    Unicorns among them 17 (including one decacorn), plus many "soonicorns"
    Cumulative FinTech funding ~$39 billion (over ~7–8 years)
    Funding in 2021 (the peak) ~$9 billion
    Funding in 2024 ~$3 billion
    India's share of GLOBAL FinTech funding 2.2% in 2018 → ~6% now
    India's global rank THIRD in both number of FinTech startups and number of FinTech unicorns — behind the US and UK

    4.1 Which segment led funding when likely MCQ

    $$\textbf{2018: PAYMENTS} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{2021: WEALTHTECH} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{2024: LENDING} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{next expected: INSURTECH}$$

    Maturity ranking: payments is by far the most evolved (because of UPI), then wealthtech (peaked 2020–22 with retail investors going digital), now lending is attracting investor interest.

    (Funding fell from the 2021 peak because capital became more expensive and investors now look for solid financials and a path to profitability.)

    4.2 The five drivers of FinTech funding (from a CXO/investor survey)

    1. Strong macro fundamentals
    2. Consumers demanding better EXPERIENCES — "7–10 years ago, opening a bank account, transferring money or getting a loan was difficult and paperwork-heavy; in 2025 all of it can be done in seconds"
    3. Tech and DATA capability — reimagined user journeys plus data-driven insight, cross-sell and analytics
    4. Regulatory push helping digitisation, with clearer FinTech-specific rules
    5. Monetisation shift from BAD PROFITS to GOOD PROFITS ← guaranteed MCQ — "bad profits" = late fees, minimum-balance fees, rewards that are hard to redeem. "Ideally you should send a reminder so the customer pays on time — but providers didn't, because they wanted to make money off it." Fintechs introduced transparency, and incumbents followed.

    4.3 The unicorn timeline guaranteed MCQ| Year | New FinTech unicorns |

    |---|---| | 2018 and before | Policybazaar, Paytm (2 unicorns total) | | 2019 | BillDesk, PhonePe | | 2020 | Zerodha, Razorpay, Pine Labs | | Last ~3 years | 20+ fintechs achieved unicorn status |

    (Later unicorns named: CRED, BharatPe, Chargebee, Perfios, Oxyzo, OneCard, CoinDCX, CoinSwitch, OfBusiness, ACKO, Moneyview, InCred.)

    1. The SIX growth strategies of scaled fintechs guaranteed MCQ
    1

    # Strategy Illustration
    1 Razor-sharp SEGMENT FOCUS Razorpay → the online SME merchant (India's Stripe). CRED → Gen Y users with a high credit score, affluent, credit-card holders
    2 Start with a HOOK, then expand into CORE ADJACENCIES CRED's hook = credit-card bill payments in one place. Razorpay's hook = an easy payment gateway for SMEs, then value-added services, working capital lending, payroll. Groww started as a mutual fund platform, added direct equity, gold, ETFs
    3 Drive ENGAGEMENT The metrics that matter are MAU (monthly active users), DAU, monthly transacting users — not total downloads. Engagement hooks: showing your credit score free, showing portfolio returns, gamified rewards (CRED)
    4 Increase CUSTOMER LIFETIME VALUE / ARPU Organic expansion into new financial-services segments — PhonePe from payments into insurance distribution and lending; CRED from card management into lending, an e-commerce store, offline payments
    5 Strategic M&A Especially in the last 3 years — acquiring for market reach/access or product capability rather than building organically
    6 PARTNERSHIPS with incumbents Funding, technology access, or unique capability (e.g. underwriting). "Ideally you want an ecosystem where incumbents coexist with insurgents."

    Full-stack examples: PhonePe — TPAP payments + acquiring (POS, payment gateway) + insurance distribution + investments + an account aggregator licence + prepaid/gift cards + PhonePe Switch (mini-apps) ⇒ a "super app" / ecosystem play. Razorpay — payment gateway → large merchants → loyalty and analytics → payouts (vendor and tax payments) → payroll → neobanking (current accounts for SMEs) → corporate cards.

    1. The FOUR responses of incumbents guaranteed MCQ
    1

    # Theme Examples
    1 Build their own DIGITAL ATTACKER to transform the core and compete DBS launched digibank (a digital-only India entry; later acquired Lakshmi Vilas Bank); HDFC Bank launched PayZapp
    2 PARTNER with FinTech insurgents to widen distribution Especially important for regional and tier-2 banks without big physical infrastructure
    3 Go beyond banking to ONE-STOP SUPER APPS / broader ecosystems Kotak Mahindra's KayMall (e-commerce); ICICI's iMobile Pay (bank-agnostic payments and lifestyle app); HDFC's PayZapp
    4 Double down on select VERTICAL ECOSYSTEMS Agriculture — SBI's Yono Krishi (built via agritech partnerships); also Bank of Baroda and ICICI. Plus health ecosystems (insurers + hospitals + pharmacies) and education (schools, colleges, students, financiers)

    Bank–FinTech partnership examples: Federal Bank and IDFC First built portfolios of partnerships across neobanks, secured lending, remittances, UPI payments, BNPL, credit cards and wealth/investing — accelerating growth without building products and distribution themselves.

    What banks want from fintechs: better customer experience and acquisition through technology, and access to ALTERNATE DATA for acquisition and underwriting. (Traditional underwriting data = bureau score, bank statement data, GST information.)

    1. The 20 themes shaping India FinTech's future guaranteed MCQ (know the segment each belongs to)
    2

    7.1 PAYMENTS — 4 themes

    Theme Detail Players
    1. Account-to-account real-time payments (UPI) Driven by TPAPs — Third Party Application Providers PhonePe, Google Pay, Paytm
    2. Payment acquiring (accepting payments) ONLINE ⇒ payment gateway aggregators holding a PA-PG licence (Payment Aggregator / Payment Gateway). OFFLINE ⇒ QR players and POS/device players Online: BillDesk, PayU, Razorpay, JusPay. Offline: Pine Labs, BharatPe, PhonePe, Mswipe, Innoviti
    3. Cross-border payments More nascent; cross-border has been slow, expensive and non-transparent Tazapay, Skydo, PayGlocal
    4. Pay-later / consumption financing at point of sale EMI options on POS; credit line on UPI; financing embedded in the purchase journey Pine Labs (offline EMI), Razorpay, PayU (online checkout), Amazon

    7.2 LENDING — 6 themes

    Theme Detail Players
    1. Digital consumer lending Unsecured personal loans — no collateral; weddings, travel, emergencies Moneyview, KreditBee, Kissht
    2. Collateralised / asset-backed lending Home, car, gold loans — digitising the journey Rupeek (gold), Cars24, CarDekho (auto)
    3. Unsecured MSME lending The shift from collateralised to unsecured via transaction-based / cash-flow-based underwriting — heavily analytics-driven BharatPe, Paytm (merchant cash advances), Lendingkart, FlexiLoans (+ supply chain finance), Credlix (invoice discounting)
    4. Loan aggregators / marketplaces Discovery platforms — compare offers across providers Paisabazaar
    5. DeFi (decentralised finance) Powered by blockchain / DLT; P2P lending via crypto. Very small and nascent in India Instadapp, UniLend
    6. Digital lending infrastructure BaaS/SaaS loan origination and management systems; alternate data providers CreditVidya, Perfios (Perfios started as a bank statement analyser)

    7.3 WEALTHTECH — 2 themes

    1. Direct distribution — low/zero-cost discount broking and direct mutual funds. Groww, Zerodha, Upstox, Dhan.
    2. Personal Finance Management (PFM) / ROBO-ADVISORY — you state a financial goal (retirement, a daughter's marriage), answer risk-profile and demographic questions, and it recommends asset allocation and helps you invest. Fisdom, INDmoney, Scripbox, ET Money, Kuvera, smallcase.

    7.4 INSURTECH — 3 themes

    1. Digital insurance manufacturing — mostly non-life / general (P&C): motor, travel, e-commerce, bite-sized insurance. (Life insurance is far harder to digitise entirely.) Digit, ACKO.
    2. Digital platforms for insurance DISTRIBUTION — the BIGGEST theme, digitising POSPs (point-of-sale persons — individual agents/distributors). PolicyBazaar/PBpartners, Turtlemint, InsuranceDekho, RenewBuy.
    3. Digital insurance enablers / infrastructure — claims management, underwriting, risk management, alternate data.

    7.5 FINTECH INFRA — 5 themes guaranteed MCQ| Theme | Detail | Players |

    |---|---|---| | 1. BaaS — Banking as a Service | API connectivity layers; modular, lightweight SaaS architecture (pay-as-you-go instead of large licence fees), built for low-value high-volume transactions | M2P, Setu, Zeta | | 2. Account Aggregator framework (consent management) | NBFC-AA licences under the Sahamati framework; ~16 licences issued | Bridge between FIPs and FIUs | | 3. CBDC — Central Bank Digital Currency | e-Rupee in India; stable coins elsewhere. Government-initiated | RBI pilots | | 4. Open banking and digital identity enablers | eKYC, digital onboarding, fraud prevention, regulatory reporting | Signzy | | 5. Business management software for MSMEs | Billing, accounting, payroll, tax, expense management — "only if you have digitised MSMEs can you see their data, trust it, and distribute financial products" | — |

    PART C — SECTOR DEEP DIVES

    1. Payments — the UPI numbers guaranteed MCQ
    1

    Metric Value
    Total retail payments (P2P + P2M), FY20 ~$870 billion, of which UPI = 30%
    Total retail payments, FY24 ~$3.5 trillion, of which UPI = 70% (~$2.4 trillion)
    FY25 (in progress) ~75% of retail payments already on UPI
    UPI growth, last 2 years 55% p.a.; projected 25–30% p.a. going forward
    UPI as % of non-cash VOLUME 92%
    Number of QR codes in India ~650 million (elsewhere quoted as 600 million) — the largest QR market in the world

    P2P vs P2M ← guaranteed MCQ: - P2M share of UPI VALUE: 10% → 25–28% over 5 years - P2M share of UPI VOLUME: 35% → 62% - Average transaction value (ATV): P2M ≈ $8 (~₹700) · P2P ≈ $30–35 ⇒ UPI now drives even the smallest-ticket consumption.

    Cash vs non-cash in P2M/consumption ← guaranteed MCQ (memorise the ladder): $$\textbf{2018: 20\% non-cash} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{2021: 30\%} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{2024: 40\%} \;\rightarrow\; \textbf{projected: 60\% non-cash}$$ "Every three years we shave 10 percentage points off cash." Cash is still ~60% of the $2.4 trillion consumption.

    UPI P2M vs credit cards: from nothing, UPI P2M value is now ~3× credit-card transaction value in 6–7 years. ~15 billion UPI transactions/year vs ~400 million credit-card transactions. UPI P2M is ~$700–750 billion in FY25, projected $1.8–2 trillion.

    The cannibalisation point ← guaranteed MCQ: UPI has NOT cannibalised credit cards in India (credit-card penetration is very low, so both grow at the cost of cash). UPI HAS cannibalised DEBIT CARDS — debit-card spends are declining in absolute terms because the proposition became weak.

    The five UPI innovations named: credit card / credit line on UPI · UPI 123Pay (feature phones, via IVR) · UPI Lite (low-value, single-factor authentication, no remitter bank system) · internationalisation (UAE; linked to Singapore's PayNow) · coin vending machines.

    1. WealthTech numbers likely MCQ
    1

    Metric Value
    Digital platforms' share of total mutual fund INVESTORS <2% → ~35% (FY25)
    Digital platforms' share of NSE active DIRECT EQUITY retail traders ~80%
    Discount brokers' share of NSE active clients 45% → 80% in 5 years
    Direct + FinTech platform share of mutual fund AUM ~28–30%
    Investors from tier-2+ cities (FY25) 50%
    Gen Z share of investors ~45%

    Four reasons discount brokers won: (1) affordability — a flat brokerage fee per order instead of 0.3–0.5% of order value; (2) accessibility, appealing to the young, especially post-COVID; (3) DPI — eKYC and UPI made onboarding simple; (4) smartphone and internet penetration.

    Full-service brokers' response: improving UI/UX, making pricing more competitive, and product variety; HNIs and the affluent still prefer them for personalised service and high-ticket investments.

    1. InsurTech — the POSP story likely MCQ
    1

    POSP = Point of Sale Person — individual agents onboarded across insurers who sell policies at point of sale.

    Metric Value
    POSP market, FY24 ~$1.6 billion
    Growth over next 5 years more than 4×, at a ~35% CAGR
    Fastest-growing channel POSP has grown faster than every other channel (bancassurance, corporate agents, agency, brokers, direct)
    Dominant product MOTOR, then health

    1. Account Aggregators guaranteed MCQ
    1

    11.1 The four parties in the AA ecosystem ← memorise

    Party Role
    FIP — Financial Information Provider Holds your data — banks, AMCs, insurers, pension funds, GSTN. Responsible for protecting it and keeping it up to date
    AA — Account Aggregator The middleware that manages CONSENT. An NBFC-AA licensed by the RBI. (~16 licences issued)
    FIU — Financial Information User The institution that wants to provide you a financial service — the bank/NBFC/FinTech giving the loan or insurance
    The customer You — the source of consent

    A single institution can be BOTH an FIP and an FIU.

    11.2 Origins and governance

    • Born out of inter-regulatory cooperation at the FSDC — Financial Stability and Development Council, chaired by the Ministry of Finance.
    • Four regulators came together: RBI · SEBI · IRDAI · PFRDA. RBI was the nodal regulator.
    • Framework notified via the RBI NBFC-AA Master Directions, 2016.
    • ReBIT — Reserve Bank Information Technology Pvt Ltd, a wholly-owned RBI subsidiary — notifies the data standards and open API specifications.
    • Consent uses the Electronic Consent Artifact from the National Electronic Consent Framework released by MeitY (Ministry of Electronics and IT) in 2019. The artifact captures the purpose, the data range, how long it is retained, and the frequency of access.
    • Sahamati — a not-for-profit industry alliance — builds the common technology network (Central Registry, token service, and an encryption library called rahasya), does certification, and runs participatory governance (operational rules built ground-up by participants, not top-down).

    India vs the UK ← guaranteed MCQ: "The UK's open banking framework started just within the banking ecosystem. India skipped open banking altogether and dived directly into OPEN FINANCE" — APIs across banks, mutual funds, insurance, pensions and GSTN.

    UPI vs AA: UPI is a money-movement pipeline within the RBI-regulated banking domain. AA is a data-sharing pipeline across regulatory domains.

    PART D — DIGITAL PUBLIC INFRASTRUCTURE

    1. What DPI is guaranteed MCQ
    3

    The G20 Task Force definition (India-led): DPI is an infrastructure-based approach that uses technology to achieve societal goals through an ecosystem comprising technology, markets and governance, built in the public interest, that leverages competitive private innovation within regulatory guardrails.

    The road analogy ← likely MCQ: "A road is built by the government, but the private sector innovates on the vehicles — EVs, hybrids, trucks, Teslas." DPI is built by the government/public entity, on top of which private sector innovation rides.

    12.1 The THREE PILLARS of DPI

    1. Open and interoperable technology — the platform must not create barriers to entry; open architecture, interoperability, adaptable to future needs, anticipating domain capture
    2. Strengthening private sector collaboration — appropriate incentives and frameworks for private participation while keeping societal goals front and centre
    3. Robust governance mechanisms — clear roles and responsibilities, customer protection and recourse, accountability, auditability, privacy and security by design, and inclusivity

    12.2 The FIVE core DPI categories and India's instantiation guaranteed MCQ| # | Category | India's version |

    |---|---|---| | 1 | Verifiable identifiers and registries | Aadhaar | | 2 | Data sharing and credentials | DigiLocker, CoWIN | | 3 | Trust infrastructure | Aadhaar e-Sign | | 4 | Discovery and fulfilment | ONDC, OCEN, GSTN | | 5 | Payments | UPI, FASTag |

    12.3 The DPI timeline guaranteed MCQ (dates matter)| Year | Milestone |

    |---|---| | 2009 | UIDAI formed | | 2010 | Aadhaar launched — the verifiable identity layer | | 2012 | Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) introduced | | 2014 | Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY) — the zero-balance account framework, at-scale financial inclusion | | 2015 | DigiLocker | | 2016 | UPI · Aadhaar-based e-Sign · Account Aggregator framework — all three launched | | 2017 | FASTag · GSTN | | 2020 & 2022 | ONDC and OCEN — the discoverability framework |

    1. The JAM Trinity guaranteed MCQ
    3

    $$\textbf{JAM} = \textbf{Jan Dhan} + \textbf{Aadhaar} + \textbf{Mobile}$$

    Enabled: a new wave of banking accounts and, for the first time, direct benefit transfers straight into bank accounts instead of through schemes. (Aadhaar provided the easy, verifiable eKYC that let banks open millions of accounts.)

    13.1 The financial inclusion numbers guaranteed MCQ| Metric | Value |

    |---|---| | Bank accounts | 147.2 million (March 2015) → 508.7 million (November 2023) — roughly tripled | | Women's share of these accounts | more than 55% (~282 million) | | Acceleration claimed | "JAM accelerated India's financial inclusion journey by 40 years — something that would have taken 46 years took just 6 years" |

    13.2 The "Jio effect" — connectivity likely MCQ| Metric | Value |

    |---|---| | Telephone connections | 93.3 crore → 120+ crore (April 2024) | | Internet connections | 25 crore → ~100 crore (June 2024) | | Cost of 1 GB of data in India | ₹308 (2014) → ₹9 today (~$0.1) | | Cost of 1 GB elsewhere | USA ~$2.59 · UK ~$0.62 |

    1. Why UPI won guaranteed MCQ
    4

    Design choice Why it mattered
    Mobile-first UI Everybody could interact easily
    DE-BUNDLING of the payments ecosystem Banks retained their strength (holding funds, safety and security) while new apps entered and played to theirs (user interaction, user environment, virality) ⇒ PhonePe, Google Pay, CRED
    Consumer experience — the "WhatsApp moment" Send money to a VPA or mobile number in one app; a STANDARDISED experience across all apps, so switching apps doesn't disorient you
    Instant feedback Alerts and SMS confirmations ⇒ assurance of security ⇒ a smooth learning curve for new-to-banking users
    Evangelisation choices — the QR code and ZERO MDR Cards needed expensive POS machines and cost the merchant per transaction. A QR code is low-cost acceptance infrastructure, and zero MDR meant "the pani puri seller accepts digital payments as easily as McDonald's"
    INTEROPERABILITY "The living heartbeat of UPI" — any bank, any app, any recipient; no barrier to entry for new banks or players

    14.1 UPI scale and reach guaranteed MCQ| Metric | Value |

    |---|---| | FY25 transactions | ~172 billion (elsewhere quoted as 185 billion) | | Visa (same period, 200 countries) | ~234 billion — UPI beat Visa acceptance numbers by July 2025 | | Monthly transactions | 20 billion+ | | Share of India's digital payment VOLUME | ~82% | | Share of household consumption that is digital | 19% (2021) → 48% (2024) | | Countries live | 7 — Bhutan, Nepal, Mauritius, Singapore, Sri Lanka, UAE, France | | Closest global competitor | Pix (Brazil) — ~6 billion transactions/month, but Brazil has only ~150 million people |

    14.2 The UPI product timeline

    Year Milestone
    2012 Ideation
    2016 Launched
    2018 100+ banks live
    2019 Crossed 1 billion transactions per month
    2022 Credit cards on UPI
    2023 UPI spends on credit cards ~₹500 crore/month; crossed 10 billion transactions per month (August)
    2024 Expanded to 7 countries

    14.3 What is actually the innovation in UPI? the best conceptual MCQ in this course

    The instructor argues that none of the following is, on its own, a UPI innovation: - Instant payment — already existed in IMPS (2010); UPI runs on the same rails - Third-party payment initiation — chronicled in Europe (PSD2-era, ~2014–15): AISPs (account information service providers) and PISPs (payment initiation service providers) - APIs and SDKs — pre-existed (reusable code, stored procedures) - QR codes — invented long before UPI, and already used at scale by WeChat and Alipay in China

    $$\boxed{\textbf{The innovation in UPI is COMBINATORIAL INNOVATION}}$$

    "Stitching together various existing components into one product or service which will be widely adopted by consumers — that is the innovation in UPI."

    14.4 UPI's enabling environment (why it can't be repeated)

    Aadhaar → eKYC → PMJDY accounts (500 million people banked) → RuPay debit cards accustoming people to digital → DBT putting money into those accounts → demonetisation → COVID.

    "We will never see another UPI. There is nothing prior to UPI, and there will be nothing after UPI."

    14.5 UPI's risks likely MCQ

    1. Concentration risk in NPCI — "NPCI is not the regulator; it is a separate company, and a company can fail"
    2. Cyber attack risk
    3. App concentration — ~85% of UPI transactions run through just two apps (Google Pay, PhonePe), both predominantly American companies ⇒ geopolitical risk (cf. SWIFT/Visa/Mastercard bans on Russia). NPCI issued a circular capping any single player at 30% market share, but the deadline keeps being extended
    4. Outages — UPI failures in March–April 2025, with no immediate backup plan for consumers who no longer carry cash

    1. Rajesh Bansal's 10 DPI innovations guaranteed MCQ (know all ten)
    2

    # Innovation Key facts
    1 Aadhaar (digital identity) Pre-2010, ~50% of Indians had no birth certificate. Aadhaar = a unique 12-digit biometric ID. Enrolling 120 crore people across 7 lakh villages — "one of the most complicated and large-scale projects in world history"
    2 e-KYC Before this, getting a SIM card took at least 3 days with in-home verification; photocopies of passports/licences were misused. ~30 billion e-KYCs have been done
    3 AePS — Aadhaar enabled Payment System Called "FI 2.0". Before it (FI 1.0), banks issued proprietary smart cards taking 6+ months, usable at neither ATMs nor branches — a siloed system. AePS standardised the Micro-ATM (handheld device with an Aadhaar/fingerprint reader). Banking agents: <20,000 → 20 lakh outlets. ~30 crore people use it; ~40 crore transactions/month. Critical during COVID for migrants
    4 UPI Real-time payments from 2016
    5 Bharat QR Before it, a shop had 5 different proprietary QR codes — you had to ask which wallet they accepted. A common interoperable QR standard built by NPCI
    6 DigiLocker Digitally signed documents — mark sheets, certificates, driving licence, vehicle ownership, Aadhaar. Used instead of physical ID at airports
    7 e-Sign Anybody in India can digitally sign a document with just an OTP — "which is rare in the world; nobody else in the world can do that"
    8 Account Aggregator (open finance) Consent-based financial data sharing; 1.4 billion accounts accessible; NBFC-AA licences
    9 ULI — Unified Lending Interface See §17
    10 CBDC — Central Bank Digital Currency e-Rupee

    Plus FASTag — recommended by a NITI Aayog committee of five chief ministers set up by the PM during demonetisation; every new vehicle is issued a FASTag. Run by NPCI (as NETC).

    15.1 The DBT leakage story likely MCQ

    Around 2012–13, government payments (fertiliser subsidy, scholarships, MGNREGS, LPG subsidy) totalled ~₹3 lakh crore, of which on average ~20% did not reach people (some estimates say 40%) ⇒ ~₹60,000 crore lost in transit. A key cause: duplicate identities in beneficiary databases. Mapping to a unique Aadhaar number fixed this.

    The COVID contrast: "Other countries like the US were sending cheques to their people. India just pressed a button and crores of people got money in their bank accounts."

    DPI's estimated contribution: 1–2% of India's GDP.

    1. The Reserve Bank Innovation Hub (RBIH) guaranteed MCQ
    3

    Attribute Detail
    Set up 2021, by the Reserve Bank of India
    Ownership 100% owned by the RBI; a NON-PROFIT organisation
    Board chair Mr Kris Gopalakrishnan, co-founder of Infosys
    Objective Evangelising and democratising FinTech entrepreneurship
    Mission (7 words) "Frictionless finance for a billion Indians"

    16.1 The three pillars of innovation

    1. PROCESS innovation — "Does it take 10 steps to get a ₹10,000 loan? How long does KYC take?"
    2. PRODUCT innovation — "The product should be what the consumer wants, not what the supplier gives" — e.g. a flower-market vendor needs daily repayment; a salaried person needs monthly
    3. POLICY innovation — acting as interlocutor between the RBI and the FinTech/banking community

    16.2 The smallholder-farmer case guaranteed MCQ on the numbers

    Before (a smallholder farmer, <2 acres, seeking a ₹1 lakh loan):

    Friction Value
    Time 6 weeks (sometimes months)
    Visits 5–6 visits
    Out-of-pocket cost more than 5% — "not the interest rate", but payments to the village officer, engineer, lawyer
    Steps in the process 9 steps, largely manual

    After the RBIH straight-through process: all 9 steps automated via API integration (including land record verification and digital signature) ⇒ loan in 3–5 minutes with no human intervention, at no additional cost, in ~10 states. The pilot began in 2022, announced by the RBI Governor in September 2022. (The pilot began with end-to-end digitisation of the Kisan Credit Card, a working-capital loan product started ~25 years ago.)

    The banks' objection and the answer: "How can an illiterate farmer do a digital signature?" — everybody in India can digitally sign with just an OTP, thanks to Aadhaar.

    Also applied to: 8 crore dairy farmers and, for product innovation, gig workers on at least four gig platforms (Swiggy, Zomato) — the hypothesis that gig workers are bankable proved true with a WEEKLY repayment loan product.

    16.3 Why 90–95% of Indian startups fail — RBIH's four causes guaranteed MCQ| Problem | RBIH's intervention |

    |---|---| | 1. Not working on the right PROBLEM STATEMENT | RBIH does extensive primary and secondary research and stakeholder consultation to sharpen the problem definition. "It can't be a three-line statement about changing the world — which segment, and what problem are you solving?" | | 2. Lack of access to INCUBATION and MENTORSHIP (unless you're from an elite institution) | Tie-ups with the top 10 incubators — IIM Bangalore, IITs, IIM Ahmedabad | | 3. FUNDING — getting the first cheque for an MVP | Presented curated startup lists to VCs, working with the India Venture Capital Association to keep it open | | 4. Bank POCs took 12–18 months | RBIH's convening power (as an RBI-owned company) got banks to commit to a POC upfront ⇒ POC time cut to 1.5–2 months — one-sixth |

    Results: 100+ startups identified in 2023–2024 via hackathons, accelerators and pre-incubation. The "100 Futurepreneurs in 100 Days" programme with the Government of Karnataka — bootcamps for 7,000 students, launched January 2025, concluded March 2025.

    1. ULI — Unified Lending Interface guaranteed MCQ
    2

    Governor Shaktikanta Das (2024): the "NEW TRINITY" is UPI + JAM + ULI. In June 2025 the Finance Minister called ULI "the future of lending."

    Conceptualised by the RBI, designed and operated by the RBIH — a common platform that unlocks alternate data so lenders can lend beyond credit bureau scores.

    17.1 The credit gap it addresses guaranteed MCQ| Metric | Value |

    |---|---| | India's credit-to-GDP ratio | ~50% — vs China 195%, US 195%, Australia and Brazil much higher | | India's private credit to GDP | one-third of the world average | | MSME credit gap | ~$250 billion | | Gig workers without credit access | 80% | | Rural women without a credit history | 75% | | Small and marginal farmers excluded | 50% (~6 crore farming families) |

    The core insight: "These segments may not have a credit score, but they are DATA RICH. An average Indian in Bihar is more data rich than somebody in Boston."

    Examples of unlocked data: milk-pouring records (~8 crore dairy farmers; 30 lakh pour milk into Amul daily) · gig-platform ride and earnings data (Uber, Ola, Rapido, Namma Yatri) · state land records (10+ state governments tied up) · DigiLocker documents for education loans (Class 12 mark sheet, passing certificate, admission letter, fee structure) · the Vahan database (all vehicles, purchase/sale, RTO data) for two- and four-wheeler loans · WDRA warehouse depository receipts for farmers.

    Status: pilot started 2023; 50+ banks live; focus on smallholder farmers (<2 acres), dairy farmers, microfinance, business and personal finance. Demonstrated to the Prime Minister in August 2024. Integrated with MeitY's Bhashini so contracts can be signed in Tamil, Telugu, Kannada etc. — "language is not a barrier."

    ULI's advantages: faster decisions (more data than just a credit score) · better risk control (alternate + traditional data) · one pipe connecting all data elements ⇒ low cost of integration · supports Viksit Bharat by broadening credit access.

    The closing line ← likely MCQ: "The way UPI is payments today, ULI has made a humble beginning as a lending platform and will gradually become the future of lending."

    PART E — PAYMENT SYSTEMS FUNDAMENTALS

    1. Definitions guaranteed MCQ
    3

    Term Definition
    Payment The act of DISCHARGING AN OBLIGATION
    Payment instrument "Any device or set of procedures that enables the transfer of VALUE from one person to another"
    Payment system "A set of instruments, procedures and interbank fund transfer systems that ensures the circulation of money." ← NOT just technology: technology + rules + procedures + legal process
    India's definition The PSS Act says a payment system enables payment to be effected between the payer and the payee, involving clearing and settlement

    18.1 The FOUR components of a payment system

    1. Payment instrument — the tool for making payments
    2. Payment infrastructure — routing, clearing and settlement
    3. Participants — banks, payment service providers (e.g. prepaid instrument issuers), third-party service providers (Google Pay, PhonePe — "they don't hold your account; they're a front end"), and the central bank
    4. Rules and governance

    18.2 On-us vs off-us guaranteed MCQ| | ON-US transaction | OFF-US transaction |

    |---|---|---| | Definition | Payment within the SAME organisation — SBI Chennai → SBI Delhi | Payment ACROSS organisations — SBI → HDFC | | Handled by | The bank's own core banking system | The payment system — routing, clearing and settlement | | Also called | — | Interoperable / interbank transactions |

    18.3 The legal framework guaranteed MCQ

    $$\textbf{The Payment and Settlement Systems (PSS) Act, 2007}$$ It gives the RBI the authority to LICENSE, REGULATE and SUPERVISE payment system operators in India.

    The process for launching a new payment product: 1. The entity gets a payment system operator LICENCE from the RBI (e.g. NPCI in 2010) 2. It conceptualises the product, constructs the messages, and writes a PROCEDURAL DOCUMENT covering membership criteria, transaction flow, routing, clearing, settlement, dispute management, entry and exit criteria, technical/messaging standards and communication protocols 3. It returns to the RBI for AUTHORISATION of that specific instrument under the PSS Act 4. Authorisation gives the product legal sanctity

    (PSS Act sections mentioned: Section 2 defines a payment instrument; Section 2(p) defines the payment system operator.)

    Payment system operators named: banks · Visa · NPCI.

    The PSS Act also gives LEGAL FINALITY TO NETTING — once the net settlement file is settled, all underlying transactions are considered settled.

    1. Classifying payment instruments guaranteed MCQ
    1

    Axis Categories
    Cash vs non-cash The first level of segregation
    Within non-cash: paper vs non-paper Paper: cheque, demand draft. Electronic: NEFT, NACH, credit/debit cards, IMPS
    Batch vs real-time Batch: NEFT (settles every half hour), NACH. Real-time: IMPS, UPI — money is available instantly
    Large-value vs low-value In India, RTGS is the large-value system: minimum ₹2,00,000 (no ₹5 transaction allowed). Retail systems typically have no lower limit; UPI's upper limit is ₹5 lakh per transaction
    Card-based Magnetic stripe or chip
    PUSH vs PULL See §21

    1. Routing, clearing and settlement guaranteed MCQ
    1

    The central infrastructure does THREE things:

    Step Definition
    1. ROUTING Transmitting the message with account details to the DESTINATION bank. The central infrastructure looks only at which institution to send it to — "RBI is only interested in knowing which institution to send the transaction to"
    2. CLEARING Identifying and netting the debits and credits across banks over a period to produce each bank's net settlement position
    3. SETTLEMENT Actually debiting the sending bank's account and crediting the recipient bank's account

    Routing codes: for RTGS/NEFT it is the IFSC — Indian Financial System Code.

    Settlement accounts: every institution participating in a payment system maintains a SETTLEMENT ACCOUNT with the RBI.

    Where the settlement happens ← guaranteed MCQ: NPCI is NOT a settlement agency — the RBI is. "The entire settlement of all retail transactions in the country happens on the Reserve Bank of India's books." NPCI creates a net settlement file and sends it to RBI–RTGS.

    20.1 Gross vs net settlement

    GROSS settlement (RTGS) NET settlement (retail systems)
    Method Every transaction settled individually and instantly Transactions aggregated over a period and netted
    Order of steps Clearing and settlement happen FIRST, then routing Order varies by product
    Why Large value ⇒ no risk taken Reduces LIQUIDITY needs — a bank both sends and receives, so only the net position must be funded

    Settlement cycles named: NEFT settles every half hour; IMPS and UPI settle roughly every 4–5 hours.

    Risk mitigation in retail systems: banks must collateralise / PRE-FUND; NPCI allows transactions only up to that amount.

    The central infrastructure also provides transaction downloads, reconciliation, DISPUTE MANAGEMENT and MIS.

    1. Push vs pull payments guaranteed MCQ
    1

    PUSH payment (credit transfer) PULL payment (debit transfer)
    Definition You log into your own system, authorise, and push the transaction from your account You AUTHORISE somebody else to pull money from your account
    Do your credentials travel? NO — you authenticate at your bank; only transaction details travel YES — your authentication credentials (PIN/signature) travel with the message to the issuing bank
    Examples RTGS · NEFT · IMPS · UPI · NACH credit Cards (credit, debit, prepaid) · Cheque · NACH debit (e-mandate)
    Recourse if goods aren't delivered Very limited — "the bank will say you authorised the transaction" Strong — you can raise a DISPUTE / CHARGEBACK

    Why commerce runs on pull ← the conceptual MCQ: "In commerce, the buyer doesn't know the seller and the seller doesn't know the buyer. Pull transactions bring trust into the ecosystem."

    21.1 The three steps of a pull transaction

    1. AUTHORISATION — you present the card and PIN; the message goes to the network and issuer; the issuer checks balance and returns an authorisation code. (The merchant is now guaranteed payment but has not yet received the money.)
    2. CLEARING — the merchant aggregates transactions and sends them to his (acquiring) bank
    3. SETTLEMENT — routed through the card network to the issuer, who debits your account; net settlement follows

    Credit card vs debit card ← likely MCQ: the flow is identical. The difference is that a debit card debits immediately (no financial risk, so lower charges) whereas a credit card extends credit (up to ~45 days free) and carries risk, hence additional charges.

    1. Payment channels likely list MCQ
    1

    Channel Notes
    Branch Traditional; still used for paper instruments and large-value RTGS/NEFT when internet limits apply
    ATM Traditionally cash withdrawal; now also PIN change, account transfer
    POS terminal Evolved from the slugging machine → swipe (magnetic stripe) → chip (dip) → NFC tap-and-go → now with QR display. (India no longer allows magnetic-stripe cards to be issued because cloning is easier.)
    Internet banking Requires multi-factor authentication (e.g. login + OTP); limits set by each bank per its risk perception
    Mobile / mobile apps Three modes: SMS · USSD · app. App-based transactions are encrypted, so limits are higher; SMS/USSD are unencrypted, so capped (~₹5,000 per transaction)
    QR code "Quick Response" code — encodes the beneficiary's account details, so the payer doesn't type them ⇒ far fewer errors and faster. ~600 million QR codes in India
    USSD *99# — NPCI created a single number that works across ALL telcos and banks (like dialling 101 for an ambulance) so a customer needn't remember a different number per bank. Built when smartphone penetration was low; the capability still exists
    Micro-ATM A POS machine carried by a business correspondent (even postmen), with biometric capture and printing capability
    Call centre / IVR Was the key 24×7 channel before internet and mobile banking
    APIs The newest channel. Google Pay and PhonePe don't hold your account — they're a front end using APIs at the backend
    Embedded finance Payment embedded in the transaction so you don't feel you're paying separately — e.g. Uber abroad auto-charging your card at ride end
    Wearables and contactless (NFC) Tap-and-go embedded in rings and watches

    1. NPCI guaranteed MCQ
    2

    Attribute Detail
    Set up 2008, as a NOT-FOR-PROFIT company under the guidance of the RBI and IBA (Indian Banks' Association)
    Vision To be the best payment network globally
    Mission Provide every Indian with access to at least one payment service
    Stakeholders 65+ banks — public sector, private, foreign, cooperative, payments banks
    Role The umbrella organisation for ALL RETAIL PAYMENTS in India

    23.1 NPCI's three technology phases

    Phase Years Character
    Legacy technology 2010–2015 Vendor-dependent, product-specific approach, inflexible infrastructure
    Transition 2015–2020 Open-source technology, own IP creation, shift from products to platforms — the foundation for UPI
    NPCI 3.0 2021–2025 New-age technology org — AI, ML, blockchain, DeFi; ecosystem-driven; MVP-based, agile

    23.2 NPCI's product portfolio guaranteed MCQ| Product | Full form | Purpose / key facts |

    |---|---|---| | NFS | National Financial Switch | NPCI's FIRST product. India's largest ATM network — 2.59 lakh ATMs. Interoperable withdrawals, balance enquiry, PIN change, mini statements, interoperable cardless withdrawal via UPI, interoperable cash deposits | | IMPS | Immediate Payment Service | Launched 2010; real-time 24×7 interbank transfer | | RuPay | — | India's domestic card network — debit, credit, prepaid and government-benefit cards. Innovations: RuPay credit cards on UPI, recurring payments, tap-and-pay contactless | | NACH | National Automated Clearing House | Bulk credits (salaries, pensions, dividends) and bulk debits (loan EMIs, insurance premiums, utility bills), with the e-mandate | | NETC | National Electronic Toll Collection — branded FASTag | RFID tags on vehicles; extending to parking, EV charging, fuel and retail | | AePS | Aadhaar enabled Payment System | Aadhaar-based banking | | BBPS | Bharat Bill Payment System | Centralised, interoperable bill payments — electricity, telecom, education, EMI, insurance | | UPI | Unified Payments Interface | Launched 2016 | | NCMC | National Common Mobility Card | Launched 2019 — transit use cases |

    1. The individual payment systems guaranteed MCQ (comparison table)
    2

    RTGS NEFT IMPS NACH UPI
    Full form Real Time Gross Settlement National Electronic Funds Transfer Immediate Payment Service (originally Interbank Mobile Payment Service) National Automated Clearing House Unified Payments Interface
    Operated by RBI RBI NPCI NPCI NPCI
    Launched in India 2004 (evolved from EFT → SEFT → NEFT) 22 November 2010 (launched by Deputy Governor Shyamala Gopinath) 2013 (replacing ECS) 2016
    Push or pull Push Push Push Both — NACH credit (push) and NACH debit (pull) Push
    Settlement GROSS — transaction by transaction, instantly NET — half-hourly batches Net (periodic) but instant credit to the beneficiary Net, in sessions Net (periodic), instant credit
    Value limits Minimum ₹2,00,000; no upper limit No lower or upper limit Retail Bulk/file-based ₹5 lakh per transaction cap
    Message standard ISO 20022 (e.g. pacs.008 bank→RBI; pain messages customer→PSP) SFMS — Structured Financial Messaging Standard (a DOMESTIC standard, not international) — ISO 20022 —
    Availability 24×7 in India — one of only a handful of countries 24×7, half-hourly batches 24×7 Session-based 24×7
    Status Systemically important payment system — the backbone; all other retail systems settle in RTGS books The biggest retail payment system by VALUE; ~10 billion transactions in FY24–25 — second only to UPI by volume India's first 24×7 instant payment system File/bulk-based Largest faster-payments system in the world

    24.1 RTGS extra points

    • Every country has an RTGS but under different names — Fedwire in the US.
    • Run by the central bank because it is critical infrastructure: "one system where they want complete control."
    • India ran RTGS only 9:30–3:30 when launched in 2004; now 24×7.
    • Why 24×7 matters: enables linkage to global payment systems (e.g. continuous linked settlement), and reduces settlement risk and liquidity requirements — otherwise 3 holidays' worth of retail transactions would pile onto a single settlement day.

    24.2 IMPS extra points likely MCQ

    • MMID = Mobile Money Identifier — a 7-digit code: a 4-digit bank identifier issued by NPCI + a 3-digit running sequence number for that customer at that bank. Combined with the mobile number, it uniquely identifies an account.
    • The 3-digit sequence lets a customer link up to 999 accounts at the same bank to the same mobile number.
    • Renamed from Interbank Mobile Payment Service to Immediate Payment Service (~2011) when account-number + IFSC initiation was added, making it more than a mobile channel.
    • Started with 7 banks; India learned from the UK's Faster Payments system.
    • IMPS is the first payment product in India promoted by the private sector (via NPCI) — everything before it (cheque clearing, RTGS, NEFT, ECS) was run by the RBI. (Cards excepted.)
    • A key design feature: BOTH sender and recipient get a confirmation — "I could track a ₹10 courier end to end but not a payment."
    • UPI runs on the IMPS rails. "UPI's front end is beautiful, but it still runs on the IMPS backend rail."

    24.3 NACH extra points

    • Replaced ECS (Electronic Clearing Service), which was regional — 16 regional clearing houses — so a pan-India company (e.g. Indian Railways) had to create separate files per region.
    • File/bulk based: initiated by corporates, not individuals. Examples: payroll for thousands of employees, government benefit disbursement, Reliance Industries paying dividends to all shareholders.
    • Session-based with a cutoff, a net settlement file settled via RTGS, distribution to banks, and a RETURN CYCLE for failed credits (account closed/invalid).
    • NACH debit (e-mandate) = a mandate replacing multiple post-dated cheques: it specifies the amount, periodicity and validity period, and can carry a maximum amount cap so a mistake can't wipe out the account. Available as a physical mandate in cheque format (so it can use the same cheque truncation imaging system, giving accept/reject within 24 hours) or as an electronic mandate.

    1. Cards — the MANIAC flow guaranteed MCQ
    2

    The instructor's mnemonic for a card transaction: M-A-N-I-A-C

    Letter Party What happens
    M Merchant You swipe/dip/tap and enter your PIN; the terminal captures card credentials and encrypts them
    A Acquirer The merchant's bank. If the card was issued by the same bank ⇒ an ON-US transaction, processed internally
    N Network Determined from the card's first 6 digits (the BIN) against a global table — Visa, Mastercard, RuPay, Discover, China UnionPay
    I Issuer The network looks up its BIN repository to find the issuing bank and forwards the message
    AC ACcount The issuer checks balance and PIN, then sends an authorisation code back along the same path

    After authorisation: the merchant prints the charge slip, hands over the goods, and is GUARANTEED payment — "it doesn't matter if you never pay your credit card bill; the merchant is guaranteed payment because he obtained authorisation."

    25.1 Why the card ecosystem is hard to displace the conceptual MCQ

    $$\textbf{The NETWORK provides TRUST between two unknown parties.}$$ - Guarantee to the merchant: payment regardless of whether the cardholder pays the bank — provided the merchant followed the rules - Guarantee to the consumer: the ability to dispute and obtain a CHARGEBACK if the goods aren't delivered and the merchant can't prove delivery - Networks keep billions of dollars in liquid funds — typically 3–4 days of average transaction value — to guarantee settlement - This is what MDR pays for: the liquidity, the rules and enforcement, and dispute management

    The card structure: a 16-digit number whose first 6 digits identify the issuer/network — "conceptually like MMID."

    1. BBPS and CBDC likely MCQ
    1

    BBPS — Bharat Bill Payment System: a centralised bill payment system operated by NPCI, connecting billers and customers via banks and apps for all recurring bills — electricity, telecom, education, EMI, insurance. Features: interoperability, instant confirmation, multiple access points.

    CBDC — Central Bank Digital Currency: - More than 135 countries were exploring CBDC as of 2024 - Centrally created and regulated by central banks, carrying the same trust and backing as currency - Two models: RETAIL CBDC (everyday use — facing limited public awareness, privacy scepticism, and debate over necessity) and WHOLESALE CBDC (interbank settlement — gaining traction faster, reducing cost, improving speed, minimising risk in large-value transactions) - India: the RBI has launched pilots, focused on how CBDC can coexist with UPI, support financial inclusion, and improve cross-border settlement efficiency

    PART F — REGULATION, TECHNOLOGY, RISK AND CAREERS

    1. The regulatory timeline guaranteed MCQ
    4

    Year Regulation
    2007 Payment and Settlement Systems Act
    2016 KYC Directions; NBFC-AA Master Directions
    2020 Guidelines for Payment Aggregators and Payment Gateways (PA-PG)
    2021 and beyond Directions on PPIs (prepaid payment instruments); stricter KYC norms; IT governance frameworks

    The framing: "The regulatory environment in India is not about restricting innovation — it is about guiding FinTech growth in a safe, transparent and sustainable way."

    27.1 RBI and Ministry of Finance initiatives

    Initiative Purpose
    SRO framework for fintechs See §27.2
    RBI Innovation Hub + the FinTech Repository Fintechs must regularly update information on their products, technology and operations ⇒ transparency for regulators
    PRAVAAH Portal A secure, centralised platform where entities apply for authorisations, licences or approvals ⇒ streamlines compliance and reduces delay
    ULI Digitises access to credit data, addressing agriculture and MSME demand
    Retail Direct Lets retail customers apply directly for government bonds and securities via smartphone

    27.2 Self-Regulatory Organisations (SROs) guaranteed MCQ

    Why: rapidly evolving industries outpace traditional regulation. An SRO is an industry-led mechanism that sets and enforces rules, monitors practices and protects consumers.

    The RBI recognised SRO for FinTech: the FinTech Association for Consumer Empowerment (FACE).

    Areas covered: fair practices, transparency, responsible lending, grievance redressal. Five benefits: consumer protection · fair practices (keeping bad actors out) · industry ownership of standards · faster adaptability · reduced burden on the central bank.

    Other Indian SROs named likely list MCQ - AMFI — Association of Mutual Funds in India - IBAI — Insurance Brokers Association of India - FIMMDA — Fixed Income Money Market and Derivatives Association - FIDC — Finance Industry Development Council (for NBFCs)

    27.3 India vs global payment systems

    System Character
    UPI (India) Instant, low-cost, INTEROPERABLE, expanding internationally
    Alipay (China) Largely a CLOSED-LOOP ecosystem
    Card networks Take days to settle
    Pix (Brazil) Similar to UPI, but UPI's global interoperability gives it an edge

    27.4 FinTech's measured impact likely numeric MCQ| Metric | Value |

    |---|---| | Active UPI users | 420+ million | | Merchants onboarded on UPI | 100 million | | Demat accounts | 171 million | | MSME loans disbursed | ~3.5 lakh per month | | Reduction in KYC cost | ~85% | | Reduction in loan processing cost | ~95% | | Reduction in account opening time | ~65% |

    The six FinTech domains: banking · insurance · lending · investments · payments · regulatory (RegTech) and tax.

    1. Emerging protocols and technologies likely MCQ
    3

    Protocol = a set of rules and standards defining how data is formatted, transmitted and governed between participants.

    Protocol Description
    Beckn An open, decentralised protocol standardising digital commerce; enables interoperability so a customer on one app can interact with services on another. Underpins ONDC
    OCEN A blockchain-based protocol for decentralised, privacy-preserving data sharing and monetisation

    Benefits of evolving protocols: interoperability · security · transparency · inclusiveness.

    28.1 AI in FinTech

    • Process side: fraud detection, preventing suspicious activity, optimising payment routing, predictive analytics for risk management
    • Client side: personalisation, chatbots, robo-advisors, targeted promotions
    • NPCI's applications ← likely MCQ: fraud and MULE detection — "unlike banks that see only a limited slice of transactions, NPCI has a network-wide view across banks, fintechs and aggregators" · SYNTHETIC DATA — artificially generated data mirroring real-world patterns without exposing actual customer information, used for fraud model training, load testing and academic research · ANPR (Automatic Number Plate Recognition) integrated into toll and parking, linked to FASTag

    28.2 Blockchain — the five components guaranteed MCQ

    $$\textbf{Nodes} \;\cdot\; \textbf{Ledger} \;\cdot\; \textbf{Blocks} \;\cdot\; \textbf{Cryptography} \;\cdot\; \textbf{Consensus}$$ (Nodes validate transactions; the ledger is the shared record; blocks hold the data; cryptography secures it; consensus ensures everyone agrees on validity.)

    Use cases: cross-border payments · cryptocurrencies · P2P lending · tokenisation of assets · tamper-proof compliance audits · digital identity verification.

    28.3 Tokenisation — four benefits likely list MCQ

    Definition: transforming real-world or digital assets (stocks, bonds, real estate, data) into digital tokens recorded and transferred on a blockchain/DLT.

    1. Instant, cost-effective settlement — near real time, fewer intermediaries
    2. FRACTIONAL OWNERSHIP — high-value assets (real estate, art) broken into smaller units, widening the investor pool
    3. Programmable finance — smart contracts coding conditions directly into transactions (automatic loan payments, dividend distribution)
    4. Security enhancement — tampering and fraud significantly reduced

    28.4 Other emerging technologies

    • Unified Ledger — a single consolidated digital ledger integrating multiple contract and transaction types; eliminates silos; a potential backbone for central bank–commercial bank collaboration
    • Financial Internet — financial services embedded directly into the infrastructure of the internet, so payments are integrated into communications and social media without separate intermediaries
    • Generative AI in finance — advanced risk modelling, hyper-personalisation, automated advisory
    • IoT — connected devices and wearables as payment channels

    1. Security, compliance, risk and governance guaranteed MCQ
    7

    29.1 The four layers of security requirements

    Layer Requirements
    Infrastructure Network security, vulnerability management, business continuity planning
    Application Secure coding, robust APIs, penetration testing
    Data Encryption, localisation, backups, strong access controls
    Privacy Clear user consent, transparency, compliance with data protection laws

    29.2 Compliance vs Risk vs Governance ← memorise the three definitions

    Focus
    COMPLIANCE Following EXTERNAL rules and regulations — AML, KYC, data privacy laws, fraud prevention standards, adapting to regulatory change. "Compliance keeps fintechs LAWFUL"
    RISK MANAGEMENT Identifying, analysing and addressing risks — financial, operational, reputational, security — with controls to minimise impact. "Risk management keeps them SAFE"
    GOVERNANCE The decision-making framework and accountability structure — leadership oversight and clearly defined responsibility. "Governance keeps them ACCOUNTABLE"

    Why they matter — four reasons: regulatory requirement (penalties, restrictions, even shutdown) · trust and reputation · consumer protection · competitive advantage (compliant fintechs scale faster and attract investment).

    Challenges: rising cyber security threats and fraud · third-party/vendor risk · reputation risk amplified by social media · adapting KYC and AML across multiple jurisdictions.

    29.3 The three types of risk guaranteed MCQ| Type | Examples |

    |---|---| | OPERATIONAL | From internal processes, people or systems — API vulnerabilities, internal threats, model bias in AI-driven decisions | | FINANCIAL | Monetary losses, credit defaults, market volatility, liquidity mismatch — e.g. unsecured lending has higher default rates | | REGULATORY | Failure to comply — KYC/AML breaches, mishandling customer data ⇒ heavy penalties and loss of trust |

    29.4 The five-step risk prevention framework guaranteed MCQ (order matters)

    $$\textbf{1. IDENTIFY} \rightarrow \textbf{2. ANALYSE} \rightarrow \textbf{3. EVALUATE} \rightarrow \textbf{4. TREAT} \rightarrow \textbf{5. MONITOR}$$

    Treat offers four options: eliminate · mitigate with controls · transfer (through insurance) · accept (within tolerance).

    29.5 The THREE LINES OF DEFENCE guaranteed MCQ| Line | Who | Role |

    |---|---|---| | 1st | Day-to-day business operations | Own and manage risk; apply controls | | 2nd | Compliance and risk oversight | Guide, monitor, ensure adherence to policies | | 3rd | Internal audit | Independent assurance to the board and senior management |

    29.6 Fraud likely MCQ

    A modern fraud pattern: victim searches for a courier helpline → lands on a fake number posted by a fraudster → is sent a phishing link disguised as a complaint tracker → installs it → malware harvests OTPs and credentials → unauthorised transactions.

    Future frauds: caller-ID spoofing apps mimicking legitimate institutions; deepfakes powered by generative AI creating convincing voices and faces ⇒ social engineering much harder to detect.

    NPCI's mitigations likely list MCQ 1. Disabling non-EMV cards and fallback transactions on the NFS network (prevents card fraud) 2. Device binding protocols to prevent malware injection during customer registration 3. Displaying the BENEFICIARY NAME exactly as recorded in the bank's core system before the transaction (also prevents wrong credits from mistyped account numbers)

    "Fraud mitigation is not a one-time fix — it's a continuous battle of innovation versus exploitation."

    29.7 The DPDP Act guaranteed MCQ

    Digital Personal Data Protection Act — India's FIRST-EVER privacy act.

    Two balanced objectives: protecting individuals' rights over their personal information, and allowing organisations to process data for legitimate purposes in a structured, responsible way.

    Key provisions: - Personal data = information that can directly or indirectly identify an individual - Processing is generally restricted to within India, but data may be transferred abroad if it is for offering goods or services to individuals in India - Covers both digital data AND digitised non-digital records

    The FOUR rights of a "data principal" (the individual) ← memorise: 1. The right to receive clear information about how their data is being used 2. The right to correct and delete their personal data 3. The right to seek grievance redressal if data is misused 4. The right to nominate someone else to exercise these rights on their behalf

    Individuals must also comply when exercising these rights; non-compliance can attract penalties up to ₹10,000.

    For organisations: stronger frameworks for consent management, data minimisation, storage and disposal.

    "Data is not just an asset — it's a fundamental right of the individual."

    1. Careers in FinTech likely MCQ
    1

    The distinguishing point: "Unlike big tech firms, for whom finance is just a side business, fintechs specialise entirely in financial innovation."

    Role What it involves Skills
    Product manager "The heart of FinTech innovation" — design and build products with engineering, design and marketing Product thinking, roadmap planning, stakeholder management
    Sales / business development Adoption and partnerships — merchant onboarding, enterprise clients, insurance partnerships Communication and negotiation
    Operations manager KYC processes, settlements, customer onboarding — essential for scaling —
    Risk and compliance analyst Monitoring AML, KYC and fraud; ensuring compliance. Growing rapidly as regulators tighten norms —
    AI/ML engineer Credit scoring, fraud detection, chatbots, personalisation Python, ML libraries, cloud platforms, APIs + financial domain knowledge
    Customer success Onboarding, training and retaining customers —
    UI/UX designer "A well-designed app makes the difference between adoption and abandonment" — one of the most in-demand skills —

    AePS = FI 2.0; what was FI 1.0? Proprietary smart cards taking 6+ months, usable at neither ATMs nor branches — a siloed system

    e-Sign's uniqueness Anyone in India can digitally sign with just an OTP — rare in the world

    DPI's contribution to GDP 1–2%

    RBIH's three pillars Process · Product · Policy innovation
    The farmer loan: before → after 6 weeks, 5–6 visits, >5% out-of-pocket, 9 manual steps → 3–5 minutes, no human intervention
    The gig-worker product finding They want WEEKLY repayment
    Why 90–95% of startups fail (RBIH's four causes) Wrong problem statement · no incubation/mentorship · funding · 12–18 month bank POCs (cut to 1.5–2 months)
    The "new trinity" UPI + JAM + ULI
    India's credit-to-GDP ratio vs China/US ~50% vs 195% — India is one-third the world average
    MSME credit gap ~$250 billion
    Excluded segments 80% of gig workers · 75% of rural women · 50% of small/marginal farmers (~6 crore families)
    ULI's core insight "They may not have a credit score, but they are DATA RICH — an average Indian in Bihar is more data rich than someone in Boston"
    ULI status Pilot from 2023; 50+ banks live; shown to the PM in August 2024; integrated with Bhashini
    A payment is… The act of DISCHARGING AN OBLIGATION
    Payment instrument defined Any device or set of procedures enabling the transfer of value
    Payment system defined Instruments + procedures + interbank fund transfer systems — technology PLUS rules and processes
    On-us vs off-us Within one bank (core banking system) vs across banks (goes to the payment system)
    The governing Act Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007 — gives RBI power to licence, regulate and supervise
    The three central-infrastructure functions Routing · Clearing · Settlement
    Routing code for RTGS/NEFT IFSC
    Who settles all retail transactions? The RBI — NPCI is NOT a settlement agency
    Why net settlement? Reduces LIQUIDITY needs
    Push vs pull You authorise and push (credentials do NOT travel) vs you authorise someone to pull (credentials DO travel)
    Push examples / pull examples RTGS, NEFT, IMPS, UPI, NACH credit / Cards, cheque, NACH debit
    Which gives dispute/chargeback recourse? PULL (cards) — push payments give very limited recourse
    USSD code for banking *99# — one number across all telcos and banks
    SMS/USSD transaction cap ~₹5,000 (unencrypted)
    NPCI — set up / type / mission 2008 · not-for-profit under RBI and IBA · "every Indian with access to at least one payment service"
    NPCI's first product NFS — National Financial Switch (2.59 lakh ATMs)
    NPCI's three tech phases Legacy (2010–15) · Transition (2015–20) · NPCI 3.0 (2021–25)
    RTGS: operator, settlement, minimum RBI · gross, real-time · ₹2,00,000 minimum; ISO 20022; 24×7 in India; launched 2004
    Fedwire is The US RTGS
    NEFT: settlement, limits, message standard Net, half-hourly batches · no lower or upper limit · SFMS (a DOMESTIC standard) — largest retail system by value
    IMPS: launched, by whom, original name 22 Nov 2010 · NPCI · Interbank Mobile Payment Service
    MMID Mobile Money Identifier — 7 digits = 4-digit bank ID + 3-digit sequence; allows up to 999 accounts per mobile number per bank
    IMPS's historical significance The first payment product in India promoted by the private sector (via NPCI), other than cards
    UPI runs on which rails? IMPS
    NACH replaced ECS, which was regional (16 clearing houses)
    NACH is initiated by Corporates, not individuals — bulk/file-based
    NACH debit mandate An e-mandate specifying amount, periodicity, validity and a maximum cap
    The card flow mnemonic M-A-N-I-A-C — Merchant, Acquirer, Network, Issuer, ACcount
    What identifies the network/issuer? The first 6 digits of the 16-digit card number (the BIN)
    What does the card network really provide? TRUST between two unknown parties — guarantee to merchant + chargeback to consumer
    Card networks hold how much liquidity? 3–4 days of average transaction value
    BBPS Bharat Bill Payment System — NPCI-operated, interoperable bill payments
    CBDC: countries exploring / India's status 135+ (2024) / RBI pilots; wholesale gaining traction faster than retail
    NCMC National Common Mobility Card (2019) — transit
    NETC National Electronic Toll Collection = FASTag (RFID)
    Regulatory timeline PSS Act 2007 · KYC Directions & NBFC-AA 2016 · PA-PG guidelines 2020 · PPI directions and IT governance 2021+
    PRAVAAH Portal Centralised platform for applying for authorisations, licences and approvals
    The RBI-recognised FinTech SRO FACE — FinTech Association for Consumer Empowerment
    Other Indian SROs AMFI · IBAI · FIMMDA · FIDC
    Alipay vs UPI Alipay is a closed loop; UPI is open and interoperable
    FinTech cost reductions KYC −85% · loan processing −95% · account opening time −65%
    Beckn / OCEN Open decentralised commerce protocol (underpins ONDC) / blockchain-based privacy-preserving data sharing
    NPCI's AI applications Fraud and MULE detection (network-wide view) · SYNTHETIC DATA · ANPR for tolls
    Blockchain's five components Nodes · Ledger · Blocks · Cryptography · Consensus
    Tokenisation's four benefits Instant settlement · FRACTIONAL OWNERSHIP · programmable finance (smart contracts) · security
    Compliance / risk / governance Lawful / safe / accountable
    The three risk types Operational · Financial · Regulatory
    The five risk-prevention steps Identify → Analyse → Evaluate → Treat → Monitor
    The four "treat" options Eliminate · mitigate · transfer (insurance) · accept
    The three lines of defence Business operations · compliance & risk oversight · internal audit
    NPCI's three fraud mitigations Disabling non-EMV/fallback on NFS · device binding · displaying the beneficiary name from the core system
    DPDP Act India's FIRST privacy act; personal data = directly or indirectly identifying; processing generally within India; covers digital AND digitised records
    The four rights of a data principal Information · correction & deletion · grievance redressal · nomination
    DPDP penalty on individuals Up to ₹10,000 for non-compliance
    Most in-demand FinTech design skill UI/UX — "the difference between adoption and abandonment"
